Semitopological Barycentric Algebras
Jean Goubault-Larrecq

Abstract
Barycentric algebras are an abstraction of the notion of convex sets, defined by a set of equations. We study semitopological and topological barycentric algebras, in the spirit of a previous study by Klaus Keimel on semitopological and topological cones (2008), which are special cases of semitopological and topological barycentric algebras. MSC classification [2020]: 52A01, 46A55, 52A07, 54E99



1
Introduction

Functional analysis is mostly done on topological vector spaces.
Klaus Keimel set out the basis for functional analysis in a
T_{0}

setting in
[
30
]
, by studying topological and
semitopological
cones
. In a nutshell, a cone is a structure
that obeys all the axioms of a real vector space, except without
opposites or multiplication by negative scalars. Many standard
theorems still hold in this setting, and the space of all (possibly
unbounded) measures on a space
X
, is a prime example of a cone that
is not a vector space.

The space of probability measures on
X
is not a cone, because you
cannot multiply a probability measure by any scalar except
1
in
order to obtain a probability measure, but it still has some intrinsic
structure. We may say that it is a convex subset of a cone, but there
is a more intrinsic view to it: it is a
barycentric algebra

with a compatible topology. Barycentric algebras are sets with binary
operations
+_{a}
with
a\in[0,1]
that model taking a convex
combination of two elements with weight
a
and
1-a
respectively,
and obey specific axioms. Barycentric algebras were studied by
Marshall H. Stone
[
47
]
and Hellmuth Kneser

[
34
]
independently, then by Walter D. Neumann

[
37
]
and Tadeusz Świrszcz

[
49
,
48
,
50
]
. Joe
Flood
[
11
]
, and then Anna B. Romanowska and
Jonathan D. H. Smith studied them in more depth
[
41
]
, as
well as other authors, which we will not cite (see

[
29
, Remarks 2.9]
for a more complete bibliography). Apart
from a (crucial) notion of algebraically open sets, no topology is
involved in those papers. Klaus Keimel and Gordon D. Plotkin studied
ordered barycentric algebras, and then barycentric algebras with a
partial ordering that is directed-complete and makes the algebraic
operations (Scott-)continuous
[
29
]
. We wish to develop
this further, and, imitating
[
30
]
somewhat, produce
a theory of semitopological and topological barycentric algebras.

2
Preliminaries

Cones will play a prominent role in this paper. By cone, we do not
mean cones embedded in real vector spaces, rather the following more
abstract version, used in
[
30
,
29
]
, but
already studied by Benno Fuchssteiner and Wolfgang Lusky

[
12
]
: a
cone
is a commutative monoid
(\mathfrak{C},+,0)

(or just
\mathfrak{C}
for short) with an action
a,x\mapsto a\cdot x
of
the semi-ring
\mathbb{R}_{+}
on
\mathfrak{C}
, that is:

\begin{array}[]{ccc}0\cdot x=0&(ab)\cdot x=a\cdot(b\cdot x)\\
1\cdot x=x&a\cdot 0=0\\
a\cdot(x+y)=a\cdot x+a\cdot y&(a+b)\cdot x=a\cdot x+b\cdot x\end{array}

(1)

for all
a,b\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
and
x,y\in\mathfrak{C}
. We will often write
ax
for
a\cdot x
.

An
preordered cone
(resp.,
ordered cone
) is a cone
\mathfrak{C}

together with a preordering (resp., an ordering)
\leq
, such that
addition and scalar multiplication are monotonic in each argument. A

semipreordered cone
(resp.,
semiordered cone
) is a cone
with a preordering (resp., an ordering) such that addition is
monotonic, and for every
a\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
,
a\cdot\_
is monotonic. In a
preordered cone
\mathfrak{C}
,
0
is a least element, since for every

x\in\mathfrak{C}
,
0=0\cdot x\leq 1\cdot x=x
; not so in a
semipreordered cone. We will not consider semipreordered and
semiordered cones, except for references to the literature.

Remark 2.1

With these definitions, we stray from the literature: e.g., Keimel

[
30
]
, Keimel and Plotkin
[
29
]
, and
several others, call
ordered
cone what we call semiordered
cone. Our ordered cones are their
pointed
ordered cones,
namely the ordered cones (in their sense) such that
0
is a least
element; this is equivalent to requiring that
\_\cdot x
is
monotonic for every
x\in\mathfrak{C}

[
30
, Lemma 3.5,
Definition 3.6]
. All our ordered, preordered or
semitopological cones will be pointed, and therefore we prefer to
follow
[
53
]
here, who assumes monotonicity both in
a
and
in
x
.

A function
f\colon\mathfrak{C}\to\mathfrak{D}
between cones is
positively
homogeneous
if and only if
f(a\cdot x)=a\cdot f(x)
for all

a\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
and
x\in\mathfrak{C}
. It is
additive
if and only if

f(x+y)=f(x)+f(y)
for all
x,y\in C
, and
linear
if
it is positively homogeneous and additive. This applies notably when

\mathfrak{D}
is the cone
\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\mathbb{R}_{+}\cup\{\infty\}
. When
\mathfrak{D}
is
a preordered cone (as
\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
is, for example),
f
is

superadditive
(resp.
subadditive
) if and only if

f(x+y)\geq f(x)+f(y)
(resp.
f(x+y)\leq f(x)+f(y)
)
for all
x,y\in C
, and is
superlinear
(resp.
sublinear
) if and only if it is superadditive (resp. subadditive) and positively homogeneous.

Cones and linear maps form a category, which we write as
\mathbf{Cone}
.

Let us say
poset
for partially ordered set. We can equip every
poset
(P,\leq)
(or just
P
for short) with various topologies. We
will be particularly interested in the
Scott topology
, whose
open subsets are the subsets
U
that are upwards-closed (for every

x\in U
, for every
y\in P
such that
x\leq y
,
y
is in
U
)
and such that that every directed family
{(x_{i})}_{i\in I}
whose
supremum exists in
P
and is in
U
, some
x_{i}
is already in
U
.
When this is needed, we write
P_{\sigma}
for
P
with its Scott
topology. We will dispense with the
\sigma
subscript when
P=\mathbb{R}_{+}
:
we will always equip
\mathbb{R}_{+}
with its Scott topology, and its open
subsets are
]a,\infty[
where
a\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
,
\mathbb{R}_{+}
and the empty set.
We will do the same with
\mathbb{R}_{+}\smallsetminus\{0\}
and with

\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\mathbb{R}_{+}\cup\{\infty\}
, where
\infty
is a fresh element
larger than every element of
\mathbb{R}_{+}
. The Scott topology is
particularly important on
dcpos
(short for “directed-complete
partial order”), which are posets in which every directed family has
a supremum;
\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
is a dcpo,
\mathbb{R}_{+}
is not. Given two posets
P

and
Q
, the continuous maps
f\colon P_{\sigma}\to Q_{\sigma}
are
exactly the
Scott-continuous
maps, namely the functions

f\colon P\to Q
that are monotonic and preserve directed suprema.
We refer to
[
19
,
13
,
1
]
for notions
of domain theory and non-Hausdorff topology.

We write
\text{int}({A})
for the interior of
A
and
cl(A)
for the
closure of
A
. Compact sets are defined without any requirement of
separation, namely they are those sets such that every open cover has
a finite subcover. Every finite set
A
is compact, as well as
\upc A
with
A
finite.

Every topological space
X
comes with a specialization preordering

\leq
, defined by
x\leq y
if and only if every open neighborhood
of
x
contains
y
. It is a partial ordering if and only if
X
is

T_{0}
. The specialization preordering of a poset
P
is its original
ordering. A subset of a space
X
is
saturated
if and
only if it is equal to the intersection of its open neighborhoods,
equivalently if it is upwards-closed in the specialization
preordering. The
saturation
of
A
in
X
is the intersection
of the open neighborhoods of
A
, and is equal to

\upc A\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\{y\in X\mid\exists x\in A,x\leq y\}
.

A
c-space
is a topological space
X
such that every point has
a basis of neighborhoods of the form
\upc y
, where

\upc y\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\{z\in X\mid y\leq z\}
; in other words, for every

x\in X
, for every open neighborhood
U
of
x
, there is a point

y\in U
such that
x\in\text{int}({\upc y})

[
9
]
. For
example,
\mathbb{R}_{+}
is a c-space in its Scott topology.

In general, all continuous posets from domain theory are c-spaces in
their Scott topology. The definition goes as follows. In every poset

P
, the
way-below
relation
\ll
is defined by
x\ll y
if
and only if every directed subset
D
of
P
whose supremum exists and
is larger than or equal to
y
contains an element larger than or
equal to
x
. Let
\hbox to0.0pt{$\downarrow$\hss}\raise 2.15277pt\hbox{$\downarrow$}x\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\{y\in P\mid y\ll x\}
.
P

is
continuous
if and only if
\hbox to0.0pt{$\downarrow$\hss}\raise 2.15277pt\hbox{$\downarrow$}x
is directed for every

x\in P
, and
x
is its supremum. If so, the Scott topology has a
base of open subsets of the form

\hbox to0.0pt{$\uparrow$\hss}\raise 2.15277pt\hbox{$\uparrow$}x\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\{y\in P\mid x\ll y\}
,
x\in P
; such sets are
equal to the interior
\text{int}({\upc x})
in the Scott topology. For
example, in
\mathbb{R}_{+}
and in
\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
,
s\ll t
if and only if
s=0
or

s<t
.

We will use various notions of embeddings. An
order embedding

of a poset
P
in a poset
Q
is a function
f\colon P\to Q
that
preserves and reflects orders, namely
f(x)\leq f(y)
if and only
if
x\leq y
. Every order embedding is injective. We decide to call

preorder embedding
any order-preserving and reflecting map
between preordered sets, despite the fact that such a map may fail to
be injective.

A
topological embedding

f\colon X\to Y
between topological
spaces is a homeomorphism of
X
onto the image of
f
, with the
subspace topology induced by the inclusion in
Y
. Equivalently, it
is an injective continuous map that is
full
, in the sense that
for every open subset
U
of
X
, there is an open subset
V
of
Y

such that
U=f^{-1}(V)
. The term “full” originates from

[
14
]
. A full continuous map is injective, hence a
topological embedding, if
X
is
T_{0}
.

A
semitopological cone
is a cone with a topology that makes
+

and
\cdot
separately continuous—the topology on
\mathbb{R}_{+}
being the
Scott topology
[
30
, Definition 4.2]
. It is

topological
if
+
and
\cdot
are jointly continuous

[
30
, Definition 4.2]
. It turns out that if
\cdot

is separately continuous, then it is jointly continuous

[
30
, Corollary 6.9(a)]
, because of the following,
which we will call Ershov’s observation

[
10
, Proposition 2]
: given a c-space
X
, and two
topological spaces
Y
and
Z
, and function

f\colon X\times Y\to Z
is jointly continuous if and only if it is
separately continuous. (In all fairness, a more general statement had
been obtained by Jimmie D. Lawson
[
36
, Theorem 2]
,
extending and fixing a previous result due to Banaschewski

[
4
, Proposition 6]
, which says that the class of
spaces
X
such that the latter property holds is the class of locally
finitary compact spaces—which include the c-spaces. A space is

locally finitary compact
if and only if every point has a base
of neighborhoods of the form
\upc E\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\bigcup_{y\in E}\upc y

with
E
finite.) It also follows from Ershov’s observation that
every
c-cone
, namely every semitopological cone whose
underlying topological space is a c-space, is topological.

Given a semitopological cone
\mathfrak{C}
, every positively homogeneous lower
semicontinuous function
f\colon\mathfrak{C}\to\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
gives rise to a
proper open subset
f^{-1}(]1,\infty])
of
\mathfrak{C}
. Conversely, every
proper open subset
U
of
\mathfrak{C}
gives rise to a positively homogeneous
lower semicontinuous function
M^{U}
, called its
upper Minkowski
functional
:

M^{U}(x)\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\sup\{r\in\mathbb{R}_{+}\smallsetminus\{0\}\mid(1/r)\cdot x\in U\}

[
30
, Section 7]
. The two constructions are
inverse of each other, and are monotonic: if
U\subseteq V
then

M^{U}\leq M^{V}
(pointwise), and if
f\leq g
then

f^{-1}(]1,\infty])\subseteq g^{-1}(]1,\infty])
. This bijective
correspondence specializes to one between superlinear, resp. sublinear, resp. linear lower semicontinuous functions and proper
convex open sets, resp. proper concave open sets, resp. proper open
half-spaces; a set is
convex
if and only if it is closed under
all operations
+_{a}
,
a\in[0,1]
,
concave
if and only if
its complement is convex, and a
half-space
if and only if it is
both convex and concave.

Here are a few examples of semitopological cones.

Example 2.2

\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
, with its usual notions of addition, multiplication, and
the Scott topology of the usual ordering
\leq
, is a c-cone, hence
a topological cone.

Example 2.3
(Example 3.5 in
[
15
]
)

For every space
X
, let
\mathcal{L}X
be the set of all lower
semicontinuous maps from
X
to
\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
, namely the set of all
continuous maps from
X
to the space
\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
with its Scott
topology. We equip it with pointwise addition, pointwise
multiplication by a scalar, and this yields a semitopological cone
when given the Scott topology of the pointwise ordering. When
X

is core-compact, namely when the lattice
{\mathcal{O}}X
of open subsets
of
X
is a continuous dcpo with respect to inclusion,
\mathcal{L}X
is
a continuous dcpo, hence a c-cone, hence a topological cone.

There are other topologies that make
\mathcal{L}X
a semitopological
cone, for example the topology of pointwise convergence (since then

\mathcal{L}X
appears as a subpace of the product
{\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}}^{X}
), or the
weak
*
upper topology (see
[
30
, Example 5.3]
);
the latter is always topological.

Example 2.4
(Section 6.1 in
[
23
]
)

(See also Example 4.5 in
[
30
]
and Example 3.6 in

[
15
]
.) Every sup-semilattice
L
that is pointed in the
sense that it has a least element
0
is canonically an ordered cone
with supremum
\vee
as addition and scalar multiplication defined
by
a\cdot x\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}x
if
a\neq 0
,
a\cdot x\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}0

otherwise. With the Scott topology, it is a semitopological cone,
and a topological cone if
L
is a continuous poset

[
23
]
.

The next example relies on a notion that is close to measures, and
which is in a sense our central object of study:
continuous
valuations

[
43
,
35
,
25
,
26
]
. Let

{\mathcal{O}}X
denote the lattice of open subsets of a space
X
. This is
in particular a dcpo; so is
\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
. A
continuous valuation

on a space
X
is a map
\nu\colon{\mathcal{O}}X\to\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
that is

strict
(
\nu(\emptyset)=0
),
modular
(for all
U,V\in{\mathcal{O}}X
,

\nu(U)+\nu(V)=\nu(U\cup V)+\nu(U\cap V)
) and
Scott-continuous. Among all continuous valuations, we find the

simple valuations

\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\delta_{x_{i}}
, where

n\in\mathbb{N}
, each
a_{i}
is in
\mathbb{R}_{+}
and each
x_{i}
is a point of
X
;
the
Dirac valuation

\delta_{x}
maps every
U\in{\mathcal{O}}X
to

1
if
x\in U
, to
0
otherwise.

We say that
\nu
is a
probability
valuation
if and only if
\nu(X)=1
, and a
subprobability
valuation if
and only if
\nu(X)\leq 1
.

We will need to touch upon Reinhold Heckmann’s notion of

point-continuous
valuation
[
23
]
from time to time.
Let
{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathrm{p}}X
denote the set of open subsets of
X
with the

pointwise topology
, whose subbasic open sets are

[x\in]\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\{U\in{\mathcal{O}}X\mid x\in U\}
. A point-continuous
valuation is a strict, modular, continuous map from
{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathrm{p}}X
to

\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
(
\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
has its Scott topology, as usual). Since every
open subset of
{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathrm{p}}X
is Scott-open, every point-continuous
valuation is a continuous valuation. Every simple valuation if a
point-continuous. There are point-continuous valuations that are not
simple, e.g.
\infty.\delta_{x}
or

\sum_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\frac{1}{2^{n}}\delta_{n}
on
\mathbb{N}
(with its
discrete topology). Point-continuous valuations and continuous
valuations coincide on continuous dcpos

[
23
, Theorem 6.9]
, but not on general spaces, not even on
dcpos
[
16
]
.

Continuous valuations are an alternative to measures that have become
popular in domain theory
[
25
,
26
]
. There are
several results that link continuous valuations to measures, starting
with
[
43
]
and
[
35
]
. To say
it briefly, if slightly inaccurately, continuous valuations and
measures are the same thing, on reasonable enough spaces, and a pretty
complete paper on this topic is
[
28
]
. More precisely,
there is a two-way correspondence between continuous valuations and
measures. In one direction, every measure on the Borel

\sigma
-algebra of
X
induces a continuous valuation on
X
by
restriction to the open sets, if
X
is hereditarily Lindelöf
(namely, if every directed family of open sets contains a cofinal
monotone sequence). This is an easy observation, and one half of
Adamski’s theorem
[
2
, Theorem 3.1]
, which states
that a space is hereditary Lindelöf if and only if every measure on
its Borel
\sigma
-algebra restricts to a continuous valuation on its
open sets. In the other direction, every continuous valuation on a
space
X
extends to a measure on the Borel sets provided that
X
is
an LCS-complete space
[
7
, Theorem 1]
. An

LCS-complete
space is a space homeomorphic to a
G_{\delta}

subset of a locally compact sober space. Every continuous dcpo in its
Scott topology, being locally compact and sober, is LCS-complete.
Every Polish space, and more generally every quasi-Polish space, is
LCS-complete; see
[
7
]
.

We write
{\mathbf{V}}X
for the space of continuous valuations on a space

X
, with the
weak topology
, whose subbasic open sets are
defined by
[U>r]\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\{\nu\in{\mathbf{V}}X\mid\nu(U)>r\}
, where

U\in{\mathcal{O}}X
and
r\in\mathbb{R}_{+}\smallsetminus\{0\}
. It will sometimes be practical
to realize that the sets
[r\ll U]\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\{\nu\in{\mathbf{V}}X\mid r\ll\nu(U)\}
with
U\in{\mathcal{O}}X
and
r\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
form a subbase of the
topology, too—simply because
[r\ll U]
is equal to
[U>r]
when

r\neq 0
and to the whole of
{\mathbf{V}}X
if
r=0
.

There is a notion of integral
\int_{x\in X}h(x)\,d\nu
, or

\int h\,d\nu
for short, where
h\in\mathcal{L}X
and

\nu\in{\mathbf{V}}X
, which was originally defined in a similar manner to
the usual Lebesgue integral by Jones
[
25
,
26
]
; see

[
15
, Section 2.2]
for a quick survey. A practical
definition is by using a Choquet formula

\int h\,d\nu\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\int_{0}^{\infty}\nu(h^{-1}(]t,\infty]))\,dt
,
where the integral on the right is a Riemann integral. The integral

\int h\,d\nu
is linear and Scott-continuous in both
h
and
\nu
.
Additionally, the weak topology also has a subbase of open sets

[h>r]\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\{\nu\in{\mathbf{V}}X\mid\int h\,d\nu>r\}
, where

h\in\mathcal{L}X
and
r\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
(or
r\in\mathbb{R}_{+}\smallsetminus\{0\}
, since

[h>0]=\bigcup_{r>0}[h>r]
).

{\mathbf{V}}_{1}X
is its subspace of probability valuations and

{\mathbf{V}}_{\leq 1}X
is its subspace of subprobability valuations. We
will also write
{\mathbf{V}}_{b}X
for the subspace of
bounded

continuous valuations, namely those
\nu\in{\mathbf{V}}X
such that

\nu(X)<\infty
. We will write
{\mathbf{V}}_{\mathrm{f}}X
for the subspace of

simple
valuations,
{\mathbf{V}}_{\mathrm{p}}X
for the subspace of

point-continuous
valuations,
{\mathbf{V}}_{\leq 1,\mathrm{f}}X
for simple
subprobability valuations,
{\mathbf{V}}_{1,\mathrm{f}}X
for simple probability
valuations,
{\mathbf{V}}_{\leq 1,\mathrm{p}}
for point-continuous subprobability
valuations,
{\mathbf{V}}_{1,\mathrm{p}}
for point-continuous probability
valuations. The specialization ordering in each case is given by the

stochastic ordering
:
\mu\leq\nu
if and only if

\mu(U)\leq\nu(U)
for every
U\in{\mathcal{O}}X
.

Example 2.5

With the obvious addition and scalar multiplication,
{\mathbf{V}}X
is a

T_{0}
topological cone for every space
X

[
15
, Proposition 3.8]
. With the stochastic ordering, it
is an ordered cone.

Let us mention some results from
[
3
]
, which should
help understand how the topology of
{\mathbf{V}}X
, or rather of

{\mathbf{V}}_{\leq 1}X
and of
{\mathbf{V}}_{1}X
relates to the vague topology on
the corresponding spaces of measures. When
X
is a compact pospace
(a compact space with an ordering which is compatible with the
topology, in the sense that its graph is closed in
X\times X
), the
space
\mathfrak{M}_{\leq 1}(X)
of all subprobability measures on

X
, with the vague topology and the stochastic ordering (defined
here, as before, by
\mu\leq\mu^{\prime}
if and only if

\mu(U)\leq\mu^{\prime}(U)
for every
U\in{\mathcal{O}}X
) is also a compact
pospace
[
3
, Theorem 31]
. There is a well-known
one-to-one correspondence between compact pospaces and certain spaces
called the
stably compact
spaces: given a compact pospace
X
,
the space
X^{\uparrow}
obtained as
X
with the topology obtained by
just keeping the open upwards-closed subsets as open sets is stably
compact, and one can recover the original topology as the
patch
topology
(the coarsest topology containing the open sets and the
compact saturated sets), and the original ordering as the
specialization ordering of
X
. Then

\mathfrak{M}_{\leq 1}(X)^{\uparrow}
is isomorphic, as a topological
cone, with
{\mathbf{V}}_{\leq 1}(X^{\uparrow})
, and therefore

\mathfrak{M}_{\leq 1}(X)
is isomorphic, as a compact pospace, to the
patch space of
{\mathbf{V}}_{\leq 1}(X^{\uparrow})

[
3
, Theorem 36]
. A similar result holds for spaces
of probability measures or valuations.

3
Barycentric algebras

3.1
Plain barycentric algebras

A
barycentric algebra
is a set
\mathfrak{B}
with a family of binary
operations
+_{a}
, for every
a\in[0,1]
, satisfying:

\displaystyle x+_{1}y

\displaystyle=x

\displaystyle x+_{a}x

\displaystyle=x

\displaystyle x+_{a}y

\displaystyle=y+_{1-a}y

\displaystyle(x+_{a}y)+_{b}c

\displaystyle=x+_{ab}(y+_{\frac{(1-a)b}{1-ab}}z)

\displaystyle\text{if $a<1$ and $b<1$},

for all
x,y,z\in\mathfrak{B}
and
a,b\in[0,1]
. An
affine map

f\colon\mathfrak{B}\to\mathfrak{A}
between barycentric algebras is a function such
that
f(x+_{a}y)=f(x)+_{a}f(y)
for all
x,y\in\mathfrak{B}
and

a\in[0,1]
. Those form a category, which we write as
\mathbf{BA}
.

Every cone is a barycentric algebra provided that we define
x+_{a}y

as
a\cdot x+(1-a)\cdot y
, and every linear map between cones is
then affine. Every convex subset of a cone is a barycentric algebra,
with the induced operations
+_{a}
. There are stranger examples of
barycentric algebras. For example, every sup-semilattice
X
is a
barycentric algebra, with
x+_{a}y\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}x\vee y
if

a\in{]0,1[}
(and
x+_{1}y\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}x
,
x+_{0}y\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}y
): see

[
11
, Example 1.3]
; Flood considers an
inf-semilattice instead, and while this is immaterial for now, the
preordered and semitopological variants will require that the
semilattice operation
\vee
be a supremum, not an infimum.

There is a free cone on every barycentric algebra
\mathfrak{B}

[
29
, Standard
construction 2.4]
, which Keimel and Plotkin write as

C_{\mathfrak{B}}
, but which we prefer to write as
\mathrm{cone}(\mathfrak{B})
. This is
obtained as the collection of pairs
(r,x)
where

r\in\mathbb{R}_{+}\smallsetminus\{0\}
and
x\in\mathfrak{B}
, plus a distinguished element

0
. It is a cone with the following operations
+
and
\cdot
:

\displaystyle a\cdot(r,x)

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}(ar,x)

\displaystyle(r,x)+(s,y)

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}(r+s,x+_{\frac{r}{r+s}}y)

\displaystyle a\cdot 0

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}0

\displaystyle(r,x)+0

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}(r,x)

\displaystyle 0\cdot(r,x)

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}0

\displaystyle 0+(s,y)

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}(s,y)

\displaystyle 0\cdot 0

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}0

\displaystyle 0+0

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}0

where
a\in\mathbb{R}_{+}\smallsetminus\{0\}
. There is a function

\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{B}}\colon\mathfrak{B}\to\mathrm{cone}(\mathfrak{B})
defined by

\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{B}}(x)\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}(1,x)
for every
x\in\mathfrak{B}
, and
\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{B}}

is injective and affine. For every function
f\colon\mathfrak{B}\to\mathfrak{C}
to a
cone
\mathfrak{C}
, there is a unique positively homogeneous map

f^{\leftslice}\colon\mathrm{cone}(\mathfrak{B})\to\mathfrak{C}
such that

f^{\leftslice}\circ\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{B}}=f
: just define
f^{\leftslice}(0)\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}0
and

f^{\leftslice}(r,x)\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}rf(x)
for all

(r,x)\in\mathrm{cone}(\mathfrak{B})\smallsetminus\{0\}
. (This function is written as

\overline{f}
in
[
29
]
.) Additionally, if
f
is affine
then
f^{\leftslice}
is linear, and this shows that
\mathrm{cone}(\mathfrak{B})
is the
free cone over the barycentric algebra
\mathfrak{B}
. This is due to Flood

[
11
, Section 1.4]
. Since
\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{B}}
is injective
and affine, we can think of
\mathfrak{B}
as being affinely embedded in the
cone
\mathrm{cone}(\mathfrak{B})
, and this can be used to simplify computations a
lot; in fact, we will almost never use the defining axioms of the
operations
+_{a}
in a barycentric algebra.

For example, this trick can be used to prove the following, originally
due to Stone
[
47
, Definition 1, Lemma 2, Lemma 3]
. We let

\Delta_{n}\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\{(\alpha_{1},\cdots,\alpha_{n})\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{n}\mid\sum_{i=1}^{n}\alpha_{i}=1\}
be the standard simplex, and we abbreviate

(\alpha_{1},\cdots,\alpha_{n})
as
\vec{\alpha}
.

Def. and Proposition 3.1
(Barycenters, part 1)

Let
\mathfrak{B}
be a barycentric algebra. For every
n\geq 1
, for all
points
x_{1}
, …,
x_{n}
of
\mathfrak{B}
and for every

(a_{1},\cdots,a_{n})\in\Delta_{n}
, there is a unique point of
\mathfrak{B}
,
which we write as
\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\cdot x_{i}
, whose image under

\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{B}}
is equal to
\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\cdot\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{B}}(x_{i})
.
This is the
barycenter
of the points
x_{i}
with weights

a_{i}
.

1.

It is equal to
x_{n}
if
a_{n}=1
and to

(\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}\frac{a_{i}}{1-a_{n}}\cdot x_{i})+_{1-a_{n}}x_{n}

otherwise,

2.

it is invariant under permutations, namely for every bijection

\sigma
of
\{1,\cdots,n\}
onto itself,
\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\cdot x_{i}=\sum_{j=1}^{n}a_{\sigma(i)}\cdot x_{\sigma(i)}
,

3.

if
a_{n}=0
, then

\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\cdot x_{i}=\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}a_{i}\cdot x_{i}
,

4.

and
\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\cdot x_{i}
is mapped by every affine
function
f
from
\mathfrak{B}
to a barycentric algebra
\mathfrak{A}
to

\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\cdot f(x_{i})
.

Proof.

Uniqueness comes from the injectivity of
\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{B}}
. For
existence, we give an explicit description of this point, by
induction on
n\geq 1
. If
n=1
, then
a_{1}=1
, and we define

\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\cdot x_{i}
as
x_{1}
. In general, if
a_{n}=1
, then
we define
\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\cdot x_{i}
as
x_{n}
. Otherwise, and if

n\geq 2
, then
1-a_{n}
is non-zero, and we define

\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\cdot x_{i}
as

(\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}\frac{a_{i}}{1-a_{n}}\cdot x_{i})+_{1-a_{n}}x_{n}
.
This fits our needs, since
\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{B}}
is affine. This also shows
item 1. Item 2 is because
\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\cdot x_{i}
and

\sum_{j=1}^{n}a_{\sigma(i)}\cdot x_{\sigma(i)}
are both mapped
by
\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{B}}
to

\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\cdot\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{B}}(x_{i})=\sum_{j=1}^{n}a_{\sigma(i)}\cdot\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{B}}(x_{\sigma(i)})
, and since
\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{B}}
is
injective.

3. If
a_{n}=0
, then
\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{B}}(\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\cdot x_{i})=\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\cdot\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{B}}(x_{i})=\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}a_{i}\cdot\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{B}}(x_{i})=\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{B}}(\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}a_{i}\cdot x_{i})
, and
we conclude since
\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{B}}
is injective.

4. Given any affine function
f
from
\mathfrak{B}
to a barycentric algebra

\mathfrak{A}
, we verify that

f(\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\cdot x_{i})=\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\cdot f(x_{i})
by
induction on
n\geq 1
. If
n=1
or if more generally
a_{n}=1
,
then both sides are equal to
f(x_{n})
. Otherwise, the left-hand
side is equal to

f(\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}\frac{a_{i}}{1-a_{n}}\cdot x_{i})+_{1-a_{n}}x_{n})
,
hence to

f(\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}\frac{a_{i}}{1-a_{n}}\cdot x_{i})+_{1-a_{n}}f(x_{n})
since
f
is affine. This is equal to

(\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}\frac{a_{i}}{1-a_{n}}\cdot f(x_{i}))+_{1-a_{n}}f(x_{n})
by induction hypothesis, hence to

\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\cdot f(x_{i})
.
\Box

The same embedding trick is also helpful in proving the following

entropic law
:

\displaystyle(x+_{a}y)+_{b}(z+_{a}t)

\displaystyle=(x+_{b}t)+_{a}(y+_{b}t)

(2)

for all
x,y,z,t\in\mathfrak{B}
and
a,b\in[0,1]

[
11
, Section 1.5,
identity (7)]
; see also
[
29
, paragraph after
Lemma 2.3]
.

3.2
Preordered and ordered barycentric algebras

Before we define the semitopological and topological variants of
barycentric algebras, we need to talk about the preordered and ordered
variants. A
preordered barycentric algebra
is a barycentric
algebra
\mathfrak{B}
with a preordering
\leq
such that
+_{a}
is monotonic
for every
a\in[0,1]
. An
ordered barycentric algebra
is
one where
\leq
is antisymmetric
[
29
, Definition 2.6]
.

Every (pre)ordered cone is a (pre)ordered barycentric algebra. Every
sup-semilattice is an ordered barycentric algebra, with

x+_{a}y\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}x\vee y
if
a\in{]0,1[}
.

For every barycentric algebra
\mathfrak{B}
and every preordered barycentric
algebra
\mathfrak{A}
, we say that a function
f\colon\mathfrak{B}\to\mathfrak{A}
is

concave
(resp.
convex
) if and only if

f(x+_{a}y)\geq f(x)+_{a}f(y)
(resp.
\leq
) for all

x,y\in\mathfrak{B}
and
a\in[0,1]
. Hence an affine map is one that is
both concave and convex.

Lemma 3.2

For every map barycentric algebra
\mathfrak{B}
and every preordered cone

\mathfrak{C}
, for every function
f\colon\mathfrak{B}\to\mathfrak{C}
,
f^{\leftslice}
is
positively homogeneous; if
f
is concave then
f^{\leftslice}
is
superlinear, if
f
is convex then
f^{\leftslice}
is sublinear, and if

f
is affine then
f^{\leftslice}
is linear.

Proof.

We have already mentioned that
f^{\leftslice}
is positively homogeneous,
and that it is linear if
f
is affine. If
f
is concave, then we
check that
f^{\leftslice}
is superadditive, hence superlinear. We have

f^{\leftslice}(u+0)=f^{\leftslice}(u)=f^{\leftslice}(u)+f^{\leftslice}(0)
for every

u\in\mathrm{cone}(\mathfrak{B})
, and it remains to verify that

f^{\leftslice}((r,x)+(s,y))\geq f^{\leftslice}(r,x)+f^{\leftslice}(s,y)
.
Indeed,

f^{\leftslice}((r,x)+(s,y))=f^{\leftslice}(r+s,x+_{\frac{r}{r+s}}y)=(r+s)f(x+_{\frac{r}{r+s}}y)\geq(r+s)(\frac{r}{r+s}f(x)+\frac{s}{r+s}f(y))=rf(x)+sf(y)=f^{\leftslice}(r,x)+f^{\leftslice}(s,y)
. Similarly, if
f
is convex, then
f^{\leftslice}
is subadditive
hence sublinear.
\Box

Keimel and Plotkin build the free semiordered cone (the free ordered
cone, in the usual terminology, see Remark
2.1
) on
an ordered barycentric algebra
\mathfrak{B}

[
29
, Standard
construction 2.7]
, and this is simply
\mathrm{cone}(\mathfrak{B})
, with
ordering defined by
0\leq 0
and
(r,x)\leq(s,y)
if and only if

r=s
and
x\leq y
in
\mathfrak{B}
. We are more interested in the free
ordered, in general the free preordered, cone.

To this end, we need a preordering
\leq
on
\mathrm{cone}(\mathfrak{B})
that
contains the Keimel-Plotkin (pre)ordering, and that satisfies

u\leq u+v
for all
u,v\in\mathrm{cone}(\mathfrak{B})
. Indeed, in a preordered
cone,
0\leq v
, whence
u\leq u+v
. Temporarily calling
\leq_{1}

the Keimel-Plotkin preordering and
\leq_{2}
the smallest preordering
such that
u\leq_{2}u+v
for all
u,v\in\mathrm{cone}(\mathfrak{B})
, we will
build the reflexive transitive closure of
\leq_{1}\cup\leq_{2}
. This
simplifies to
\leq_{2};\leq_{1}
, where
u\leq_{2};\leq_{1}v
if and
only if
u\leq_{2}w\leq_{1}v
for some
w\in\mathrm{cone}(\mathfrak{B})
, and we
arrive at the following definition.

Definition 3.3
(
\leq^{\mathrm{cone}}
)

For every preordered barycentric algebra
\mathfrak{B}
, the relation
\leq^{\mathrm{cone}}

on
\mathrm{cone}(\mathfrak{B})
is defined by letting
u\leq^{\mathrm{cone}}v
if and only if:

•

either
u=v=0
,

•

or
v
can be written as
(s,y)
and there are

u^{\prime}\in\mathrm{cone}(\mathfrak{B})
and
y_{1}\in\mathfrak{B}
such that
u+u^{\prime}=(s,y_{1})

and
y_{1}\leq y
.

Proposition 3.4

For every preordered barycentric algebra
\mathfrak{B}
:

1.

the relation
\leq^{\mathrm{cone}}
is a preordering, and turns

\mathrm{cone}(\mathfrak{B})
into a preordered cone;

2.

\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{B}}
is an injective affine preorder embedding;

3.

for every monotonic map
f\colon\mathfrak{B}\to\mathfrak{C}
to a preordered
cone
\mathfrak{C}
, there is a unique monotonic positively homogeneous map

f^{\leftslice}\colon\mathrm{cone}(\mathfrak{B})\to\mathfrak{C}
such that

f^{\leftslice}\circ\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{B}}=f
; if
f
is affine (resp. concave,
convex) then
f^{\leftslice}
is linear (resp. superlinear, sublinear).

4.

In particular,
\mathrm{cone}(\mathfrak{B})
with the preordering
\leq^{\mathrm{cone}}
is
the free preordered cone over the preordered barycentric algebra

\mathfrak{B}
.

Proof.

1. The relation
\leq^{\mathrm{cone}}
just defined on
\mathrm{cone}(\mathfrak{B})
is reflexive.
We also observe that
0\leq^{\mathrm{cone}}v
for every
v\in\mathrm{cone}(\mathfrak{B})
: when

v
is of the form
(s,y)
, this follows by taking

u^{\prime}\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}(s,y)
and
y_{1}\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}y
.

Let us show that
\leq^{\mathrm{cone}}
is transitive. We assume three points

u,v,w\in\mathrm{cone}(\mathfrak{B})
such that
u\leq^{\mathrm{cone}}v\leq^{\mathrm{cone}}w
. If
u=v

or
v=w
, then
u\leq^{\mathrm{cone}}w
is trivial, so we assume
u\neq v
and

v\neq w
. If
u=0
, then
u\leq^{\mathrm{cone}}w
, since
0
is below every
point, as we have seen. If
v=0
, then
u\leq^{\mathrm{cone}}v
is only possible
if
u=0
, whence
u\leq^{\mathrm{cone}}w
. Otherwise, none of
u,v,w
is equal
to
0
; hence we write
u
as
(r,x)
,
v
as
(s,y)
, and
w
as

(t,z)
. By Definition
3.3
, there are
points
u^{\prime},v^{\prime}\in\mathrm{cone}(\mathfrak{B})
and
y_{1},z_{1}\in\mathfrak{B}
such that

u+u^{\prime}=(s,y_{1})
,
y_{1}\leq y
,
v+v^{\prime}=(t,z_{1})
, and
z_{1}\leq z
.
Since
u\neq v
,
u^{\prime}\neq 0
, so we can write
u^{\prime}
as
(r^{\prime},x^{\prime})
;
similarly we can write
v^{\prime}
as
(s^{\prime},y^{\prime})
. Then

u+(u^{\prime}+v^{\prime})=(u+u^{\prime})+v^{\prime}
(since
\mathrm{cone}(\mathfrak{B})
is a cone)

=(s,y_{1})+(s^{\prime},y^{\prime})=(s+s^{\prime},y_{1}+_{\frac{s}{s+s^{\prime}}}y^{\prime})
. Now

v+v^{\prime}=(s,y)+(s^{\prime},y^{\prime})=(s+s^{\prime},y+_{\frac{s}{s+s^{\prime}}}y^{\prime})
is
equal to
(t,z_{1})
, so
t=s+s^{\prime}
and

y+_{\frac{s}{s+s^{\prime}}}y^{\prime}=z_{1}
. Since
t=s+s^{\prime}
,

u+(u^{\prime}+v^{\prime})=(t,y_{1}+_{\frac{s}{s+s^{\prime}}}y^{\prime})
. Then

y_{1}+_{\frac{s}{s+s^{\prime}}}y^{\prime}\leq y+_{\frac{s}{s+s^{\prime}}}y^{\prime}
(since

y_{1}\leq y
and
\mathfrak{B}
is preordered)
=z_{1}\leq z
. This shows
that
u\leq^{\mathrm{cone}}(t,z)=w
.

We verify that the cone operations are monotonic on
\mathrm{cone}(\mathfrak{B})
.
We start with
\_\cdot w
, where
w\in\mathrm{cone}(\mathfrak{B})
. If
w=0
,
this is the constant
0
map, which is monotonic, so we assume that

w
is of the form
(t,z)
. For all
a\leq b
in
\mathbb{R}_{+}
, if
a=0

then
a\cdot w=0
, which is less than or equal to every element
of
\mathrm{cone}(\mathfrak{B})
, hence to
b\cdot w
. If
a=b
, the inequality

a\cdot w\leq^{\mathrm{cone}}b\cdot w
is clear, too. Otherwise,
0<a<b
.
Let
u^{\prime}\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}((b-a)t,z)
. Then

(a\cdot w)+u^{\prime}=(at,z)+((b-a)t,z)=(bt,z+_{a/b}z)=(bt,z)=b\cdot w
, so
a\cdot w\leq^{\mathrm{cone}}b\cdot w
.

We proceed with
a\cdot\_
, where
a\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
. Let
u\leq v
in

\mathrm{cone}(\mathfrak{B})
. We need to show that
a\cdot u\leq a\cdot v
.
This is clear if
u=v=0
or if
a=0
. Hence we assume that
a>0
,
and that
u+u^{\prime}=(s,y_{1})
and
v=(s,y)
for some

u^{\prime}\in\mathrm{cone}(\mathfrak{B})
and
y_{1}\leq y
in
\mathfrak{B}
. Then

(a\cdot u)+(a\cdot u^{\prime})=a\cdot(u+u^{\prime})=(as,y_{1})
,

y_{1}\leq y
, and
a\cdot v=(as,y)
, so

a\cdot u\leq a\cdot v
.

Finally, we show that
\_+w
is monotonic for every

w\in\mathrm{cone}(\mathfrak{B})
. If
w=0
, this is the identity map, for which
the claim is clear, so we assume that
w
is of the form
(t,z)
.
Let
u\leq^{\mathrm{cone}}v
in
\mathrm{cone}(\mathfrak{B})
. If
u=v=0
, then

u+w=w=v+w
, so
u+w\leq^{\mathrm{cone}}v+w
. Otherwise,

u+u^{\prime}=(s,y_{1})
for some
u^{\prime}\in\mathrm{cone}(\mathfrak{B})
and some

y_{1}\leq y
such that
v=(s,y)
. Then

(u+w)+u^{\prime}=(u+u^{\prime})+w=(s,y_{1})+(t,z)=(s+t,y_{1}+_{s/(s+t)}z)
and
v+w=(s,y)+(t,z)=(s+t,y+_{s/(s+t)}z)
. Since

+_{s/(s+t)}
is monotonic and
y_{1}\leq y
, we have

y_{1}+_{s/(s+t)}z\leq y+_{s/(s+t)}z
, so
u+w\leq^{\mathrm{cone}}v+w
.

2. We have seen that the map
\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{B}}
is affine and injective.
For all
x\leq y
in
\mathfrak{B}
, we have

\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{B}}(x)\leq^{\mathrm{cone}}\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{B}}(y)
, namely
(1,x)\leq^{\mathrm{cone}}(1,y)
:
just take
u^{\prime}\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}0
,
y_{1}\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}x
and
s\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}1
in
Definition
3.3
. Conversely, if

\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{B}}(x)\leq^{\mathrm{cone}}\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{B}}(y)
, then there are

u^{\prime}\in\mathrm{cone}(\mathfrak{B})
and
y_{1}\in\mathfrak{B}
such that

(1,x)+u^{\prime}=(1,y_{1})
and
y_{1}\leq y
. The point
u^{\prime}
cannot be
of the form
(r^{\prime},x^{\prime})
, since then
(1,x)+(r^{\prime},x^{\prime})
would be equal to

(1+r^{\prime},x+_{\frac{1}{1+r^{\prime}}}x^{\prime})
, whose first component is not
equal to
1
. Hence
u^{\prime}=0
; then
y_{1}=x
, and therefore

x\leq y
, since
y_{1}\leq y
.

3. Let
f
be any monotonic map from
\mathfrak{B}
to
\mathfrak{C}
. If
f^{\leftslice}

exists, because
\mathrm{cone}(\mathfrak{B})
is the free cone on
\mathfrak{B}
. In order
to show that
f^{\leftslice}
exists, we build it as before, letting

f^{\leftslice}(0)\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}0
and
f^{\leftslice}(a,x)\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}af(x)
; then

f^{\leftslice}
is positively homogeneous and

f^{\leftslice}\circ\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{B}}=f
. We claim that
f^{\leftslice}
is
monotonic. Let
u\leq^{\mathrm{cone}}v
in
\mathrm{cone}(\mathfrak{B})
. If
u=v=0
, then
clearly
f^{\leftslice}(u)\leq f^{\leftslice}(v)
. Otherwise, there are

u^{\prime}\in\mathrm{cone}(\mathfrak{B})
and
y_{1}\in\mathfrak{B}
such that
u+u^{\prime}=(s,y_{1})
,

v=(s,y)
, and
y_{1}\leq y
. Then

f^{\leftslice}(u+u^{\prime})=f^{\leftslice}(u)+f^{\leftslice}(u^{\prime})
since
f^{\leftslice}
is
linear. Since
\mathfrak{C}
is preordered (in our sense, see
Remark
2.1
),
0\leq f^{\leftslice}(u^{\prime})
, so

f^{\leftslice}(u)\leq f^{\leftslice}(u+u^{\prime})
. Also,

f^{\leftslice}(u+u^{\prime})=sf(y_{1})\leq sf(y)
(since
f
is monotonic)

=f^{\leftslice}(v)
. Hence
f^{\leftslice}(u)\leq f^{\leftslice}(v)
.

The fact that
f^{\leftslice}
is superlinear (resp. sublinear, affine) If

f
is concave (resp. convex, affine), is by
Lemma
3.2
.

4. Obvious consequence of item 3.
\Box

Since
\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{B}}
is an injective affine preorder embedding, it
follows that, up to affine bijective preorder embeddings, the
preordered barycentric algebras are exactly the convex subsets of
preordered cones.

Remark 3.5

Given a preordered barycentric algebra
\mathfrak{B}
, we have
u\leq^{\mathrm{cone}}u+v

for all
u,v\in\mathrm{cone}(\mathfrak{B})
, just as in every preordered cone.
In particular, for all
r,s\in\mathbb{R}_{+}\smallsetminus\{0\}
and for every

x\in\mathfrak{B}
,
(r,x)\leq^{\mathrm{cone}}(r+s,x)
. Indeed,

(r,x)+(s,x)=(r+s,x+_{\frac{r}{r+s}}x)=(r+s,x)
.

We also notice the following.

Lemma 3.6

Given an ordered (not just preordered) barycentric algebra
\mathfrak{B}
, the
preordering
\leq^{\mathrm{cone}}
on
\mathrm{cone}(\mathfrak{B})
is antisymmetric, hence a
partial ordering.

Proof.

Let us assume that
u\leq^{\mathrm{cone}}v\leq^{\mathrm{cone}}u
in
\mathrm{cone}(\mathfrak{B})
. Apart from
the trivial case where
u=v=0
,
u\leq^{\mathrm{cone}}v
states that there are

u^{\prime}\in\mathrm{cone}(\mathfrak{B})
and
y_{1}\in\mathfrak{B}
such that
u+u^{\prime}=(s,y_{1})
,

v=(s,y)
, and
y_{1}\leq y
. Similarly,
v\leq^{\mathrm{cone}}u
states that
there are
v^{\prime}\in\mathrm{cone}(\mathfrak{B})
and
x_{1}\in\mathfrak{B}
such that

v+v^{\prime}=(r,x_{1})
,
u=(r,x)
and
x_{1}\leq x
.

Since
u+u^{\prime}=(s,y_{1})
and
u=(r,x)
, we have
r\leq s
: either

u^{\prime}=0
and this is clear, or
u^{\prime}
is of the form
(r^{\prime},x^{\prime})
, and
then
u+u^{\prime}=(r+r^{\prime},x+_{\frac{r}{r+r^{\prime}}}x^{\prime})
, with
r<r+r^{\prime}
.
Similarly, since
v+v^{\prime}=(r,x_{1})
and
v=(s,y)
, we have

s\leq r
. Therefore
r=s
.

Then,
u+u^{\prime}=(s,y_{1})
and
u=(r,x)
with
r=s
imply that

u^{\prime}=0
(otherwise
s=r+r^{\prime}>r
, where
u^{\prime}=(r^{\prime},x^{\prime})
).
Similarly,
v+v^{\prime}=(r,x_{1})
and
v=(s,y)
with
s=r
imply that

v^{\prime}=0
.

Therefore
u=(s,y_{1})=(r,y_{1})
, so
y_{1}=x
; since
y_{1}\leq y
,
it follows that
x\leq y
. Similarly,
v=(r,x_{1})=(s,x_{1})
,
so
x_{1}=y
; since
x_{1}\leq x
,
y\leq x
. Since
\mathfrak{B}
is ordered,
it follows that
x=y
.
\Box

3.3
Semitopological and topological barycentric algebras

Definition 3.7

A barycentric algebra
\mathfrak{B}
with a topology is:

•

semitopological
if and only if the function

(x,a,y)\mapsto x+_{a}y
is separately continuous from

\mathfrak{B}\times[0,1]\times\mathfrak{B}
to
\mathfrak{B}
,

•

quasitopological
if and only if the map

(x,a)\mapsto x+_{a}y
is (jointly) continuous from

\mathfrak{B}\times[0,1]
, for every fixed
y\in\mathfrak{B}
,

•

topological
if and only if the function

(x,a,y)\mapsto x+_{a}y
is jointly continuous from

\mathfrak{B}\times[0,1]\times\mathfrak{B}
to
\mathfrak{B}
,

where in each case
[0,1]
is given its usual, metric topology.

There are corresponding categories
\mathbf{sTBA}
of semitopological
barycentric algebras and
\mathbf{TBA}
of topological barycentric algebras,
with affine continuous maps as morphisms. Quasitopological
barycentric algebras will almost play no role in the sequel, except in
Lemma
3.8
below.

For barycentric algebras, the implications topological
\mathrel{\Rightarrow}

quasitopological
\mathrel{\Rightarrow}
semitopological hold.

Lemma 3.8

Given any convex subset
A
of a cone
\mathfrak{C}
,
A
is a barycentric
algebra with the induced operation

x+_{a}y\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}a\cdot x+(1-a)\cdot y
.

If
\mathfrak{C}
is a preordered cone, then
A
is a preordered barycentric
algebra.

If
\mathfrak{C}
is a semitopological cone, then
A
is a quasitopological,
hence a semitopological barycentric algebra in the subspace
topology.

If
\mathfrak{C}
is a topological cone, then
A
is a topological barycentric
algebra.

Proof.

The first part is clear, as the second part on the preordered case.
If
\mathfrak{C}
is semitopological, then let us fix
y\in A
, and let us
define
f\colon\mathfrak{C}\times\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}_{+}\to\mathfrak{C}
by

f(x,a,b)\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}a\cdot x+b\cdot y
. The function
f
is
separately continuous, hence jointly continuous by two applications
of Ershov’s observation. Let
g\colon[0,1]\to\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}_{+}

be defined by
g(a)\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}(a,1-a)
. This is a continuous map,
since
g^{-1}({]s,\infty[}\times{]t,\infty[})
is equal to

]s,1-t[
if
s<1-t<1
, to
]s,1]
if
s<1\leq 1-t
, and is
empty otherwise. Letting
i
be the inclusion of
A
into
\mathfrak{C}
, it
follows that
f\circ(i\times g)\colon A\times[0,1]\to\mathfrak{C}
,
which maps
(x,a)
to
x+_{a}y
, is (jointly) continuous.

If
\mathfrak{C}
is topological, then the function

f^{\prime}\colon\mathfrak{C}\times\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathfrak{C}\to\mathfrak{C}
defined by

f^{\prime}(x,a,b,y)\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}a\cdot x+b\cdot y
is (jointly)
continuous. We see
f^{\prime}
as a function from

\mathfrak{C}\times(\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}_{+})\times\mathfrak{C}
to
\mathfrak{C}
. Then

f^{\prime}\times(i\times g\times i)\colon A\times[0,1]\times A\to\mathfrak{C}
is (jointly) continuous, and maps
(x,a,y)
to
x+_{a}y
.
\Box

Since every cone is a barycentric algebra, there is a risk of conflict
between the uses of “semitopological” and “topological” in each
case. This risk is in fact nonexistent because of the following.

Lemma 3.9

Let
\mathfrak{C}
be a cone with a topology that makes scalar multiplication
separately continuous. Seen as a barycentric algebra,
\mathfrak{C}
is
semitopological (resp. topological) if and only if
\mathfrak{C}
is a
semitopological (resp. topological) cone.

Proof.

If
\mathfrak{C}
is a semitopological (resp. topological) cone, then by
taking
A\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\mathfrak{C}
in Lemma
3.8
, we obtain
that
\mathfrak{C}
is a semitopological (resp. topological) barycentric
algebra. Conversely, let us assume that
\mathfrak{C}
is semitopological
(resp. topological) as a barycentric algebra. The function
+
is
the composition of
(x,y)\mapsto x+_{1/2}y
with

z\mapsto 2\cdot z
. The latter is continuous by assumption, and
the former is separately (resp. jointly) continuous. Hence
\mathfrak{C}
is
a semitopological (resp. topological) cone.
\Box

Example 3.10

By Lemma
3.8
and Example
2.5
,

{\mathbf{V}}_{\leq 1}X
,
{\mathbf{V}}_{1}X
and
{\mathbf{V}}_{b}X
are topological
barycentric algebras, being convex subspaces of the topological cone

{\mathbf{V}}X
. The same holds of
{\mathbf{V}}_{\mathrm{f}}X
,
{\mathbf{V}}_{\leq 1,\mathrm{f}}X
,

{\mathbf{V}}_{1,\mathrm{f}}X
,
{\mathbf{V}}_{\mathrm{p}}X
,
{\mathbf{V}}_{\leq 1,\mathrm{p}}X
,
{\mathbf{V}}_{1,\mathrm{p}}X
. For the latter three, we need to observe that
point-continuous valuations are closed under finite linear
combinations
[
23
, Section 3.2]
.

Example 3.11

Every sup-semilattice
L
is a quasitopological barycentric algebra,
with its Scott topology, and where
x+_{a}y
is defined as the
supremum
x\vee y
for all
a\in{]0,1[}
(as
x
when
a=1
, as

y
when
a=0
). It is topological if
L
is a continuous poset.
Indeed, the lift
L_{\bot}
, obtained by adding a fresh element
\bot

below every element of
L
, is a pointed sup-semilattice, hence a
semitopological cone by Example
2.4
, which is
even topological if
L
is a continuous poset. Additionally,
L
is
convex in
L_{\bot}
, so we conclude by
Lemma
3.8
.

Example 3.12

Let us call
s-barycentric algebra
any ordered barycentric
algebra
\mathfrak{B}
such that
+_{a}
is Scott-continuous for every

a\in[0,1]
and such that for all
x,y\in\mathfrak{B}
, the map

a\mapsto x+_{a}y
is continuous from
[0,1]
, with its usual
metric topology, to
\mathfrak{B}_{\sigma}
. (An
s-cone
is an ordered
cone that is an s-barycentric algebra
[
30
, Definition
6.1]
.) By definition, every s-barycentric algebra
is a semitopological barycentric algebra in its Scott topology.

Every continuous s-barycentric algebra is a topological barycentric
algebra in its Scott topology, by Ershov’s observation.

Proposition 3.13

Every semitopological barycentric algebra
\mathfrak{B}
whose underlying
topological space is locally finitary compact (e.g., a c-space, a
continuous dcpo) is a topological barycentric algebra.

Proof.

We use the Banaschewski-Lawson theorem

[
36
, Theorem 2]
; Ershov’s observation is enough
if
\mathfrak{B}
is a c-space. For each
y\in\mathfrak{B}
, the map

(x,a)\mapsto x+_{a}y
is separately continuous, hence jointly
continuous since
\mathfrak{B}
is locally finitary compact, from

\mathfrak{B}\times[0,1]
to
\mathfrak{B}
. Hence
\mathfrak{B}
is quasitopological. The
map
(x,a,y)\mapsto x+_{a}y
is then separately continuous in

(x,a)
and in
y
, hence jointly continuous since
\mathfrak{B}
is locally
finitary compact.
\Box

We have seen that every (preordered) barycentric algebra
\mathfrak{B}
embeds
in a (preordered) cone, namely in its free (preordered) cone

\mathrm{cone}(\mathfrak{B})
. The situation is different with semitopological
barycentric algebras.

Definition 3.14
(Embeddable)

An
embedding
of a semitopological barycentric algebra
\mathfrak{B}
in
a semitopological cone
\mathfrak{C}
is an affine topological embedding of

\mathfrak{B}
in
\mathfrak{C}
.
\mathfrak{B}
is
embeddable
if and only if there is an
embedding of
\mathfrak{B}
in some semitopological cone
\mathfrak{C}
.

Not all semitopological barycentric algebras are embeddable: we will
give a counterexample in Example
3.24
. We will
see that
\mathfrak{B}
is embeddable if and only if
\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{B}}
is an
embedding in
\mathrm{cone}(\mathfrak{B})
, which will be the free semitopological
cone over
\mathfrak{B}
. We need to give
\mathrm{cone}(\mathfrak{B})
a particular topology.

Definition 3.15
(Cone topology)

For every semitopological barycentric algebra
\mathfrak{B}
, the
cone
topology
on
\mathrm{cone}(\mathfrak{B})
has a subbase of open subsets
consisting of those open subsets of

(\mathbb{R}_{+}\smallsetminus\{0\})_{\sigma}\times\mathfrak{B}
(with the product topology)
that are upwards-closed with respect to the preordering
\leq^{\mathrm{cone}}
of
Definition
3.3
.

Remark 3.16

The specialization preordering of
\mathrm{cone}(\mathfrak{B})
, with the cone
topology of Definition
3.15
, is
not

\leq^{\mathrm{cone}}
in
general: we will give a counterexample in
Example
3.24
. This may seem paradoxical,
since we have defined the topology on
\mathrm{cone}(\mathfrak{B})
as certain open
sets that are upwards-closed with respect to
\leq^{\mathrm{cone}}
, and this can
lead to subtle errors. The best we can say is that for all

u,v\in\mathrm{cone}(\mathfrak{B})
, if
u\leq^{\mathrm{cone}}v
then
u
is less than or
equal to
v
in the specialization ordering of
\mathrm{cone}(\mathfrak{B})
.

Studying the cone topology will be easier with the following notion.

Definition 3.17
(Semi-concave)

A
semi-concave map
from a semitopological barycentric algebra

\mathfrak{B}
to a preordered cone
\mathfrak{C}
is a function
h\colon\mathfrak{B}\to\mathfrak{C}

such that for all
x,y\in\mathfrak{B}
and
a\in[0,1]
,

h(x+_{a}y)\geq a\,h(x)
.

We will almost exclusively use this notion when
\mathfrak{C}=\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
.

The following is immediate.

Fact 3.18

Let
\mathfrak{B}
be a semitopological barycentric algebra. Every concave map
from
\mathfrak{B}
to
\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
, and in particular every affine map from
\mathfrak{B}
to

\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
is semi-concave. The collection of semi-concave lower
semicontinuous maps on
\mathfrak{B}
is closed under scalar multiplication by
non-negative numbers, addition, pointwise binary infima and
pointwise arbitrary suprema.

Fact 3.19

Every positively homogeneous lower semicontinuous map
f
from a
semitopological cone
\mathfrak{C}
to
\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
is semi-concave.

Indeed,

f(x+_{a}y)=f(a\cdot x+(1-a)\cdot y)\geq f(a\cdot x)=a\,f(x)
, since
(1-a)\cdot y\geq 0
, and
+
and
f
are monotonic.

For every function
h
from a barycentric algebra
\mathfrak{B}
to
\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
,
there is a unique positively homogeneous map

h^{\leftslice}\colon\mathrm{cone}(\mathfrak{B})\to\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
defined by

h^{\leftslice}(0)\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}0
,
h^{\leftslice}(r,x)\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}rh(x)
. We use this
in the following.

Proposition 3.20

For every semitopological barycentric algebra
\mathfrak{B}
, the open subsets
of
\mathrm{cone}(\mathfrak{B})
in its cone topology are exactly the subsets of the
form
(h^{\leftslice})^{-1}(]1,\infty])
, where
h
ranges over the
semi-concave lower semicontinuous maps from
\mathfrak{B}
to
\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
, plus

\mathrm{cone}(\mathfrak{B})
itself.

Proof.

Let us call the
pointed product topology
the topology on

\mathrm{cone}(\mathfrak{B})
consisting of the open subsets of

(\mathbb{R}_{+}\smallsetminus\{0\})_{\sigma}\times\mathfrak{B}
with the product topology, plus
the whole of
\mathrm{cone}(\mathfrak{B})
. The cone topology consists of the open
subsets in the pointed product topology that are upwards-closed. We
call the
semi-concave topology
the topology consisting of the
sets
(h^{\leftslice})^{-1}(]1,\infty])
, where
h
ranges over the
semi-concave lower semicontinuous maps from
\mathfrak{B}
to
\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
, plus
the whole set
\mathrm{cone}(\mathfrak{B})
.

For every open subset
V
of
\mathrm{cone}(\mathfrak{B})
in the semi-concave
topology, we claim that
V
is open in the cone topology. If

V=\mathrm{cone}(\mathfrak{B})
, this is clear, so we assume that
V
is proper.
We write
V
as
(h^{\leftslice})^{-1}(]1,\infty[)
for some semi-concave
lower semicontinuous map
h
from
\mathfrak{B}
to
\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
. For every

u\in V
, necessarily of the form
(r,x)
with
r>0
,

h^{\leftslice}(u)=rh(x)>1
. Multiplication is Scott-continuous on

\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
, and
(r,h(x))
is the supremum of some directed family
of points
(a,b)\ll(r,h(x))
, equivalently
a\ll r
and

b\ll h(x)
. Hence there are two numbers
a,b\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
such that

a\ll r
,
b\ll h(x)
and
ab>1
; in particular, both
a
and

b
are non-zero, so
r>a
and
h(x)>b
. The open rectangle

{]a,\infty[}\times h^{-1}(]b,\infty])
then contains
u
, and
for every point
(s,y)
in that open rectangle,

h^{\leftslice}(s,y)=sh(y)>ab>1
, so the open rectangle is included
in
(h^{\leftslice})^{-1}(]1,\infty])=V
. This shows that
V
is open in
the pointed product topology. Additionally, for every

u\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}(r,x)
in
V
, for every
v\in\mathrm{cone}(\mathfrak{B})
such that

u\leq^{\mathrm{cone}}v
, by definition (see
Definition
3.3
) we can write
v
as

(s,y)
so that for some
u^{\prime}\in\mathrm{cone}(\mathfrak{B})
and some
y_{1}\in\mathfrak{B}
,

u+u^{\prime}=(s,y_{1})
and
y_{1}\leq y
. Since

(r,x)\in V=(h^{\leftslice})^{-1}(]1,\infty])
,
h^{\leftslice}(u)=rh(x)>1
.
If
u^{\prime}=0
, then
h^{\leftslice}(u+u^{\prime})=h^{\leftslice}(u)>1
. Otherwise, we write

u^{\prime}
as
(r^{\prime},x^{\prime})
, and then

h^{\leftslice}(u+u^{\prime})=h^{\leftslice}(r+r^{\prime},x+_{\frac{r}{r+r^{\prime}}}x^{\prime})=(r+r^{\prime})h(x+_{\frac{r}{r+r^{\prime}}}x^{\prime})\geq rh(x)
(because
h
is semi-concave)

=h^{\leftslice}(u)>1
. In any case,
h^{\leftslice}(u+u^{\prime})>1
. Since

h^{\leftslice}(u+u^{\prime})=h^{\leftslice}(s,y_{1})=sh(y_{1})
, since
y_{1}\leq y
and
h
,
being lower semicontinuous, is monotonic, we also have

sh(y)>1
, namely
h^{\leftslice}(v)>1
. In other words,

v\in(h^{\leftslice})^{-1}(]1,\infty])=V
, for every
v
such that

u\leq^{\mathrm{cone}}v
. Hence
V
is upwards-closed. It follows that
V
is
open in the cone topology.

Conversely, let
V
be an open subset of
\mathrm{cone}(\mathfrak{B})
in the cone
topology. If
0\in V
, then
V=\mathrm{cone}(\mathfrak{B})
, which is open in

\mathrm{cone}(\mathfrak{B})
. Henceforth, we will assume that
0\not\in V
,
namely that
V
is proper. Let

h(x)\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\sup\{r\in\mathbb{R}_{+}\smallsetminus\{0\}\mid(1/r,x)\in V\}
,
where the the supremum is taken in
\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
, and the supremum of the
empty set if
0
.

We claim that the function
h
is lower semicontinuous. Let

x\in\mathfrak{B}
and let us assume that
h(x)>t
, where
t\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
.
There is an
r\in\mathbb{R}_{+}\smallsetminus\{0\}
such that
r>t
and

(1/r,x)\in V
. By definition of the cone topology, there is an
open rectangle
{]a,\infty[}\times U
, with
a>0
and
U
open
in
\mathfrak{B}
, that contains
(1/r,x)
and is included in
V
. Then

1/r>a
,
x\in U
, and for every
x^{\prime}\in U
,
(1/r,x^{\prime})
is in

{]a,\infty[}\times U\subseteq V
, so
h(x^{\prime})\geq r>t
. Hence

U
is an open neighborhood of
x
included in

h^{-1}(]t,\infty])
. Since
x
is arbitrary in

h^{-1}(]t,\infty])
,
h^{-1}(]t,\infty])
is open in
\mathfrak{B}
.

Ve verify that
h
is semi-concave. For all
x,y\in\mathfrak{B}
and

a\in[0,1]
, we show that
h(x+_{a}y)\geq ah(x)
. This is
clear if
a=0
or if
a=1
, so we assume that
0<a<1
. Then, it
suffices to show that for every
t<ah(x)
,
t<h(x+_{a}y)
.
From
t<ah(x)
, we obtain that there is an

r\in\mathbb{R}_{+}\smallsetminus\{0\}
such that
ar>t
and
(1/r,x)\in V
.
Then

(1/r,x)+((1-a)/(ar),y)=(1/(ar),x+_{\frac{1/r}{1/(ar)}}y)=(1/(ar),x+_{a}y)
is larger than or equal to
(1/r,x)
by
Remark
3.5
. By definition of the cone topology,

V
is upwards-closed; since
(1/r,x)\in V
,
(1/(ar),x+_{a}y)

is also in
V
. By definition of
h
,
h(x+_{a}y)\geq ar>t
.

For every point
u\in\mathrm{cone}(\mathfrak{B})
,
h^{\leftslice}(u)>1
if and only if

u
is of the form
(s,x)
with
s>0
and
sh(x)>1
. Then,

sh(x)>1
if and only if there is an
r\in\mathbb{R}_{+}\smallsetminus\{0\}

such that
rs>1
and
(1/r,x)\in V
, if and only if

(1/r,x)\in V
for some
r\in\mathbb{R}_{+}\smallsetminus\{0\}
such that

1/r<s
. If so, then
(1/r,x)\leq^{\mathrm{cone}}(s,x)
by
Remark
3.5
, since
s>1/r
, and since
V
is
upwards-closed, it follows that
u=(s,x)
is in
V
. Conversely,
if
u\in V
, we claim that
h^{\leftslice}(u)>1
. Since
0\not\in V
, we
may write
u
as
(s,x)
with
s>0
. By definition of the cone
topology, there is an open rectangle
{]a,\infty[}\times U
that
contains
(s,x)
and is included in
V
. In particular,
s>a

and
x\in U
. Let
r\in\mathbb{R}_{+}\smallsetminus\{0\}
be such that

s>1/r>a
, for example
\frac{2}{s+a}
. Then
h(x)\geq r
, so

h^{\leftslice}(u)=sh(x)\geq rs>1
. It follows that

V={(h^{\leftslice})}^{-1}(]1,\infty])
. Hence
V
is open in the cone
topology on
\mathrm{cone}(\mathfrak{B})
.
\Box

Lemma 3.21

For every semi-concave lower semicontinuous map
h
on a
semitopological barycentric algebra
\mathfrak{B}
, for all
x,y\in\mathfrak{B}
, the
map
a\mapsto\frac{1}{1-a}h(x+_{a}y)
is Scott-continuous from

[0,1[
to
\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
.

Proof.

Let
f
be this map. We first show that
f
is monotonic. Let

0\leq a<b<1
. If
a=0
, then

\frac{1}{1-a}h(x+_{a}y)\leq\frac{1}{1-b}h(x+_{b}y)
reduces
to
h(y)\leq\frac{1}{1-b}h(x+_{b}y)
, namely to

(1-b)h(y)\leq h(x+_{b}y)
, or equivalently to

(1-b)h(y)\leq h(y+_{1-b}x)
, which follows from the fact that

h
is semi-concave. Hence let us assume
0<a<b<1
. Let

z\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}y+_{1-a}x
. We have

z+_{(1-b)/(1-a)}x=(y+_{1-a}x)+_{(1-b)/(1-a)}x=y+_{(1-a)\cdot(1-b)/(1-a)}(x+_{\frac{a(1-b)/(1-a)}{1-(1-a)\cdot(1-b)/(1-a)}}x)=y+_{1-b}x
, and since
h
is semi-concave,

h(z+_{(1-b)/(1-a)}x)\geq\frac{1-b}{1-a}h(z)
. In other
words,
h(y+_{(1-b}x)\geq\frac{1-b}{1-a}h(y+_{1-a}x)
.
Therefore

\frac{1}{1-b}h(y+_{1-b}x)\geq\frac{1}{1-a}h(y+_{1-a}x)
,
or equivalently

\frac{1}{1-b}h(x+_{b}y)\geq\frac{1}{1-a}h(x+_{a}y)
.

The function
f
is continuous from
[0,1[
with its standard
topology to
\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
. This is because
a\mapsto x+_{a}y
is
continuous from
[0,1]
to
\mathfrak{B}
,
h
is continuous from
\mathfrak{B}
to

\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
, and multiplication by
\frac{1}{1-a}
is
Scott-continuous. But the continuous monotonic maps
f
from

[0,1[
to
\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
are exactly the Scott-continuous maps: if
f

is continuous and monotonic, for every
t\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
,

f^{-1}(]t,\infty])
is upwards-closed, hence of the form
[r,1[

or
]r,1[
with
0\leq r\leq 1
, and the first form is open in

[0,1[
only if
r=0
.
\Box

Theorem 3.22

For every semitopological barycentric algebra
\mathfrak{B}
,

1.

\mathrm{cone}(\mathfrak{B})
is a semitopological cone in its cone topology.

2.

The map
\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{B}}
is injective, affine and continuous.

3.

For every semi-concave (resp., concave, affine) continuous map

f\colon\mathfrak{B}\to\mathfrak{C}
to a semitopological cone
\mathfrak{C}
, there is a
unique positively homogeneous (resp., superlinear, linear)
continuous map
f^{\leftslice}\colon\mathrm{cone}(\mathfrak{B})\to\mathfrak{C}
such that

f^{\leftslice}\circ\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{B}}=f
.

In particular,
\mathrm{cone}(\mathfrak{B})
with the cone topology is the free
semitopological cone over
\mathfrak{B}
.

4.

The following are equivalent:

(a)

\mathfrak{B}
is embeddable, that is, there is an affine topological
embedding of
\mathfrak{B}
in a semitopological cone;

(b)

\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{B}}\colon\mathfrak{B}\to\mathrm{cone}(\mathfrak{B})
is a topological
embedding;

(c)

the open subsets of
\mathfrak{B}
are exactly the sets

h^{-1}(]1,\infty])
where
h
ranges over the semi-concave
lower semicontinuous maps from
\mathfrak{B}
to
\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
;

(d)

the sets
h^{-1}(]1,\infty])
where
h
ranges over the
semi-concave lower semicontinuous maps from
\mathfrak{B}
to
\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
form
a subbase of its topology.

Proof.

1. We know that
\mathrm{cone}(\mathfrak{B})
is a cone. For every

u\in\mathrm{cone}(\mathfrak{B})
, we claim that
\_\cdot u
is continuous from

\mathbb{R}_{+}
to
\mathrm{cone}(\mathfrak{B})
. Using
Proposition
3.20
, it suffices to
show that

(\_\cdot u)^{-1}((h^{\leftslice})^{-1}(]1,\infty]))
—alternatively,

\{a\in\mathbb{R}_{+}\mid h^{\leftslice}(a\cdot u)>1\}
—is Scott-open in

\mathbb{R}_{+}
for every semi-concave lower semicontinuous map

h\colon\mathfrak{B}\to\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
. If
u=0
, then

h^{\leftslice}(a\cdot u)=h^{\leftslice}(0)=0
, so

\{a\in\mathbb{R}_{+}\mid h^{\leftslice}(a\cdot u)>1\}
is empty; if
u
is of
the form
(r,x)
with
r>0
, then

h^{\leftslice}(a\cdot u)=ar\,h(x)
, so

\{a\in\mathbb{R}_{+}\mid h^{\leftslice}(a\cdot u)>1\}
is empty if
h(x)=0
,
the whole of
]0,\infty[
if
h(x)=\infty
, and

]1/(rh(x)),\infty[
otherwise.

Let
a\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
. Similarly, in order to see that
a\cdot\_
is
continuous, it suffices to show that

(a\cdot\_)^{-1}((h^{\leftslice})^{-1}(]1,\infty]))
is open for every
semi-concave lower semicontinuous map
h\colon\mathfrak{B}\to\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
. But

(a\cdot\_)^{-1}((h^{\leftslice})^{-1}(]1,\infty]))=\{u\in\mathrm{cone}(\mathfrak{B})\mid h^{\leftslice}(a\cdot u)>1\}=\{(r,x)\in\mathrm{cone}(\mathfrak{B})\smallsetminus\{0\}\mid ar\,h(x)>1\}=((ah)^{\leftslice})^{-1}(]1,\infty])
. Since
ah

is semi-concave and lower semicontinuous by Fact
3.18
,
the latter set is open by
Proposition
3.20
.

Let
v\in\mathrm{cone}(\mathfrak{B})
. In order to show that
\_+v
is
continuous, we once again rely on
Proposition
3.20
, and we show that

V\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}(\_+v)^{-1}((h^{\leftslice})^{-1}(]1,\infty]))
is open in

\mathrm{cone}(\mathfrak{B})
, for every semi-concave lower semicontinuous map

h\colon\mathfrak{B}\to\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
. This is clear if
v=0
, since in that case

V=(h^{\leftslice})^{-1}(]1,\infty])
is open in the cone topology, by
Proposition
3.20
. Hence we assume
that
v\neq 0
, and we write
v
as
(s,y)
, with
s>0
.

If
0\in V
, then
h^{\leftslice}(v)>1
. For every
u\in\mathrm{cone}(\mathfrak{B})
,

v\leq^{\mathrm{cone}}u+v
(see Remark
3.5
), and since

(h^{\leftslice})^{-1}(]1,\infty])
is open in the cone topology, as we have
just recalled, it is upwards-closed, and therefore
h^{\leftslice}(u+v)>1
.
This shows that every
u\in\mathrm{cone}(\mathfrak{B})
is in

V=(\_+v)^{-1}((h^{\leftslice})^{-1}(]1,\infty]))
, so

V=\mathrm{cone}(\mathfrak{B})
, which is open.

We therefore assume that
0\not\in V
. For every point

(r,x)\in\mathrm{cone}(\mathfrak{B})\smallsetminus\{0\}
,
(r,x)\in V
if and only if

h^{\leftslice}((r,x)+(s,y))>1
, equivalently

(r+s)h(x+_{\frac{r}{r+s}}y)>1
. Let

f\colon(\mathbb{R}_{+}\smallsetminus\{0\})_{\sigma}\times\mathfrak{B}\to\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
map

(r,x)
to
(r+s)h(x+_{\frac{r}{r+s}}y)
: we have just
shown that
V=f^{-1}(]1,\infty])
. We claim that
V
is open in
the product topology on
(\mathbb{R}_{+}\smallsetminus\{0\})_{\sigma}\times\mathfrak{B}
, and
upwards-closed in
\mathrm{cone}(\mathfrak{B})
.

By Proposition
3.20
,

(h^{\leftslice})^{-1}(]1,\infty])
is open in the cone topology, hence
upwards-closed. The function
\_+v
is monotonic by
Proposition
3.4
, item 1, so

V=(\_+v)^{-1}((h^{\leftslice})^{-1}(]1,\infty]))
is upwards-closed.

With
x
fixed,
f(\_,x)
is the composition of

a\mapsto\frac{1}{1-a}h(x+_{a}y)
(which is Scott-continuous by
Lemma
3.21
) with

r\mapsto\frac{r}{r+s}
, which is also Scott-continuous from

\mathbb{R}_{+}\smallsetminus\{0\}
to
]0,1[
(indeed, it is continuous with respect
to the standard topology on the reals, and monotonic since we can
write
\frac{r}{r+s}
as
1-\frac{s}{r+s}
); hence
f(\_,x)

is Scott-continuous. With
r
fixed,
f(r,\_)
is continuous from

\mathfrak{B}
to
\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
, too, since
h
is, scalar multiplication by
r+s

is, and
x\mapsto x+_{\frac{r}{r+s}}y
is continuous, owing to
the fact that
\mathfrak{B}
is semitopological. Therefore
f
is separately
continuous from
(\mathbb{R}_{+}\smallsetminus\{0\})\times\mathfrak{B}
to
\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
. By
Ershov’s observation, and since
\mathbb{R}_{+}\smallsetminus\{0\}
is a continuous
poset (with
<
as way-below relation),
f
is jointly continuous.
Hence
V=f^{-1}(]1,\infty])
is open in the product topology on

(\mathbb{R}_{+}\smallsetminus\{0\})_{\sigma}\times\mathfrak{B}
.

2. The map
\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{B}}
is affine and injective. In order to show
that it is continuous, it suffices to show that

(\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{B}})^{-1}((h^{\leftslice})^{-1}(]1,\infty])
is open in
\mathfrak{B}

for every semi-concave lower semicontinuous map

h\colon\mathfrak{B}\to\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
, by
Proposition
3.20
. Now

(\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{B}})^{-1}((h^{\leftslice})^{-1}(]1,\infty])=(h^{\leftslice}\circ\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{B}})^{-1}(]1,\infty])=h^{-1}(]1,\infty])
(by
Proposition
3.4
, item 3), which is open
since
h
is lower semicontinuous.

3. Let
f\colon\mathfrak{B}\to\mathfrak{C}
be semi-concave and continuous. Since
every semitopological barycentric algebra (resp., cone) is a
preordered barycentric algebra (resp., cone) in its specialization
preordering, and since continuous maps are monotonic with respect to
the specialization preorderings, the uniqueness of
f^{\leftslice}
follows
from Proposition
3.4
, item 3. Similarly,
if
f^{\leftslice}
exists, then if is positively homogeneous, and if
f
is
concave (resp. affine), then it is superlinear (resp. linear).

We recall that
f^{\leftslice}
is defined by
f^{\leftslice}(0)\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}0
and

f^{\leftslice}(r,x)\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}rf(x)
. It remains to show that
f^{\leftslice}

is continuous. Let
V
be an open subset of
\mathfrak{C}
. If
0\in V
,
then
V=\mathfrak{C}
since
0
is least in
\mathfrak{C}
, and then

(f^{\leftslice})^{-1}(V)=\mathrm{cone}(\mathfrak{B})
is open. Hence we assume that

0\not\in V
. Then
(f^{\leftslice})^{-1}(V)
does not contain
0
, and
we show that
(f^{\leftslice})^{-1}(V)
is open in

(\mathbb{R}_{+}\smallsetminus\{0\})_{\sigma}\times\mathfrak{B}
and upwards-closed in

\mathrm{cone}(\mathfrak{B})
.

For every
u\in(f^{\leftslice})^{-1}(V)
, for every
v\in\mathrm{cone}(\mathfrak{B})

such that
u\leq^{\mathrm{cone}}v
, we verify that
v\in(f^{\leftslice})^{-1}(V)
.
Since
u\neq 0
,
v
is also different from
0
, and we write
v

as
(s,y)
. There is a point
u^{\prime}\in\mathrm{cone}(\mathfrak{B})
and an element

y_{1}\in\mathfrak{B}
such that
u+u^{\prime}=(s,y_{1})
and
y_{1}\leq y
. Then

f^{\leftslice}(v)=sf(y)\geq sf(y_{1})
(since
f
, being continuous,
is monotonic)
=f^{\leftslice}(u+u^{\prime})\geq f^{\leftslice}(u)
(since
f
is
monotonic and
u+u^{\prime}\geq u
by Remark
3.5
).
Since
V
is upwards-closed,
f^{\leftslice}(v)\in V
. Therefore

v\in(f^{\leftslice})^{-1}(V)
.

Next, we check that
(f^{\leftslice})^{-1}(V)
is open in

(\mathbb{R}_{+}\smallsetminus\{0\})_{\sigma}\times\mathfrak{B}
. The restriction of
f^{\leftslice}

to
(\mathbb{R}_{+}\smallsetminus\{0\})_{\sigma}\times\mathfrak{B}
is the function

(r,x)\mapsto rf(x)
. This is Scott-continuous in
r
and
continuous in
x
, since
f
is continuous. By Ershov’s
observation, this restriction of
f^{\leftslice}
is jointly continuous
from
(\mathbb{R}_{+}\smallsetminus\{0\})\times\mathfrak{B}
to
\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
, so

(f^{\leftslice})^{-1}(V)
is open in
(\mathbb{R}_{+}\smallsetminus\{0\})_{\sigma}\times\mathfrak{B}

with the product topology.

4.
(a)\mathrel{\Rightarrow}(b)
: Let
i\colon\mathfrak{B}\to\mathfrak{C}
be an affine
topological embedding of
\mathfrak{B}
in a semitopological cone
\mathfrak{C}
. For
every open subset
U
of
\mathfrak{B}
, there is an open subset
V
of
\mathfrak{C}

such that
U=i^{-1}(V)
. By item 3, there is a linear continuous
map
i^{\leftslice}\colon\mathrm{cone}(\mathfrak{B})\to\mathfrak{C}
such that

i^{\leftslice}\circ\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{B}}=i
. Hence

U=(i^{\leftslice}\circ\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{B}})^{-1}(V)=(\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{B}})^{-1}((i^{\leftslice})^{-1}(V))
, showing that
U=(\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{B}})^{-1}(V^{\prime})

where
V^{\prime}
is the open set
(i^{\leftslice})^{-1}(V)
. It follows that

\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{B}}
is full, and therefore a topological embedding, using
item 2.

(b)\mathrel{\Rightarrow}(c)
. By item
(b)
, the open subsets of
\mathfrak{B}
are exactly
the sets of the form
(\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{B}})^{-1}(V)
where
V
is open in

\mathrm{cone}(\mathfrak{B})
. If
V
is the whole of
\mathrm{cone}(\mathfrak{B})
, then

(\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{B}})^{-1}(V)=\mathfrak{B}
, which we can write as

h^{-1}(]1,\infty])
, where
h
is the constant function
2
, say;
clearly, this function is semi-concave and lower semicontinuous. If

V
is proper, then
V=(h^{\leftslice})^{-1}(]1,\infty])
for some
semi-concave lower semicontinuous map
h\colon\mathfrak{B}\to\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
by
Proposition
3.20
; then

(\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{B}})^{-1}(V)=(h^{\leftslice}\circ\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{B}})^{-1}(]1,\infty])=h^{-1}(]1,\infty])
.

(c)\mathrel{\Rightarrow}(d)
: this is clear.

(d)\mathrel{\Rightarrow}(b)\mathrel{\Rightarrow}(a)
. We assume that the sets

h^{-1}(]1,\infty])
where
h
ranges over the semi-concave lower
semicontinuous maps from
\mathfrak{B}
to
\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
form a subbase of the
topology on
\mathfrak{B}
. Since
h^{\leftslice}\circ\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{B}}=h
, every
subbasic open set
h^{-1}(]1,\infty])
is equal to

{(\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{B}})}^{-1}((h^{\leftslice})^{-1}(]1,\infty]))
. Hence

\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{B}}
is full, and using item 2,
\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{B}}
is a
topological embedding. This shows
(b)
, hence
(a)
, thanks to
item 1.
\Box

Remark 3.23

Let
\mathfrak{C}
be a semitopological cone, for example
\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
. Using
Theorem
3.22
, item 3, there is a bijection

f\mapsto f^{\leftslice}
, with inverse
M\mapsto M\circ\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{B}}
,
between semi-concave, resp. concave, resp. affine lower
semicontinuous maps
f
from
\mathfrak{B}
to
\mathfrak{C}
and positively
homogeneous, resp. superlinear, resp. sublinear lower
semicontinuous maps
M
from
\mathrm{cone}(\mathfrak{B})
to
\mathfrak{C}
. The only
missing argument is that
M\circ\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{B}}
is semi-concave,
resp. concave, resp. affine if
M
is positively homogeneous,
resp. superlinear, resp. sublinear, and this is easily verified,
using Fact
3.19
notably. Additionally, by
definition of
f^{\leftslice}
,
f\mapsto f^{\leftslice}
is monotonic, preserves
pointwise suprema, binary pointwise infima, sums and scalar products
of semi-concave lower semicontinuous maps, and so does its inverse

M\mapsto M\circ\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{B}}
.

We now have enough to exhibit our prime example of a non-embeddable
semitopological barycentric algebra.

Example 3.24

Let
\mathfrak{B}^{-}\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}{]-\infty,0]}
, with the Scott topology of its
usual ordering. We define
x+_{a}y
for all
x,y\in\mathfrak{B}^{-}
and

a\in[0,1]
as
a\cdot x+(1-a)\cdot y
.

1.

With these operations,
\mathfrak{B}^{-}
is a topological cone.

2.

The only semi-concave lower semicontinuous maps on
\mathfrak{B}^{-}
are
the constant maps, and therefore
\mathfrak{B}^{-}
is not embeddable.

3.

The injective affine continuous map
\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{B}}
is not a
topological embedding.

4.

The open subsets in the cone topology on
\mathrm{cone}(\mathfrak{B}^{-})
are

\mathrm{cone}(\mathfrak{B}^{-})
, the empty set, and the sets

{\mathcal{O}}_{t}\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\{(s,x)\in\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathfrak{B}^{-}\mid s>t\}
,

t\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
.

5.

The specialization preordering of
\mathrm{cone}(\mathfrak{B}^{-})
is given by:

u
is less than or equal to
v
if and only if

\ell(u)\leq\ell(v)
, where the level
\ell(u)
of an element

u
is defined by
\ell(0)\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}0
,
\ell(r,x)\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}r
.

6.

\mathrm{cone}(\mathfrak{B})
is not
T_{0}
, although
\mathfrak{B}^{-}
is.

Proof.

1. The barycentric algebra equations are obvious. Let

(x_{0},a_{0},y_{0})\in\mathfrak{B}^{-}\times[0,1]\times\mathfrak{B}^{-}
and let us
consider a basic open neighborhood
{]b,0]}
of
x_{0}+_{a_{0}}y_{0}

in
\mathfrak{B}^{-}
. We have
a_{0}x_{0}+(1-a_{0})y_{0}>b
. Let
\epsilon>0
be
such that:
(a)

a_{0}x_{0}+(1-a_{0})y_{0}+\epsilon(x_{0}+y_{0}-1)>b
.

For every
a\in[0,1]
such that
|a-a_{0}|<\epsilon
, for every

x>x_{0}-\epsilon
, for every
y>y_{0}-\epsilon
, we have

ax+(1-a)y>a(x_{0}-\epsilon)+(1-a)(y_{0}-\epsilon)
(the large
inequality
\geq
is clear, and the inequality is strict because at
least one of
a
,
1-a
is non-zero)

=ax_{0}+(1-a)y_{0}-\epsilon\geq(a_{0}+\epsilon)x_{0}+(1-a_{0}+\epsilon)y_{0}-\epsilon
(taking into account that

x_{0},y_{0}\leq 0
)

=a_{0}x_{0}+(1-a_{0})y_{0}+\epsilon(x_{0}+y_{0}-1)>b
(by
condition
(a)
).

Hence the triples
(x,a,y)
in
\mathfrak{B}^{-}\times[0,1]\times\mathfrak{B}^{-}

such that
a\in{]a_{0}-\epsilon,a_{0}+\epsilon[}
,

x>x_{0}-\epsilon
and
y>y_{0}-\epsilon
, which form an open
set, are all mapped to
{]b,0]}
by the
\_+_{\_}\_
operation: the

\_+_{\_}\_
operation is jointly continuous.

2. Let
C
be a closed subset of
\mathfrak{B}^{-}
of the form

h^{-1}([0,1])
with
h
semi-concave and lower semicontinuous.
We claim that
C
is trivial, namely empty or equal to the whole of

\mathfrak{B}^{-}
. To this end, we assume that
C
is non-empty. By
definition of the Scott topology, it must be of the form

]-\infty,a_{0}]
for some
a_{0}\in{]-\infty,0]}
. Since

a_{0}\in C
,
h(a_{0})\leq 1
. For every
n\in\mathbb{N}
,

a_{0}=0+_{1-1/2^{n}}(a_{0}2^{n})
, so
h(a_{0})\geq(1-1/2^{n})h(0)
,
since
h
is semi-concave. Taking suprema over
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
h(a_{0})\geq h(0)
, so
h(0)\leq 1
. It follows that
0\in C
,
hence
a_{0}=0
and
C=\mathfrak{B}^{-}
.

Let
h\colon\mathfrak{B}^{-}\to\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
be any semi-concave lower
semicontinuous map. If
h
is not constant, we can find two points

x
and
y
such that
h(x)<h(y)
. Let
a\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
be such that

h(x)<a<h(y)
. Then
(1/a)h
is semi-concave and lower
semicontinuous by Remark
3.18
. It follows that

((1/a)h)^{-1}([0,1])
is trivial. But
((1/a)h)^{-1}([0,1])

contains
x
and not
y
, and this is impossible. Therefore
h
is
constant. Conversely, every constant map is semi-concave and lower
semicontinuous by Remark
3.18
.

Any non-trivial closed subset, such as
]-\infty,a]
with
a<0
,
shows that item 4
(d)
of Theorem
3.22
fails.
Hence item 4
(a)
fails, too:
\mathfrak{B}^{-}
is not embeddable.

3. Because item 4
(b)
of Theorem
3.22
must
also fail.

4. The only semi-concave lower semicontinuous maps from
\mathfrak{B}^{-}
to

\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
are the constant maps. For a constant map
h
with value

a
,
h^{\leftslice}
maps
0
to
0
and
(r,x)
to
ra
. Hence

{(h^{\leftslice})}^{-1}(]1,\infty])
is

\{(r,x)\in\mathrm{cone}(\mathfrak{B})\smallsetminus\{0\}\mid r>1/a\}={\mathcal{O}}_{1/a}

if
a\neq 0,\infty
, the empty set if
a=0
, and
{\mathcal{O}}_{0}
if

a=\infty
.

5. By item 4,
u
is less than or equal to
v
in the
specialization preordering of
\mathrm{cone}(\mathfrak{B}^{-})
if and only if for
every
a\in\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
,
u\in{\mathcal{O}}_{a}
implies
v\in{\mathcal{O}}_{a}
. If

u=0
, this is always true. Let therefore
u
be of the form

(r,x)
, with
r\in\mathbb{R}_{+}\smallsetminus\{0\}
. Then
u
is less than or
equal to
v
if and only if for every
a\in\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
such that

a<r
,
v
is of the form
(s,y)
with
a<s
; that is
equivalent to
r\leq s
.

6. By item 5, any two elements with the same level are less than or
equal to each other.
\Box

Theorem 3.25

For every topological barycentric algebra
\mathfrak{B}
,
\mathrm{cone}(\mathfrak{B})
is a
topological cone.

Proof.

The cone
\mathrm{cone}(\mathfrak{B})
is semitopological by
Theorem
3.22
, item 1. It remains to show that

+
is jointly continuous on
\mathrm{cone}(\mathfrak{B})
.

Let
W
be an open subset of
\mathrm{cone}(\mathfrak{B})
. We claim that

+^{-1}(W)
is open in
\mathrm{cone}(\mathfrak{B})\times\mathrm{cone}(\mathfrak{B})
. This is
clear if
W=\mathrm{cone}(\mathfrak{B})
, since then

+^{-1}(W)=\mathrm{cone}(\mathfrak{B})\times\mathrm{cone}(\mathfrak{B})
. Hence we will assume
that
W
is proper.

Let
(u,v)\in+^{-1}(W)
. If
u=0
, then
v\in W
, and for
every
u^{\prime}\in\mathrm{cone}^{\prime}(\mathfrak{B})
and for every
v^{\prime}\in W
,

u^{\prime}+v^{\prime}\geq 0+v^{\prime}
(since
0
is least and
+
is monotonic in a
preordered cone)
=v^{\prime}\in W
, so
\mathrm{cone}(\mathfrak{B})\times W
is an open
neighborhood of
(u,v)
that is included in
+^{-1}(W)
. The
argument is similar if
v=0
.

Let therefore
u
be written as
(r,x)
and
v
as
(s,y)
, where

r,s>0
. Since
u+v\in W
and
W
is proper, there is an open
rectangle
{]a,\infty[}\times A
containing
u+v
and included in

W
, where
a>0
and
A
is open in
\mathfrak{B}
. Since

u+v=(r+s,x+_{\frac{r}{r+s}}y)
, we have
r+s>a
and

x+_{\frac{r}{r+s}}y\in A
.
\mathfrak{B}
is topological, so there are an

\epsilon>0
, an open neighborhood
U
of
x
and an open
neighborhood
V
of
y
