such that for every

b\in{]\frac{r}{r+s}-\epsilon,\frac{r}{r+s}+\epsilon[}
, for
every
x^{\prime}\in U
and for every
y^{\prime}\in V
,
x^{\prime}+_{b}y\in A
. We
take
\epsilon
so small that
0<\frac{r}{r+s}-\epsilon
and

\frac{r}{r+s}+\epsilon<1
. The function

(r^{\prime},s^{\prime})\mapsto\frac{r^{\prime}}{r^{\prime}+s^{\prime}}
is (jointly) continuous from

{]0,\infty[}\times{]0,\infty[}
to
]0,\infty[
, where

]0,\infty[
is given its usual metric topology, and it takes the
value
\frac{r}{r+s}
at
(r^{\prime},s^{\prime})\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}(r,s)
. Hence there is
an
\eta>0
such that for every
r^{\prime}\in{]r-\eta,r+\eta[}
and
every
s^{\prime}\in{]s-\eta,s+\eta[}
,
\frac{r^{\prime}}{r^{\prime}+s^{\prime}}
is in

{]\frac{r}{r+s}-\epsilon,\frac{r}{r+s}+\epsilon[}
. We can
take
\eta
so small that
r-\eta>0
,
s-\eta>0
,

r+\eta<1
,
s+\eta<1
, and
2\eta<r+s-a
, too. We
consider the open rectangle

({]r-\eta,\infty[}\times U)\times({]r-\eta,\infty[}\times V)
. By construction, this open rectangle contains

((r,x),(s,y))
.

For every pair of points
(r^{\prime},x^{\prime})
and
(s^{\prime},y^{\prime})
in the open
rectangle

({]r-\eta,\infty[}\times U)\times({]r-\eta,\infty[}\times V)
, we claim that
(r^{\prime},x^{\prime})+(s^{\prime},y^{\prime})\in+^{-1}(W)
.

Case 1.
We verify that
(r^{\prime},x^{\prime})+(s^{\prime},y^{\prime})\in+^{-1}(W)

when
(r^{\prime},x^{\prime})\in{]r-\eta,r+\eta[}\times U
and

(s^{\prime},y^{\prime})\in{]r-\eta,r+\eta[}\times V
. In that case,

(r^{\prime},x^{\prime})+(s^{\prime},y^{\prime})=(r^{\prime}+s^{\prime},x^{\prime}+_{\frac{r^{\prime}}{r^{\prime}+s}}y)
is such
that
r^{\prime}+s^{\prime}>r+s-2\eta>a
, since
2\eta<r+s-a
; since

r^{\prime}\in{]r-\eta,r+\eta[}
and
s^{\prime}\in{]s-\eta,s+\eta[}
,

\frac{r^{\prime}}{r^{\prime}+s^{\prime}}
is in

{]\frac{r}{r+s}-\epsilon,\frac{r}{r+s}+\epsilon[}
, and using
the fact that
x^{\prime}\in U
and
y^{\prime}\in V
,

x^{\prime}+_{\frac{r^{\prime}}{r^{\prime}+s^{\prime}}}y
is in
A
. Therefore

(r^{\prime},x^{\prime})+(s^{\prime},y^{\prime})\in{]a,\infty[}\times A\subseteq W
.

Case 2.
We verify that
(r^{\prime},x^{\prime})+(s^{\prime},y^{\prime})\in+^{-1}(W)

when
(r^{\prime},x^{\prime})\in{[r,\infty[}\times U
and

(s^{\prime},y^{\prime})\in{]s-\eta,s+\eta[}\times V
. Since
r^{\prime}\geq r
, we
can write
(r^{\prime},x^{\prime})
as
(r,x^{\prime})+(r^{\prime}-r,x^{\prime})
(see Remark
3.5
).
Then
(r^{\prime},x^{\prime})+(s^{\prime},y^{\prime})=((r,x^{\prime})+(s^{\prime},y^{\prime}))+(r^{\prime}-r,x^{\prime})
is
larger than or equal to
(r,x^{\prime})+(s^{\prime},y^{\prime})
, and the latter is in

W
by Case 1. Since
W
is upwards-closed,

(r^{\prime},x^{\prime})+(s^{\prime},y^{\prime})\in W
.

Together with Case 1, we have shown that
(r^{\prime},x^{\prime})+(s^{\prime},y^{\prime})\in+^{-1}(W)

for all
(r^{\prime},x^{\prime})\in{]r-\eta,\infty[}\times U
and

(s^{\prime},y^{\prime})\in{]s-\eta,s+\eta[}\times V
.

Case 3.
For every
(r^{\prime},x^{\prime})\in{]r-\eta,\infty[}\times U

and for every
(s^{\prime},y^{\prime})\in{[s,\infty[}\times V
, we write

(s^{\prime},y^{\prime})
as
(s,y^{\prime})+(s^{\prime}-s,y)
, using the fact that
s^{\prime}\geq s

(see Remark
3.5
). Then

(r^{\prime},x^{\prime})+(s^{\prime},y^{\prime})=((r^{\prime},x^{\prime})+(s,y^{\prime}))+(s^{\prime}-s,y^{\prime})
is larger
than or equal to
(r^{\prime},x^{\prime})+(s,y^{\prime})
, which is in
W
by Cases 1
and 2. Since
W
is upwards-closed,
(r^{\prime},x^{\prime})+(s^{\prime},y^{\prime})
is in

W
.
\Box

3.4
The free semitopological cone over
{\mathbf{V}}_{1}X

Since one of our primary examples of barycentric algebras is

{\mathbf{V}}_{1}X
, it is interesting to note that

{\mathbf{V}}_{b}X\cong\mathrm{cone}({\mathbf{V}}_{1}X)
. This is less trivial than it may
seem, and in order to prove it, we will need some preparation, and
notably a decomposition lemma due to Matthias Schröder and Alex
Simpson. This lemma is implicit in
[
44
, paragraph after
Theorem 4.2]
, and is stated in

[
45
, Slide 38]
. Since no proof seems to exist in
print, we include one here, as Proposition
3.26

below; but the result and the proof must be credited to Matthias
Schröder and Alex Simpson.

In order to deal with it in full generality, we extend the notion of
valuation slightly. A
lattice of subsets

\mathcal{L}
of
X
is a
collection of subsets of
X
that is closed under finite intersection
and finite union. Extending our previous notion, a
valuation

on
(X,\mathcal{L})
is a strict, modular and monotonic (Scott-continuity
is not required) map from
\mathcal{L}
to
\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
; it is
bounded
if

\nu(X)<\infty
.
An
algebra of subsets
if a lattice of subsets that is closed
under complements. Let
\mathcal{A}(\mathcal{L})
be the smallest algebra
of subsets of
X
containing
\mathcal{L}
; its elements are the finite
disjoint unions of
crescents
, where a crescent on
\mathcal{L}
is a
difference
U\smallsetminus V
of two elements
U,V\in\mathcal{L}
. The

Smiley-Horn-Tarski theorem
states that every bounded valuation

\nu
on
(X,\mathcal{L})
extends to a unique valuation on

(X,\mathcal{A}(\mathcal{L}))
—still written
\nu
—, which is bounded,
and satisfies

\nu(U\smallsetminus V)=\nu(U)-\nu(U\cap V)=\nu(U\cup V)-\nu(V)
for all
U,V\in\mathcal{L}
. This is sometimes attributed to Billy
J. Pettis
[
39
]
, but similar results had been proved by
Malcolm F.
[
46
]
and Alfred Horn and Alfred Tarski

[
24
]
.

Given any bounded valuation
\nu
on
(X,\mathcal{L})
and crescent
C
on

\mathcal{L}
, one can then define a new bounded valuation
\nu_{|C}
on
(X,\mathcal{L})
by
\nu_{|C}(U)\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\nu(U\cap C)
for every
U\in\mathcal{L}
. This is sometimes called the restriction of
\nu
to
C
, but
we prefer to use the term
constriction
: we wish to avoid
confusing this with, say, the restriction of
\nu
to a smaller
lattice of subsets.

When
X
is a topological space,
\mathcal{L}={\mathcal{O}}X
and
\nu
is a
bounded
continuous
valuation, then
\nu_{|C}
is a bounded
continuous valuation. Indeed, let us write
C
as
U\smallsetminus V
, where

U,V\in{\mathcal{O}}X
. For every directed family
{(U_{i})}_{i\in I}
of
open subsets of
X
,

\nu_{|C}(\bigcup_{i\in I}U_{i})=\nu(\bigcup_{i\in I}U_{i}\cap U\smallsetminus V)=\nu((\bigcup_{i\in I}U_{i}\cap U)\cup V)-\nu(V)=\sup_{i\in I}\nu((U_{i}\cap U)\cup V)-\nu(V)=\sup_{i\in I}\nu_{|C}(U_{i})
.

Here is the announced Schröder-Simpson decomposition lemma.

Proposition 3.26
(Schröder-Simpson)

Let
X
be a set with a lattice of subsets
\mathcal{L}
, and
\mu
and

\nu
be two bounded valuations on
(X,\mathcal{L})
. Let

L\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\{U_{1},\cdots,U_{n}\}
be a finite lattice of subsets of

X
included in
\mathcal{L}
, and let us assume that

\mu(U_{i})\leq\nu(U_{i})
for every
i
,
1\leq i\leq n
. Then
there are two bounded valuations
\nu_{1}
and
\nu_{2}
on
(X,\mathcal{L})

such that:

1.

\nu=\nu_{1}+\nu_{2}
;

2.

\mu(U_{i})\leq\nu_{1}(U_{i})
for every
i
,
1\leq i\leq n
;

3.

and
\mu(X)=\nu_{1}(X)
.

Additionally,
\nu_{1}
and
\nu_{2}
are finite linear combinations of
constrictions
\nu_{|C}
of
\nu
to pairwise disjoint crescents
C
.

Proof.

We extend
\mu
and
\nu
uniquely to bounded valuations on

\mathcal{A}(\mathcal{L})
, using the Smiley-Horn-Tarski theorem.

Let
M
be
\mathbb{P}(\{1,\cdots,n\})
, ordered by inclusion. We will
silently see
M
as a topological space, with the Alexandroff
topology of inclusion, that is, its open sets are the upwards-closed
subsets of
M
. For each
I\in M
, let
C_{I}
be the crescent

\bigcap_{i\in I}U_{i}\smallsetminus\bigcup_{i\not\in I}U_{i}
, and let us
note that the sets
C_{I}
are pairwise disjoint.

We define a map
f\colon X\to M
by letting
f(x)
be the set of
indices
i
,
1\leq i\leq n
, such that
x\in U_{i}
, and we note
that
C_{I}=f^{-1}(\{I\})
for every
I\in M
. It follows that
for every
A\subseteq M
,
f^{-1}(A)
is the disjoint union

\bigcup_{I\in A}C_{I}
.

Given any fixed element
I
of
M
, let us show that

f^{-1}(\upc I)=\bigcap_{i\in I}U_{i}
. For every

x\in f^{-1}(\upc I)
,
f(x)
contains
I
, so for every

i\in I
,
i\in f(x)
, meaning that
x
is in
U_{i}
: in other
words,
x\in\bigcap_{i\in I}U_{i}
. Conversely, for every

x\in\bigcap_{i\in I}U_{i}
,
f(x)
must contain at least all the
indices in
I
, so
I\subseteq f(x)
, meaning that

x\in f^{-1}(\upc I)
.

Since
L
is closed under finite intersections,
f^{-1}(\upc I)
is
in
L
.

For every upwards-closed subset
A
of
M
,
A
is equal to the
union of all sets
\upc I
,
I\in A
, so

f^{-1}(A)=\bigcup_{I\in A}f^{-1}(\upc I)
. Since
L
is
closed under finite unions,
f^{-1}(A)\in L
for every
upwards-closed subset
A
of
M
.

There is an image valuation
f[\mu]
, defined by

f[\mu](A)\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\mu(f^{-1}(A))
for every open
(upwards-closed) subset
A
of
M
, and it is bounded; similarly
with
f[\nu]
. We observe that
f[\mu]\leq f[\nu]
. Indeed,
for every upwards-closed subset
A
of
M
,

f[\mu](A)=\mu(f^{-1}(A))
and

f[\nu](A)=\nu(f^{-1}(A))
. Since
f^{-1}(A)
is in
L
, it
is equal to
U_{i}
for some
i
, and the claim follows from our
assumption that
\mu(U_{i})\leq\nu(U_{i})
.

Since
M
is finite,
f[\mu]
and
f[\nu]
are simple valuations.
Explicitly,
f[\mu]=\sum_{I\in M}a_{I}\delta_{I}
where

a_{I}\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\mu(C_{I})
. Indeed, for every (upwards-closed) subset

A
of
M
, we write
f^{-1}(A)
as the disjoint union of crescents

C_{I}
,
I\in A
, so
\mu(f^{-1}(A))=\sum_{I\in A}\mu(C_{I})
.
Similarly, let us write
f[\nu]
as
\sum_{J\in M}b_{J}\delta_{J}
.

We can now use Claire Jones’ splitting lemma. This was first stated
in
[
26
, Theorem 4.10]
(for subprobability valuations,
although that is not immediately apparent), and we will use the
slightly more general version of
[
13
, Proposition
IV-9.18]
. Two simple valuations, such as
f[\mu]

and
f[\nu]
above, as such that the first one is less than or
equal to the second one in the stochastic ordering if and only if
there is a
transport matrix

{(t_{IJ})}_{I,J\in M}
of
non-negative real numbers such that:
t_{IJ}\neq 0
implies

I\subseteq J
,
\sum_{J\in M}t_{IJ}=a_{I}
for every
I\in M
,
and
\sum_{I\in M}t_{IJ}\leq b_{J}
for every
J\in M
. We define

\nu_{1}
as
\sum_{J\in M}c_{J}\nu_{|C_{J}}
and
\nu_{2}
as

\sum_{J\in M}(1-c_{J})\nu_{|C_{J}}
, where

c_{J}\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\frac{\sum_{I\in M}t_{IJ}}{b_{J}}
for every

J\in M
, provided that
b_{J}\neq 0
, and
c_{J}=0
otherwise.
Those are bounded valuations, and they are continuous if
\mathcal{L}
is
a topology and
\mu
and
\nu
are continuous.

1. By construction,
\nu_{1}+\nu_{2}=\sum_{J\in M}\nu_{|C_{J}}
, and
the latter equals
\nu
: for every
U\in\mathcal{L}
,

\sum_{J\in M}\nu_{|C_{J}}(U)=\sum_{J\in M}\nu(U\cap C_{J})=\nu(\bigcup_{J\in M}U\cap C_{J})=\nu(U\cap\bigcup_{J\in M}C_{J})=\nu(U)
, because
X
is the disjoint union of all
C_{J}
s
(
\bigcup_{J\in M}C_{J}=\bigcup_{J\in M}f^{-1}(\{J\})=f^{-1}(M)=X
).

2. For every
i
,
1\leq i\leq n
, we check that

\mu(U_{i})\leq\nu_{1}(U_{i})
. Let

A\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\{I\in M\mid i\in I\}=\upc\{i\}
. This is
upwards-closed in
M
, and
U_{i}
is the disjoint union

\bigcup_{I\in A}C_{I}
, because the latter is equal to

\bigcup_{I\in A}f^{-1}(\{I\})=f^{-1}(\upc\{i\})=U_{i}
.
Also,
\mu(C_{I})=\mu(f^{-1}(\{I\}))=a_{I}
. Then

\mu(U_{i})=\sum_{I\in A}\mu(C_{I})=\sum_{I\in A}a_{I}=\sum_{I\in A,J\in M}t_{IJ}
. The terms
t_{IJ}
with
I\in A
and

J\not\in A
are zero, and can be removed from the sum; indeed, if

t_{IJ}
were non-zero, then
I\subseteq J
, and since
I\in A

and
A
is upwards-closed,
J
would also be in
A
. Therefore

\mu(U_{i})=\sum_{I,J\in A}t_{IJ}
. We compare this to

\nu_{1}(U_{i})
, which is equal to

\sum_{J\in A}c_{J}\nu(U_{i}\cap C_{J})
. For every
J\in A
, since

i\in J
we have
C_{J}\subseteq U_{i}
, so

\nu_{1}(U_{i})=\sum_{J\in A}c_{J}\nu(C_{J})=\sum_{J\in A}c_{J}b_{J}\geq\sum_{I\in M,J\in A}t_{IJ}
, and that is larger than or
equal to
\sum_{I,J\in A}t_{IJ}=\mu(U_{i})
.

3. We use a similar argument with
A\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}M
:

\mu(X)=\sum_{I\in M}\mu(C_{I})=\sum_{I\in M}a_{I}=\sum_{I,J\in M}t_{IJ}=\sum_{J\in M}c_{J}b_{J}
by definition ot
c_{J}
.
We have
b_{J}=\nu(C_{J})
, so

\mu(X)=\sum_{J\in M}c_{J}\nu(C_{J})=\nu_{1}(X)
.
\Box

Theorem 3.27

For every topological space
X
,
{\mathbf{V}}_{b}X
is the free cone over
the barycentric algebra
{\mathbf{V}}_{1}X
and
{\mathbf{V}}_{b}X
is the free
semitopological cone over the semitopological barycentric algebra

{\mathbf{V}}_{1}X
.

As a consequence,
{\mathbf{V}}_{b}X
and
\mathrm{cone}({\mathbf{V}}_{1}X)
are naturally
isomorphic semitopological cones.

Similarly,
{\mathbf{V}}_{b,\mathrm{p}}X
is the free semitopological cone over
the semitopological barycentric algebra
{\mathbf{V}}_{1,\mathrm{p}}X
.

The first part of the proposition states, equivalently, that for every
cone
\mathfrak{C}
, every affine map
f\colon{\mathbf{V}}_{1}X\to\mathfrak{C}
extends to a
unique linear map
\hat{f}
from
{\mathbf{V}}_{b}X
to
\mathfrak{C}
, which is
continuous if
f
is (assuming that
\mathfrak{C}
is a semitopological cone,
and that
{\mathbf{V}}_{1}X
and
{\mathbf{V}}_{b}X
are given the weak topology).

Proof.

The second part of the proposition follows from the first part and
the fact that free constructions are always unique up to natural
isomorphisms. We focus on the first part.

If
\hat{f}
exists, then for every
\nu\in{\mathbf{V}}_{b}X
, either
\nu

is the zero valuation and
\hat{f}(\nu)
must be equal to
0
, or

a\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\nu(X)
is non-zero, and in
\mathbb{R}_{+}
since
\nu
is bounded.
In that case,
\frac{1}{a}\cdot\nu
is in
{\mathbf{V}}_{1}X
, and since

\hat{f}
is positively homogeneous we must have

\hat{f}(\nu)=a\cdot\hat{f}\Big(\frac{1}{a}\cdot\nu\Big)=a\cdot f\Big(\frac{1}{a}\cdot\nu\Big)
. This shows that
\hat{f}
is
unique if it exists.

Let us define
\hat{f}
as mapping the zero valuation to
0
and
every non-zero element
\nu\in{\mathbf{V}}_{b}X
to

\nu(X)\cdot f\Big(\frac{1}{\nu(X)}\cdot\nu\Big)
. The
function
\hat{f}
is strict by definition. For every

a\in\mathbb{R}_{+}\smallsetminus\{0\}
, for every
\nu\in{\mathbf{V}}_{b}X
, it is easy to
see that
\hat{f}(a\cdot\nu)=a\cdot\hat{f}(\nu)
. Let us show
that
\hat{f}
is additive. For all
\mu,\nu\in{\mathbf{V}}_{b}X
, if

\mu
is the zero valuation then

\hat{f}(\mu+\nu)=\hat{f}(\nu)=0+\hat{f}(\nu)=\hat{f}(\mu)+\hat{f}(\nu)
, and similarly if
\nu
is the zero valuation.
Otherwise, let
a\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\mu(X)
,
b\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\nu(X)
, then:

\displaystyle\hat{f}(\mu+\nu)

\displaystyle=(a+b)\cdot f\left(\frac{1}{a+b}\cdot(\mu+\nu)\right)

\displaystyle=(a+b)\cdot f\left(\frac{a}{a+b}\cdot\frac{1}{a}\cdot\mu+\frac{b}{a+b}\cdot\frac{1}{b}\cdot\nu\right)

\displaystyle=(a+b)\cdot\biggl(\frac{a}{a+b}\cdot f\left(\frac{1}{a}\cdot\mu\right)

\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{b}{a+b}\cdot f\left(\frac{1}{b}\cdot\nu\right)\biggr)\quad\text{since $f$ is affine}

\displaystyle=a\cdot f\left(\frac{1}{a}\cdot\mu\right)+b\cdot f\left(\frac{1}{b}\cdot\nu\right)

\displaystyle=\hat{f}(\mu)+\hat{f}(\nu).

The semitopological case.
If
\mathfrak{C}
is a semitopological cone
and
f
is continuous from
{\mathbf{V}}_{1}X
to
\mathfrak{C}
, we claim that

\hat{f}
is continuous from
{\mathbf{V}}_{b}X
to
\mathfrak{C}
. This is the hard
part. Let
V
be an open subset of
\mathfrak{C}
. We aim to show that

{\hat{f}}^{-1}(V)
is open. This is obvious if
V=\mathfrak{C}
, so let us
assume that
V
is proper. Equivalently,
0
is not in
V
. We fix

\mu
in
{\hat{f}}^{-1}(V)
, and we will find a basic open set

\mathcal{U}
containing
\mu
and included in
{\hat{f}}^{-1}(V)
.

The set
\{\alpha\in\mathbb{R}_{+}\mid\alpha\cdot\hat{f}(\mu)\in V\}
is
open in
\mathbb{R}_{+}
, since scalar multiplication is continuous in its
first argument, and it contains
1
. Therefore we can find a value

\alpha\in{]0,1[}
such that
\alpha\cdot\hat{f}(\mu)
is in in

V
.

Let
a\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\mu(X)
. If
a
were equal to
0
, then
\mu
would
be the zero valuation, so
\hat{f}(\mu)
would be equal to
0
, but

\hat{f}(\mu)\in V
and
0\not\in V
. Hence
a\neq 0
, and

\hat{f}(\mu)=a\cdot f\Big(\frac{1}{a}\cdot\mu\Big)
. Then

\alpha a\cdot f\Big(\frac{1}{a}\cdot\mu\Big)
is in
V
, so

f\Big(\frac{1}{a}\cdot\mu\Big)
is in

\frac{1}{\alpha a}\cdot V\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\Big\{\frac{1}{\alpha a}\cdot y\mid y\in V\Big\}
. The latter is open, as the inverse image of

V
under the map
y\mapsto\alpha a\cdot y
. It follows that

\frac{1}{a}\cdot\mu
is in the open set

f^{-1}\Big(\frac{1}{\alpha a}\cdot V\Big)
.

By definition of the weak topology,
\frac{1}{a}\cdot\mu
is in some
finite intersection
\bigcap_{i=1}^{n}[r_{i}\ll U_{i}]_{1}
of subbasic
open sets of
{\mathbf{V}}_{1}X
, itself included in

f^{-1}\Big(\frac{1}{\alpha a}\cdot V\Big)
(we write

[r\ll U]_{1}
for
[r\ll U]\cap{\mathbf{V}}_{1}X
, in order to avoid
confusion with
[r\ll U]
, which we keep as a notation for an open
subset of
{\mathbf{V}}_{b}X
).

Let
L
be the smallest lattice of sets that contains every
U_{i}
.

L
consists of all unions of intersections of sets among
U_{1}
,
…,
U_{n}
. It is easy to see that
L
is finite (and contains
at most
2^{2^{n}}
elements). By taking the intersection of

\bigcap_{i=1}^{n}[r_{i}\ll U_{i}]_{1}
with the extra sets
[0\ll U]_{1}
,
where
U\in L\smallsetminus\{U_{1},\cdots,U_{n}\}
, and replacing

\{U_{1},\cdots,U_{n}\}
by
L
, we may assume that

\{U_{1},\cdots,U_{n}\}
is itself a lattice of subsets of
X
.

Let
\mathcal{U}
be the basic open set

\bigcap_{i=1}^{n}[\alpha\mu(U_{i})\ll U_{i}]
in
{\mathbf{V}}_{b}X
. Since

\alpha<1
,
\mu
is in
\mathcal{U}
.

We check that
\mathcal{U}
is included in
{\hat{f}}^{-1}(V)
. For
every
\nu\in\mathcal{U}
,
\alpha\mu(U_{i})\leq\nu(U_{i})
for
every
i
,
1\leq i\leq n
. By the Schröder and Simpson
decomposition lemma (Proposition
3.26
), there
are two bounded valuations
\nu_{1}
and
\nu_{2}
such that

\nu=\nu_{1}+\nu_{2}
,
\alpha\mu(U_{i})\leq\nu_{1}(U_{i})
for every

i
, and
\alpha\mu(X)=\nu_{1}(X)
—namely,

\nu_{1}(X)=\alpha a
. Then

\hat{f}(\nu)=\hat{f}(\nu_{1})+\hat{f}(\nu_{2})
(since
\hat{f}
is
additive)

\geq\hat{f}(\nu_{1})=(\alpha a)\cdot f\Big(\frac{1}{\alpha a}\cdot\nu_{1}\Big)
.

For every
i
,
\alpha\mu(U_{i})\leq\nu_{1}(U_{i})
and
r_{i}\ll\frac{1}{a}\cdot\mu(U_{i})
(since
\frac{1}{a}\cdot\mu
is in
[r_{i}\ll U_{i}]_{1}
), so
r_{i}\ll\frac{1}{\alpha a}\cdot\nu_{1}(U_{i})
. Hence
\frac{1}{\alpha a}\cdot\nu_{1}
is in
\bigcap_{i=1}^{n}[r_{i}\ll U_{i}]_{1}
, and the latter is included in
f^{-1}\Big(\frac{1}{\alpha a}\cdot V\Big)
. In other words,
f\Big(\frac{1}{\alpha a}\cdot\nu_{1}\Big)
is in
\frac{1}{\alpha a}\cdot V
, and therefore the larger value
\hat{f}(\nu)
is in

V
. Since
\nu
is arbitrary in
\mathcal{U}
,
\mathcal{U}\subseteq{\hat{f}}^{-1}(V)
.

In the case of
{\mathbf{V}}_{b,\mathrm{p}}X
and
{\mathbf{V}}_{1,\mathrm{p}}X
, we reason in
exactly the same way. We only have to pay attention that the two
valuations
\nu_{1}
and
\nu_{2}
obtained by using Schröder and
Simpson’s decomposition lemma
(Proposition
3.26
) are point-continuous. By
Proposition
3.26
,
\nu_{1}
and
\nu_{2}
are
obtained as finite linear combinations of constrictions
\nu_{|C}

of
\nu
to pairwise disjoint crescents
C
. Such constrictions are
point-continuous: this is an immediate consequence of the two
bulleted items of
[
23
, Section 3.3]
. Additionally,
finite linear combinations of point-continuous valuations are
point-continuous by
[
23
, Section 3.2]
.

3.5
Barycenters, part 2

Let us return to the barycenter construction

\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\cdot x_{i}
of Definition and
Proposition
3.1
. We topologize the standard simplex

\Delta_{n}
with the subspace topology induced by its inclusion in

\mathbb{R}^{n}
, where
\mathbb{R}
has its usual metric topology.

Proposition 3.28

For every topological barycentric algebra
\mathfrak{B}
, the map

(a_{1},\cdots,\allowbreak a_{n},x_{1},\cdots,x_{n})\mapsto\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\cdot x_{i}
is (jointly) continuous from

\Delta_{n}\times\mathfrak{B}\times\cdots\times\mathfrak{B}
to
\mathfrak{B}
.

Proof.

Let
\beta_{n}
be this map. We prove the claim by induction on

n\geq 1
. When
n=1
, this is clear. Otherwise, let us fix

(a_{1},\cdots,a_{n},x_{1},\cdots,x_{n})\in\Delta_{n}\times\mathfrak{B}\times\cdots\times\mathfrak{B}
and let us assume that

\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\cdot x_{i}
is in some open subset
W
of
\mathfrak{B}
. We
will find an open neighborhood of

(a_{1},\cdots,a_{n},x_{1},\cdots,x_{n})
whose image under
\beta_{n}

is included in
W
. By invariance under permutations (Definition
and Proposition
3.1
, item 2), we may assume that

a_{n}\neq 1
: since
n\geq 2
and
\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}=1
, some
a_{i}

is different from
1
, and we may permute indices
i
and
n
if
necessary. Then

\beta_{n}(a_{1},\cdots,a_{n},x_{1},\cdots,x_{n})=\beta_{n-1}(\frac{a_{1}}{1-a_{n}},\cdots,\frac{a_{n-1}}{1-a_{n}},x_{1},\cdots,x_{n-1})+_{1-a_{n}}x_{n}
by Definition and
Proposition
3.1
, item 1. Since
\mathfrak{B}
is topological,
there is an open neighborhood
V
of

\beta_{n-1}(\frac{a_{1}}{1-a_{n}},\cdots,\frac{a_{n-1}}{1-a_{n}},\allowbreak x_{1},\cdots,x_{n-1})
, an open neighborhood
U_{n}
of

x_{n}
, and an open neighborhood
I
of
a_{n}
such that for all

y\in V
,
x^{\prime}_{n}\in U_{n}
and
a^{\prime}_{n}\in I_{n}
,

y+_{1-a^{\prime}_{n}}x^{\prime}_{n}\in W
. By taking a smaller neighborhood if
necessary, and since
a_{n}<1
, we may assume that
I
is included
in
[0,1[
. By induction hypothesis, and by definition of the
topology on
\Delta_{n-1}\times\mathfrak{B}^{n-1}
, there is an open
rectangle
I^{\prime}_{1}\times\cdots\times I^{\prime}_{n-1}
containing

(\frac{a_{1}}{1-a_{n}},\cdots,\frac{a_{n-1}}{1-a_{n}},x_{1},\cdots,x_{n-1})
, and there are open neighborhoods
U_{1}
of
x_{1}
, …,

U_{n-1}
of
x_{n-1}
such that the image of

((I^{\prime}_{1}\times\cdots\times I^{\prime}_{n-1})\cap\Delta_{n-1})\times(U_{1}\times\cdots\times U_{n-1})
under
\beta_{n-1}
is included
in
V
. Let
f\colon(s,t)\mapsto\frac{s}{1-t}
: since
f
is
continuous from
\mathbb{R}\times{[0,1[}
to
\mathbb{R}
, there are open
rectangles
I_{1}\times J_{1}
containing
(a_{1},a_{n})
, …,

I_{n-1}\times J_{n-1}
containing
(a_{n-1},a_{n})
such that
f

maps every pair in
I_{1}\times J_{1}
to
I^{\prime}_{1}
, …, every pair in

I_{n-1}\times J_{n-1}
to
I^{\prime}_{n-1}
. Letting

I_{n}\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}J_{1}\cap\cdots\cap J_{n-1}\cap I
,

((I_{1}\times\cdots\times I_{n})\cap\Delta_{n})\times(U_{1}\times\cdots\times U_{n})
is an open neighborhood of

(a_{1},\cdots,a_{n},x_{1},\cdots,x_{n})
that is mapped by
\beta_{n}

to
W
.
\Box

Before we close this section, we mention the following, out of
curiosity; this will be useful later. The topology of
\Delta_{n}
is
defined as the subspace topology from
\mathbb{R}^{n}
, where
\mathbb{R}
has its
usual metric topology. The topology of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
is very different
from the Scott topology of the pointwise ordering. Despite this, the
following holds.

Lemma 3.29

For every
n\geq 1
, the (Scott) topology on
(\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+})^{n}
and the
product topology on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
(where
\mathbb{R}
has its metric
topology) induce the same topology on the subspace
\Delta_{n}
. With
this topology,
\Delta_{n}
is compact Hausdorff.

Proof.

Let
\tau_{1}
be the Scott topology on
\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}^{n}
. Since
\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
is
a continuous dcpo,
\tau_{1}
is also the product of the Scott
topologies on each copy of
\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
. The Scott topology on
\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}

is coarser than the standard topology, so
\tau_{1}
is coarser than
the product topology on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
; we will call
\tau_{2}
the latter.
In order to show the first part of the lemma, it is then enough to
show that that for every basic open subset

U\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\prod_{i=1}^{n}{]a_{i},b_{i}[}
of
\tau_{2}
,
U\cap\Delta_{n}

is open in the subspace topology induced by
\tau_{1}
. The elements

\vec{\alpha}
of
U\cap\Delta_{n}
are exactly those such that

\alpha_{i}>a_{i}
and

\sum_{1\leq j\leq n,j\neq i}\alpha_{j}>1-b_{i}
(because

\sum_{1\leq j\leq n,j\neq i}\alpha_{j}=1-\alpha_{i}
). Hence

U\cap\Delta_{n}=V\cap\Delta_{n}
, where

V\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\{\vec{\alpha}\in\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}^{n}\mid\forall i\in\{1,\cdots,n\},\alpha_{i}>a_{i}\text{ and }\sum_{1\leq j\leq n,j\neq i}\alpha_{j}>1-b_{i}\}
, which is easily seen to be Scott-open.

Since
\mathbb{R}^{n}
is Hausdorff, its subspace
\Delta_{n}
is Hausdorff.
It is closed in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
, because it is equal to the inverse image
of the closed set
\{1\}
under the continuous map

\vec{\alpha}\mapsto\sum_{i=1}^{n}\alpha_{i}
. It is included in

[0,1]^{n}
, which is compact by Tychonoff’s theorem, hence

\Delta_{n}
is a compact subset of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
, and therefore also a
compact subspace of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
.
\Box

4
Pointed barycentric algebras

4.1
Pointed barycentric algebras and preordered pointed
barycentric algebras

Among the preordered barycentric algebras, some have a least element

\bot
. This is the case of
{\mathbf{V}}_{\leq 1}X
and of
{\mathbf{V}}_{b}X
,
where
\bot
is the zero valuation, but not of
{\mathbf{V}}_{1}X
, for
example.

Definition 4.1

A
pointed barycentric algebra
is a barycentric algebra
\mathfrak{B}

with a distinguished element.

A
preordered pointed barycentric algebra
is a pointed
barycentric algebra and a preordered barycentric algebra in which
the distinguished element is least. An
ordered pointed
barycentric algebra
is a preordered pointed barycentric algebra
whose preordering is antisymmetric.

A
pointed semitopological barycentric algebra
is a
semitopological barycentric algebra and a semitopological
barycentric algebra such that the distinguished element is least in
the specialization preordering.

In all cases, we will write
\bot
for the distinguished element,
and
a\cdot x
for
x+_{a}\bot
, for all
a\in[0,1]
and

x\in\mathfrak{B}
.

Remark 4.2

Beware that there may be more than one least element in a preordered
barycentric algebra. In Definition
4.1
, we
only require the distinguished element
\bot
to
one
of the
least elements. There is no such ambiguity if
\mathfrak{B}
is ordered, or
if
\mathfrak{B}
is semitopological and
T_{0}
.

The operation
a\cdot x
obeys almost the same properties as in
cones, except that
a
has to be smaller than or equal to
1
.

Lemma 4.3

In a pointed barycentric algebra
\mathfrak{B}
with distinguished element

\bot
, the following hold:

\begin{array}[]{ccc}0\cdot x=\bot&(ab)\cdot x=a\cdot(b\cdot x)\\
1\cdot x=x&a\cdot\bot=\bot\\
\lx@intercol\hfil a\cdot(x+_{b}y)=a\cdot x+_{b}a\cdot y\hfil\lx@intercol\\
\lx@intercol\hfil a\cdot x+_{r}b\cdot x=(ra+(1-r)b)\cdot x\hfil\lx@intercol\end{array}

for all
a,b,r\in[0,1]
and
x,y\in\mathfrak{B}
.

If
\mathfrak{B}
is preordered or semitopological, additionally,

(a,x)\mapsto a\cdot x
is monotonic in both
a
and
x
. If

\mathfrak{B}
is a pointed semitopological barycentric algebra, it is
Scott-continuous in
a
and continuous in
x
, hence jointly
continuous from
[0,1]_{\sigma}\times\mathfrak{B}
to
\mathfrak{B}
.

Proof.

First,
0\cdot x=x+_{0}\bot=\bot
,

1\cdot x=x+_{1}\bot=x
, and

a\cdot\bot=\bot+_{a}\bot=\bot
. Second,

(ab)\cdot x=x+_{ab}\bot
, and we compute

a\cdot(b\cdot x)
: if
a=1
, this is equal to

b\cdot x=x+_{b}\bot=x+_{ab}\bot
; if
b=1
, this is equal to

a\cdot x=x+_{a}\bot=x+_{ab}\bot
; otherwise, it is equal to

(b\cdot x)+_{a}\bot=(x+_{b}\bot)+_{a}\bot=x+_{ab}(\bot+_{\frac{(1-b)a}{1-ab}}\bot)=x+_{ab}\bot
. Third, using the
entropic law (
2
),

a\cdot x+_{b}a\cdot y=(x+_{a}\bot)+_{b}(y+_{a}\bot)=(x+_{b}y)+_{a}(\bot+_{b}\bot)=(x+_{b}y)+_{a}\bot=a\cdot(x+_{b}y)
. In
order to show the final law

a\cdot x+_{r}b\cdot x=(ra+(1-r)b)\cdot x
, we use the affine
embedding
\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{B}}
of
\mathfrak{B}
in the free cone
\mathrm{cone}(\mathfrak{B})
.
Beware that
\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{B}}(a\cdot x)
is not equal to

a\cdot\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{B}}(x)
, rather to

\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{B}}(x+_{a}\bot)=(1,x+_{a}\bot)=(a,x)+(1-a,\bot)
.
Then, using this and the fact that
\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{B}}
is affine,

\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{B}}(a\cdot x+_{r}b\cdot x)=r\cdot((a,x)+(1-a,\bot))+(1-r)\cdot((b,x)+(1-b,\bot))=(ra,x)+(r(1-a),\bot)+((1-r)b,x)+((1-r)(1-b),\bot)=(ra+(1-r)b,x)+(r(1-a)+(1-r)(1-b),\bot)
, while

\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{B}}((ra+(1-r)b)\cdot x)=(ra+(1-r)b,x)+(1-ra-(1-r)b,\bot)
, which is the same value. We conclude since
\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{B}}
is
injective.

Let now
\mathfrak{B}
be preordered or semitopological.
The map
(a,x)\mapsto a\cdot x=x+_{a}\bot
is monotonic in
x

since
+_{a}
is monotonic. In order to show that it is monotonic in

a
, let
a,b\in[0,1]
with
a<b
.
Let
c\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\frac{1-b}{1-a}
, so that
1-c(1-a)=b
; also,

c<1
since
a<b
. Then

a\cdot x=x+_{a}\bot=\bot+_{1-a}x\leq(\bot+_{c}x)+_{1-a}x
(since
+_{1-a}
is monotonic and
\bot
is least)

=\bot+_{c(1-a)}(x+_{\frac{(1-c)(1-a)}{1-c(1-a)}}x)=\bot+_{c(1-a)}x=x+_{1-c(1-a)}\bot=x+_{b}\bot=b\cdot x
.

Let now
\mathfrak{B}
be a pointed semitopological barycentric algebra. Then

a\cdot x=x+_{a}\bot
is continuous in
x
since
+_{a}
is
separately continuous. For every
x\in\mathfrak{B}
, the inverse image

(\_\cdot x)^{-1}(U)
of an open subset
U
of
\mathfrak{B}
is open in

[0,1]
with its usual metric topology because

a\mapsto x+_{a}\bot
is continuous, and is upwards-closed: if

a\in(\_\cdot x)^{-1}(U)
and
a\leq b
in
[0,1]
, then

a\cdot x\in U
,
a\cdot x\leq b\cdot x
as we have proved
above, and since
U
is upwards-closed,
b\cdot x
is also in
U
.
The upwards-closed open subsets of
[0,1]
are the Scott-open
subsets of
[0,1]
, so
(\_\cdot x)^{-1}(U)
is open in

[0,1]_{\sigma}
, hence
\_\cdot x
is continuous, for every

x\in\mathfrak{B}
. This shows that
\cdot
is separately continuous from

[0,1]_{\sigma}\times\mathfrak{B}\to\mathfrak{B}
, hence jointly continuous by
Ershov’s observation, since
[0,1]
is a continuous poset in its
usual ordering.
\Box

Preordered pointed barycentric algebras form a category
\mathbf{BA}_{\bot}
,
whose morphisms are the
strict
affine monotonic maps, namely
the affine monotonic functions mapping
\bot
to
\bot
. We will not
be so much interested in pointed barycentric algebras that are not at
least preordered. Similarly, we write
\mathbf{sTBA}_{\bot}
for the category
of pointed semitopological barycentric algebras and strict affine
continuous maps, and
\mathbf{TBA}_{\bot}
for its full subcategory of pointed
topological barycentric algebras.

We will use the following additional classes of maps. We reuse the
names of the corresponding classes of maps on cones.

Definition 4.4

A function
f\colon\mathfrak{B}\to\mathfrak{A}
between preordered pointed
barycentric algebras is:

•

\mathbb{I}
-homogeneous
if and only if

f(a\cdot x)=a\cdot f(x)
for all
a\in[0,1]
and

x\in\mathfrak{B}
(
\mathbb{I}
is a common name for
[0,1]
);

•

superlinear
if and only if it is
\mathbb{I}
-homogeneous and concave;

•

sublinear
if and only if it is
\mathbb{I}
-homogeneous and convex;

•

linear
if and only if it is
\mathbb{I}
-homogeneous and
affine.

There is no risk of confusion with the similar notions on cones: on
cones, the similarly named notions mean the same thing, as the
following remark states.

Remark 4.5

Let
f\colon\mathfrak{C}\to\mathfrak{D}
be a function between preordered cones.
Then:

•

f
is
\mathbb{I}
-homogeneous if and only if it is positively
homogeneous. Indeed, if
f(a\cdot x)=a\cdot f(x)
for all

a\in[0,1]
and
x\in\mathfrak{C}
, then

f(a\cdot x)=a\cdot f(x)
also for all
a\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
: when

a>1
, this is due to the fact that
1/a<1
, so

f((1/a)\cdot(a\cdot x))
, which is equal to
f(x)
, is also
equal to
(1/a)\cdot f(a\cdot x)
by
Definition
4.4
, whence

f(a\cdot x)=a\cdot f(x)
by multiplying both sides by
a
.

•

Hence
f\colon\mathfrak{C}\to\mathfrak{D}
is superlinear (resp. sublinear,
linear) as a function between preordered cones if and only if it
is superlinear (resp. sublinear, linear) as a function between
preordered pointed barycentric algebras
(Definition
4.4
). Indeed, the only
if direction is easy, while in the if direction, we assume
f

\mathbb{I}
-homogenous (hence positively homogeneous, by the previous
point) and concave (resp. convex, affine). For all

x,y\in\mathfrak{C}
,

f(x+y)=f(2\cdot(x+_{1/2}y))=2\cdot f(x+_{1/2}y)\geq 2\cdot(f(x)+_{1/2}f(y))
(resp.
\leq
,
=
)

=f(x)+f(y)
.

The morphisms of the category of pointed barycentric algebras are the
strict affine maps or equivalent the linear maps, thanks to the
following proposition.

Proposition 4.6

A function
f\colon\mathfrak{B}\to\mathfrak{A}
between pointed barycentric
algebras is linear if and only if it is strict and affine.

Proof.

In one direction, every linear map is affine, and being

\mathbb{I}
-homogeneous, is strict: we have

f(0\cdot x)=0\cdot f(x)
for every point
x
, in particular
for
x\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\bot
; then
0\cdot\bot=\bot+_{0}\bot=\bot
, and

0\cdot f(x)=f(x)+_{0}\bot=\bot
.

In the other direction, it suffices to show that every strict affine
map
f
is
\mathbb{I}
-homogeneous. Indeed,

f(a\cdot x)=f(x+_{a}\bot)=f(x)+_{a}f(\bot)
(since
f
is
affine)
=f(x)+_{a}\bot
(since
f
is strict)
=a\cdot f(x)
.
\Box

The following shows how close cones and pointed barycentric algebras
are: the only thing missing in the latter to become cones is a
doubling map. (Multiplication by any number strictly larger than
1

would work, too.)

Proposition 4.7

Let
\mathfrak{B}
be a pointed barycentric algebra with a linear map

\mathrm{dbl}\colon\mathfrak{B}\to\mathfrak{B}
(the
doubling map
) such that

\mathrm{dbl}(\frac{1}{2}\cdot x)=x
for every
x\in\mathfrak{B}
. Then
\mathfrak{B}
is a
cone with the following operations:

•

addition
x+y
is defined as
\mathrm{dbl}(x+_{1/2}y)
for all
x,y\in\mathfrak{B}
;

•

zero is defined as the distinguished element
\bot
of
\mathfrak{B}
;

•

scalar multiplication is defined by
a\cdot x\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\mathrm{dbl}^{k}(\frac{a}{2^{k}}\cdot x)
for all
a\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
and
x\in\mathfrak{B}
, where

k
is any natural number large enough that
\frac{a}{2^{k}}\leq 1
, and
\mathrm{dbl}^{k}
is
\mathrm{dbl}
composed with itself
k
times.

If
\mathfrak{B}
is a preordered barycentric algebra and
\mathrm{dbl}
is monotonic,
this construction turns
\mathfrak{B}
into a preordered cone.

If
\mathfrak{B}
is a semitopological, resp. topological barycentric algebra
and
\mathrm{dbl}
is continuous, this construction turns
\mathfrak{B}
into a
semitopological, resp. topological cone.

Proof.

We first verify that scalar multiplication is well-defined. In
order to clarify, the term
\frac{a}{2^{k}}\cdot x
is equal to

x+_{\frac{a}{2^{k}}}\bot
, which makes sense in any pointed
barycentric algebra with distinguished element
\bot
. For all

k,k^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}
such that
\frac{a}{2^{k}},\frac{a}{2^{k^{\prime}}}\leq 1
,
we observe that

\mathrm{dbl}^{k}(\frac{a}{2^{k}}\cdot x)=\mathrm{dbl}^{k^{\prime}}(\frac{a}{2^{k^{\prime}}}\cdot x)
, so the definition of
a\cdot x
is indeed independent of the
chosen
k
. Indeed, and assuming
k\leq k^{\prime}
without loss of
generality,

\mathrm{dbl}^{k}(\frac{a}{2^{k}}\cdot x)=\mathrm{dbl}^{k}(\mathrm{dbl}(\frac{1}{2}\cdot\frac{a}{2^{k}}\cdot x))
(since
\mathrm{dbl}(\frac{1}{2}\cdot\_)
is the
identity map)
=\mathrm{dbl}^{k+1}(\frac{a}{2^{k+1}}\cdot x)
(by the
second equation of Lemma
4.3
); iterating
this shows the claim.

We have one notation
a\cdot x
for potentially two different
things when
a\in[0,1]
: either the scalar multiplication that
already exists in
\mathfrak{B}
, namely
x+_{a}\bot
, or the new scalar
multiplication defined in Proposition
4.7
. They
turn out to be the same: since
a\leq 1
, our newly-defined notion

a\cdot x
is equal to
\mathrm{dbl}^{0}(\frac{a}{2^{0}}\cdot x)
by picking

k\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}0
, which is the old notion of
a\cdot x
.

Let us check the cone equations. We start with the properties of

+
. Addition is commutative, since
x+_{1/2}y=y+_{1/2}x
for
all
x,y\in\mathfrak{B}
. For every
x\in\mathfrak{B}
,

x+0=\mathrm{dbl}(x+_{1/2}\bot)
(since
0
is defined as
\bot
)

=\mathrm{dbl}(\frac{1}{2}\cdot x)=x
. In order to show associativity,
let
x,y,z,\in\mathfrak{B}
. Then

(x+y)+z=\mathrm{dbl}(\mathrm{dbl}(x+_{1/2}y)+_{1/2}z)=\mathrm{dbl}(\mathrm{dbl}(x+_{1/2}y)+_{1/2}\mathrm{dbl}(z+_{1/2}\bot))
(since

z=\mathrm{dbl}(\frac{1}{2}\cdot z)
and

\frac{1}{2}\cdot z=z+_{1/2}\bot
)

=\mathrm{dbl}^{2}((x+_{1/2}y)+_{1/2}(z+_{1/2}\bot))
, since
\mathrm{dbl}
is
linear hence affine. Similarly,

x+(y+z)=\mathrm{dbl}^{2}((x+_{1/2}\bot)+_{1/2}(y+_{1/2}z))
. It
then suffices to show that

(x+_{1/2}y)+_{1/2}(z+_{1/2}\bot)=(x+_{1/2}\bot)+_{1/2}(y+_{1/2}z)
, and this follows from the fact that they are both
mapped to

\frac{1}{4}\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{B}}(x)+\frac{1}{4}\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{B}}(y)+\frac{1}{4}\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{B}}(z)+\frac{1}{4}\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{B}}(\bot)
by the injective
affine map
\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{B}}
, or by the entropic law (
2
).

We now check the six explicitly listed equations (
1
),
see Section
2
. First, for every
x\in\mathfrak{B}
,

0\cdot x=\bot=0
by the first equation of
Lemma
4.3
. Second, for all
a,b\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
,
let
k
be so large that
\frac{a}{2^{k}},\frac{b}{2^{k}}\leq 1
,
hence also
\frac{ab}{2^{2k}}\leq 1
. Then

a\cdot(b\cdot x)=\mathrm{dbl}^{k}(\frac{a}{2^{k}}\cdot\mathrm{dbl}^{k}(\frac{b}{2^{k}}\cdot x))=\mathrm{dbl}^{2k}(\frac{a}{2^{k}}\cdot\frac{b}{2^{k}}\cdot x)
(since
\mathrm{dbl}
is linear hence
\mathbb{I}
-homogeneous)

=\mathrm{dbl}^{2k}(\frac{ab}{2^{2k}}\cdot x)
(by the second equation
of Lemma
4.3
)
=(ab)\cdot x
. Third, we
have
1\cdot x=x
by the third equation of
Lemma
4.3
. Fourth,

a\cdot 0=\mathrm{dbl}^{k}(\frac{a}{2^{k}}\cdot\bot)
for some
k
large
enough, and that is equal to
\mathrm{dbl}^{k}(\bot)
by the
fourth equation of
Lemma
4.3
, which is equal to
\bot=0
,
since
\mathrm{dbl}
is linear hence strict. Fifth, for all
x,y\in\mathfrak{B}
,

a\cdot(x+y)=\mathrm{dbl}^{k}(\frac{a}{2^{k}}\cdot(\mathrm{dbl}(x+_{1/2}y)))

where
k
is large enough that
\frac{a}{2^{k}}\leq 1
. Since
\mathrm{dbl}

is linear hence
\mathbb{I}
-homogeneous, that is equal to

\mathrm{dbl}^{k+1}(\frac{a}{2^{k}}\cdot(x+_{1/2}y))
, which in turn is
equal to

\mathrm{dbl}^{k+1}((\frac{a}{2^{k}}\cdot x)+_{1/2}(\frac{a}{2^{k}}\cdot y))
(by the fifth equation of
Lemma
4.3
), which is equal to

\mathrm{dbl}((\mathrm{dbl}^{k}(\frac{a}{2^{k}}\cdot x)+_{1/2}(\mathrm{dbl}^{k}(\frac{a}{2^{k}}\cdot y)))
since
\mathrm{dbl}
, hence also
\mathrm{dbl}^{k}
, is affine; and
what we wrote is just
(a\cdot x)+(a\cdot y)
, so

a\cdot(x+y)=(a\cdot x)+(a\cdot y)
. Sixth, for all

a,b\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
, let
k\in\mathbb{N}
be such that

\frac{a}{2^{k}},\frac{b}{2^{k}}\leq 1
; in particular,

\frac{a+b}{2^{k+1}}\leq 1
. Then

(a+b)\cdot x=\mathrm{dbl}^{k}(\frac{a+b}{2^{k}}\cdot x)

a\cdot x+b\cdot x=\mathrm{dbl}(\mathrm{dbl}^{k}(\frac{a}{2^{k}}\cdot x)+_{1/2}\mathrm{dbl}^{k}(\frac{b}{2^{k}}\cdot x))=\mathrm{dbl}^{k+1}(\frac{a}{2^{k}}\cdot x+_{1/2}\frac{b}{2^{k}}\cdot x)
(since
\mathrm{dbl}
, hence

\mathrm{dbl}^{k}
, is affine)
=\mathrm{dbl}^{k+1}(\frac{a+b}{2^{k}}\cdot x)
(by
the last equation of Lemma
4.3
)

=(a+b)\cdot x
.

If
\mathfrak{B}
is a preordered barycentric algebra and
\mathrm{dbl}
is monotonic,
then addition is monotonic as a composition of monotonic maps, zero
is least by definition, and
a\cdot\_
is monotonic for every

a\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
, as the composition of the monotonic maps
\mathrm{dbl}^{k}
and

\frac{a}{2^{k}}\cdot\_
(the latter being monotonic by
Lemma
4.3
). This makes
\mathfrak{B}
a pointed
semipreordered cone, hence a preordered cone. Explicitly,

\_\cdot x
is also monotonic: if
a\leq b
, then

b\cdot x=a\cdot x+(b-a)\cdot x\geq a\cdot x+0=a\cdot x
, where the middle inequality is because
0
is least.

We now assume that
\mathfrak{B}
is a semitopological (resp. topological)
barycentric algebra and that
\mathrm{dbl}
is continuous. It is clear that
addition is separately (resp. jointly) continuous, and that

a\cdot\_
is continuous for every
a\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
. It remains to
show that
a\mapsto a\cdot x
is continuous from
\mathbb{R}_{+}
to
\mathfrak{B}

for every
x\in\mathfrak{B}
. Let
a_{0}\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
, and let
V
be an open
neighborhood of
a_{0}\cdot x
. If
V
is the whole of
\mathfrak{B}
, then
its inverse image under
\_\cdot x
is the whole of
\mathbb{R}_{+}
, which is
Scott-open, so we will assume that
V
is proper, or equivalently
that
\bot=0
is not in
V
. We fix
k\in\mathbb{N}
so that

\frac{a_{0}}{2^{k}}\leq 1
. The map

a\mapsto\mathrm{dbl}^{k}\circ(\frac{a}{2^{k}}\cdot x)
is continuous from

[0,2^{k}]_{\sigma}
to
\mathfrak{B}
, in particular because

b\mapsto b\cdot x
is continuous by
Lemma
4.3
. Hence there is a Scott-open
subset
U
of
[0,2^{k}]
that contains
a_{0}
and whose image under

a\mapsto\mathrm{dbl}^{k}\circ(\frac{a}{2^{k}}\cdot x)
is included in
V
.
Since
\bot\not\in V
,
0
is not in
U
. Therefore
U
is an
interval of the form
]r,2^{k}]
, with
0\leq r<a_{0}
. It remains
to see that
]r,\infty[
is a Scott-open neighborhood of
a_{0}
such
that
a\cdot x\in V
for every
a\in{]r,\infty[}
. This is
true for
a\in{]r,2^{k}]}
by construction. Seeing
\mathfrak{B}
as a
preordered barycentric algebra, with its specialization preordering,
we have see that
\_\cdot x
is monotonic, so this is also true for
larger values of
a
, since
V
is upwards-closed.
\Box

4.2
Telescopes

There are several ways of building the free preordered cone over a
preordered pointed barycentric algebra
\mathfrak{B}
. The cone
\mathrm{cone}(\mathfrak{B})

itself cannot be the free preordered cone over
\mathfrak{B}
as a

pointed
barycentric algebra, because
\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{B}}
is not a
morphism in the category of pointed barycentric algebras: it is not
strict, since it maps the the distinguished element
\bot
of
\mathfrak{B}
to

(1,\bot)
, which is not the distinguished element
0
of

\mathrm{cone}(\mathfrak{B})
. One way of building the desired free preordered cone
is by taking a quotient of
\mathrm{cone}(\mathfrak{B})
, under the smallest
equivalence relation
\equiv_{\bot}
such that
(r,\bot)\equiv_{\bot}0

and
(r,a\cdot x)\equiv_{\bot}(ra,x)
for all

r\in\mathbb{R}_{+}\smallsetminus\{0\}
,
a\in{]0,1[}
and
x\in\mathfrak{B}
. We will
leave this as an exercise to the reader. A more interesting
construction, although it appears to depend on a spurious parameter

\alpha\in{]0,1[}
, is as follows. We build the colimit, taken in
the category of sets, or of preordered sets, or of topological spaces,
of the diagram:

\textstyle{\mathfrak{B}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\alpha\cdot\_}
\textstyle{\mathfrak{B}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\alpha\cdot\_}
\textstyle{\cdots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\alpha\cdot\_}
\textstyle{\mathfrak{B}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\alpha\cdot\_}
\textstyle{\cdots}

where
\alpha
is a fixed real number such that
0<\alpha<1
.
We first build the coproduct
\coprod_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\mathfrak{B}
, whose elements
are pairs
(n,x)
with
x\in\mathfrak{B}
, and then we take a quotient by an
appropriate equivalence relation. Here is an explicit definition.

Definition 4.8
(Telescope)

For every pointed barycentric algebra
\mathfrak{B}
and every given number

\alpha\in{]0,1[}
, let
\equiv_{\alpha}
be the smallest equivalence
relation on
\coprod_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\mathfrak{B}
such that
(n,x)\equiv_{\alpha}(n+1,\alpha\cdot x)
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
x\in\mathfrak{B}
.

The
\alpha
-telescope
on
\mathfrak{B}
is the quotient

\mathrm{tscope}_{\alpha}(\mathfrak{B})\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}{(\coprod_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\mathfrak{B})}/{\equiv_{\alpha}}
. We write
[(n,x)]_{\alpha}
for the equivalence
class of
(n,x)
.

Every element of
\mathrm{tscope}_{\alpha}(\mathfrak{B})
can be written

[(n,x)]_{\alpha}
, for each sufficiently large natural number
n
.
Explicitly, one can write it as
[(n_{0},x_{0})]_{\alpha}
for some

n_{0}\in\mathbb{N}
and
x_{0}\in\mathfrak{B}
, and therefore also as

[(n,\alpha^{n-n_{0}}\cdot x_{0})]_{\alpha}
for every
n\geq n_{0}
. It
follows that, given two points of
\mathrm{tscope}_{\alpha}(\mathfrak{B})
, we can write
them as
[(n,x)]_{\alpha}
and
[(n,x)]_{\alpha}
with the same value of

n\in\mathbb{N}
. We will use these facts often.

Lemma 4.9

Let
\mathfrak{B}
be a pointed barycentric algebra. For every

\alpha\in{]0,1[}
, for all
m,n\in\mathbb{N}
and
x,y\in\mathfrak{B}
,

(m,x)\equiv_{\alpha}(n,y)
if and only if

\alpha^{k-m}\cdot x=\alpha^{k-n}\cdot y
for some

k\geq m,n
.

Proof.

Let us define the relation
\cong_{\alpha}
on

\coprod_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\mathfrak{B}
by by
(m,x)\cong_{\alpha}(n,y)
if and
only if
\alpha^{k-m}\cdot x=\alpha^{k-n}\cdot y
for some

k\geq m,n
.

If
(m,x)\cong_{\alpha}(n,y)
, then, taking
k
as above,

(m,x)\equiv_{\alpha}(m+1,\alpha\cdot x)\equiv_{\alpha}\cdots\equiv_{\alpha}(k,\alpha^{k-m}\cdot x)=(k,\alpha^{k-n}\cdot y)\equiv_{\alpha}(k-1,\alpha^{k-n-1}\cdot y)\equiv_{\alpha}\cdots\equiv_{\alpha}(n,y)
. Hence
\cong_{\alpha}
is smaller than or
equal to (included in)
\equiv_{\alpha}
.

It is clear that
\cong_{\alpha}
is reflexive and symmetric. Let now

(m,x)\cong_{\alpha}(n,y)
and
(n,y)\cong_{\alpha}(p,z)
. Then

\alpha^{k-m}\cdot x=\alpha^{k-n}\cdot y
for some

k\geq m,n
, and
\alpha^{k^{\prime}-n}\cdot y=\alpha^{k^{\prime}-p}\cdot z

for some
k^{\prime}\geq n,p
. Let us pick
k^{\prime\prime}\geq k,k^{\prime}
. Then

k^{\prime\prime}\geq m,p
and

\alpha^{k^{\prime\prime}-m}\cdot x=\alpha^{k^{\prime\prime}-k}\cdot\alpha^{k-m}\cdot y=\alpha^{k^{\prime\prime}-k}\cdot\alpha^{k-n}\cdot y=\alpha^{k^{\prime\prime}-n}\cdot y=\alpha^{k^{\prime\prime}-k^{\prime}}\cdot\alpha^{k^{\prime}-n}\cdot y=\alpha^{k^{\prime\prime}-k^{\prime}}\cdot\alpha^{k^{\prime}-p}\cdot z=\alpha^{k^{\prime\prime}-p}\cdot z
, so

(m,x)\cong_{\alpha}(p,z)
. Therefore
\cong_{\alpha}
is
transitive, and hence, an equivalence relation.

For all
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
x\in\mathfrak{B}
,

(n,x)\cong_{\alpha}(n+1,\alpha\cdot x)
. Indeed, let

k\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}n+1
. Then

\alpha^{k-n}\cdot x=\alpha^{k-(n+1)}\cdot\alpha\cdot x
,
since both sides are equal to
\alpha\cdot x
, using the second
equation of Lemma
4.3
to simplify the
right-hand side. Therefore
\cong_{\alpha}
is an equivalence
relation such that
(n,x)\cong_{\alpha}(n+1,\alpha\cdot n)
for
all
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
x\in\mathfrak{B}
. Since
\equiv_{\alpha}
is the
smallest such relation, it is smaller than or equal to

\cong_{\alpha}
. Therefore
\equiv_{\alpha}
and
\cong_{\alpha}

coincide.
\Box

Remark 4.10

It is not in general the case that
(n,x)\equiv_{\alpha}(n,y)

(with the same
n
, chosen in advance) if and only if
x=y
. A
counterexample will be given in
Example
4.11
below. The case of
pointed
T_{0}
semitopological barycentric algebras is nicer, as we
will see in Remark
4.17
.

Example 4.11

Keimel and Plotkin give an example of an ordered pointed barycentric
algebra with two distinct points
x
and
y
such that

r\cdot x=r\cdot y
for every
r\in{]0,1[}

[
29
, Example 2.19]
. We reformulate it as follows. Let

\mathfrak{B}^{KP}
be the subset of
(\mathbb{R}\cup\{-\infty\})\times\mathbb{R}_{+}

consisting of the points
(-\infty,s)
with
s\in[0,1]
, and the
point
(0,1)
. We equip
\mathfrak{B}^{KP}
with the pointwise ordering and
the pointwise operations
+_{a}
, so that

(-\infty,s)+_{a}(-\infty,t)=(-\infty,as+(1-a)t)
,

(-\infty,s)+_{a}(0,1)=(-\infty,as+1-a)
if
a>0
,
(0,1)

if
a=0
, in particular. This is a preordered pointed barycentric
algebra, with distinguished (least) element

\bot\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}(-\infty,0)
. Letting
x\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}(0,1)
and

y\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}(-\infty,1)
, we have
r\cdot x=r\cdot y
for every

r\in{]0,1[}
, but
x\not\leq y
: we recall that

r\cdot x=x+_{r}\bot
, and similarly for
r\cdot y
, from which
we obtain that both are equal to
(-\infty,r)
. In particular,

[(0,x)]_{\alpha}=[(0,y)]_{\alpha}
, but
x\neq y
.

Proposition 4.12

For every pointed barycentric algebra
\mathfrak{B}
, for every

\alpha\in{]0,1[}
,

1.

\mathrm{tscope}_{\alpha}(\mathfrak{B})
has the structure of a pointed
barycentric algebra, with distinguished element

[(0,\bot)]_{\alpha}
, and with
+_{a}
defined by:

\displaystyle[(m,x)]_{\alpha}+_{a}[(n,y)]_{\alpha}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}[(\max(m,n),

\displaystyle\qquad(\alpha^{\max(m,n)-m}\cdot x)+_{a}(\alpha^{\max(m,n)-n}\cdot y))]_{\alpha}

for every
a\in[0,1]
and for all elements
[(m,x)]_{\alpha}

and
[(n,y)]_{\alpha}
of
\mathrm{tscope}_{\alpha}(\mathfrak{B})
—in particular,

[(n,x)]_{\alpha}+_{a}[(n,y)]_{\alpha}\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}[(n,x+_{a}y)]_{\alpha}
.

2.

a\cdot[(n,x)]_{\alpha}\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}[(n,a\cdot x)]_{\alpha}
for
all
a\in[0,1]
,
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
x\in\mathfrak{B}
.

3.

The latter formula extends to all
a\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
by letting

a\cdot([(n,x)]_{\alpha})\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}[(n+k,(\alpha^{k}a)\cdot x)]_{\alpha}
for all
a\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
,
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
x\in\mathfrak{B}
,
where
k
is so large that
\alpha^{k}a\leq 1
. The map

a\cdot\_
is linear.

4.

In particular,
2\cdot\_
is a doubling map, and therefore

\mathrm{tscope}_{\alpha}(\mathfrak{B})
has a cone structure, given by the
construction of Proposition
4.7
.

If
\mathfrak{B}
is a preordered pointed barycentric algebra, then:

5.

\mathrm{tscope}_{\alpha}(\mathfrak{B})
is itself a preordered pointed
barycentric algebra, with the preordering
\leq_{\alpha}
defined as
follows: for all
u,v\in\mathrm{tscope}_{\alpha}(\mathfrak{B})
,
u\leq_{\alpha}v
if
and only if one can write
u
as
[(n,x)]_{\alpha}
,
v
as

[(n,y)]_{\alpha}
with the same
n\in\mathbb{N}
and with
x\leq y

in
\mathfrak{B}
; in particular, the distinguished element
[(0,\bot)]_{\alpha}
is least;

6.

for every
a\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
, the map

a\cdot\_\colon\mathrm{tscope}_{\alpha}(\mathfrak{B})\to\mathrm{tscope}_{\alpha}(\mathfrak{B})
of
item 3 is monotonic;

7.

in particular,
2\cdot\_
is a monotonic doubling map, and
therefore
\mathrm{tscope}_{\alpha}(\mathfrak{B})
has a preordered cone structure,
given by the construction of Proposition
4.7
.

Proof.

1. We verify that the operation
+_{a}
on
\mathrm{tscope}_{\alpha}(\mathfrak{B})
is
well-defined. In order to do so, letting

f((m,x),(n,y))\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}(\max(m,n),(\alpha^{\max(m,n)-m}\cdot x)+_{a}(\alpha^{\max(m,n)-n}\cdot y))
, or equivalently

f((m,x),(n,y))\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}(\max(m,n),(\alpha^{\max(n-m,0)}\cdot x)+_{a}(\alpha^{\max(m-n,0)}\cdot y))
, we show that if

(m,x)\equiv_{\alpha}(m^{\prime},x^{\prime})
then

f((m,x),(n,y))\equiv_{\alpha}f((m^{\prime},x^{\prime}),(n,y))
. It is
enough to prove this when
m^{\prime}\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}m+1
and

x^{\prime}\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\alpha\cdot x
. Then:

\displaystyle f((m+1,\alpha\cdot x),(n,y))

\displaystyle=(\max(m+1,n),

\displaystyle\qquad(\alpha^{\max(n-m-1,0)}\cdot\alpha\cdot x)+_{a}(\alpha^{\max(m+1-n,0)}\cdot y))

\displaystyle=(\max(m+1,n),

\displaystyle\qquad(\alpha^{\max(n-m,1)}\cdot x)+_{a}(\alpha^{\max(m+1-n,0)}\cdot y)),

using the second equation of Lemma
4.3
.

If
m\geq n
, then
\max(m+1,n)=m+1
,
\max(n-m,1)=1
and

\max(m+1-n,0)=m+1-n
, so

f((m+1,\alpha\cdot x),(n,y))=(m+1,(\alpha\cdot x)+_{a}(\alpha^{m+1-n}\cdot y))=(m+1,(\alpha\cdot x)+_{a}(\alpha\cdot\alpha^{m-n}\cdot y))
by the second equation of
Lemma
4.3
. By the
fifth equation of
Lemma
4.3
, that
is equal to
(m+1,\alpha\cdot(x+_{a}(\alpha^{m-n}\cdot y))
,
which is
\equiv_{\alpha}
-equivalent to

(m,x+_{a}(\alpha^{m-n}\cdot y)
; and

f((m,x),(n,y))=(m,x+_{a}\alpha^{m-n}\cdot y)
, since

m\geq n
.

If
m<n
, then
\max(m+1,n)=n
,
\max(n-m,1)=n-m
and

\max(m+1-n,0)=0
, so

f((m+1,\alpha\cdot x),(n,y))=(n,(\alpha^{n-m}\cdot x)+_{a}y)
, while
\max(m,n)=n
,
\max(n-m,0)=n-m
and

\max(m-n,0)=0
, so

f((m,x),(n,y))=(n,(\alpha^{n-m}\cdot x)+_{a}y)
, which is
the exact same value.

Hence
+_{a}
is well-defined on equivalence classes. Since we can
write any element of
\mathrm{tscope}_{\alpha}(\mathfrak{B})
as
[(n,x)]_{\alpha}
with

n
large enough, in any expression involving finitely many points
of
\mathrm{tscope}_{\alpha}(\mathfrak{B})
, we may assume that all these points are
written as
[(n,x)]_{\alpha}
with the same
n
(and possibly
different
x
). With the formula

[(n,x)]_{\alpha}+_{a}[(n,y)]_{\alpha}\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}[(n,x+_{a}y)]
, it is
then clear that
\mathrm{tscope}_{\alpha}(\mathfrak{B})
is a barycentric algebra.

2. For every
a\in[0,1]
, for every

[(n,x)]_{\alpha}\in\mathrm{tscope}_{\alpha}(\mathfrak{B})
, then,

a\cdot[(n,x)]_{\alpha}=[(n,x)]_{\alpha}+_{a}[(n,\bot)]_{\alpha}=[(n,x+_{a}\bot)]_{\alpha}=[(n,a\cdot x)]_{\alpha}
.

3. We extend the formula of item 2 and we define

a\cdot[(n,x)]_{\alpha}\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}[(n+k,(\alpha^{k}a)\cdot x)]_{\alpha}
for every
a\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
, not just in
[0,1]
, where
k

is so large that
\alpha^{k}a\leq 1
. (If
a\in[0,1]
, we can
take
k\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}0
, which gives back the previous definition.) Such
a
k
always exists because
\alpha\in{]0,1[}
. It does not
matter which
k
we choose, since for all
k,k^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}
such that

k\leq k^{\prime}
and
\alpha^{k}a\leq 1
,

(n+k,(\alpha^{k}a)\cdot x)\equiv_{\alpha}(n+k^{\prime},\alpha^{k^{\prime}-k}\cdot(\alpha^{k}a)\cdot x)=(n+k^{\prime},(\alpha^{k^{\prime}}a)\cdot x)
,
using the second equation of Lemma
4.3
.
The definition of scalar multiplication is also independent of the
representative
(n,x)
in the equivalence class
[(n,x)]_{\alpha}
.
Indeed, letting
f(n,x)\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}(n+k,(\alpha^{k}a)\cdot x)
, it
suffices to show that

f(n+1,\alpha\cdot x)\equiv_{\alpha}f(n,x)
: indeed,

f(n+1,\alpha\cdot x)=(n+k+1,(\alpha^{k}a)\cdot\alpha\cdot x)=(n+k+1,(\alpha^{k+1}a)\cdot x)=(n+k+1,\alpha\cdot(\alpha^{k}a)\cdot x)
(by the second equation of
Lemma
4.3
)

\equiv_{\alpha}(n+k,(\alpha^{k}a)\cdot x)=f(n,x)
. Hence

a\cdot\_
is well-defined on
\mathrm{tscope}_{\alpha}(\mathfrak{B})
for every

a\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
.

The map
a\cdot\_
is strict:

a\cdot[(0,\bot)]_{\alpha}=[(k,(\alpha^{k}a)\cdot\bot)]_{\alpha}

(with
k
so large that
\alpha^{k}a\leq 1
)
=[(k,\bot)]_{\alpha}

(by
the fourth equation of
Lemma
4.3
)
=[(0,\bot)]_{\alpha}
.

We claim that the map
a\cdot\_
is affine. We have

a\cdot([(n,x)]_{\alpha}+_{a}[(n,y)]_{\alpha})=(a\cdot[(n,x)]_{\alpha})+_{a}(a\cdot[(n,y)]_{\alpha})
. Picking
k
such that

\alpha^{k}a\leq 1
as above, the left-hand side is equal to

[(n+k,(\alpha^{k}a)\cdot(x+_{a}y))]_{\alpha}
, while the right-hand
side is equal to

[(n+k,((\alpha^{k}a)\cdot x)+_{a}((\alpha^{k}a)\cdot y))]
. Those
are equal by the fifth equation of
Lemma
4.3
.

Being strict and affine,
a\cdot\_
is linear, thanks to
Proposition
4.6
.

4. In order to show that
2\cdot\_
is a doubling map, it remains
to verify that
2\cdot\frac{1}{2}\cdot u=u
for every

u\in\mathrm{tscope}_{\alpha}(\mathfrak{B})
. We write
u
as
[(n,x)]_{\alpha}
, and
we pick
k
so that
2\alpha^{k}\leq 1
. Then

2\cdot\frac{1}{2}\cdot u=2\cdot[(n,\frac{1}{2}\cdot x)]_{\alpha}=[(n+k,(2\alpha^{k})\cdot\frac{1}{2}\cdot x)]_{\alpha}=[(n+k,\alpha^{k}\cdot x)]_{\alpha}
(by the second equation of
Lemma
4.3
)
=u
, since

(n+k,\alpha^{k}\cdot x)\equiv_{\alpha}(n,x)
. Therefore

\mathrm{tscope}_{\alpha}(\mathfrak{B})
is a cone by Proposition
4.7
.

5. We now assume that
\mathfrak{B}
is a preordered pointed barycentric
algebra. The definition of the preordering
\leq_{\alpha}
on

\mathrm{tscope}_{\alpha}(\mathfrak{B})
is a bit subtle. If
x\leq y
, then

[(n,x)]_{\alpha}\leq[(n,y)]_{\alpha}
for some
n
, but not
necessarily for the least
n
such that we can write both sides of
the inequality with the same
n
.

At any rate, this defines a preordering. Reflexivity is obvious,
while transitivity is proved as follows. If
u\leq_{\alpha}v
and

v\leq_{\alpha}w
, we can write
u
as
[(n,x)]_{\alpha}
,
v
as

[(n,y)]_{\alpha}
with
x\leq y
, and we can also write
v
as

[(n^{\prime},y^{\prime})]_{\alpha}
and
w
as
[(n^{\prime},z)]_{\alpha}
with
y^{\prime}\leq z
.
Let
n^{\prime\prime}\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\max(n,n^{\prime})
. Now

(n,x)\equiv_{\alpha}(n+1,\alpha\cdot x)\equiv_{\alpha}\cdots\equiv_{\alpha}(n^{\prime\prime},\alpha^{n^{\prime\prime}-n}\cdot x)
, similarly

(n,y)\equiv_{\alpha}(n^{\prime\prime},\alpha^{n^{\prime\prime}-n}\cdot y)
,

(n^{\prime},y^{\prime})\equiv_{\alpha}(n^{\prime\prime},\alpha^{n^{\prime\prime}-n^{\prime}}\cdot y^{\prime})
, and

(n^{\prime},z)\equiv_{\alpha}(n^{\prime\prime},\alpha^{n^{\prime\prime}-n^{\prime}}\cdot z)
. We recall
that
v=[(n,y)]_{\alpha}=[(n^{\prime},y^{\prime})]_{\alpha},
so

(n,y)\equiv_{\alpha}(n^{\prime},y^{\prime})
, and therefore

(n^{\prime\prime},\alpha^{n^{\prime\prime}-n}\cdot y)\equiv_{\alpha}(n^{\prime\prime},\alpha^{n^{\prime\prime}-n^{\prime}}\cdot y^{\prime})
. By Lemma
4.9
,

\alpha^{k-n^{\prime\prime}}\cdot\alpha^{n^{\prime\prime}-n}\cdot y=\alpha^{k-n^{\prime\prime}}\cdot\alpha^{n^{\prime\prime}-n^{\prime}}\cdot y^{\prime}
for some
k\geq n^{\prime\prime}
, therefore

\alpha^{k-n}\cdot y=\alpha^{k-n^{\prime}}\cdot y^{\prime}
. Then,

(n,x)\equiv_{\alpha}(n^{\prime\prime},\alpha^{n^{\prime\prime}-n}\cdot x)\equiv_{\alpha}(n^{\prime\prime}+1,\alpha^{n^{\prime\prime}+1-n}\cdot x)\equiv_{\alpha}\cdots\equiv_{\alpha}(k,\alpha^{k-n}\cdot x)
, similarly

(n^{\prime},z)\equiv_{\alpha}(k,\alpha{k-n^{\prime}}\cdot z)
, so we can write

u
as
[(k,\alpha^{k-n}\cdot x)]_{\alpha}
,
w
as

[(k,\alpha^{k-n^{\prime}}\cdot z)]_{\alpha}
, and

\alpha^{k-n}\cdot x\leq\alpha^{k-n}\cdot y=\alpha^{k-n^{\prime}}\cdot y^{\prime}\leq\alpha^{k-n^{\prime}}\cdot z
, showing that
u\leq_{\alpha}w
.

We check that
\_+_{a}w
is monotonic from
\mathrm{tscope}_{\alpha}(\mathfrak{B})
to
itself, for every
a\in[0,1]
and for every

w\in\mathrm{tscope}_{\alpha}(\mathfrak{B})
. Let
u\leq_{\alpha}v
, with

u\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}[(n,x)]_{\alpha}
,
v\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}[(n,y)]_{\alpha}
and

x\leq y
. We may write
w
as
[(n,z)]_{\alpha}
, with the same

n
, possibly increasing the value of
n
for
u
,
v
, and
w
.
Then
x+_{a}y\leq z
, so
u+v=[(n,x+_{a}y)]_{\alpha}\leq_{\alpha}w
.
Hence
\mathrm{tscope}_{\alpha}(\mathfrak{B})
is a preordered barycentric algebra.

The equivalence class of
(0,\bot)
contains all the pairs

(n,\alpha^{n}\cdot\bot)
, namely all the pairs
(n,\bot)
,

n\in\mathbb{N}
, because
\alpha^{n}\cdot\bot=\bot
, by
the fourth equation of
Lemma
4.3
. For every
x\in\mathfrak{B}
,

\bot\leq x
, so

[(0,\bot)]_{\alpha}=[(n,\bot)]_{\alpha}\leq_{\alpha}[(n,x)]_{\alpha}
.
Hence
[(0,\bot)]_{\alpha}
is least in
\mathrm{tscope}_{\alpha}(\mathfrak{B})
, and
therefore
\mathrm{tscope}_{\alpha}(\mathfrak{B})
is a preordered pointed barycentric
algebra.

6. We verify that
a\cdot\_
is monotonic. If
x\leq y
, so
that
[(n,x)]_{\alpha}\leq_{\alpha}[(n,y)]_{\alpha}
, then

a\cdot[(n,x)]_{\alpha}\leq_{\alpha}a\cdot[(n,y)]_{\alpha}
since

(\alpha^{k}a)\cdot x\leq(\alpha^{k}a)\cdot x
(we recall that
scalar multiplication on
\mathfrak{B}
is monotonic, by
Lemma
4.3
), where
k
is sufficiently
large, so that
\alpha^{k}a\leq 1
.

7. By items 4 and 6,
2\cdot\_
is a monotonic doubling map, so

\mathrm{tscope}_{\alpha}(\mathfrak{B})
is a preordered cone by
Proposition
4.7
.
\Box

Theorem 4.13

For every preordered pointed barycentric algebra
\mathfrak{B}
and every

\alpha\in{]0,1[}
,
\mathrm{tscope}_{\alpha}(\mathfrak{B})
is a preordered cone,
and:

1.

the function
\eta^{\mathrm{tscope},\alpha}_{\mathfrak{B}}\colon x\mapsto[(0,x)]_{\alpha}
is
a monotonic linear map from
\mathfrak{B}
to
\mathrm{tscope}_{\alpha}(\mathfrak{B})
;

2.

for every map
f\colon\mathfrak{B}\to\mathfrak{C}
to a cone
\mathfrak{C}
that
commutes with
\alpha\cdot\_
(namely such that

f(\alpha\cdot x)=\alpha\cdot f(x)
for every
x\in\mathfrak{B}
),
there is a unique map

f^{((\alpha))}\colon\mathrm{tscope}_{\alpha}(\mathfrak{B})\to\mathfrak{C}
that
commutes with
\alpha\cdot\_
and such that

f^{((\alpha))}\circ\eta^{\mathrm{tscope},\alpha}_{\mathfrak{B}}=f
, and it is given by the
formula

f^{((\alpha))}([(n,x)]_{\alpha})\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}(1/\alpha)^{n}\cdot f(x)
; if
f
is affine, resp. strict, then so is

f^{((\alpha))}
, in particular
f^{((\alpha))}
is linear
if
f
is; if
f
is
\mathbb{I}
-homogeneous, then
f^{((\alpha))}

is positively homogeneous;

3.

if
\mathfrak{C}
is a preordered cone, and if
f
is monotonic (resp. concave, convex, superlinear, sublinear in the sense of
Definition
4.4
), then

f^{((\alpha))}
is monotonic (resp. concave, convex,
superlinear, sublinear).

4.

In particular,
\mathrm{tscope}_{\alpha}(\mathfrak{B})
is the free cone, and
also the free preordered cone, over the preordered pointed
barycentric algebra
\mathfrak{B}
, and this is independent of
\alpha
(up
to natural isomorphism of preordered cones).

Proof.

We know that
\mathrm{tscope}_{\alpha}(\mathfrak{B})
is a preordered cone from
Proposition
4.12
.

1. For all
x,y\in\mathfrak{B}
such that
x\leq y
, we have

[(0,x)]_{\alpha}\leq_{\alpha}[(0,y)]_{\alpha}
, so
\eta^{\mathrm{tscope},\alpha}_{\mathfrak{B}}
is
monotonic. It is strict by definition of the zero element of

\mathrm{tscope}_{\alpha}(\mathfrak{B})
. In order to see that it is linear, it
remains to show that it is affine, by Proposition
4.6
,
and this follows from the equality

[(n,x)]_{\alpha}+_{a}[(n,y)]_{\alpha}=[(n,x+_{a}y)]_{\alpha}
(see
Proposition
4.12
, item 1) with
n\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}0
.

2. We first characterize the map
\alpha\cdot\_
on

\mathrm{tscope}_{\alpha}(\mathfrak{B})
. By Proposition
4.12
,
item 4, for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
x\in\mathfrak{B}
,

\alpha\cdot[(n,x)]_{\alpha}=[(n,\alpha\cdot x)]_{\alpha}
, so

\alpha\cdot[(n,x)]_{\alpha}=[(n-1,x)]_{\alpha}
if
n\geq 1
.
By induction on
n
, we obtain that

\alpha^{n}\cdot([(n,x)]_{\alpha})=[(0,x)]_{\alpha}
. If

f^{((\alpha))}
exists and commutes with
\alpha\cdot
, then
for every point
[(n,x)]_{\alpha}
of
\mathrm{tscope}_{\alpha}(\mathfrak{B})
,

f^{((\alpha))}(\alpha^{n}\cdot[(n,x)]_{\alpha})
is equal both
to
\alpha^{n}\cdot(f^{((\alpha))}([(n,x)]_{\alpha}))
and to

f^{((\alpha))}([(0,x)]_{\alpha})=(f^{((\alpha))}\circ\eta^{\mathrm{tscope},\alpha}_{\mathfrak{B}})(x)=f(x)
. Therefore

f^{((\alpha))}([(n,x)]_{\alpha})
must be equal to

(1/\alpha)^{n}\cdot f(x)
, a notation that makes sense since scalar
multiplication is taken in the cone
\mathfrak{C}
. It follows that

f^{((\alpha))}
is unique if it exists.

As for existence, we define
f^{((\alpha))}
as mapping every

[(n,x)]_{\alpha}
to
(1/\alpha)^{n}\cdot f(x)
. This is
independent of the chosen representative in the equivalence class of

(n,x)
. Indeed, it suffices to show that

(1/\alpha)^{n+1}\cdot f(\alpha\cdot x)=(1/\alpha)^{n}\cdot f(x)
, which follows from the fact that
f
commutes with

\alpha\cdot\_
.

If
f
is strict, then
f^{((\alpha))}([(0,\bot)]_{\alpha})=(1/\alpha)^{0}\cdot f(\bot)=f(\bot)=0
, so
f^{((\alpha))}

is strict.

If
f
is affine, then for all points
[(n,x)]_{\alpha}
and

[(n,y)]_{\alpha}
of
\mathrm{tscope}_{\alpha}(\mathfrak{B})
(with the same
n
), for
every
a\in[0,1]
,

f^{((\alpha))}([(n,x)]_{\alpha}+_{a}[(n,y)]_{\alpha})=f^{((,))\alpha}([(n,x+_{a}y)]_{\alpha})=(1/\alpha)^{n}\cdot f(x+_{a}y)=(1/\alpha)^{n}\cdot(a\cdot f(x)+(1-a)\cdot f(y))=a\cdot(1/\alpha)^{n}\cdot f(x)+(1-a)\cdot(1/\alpha)^{n}\cdot f(y)=a\cdot f^{((\alpha))}([(n,x)]_{\alpha})+(1-a)\cdot f^{((,))\alpha}([(n,y)]_{\alpha})
, so
f^{((\alpha))}
is affine.

By Proposition
4.6
, it follows that
f^{((\alpha))}

is linear if
f
is.

If
f
is
\mathbb{I}
-homogeneous, then we check that
f^{((\alpha))}

is positively homogeneous. Let
a\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
and
[(n,x)]_{\alpha}
be
a point of
\mathrm{tscope}_{\alpha}(\mathfrak{B})
. We pick
k
so that

\alpha^{k}a\leq 1
. Then

f^{((\alpha))}(a\cdot[(n,x)]_{\alpha})=f^{((\alpha))}([(n+k,(\alpha^{k}a)\cdot x)]_{\alpha})=(1/\alpha)^{n+k}\cdot f((\alpha^{k}a)\cdot x)=(1/\alpha)^{n+k}\cdot(\alpha^{k}a)\cdot f(x)
(since
f
is
\mathbb{I}
-homogeneous and
\alpha^{k}a\leq 1
)

=a\cdot(1/\alpha)^{n}\cdot f(x)=a\cdot f^{((\alpha))}([(n,x)]_{\alpha})
.

3. Let us assume that
\mathfrak{C}
is preordered. If
f
is monotonic,
then we check that for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
, for all
x,y\in\mathfrak{B}

such that
x\leq y
,

f^{((\alpha))}([(n,x)]_{\alpha})\leq f^{((\alpha))}([(n,y)]_{\alpha})
; and indeed,

f^{((\alpha))}([(n,x)]_{\alpha})=(1/\alpha)^{n}\cdot f(x)\leq(1/\alpha)^{n}\cdot f(y)=f^{((\alpha))}([(n,y)]_{\alpha})
,
since
f
is monotonic.

If
f
is concave, we reason as in the case where
f
is affine (see
item 2):

f^{((\alpha))}([(n,x)]_{\alpha}+_{a}[(n,y)]_{\alpha})=f^{((,))\alpha}([(n,x+_{a}y)]_{\alpha})=(1/\alpha)^{n}\cdot f(x+_{a}y)\geq(1/\alpha)^{n}\cdot(a\cdot f(x)+(1-a)\cdot f(y))=a\cdot(1/\alpha)^{n}\cdot f(x)+(1-a)\cdot(1/\alpha)^{n}\cdot f(y)=a\cdot f^{((\alpha))}([(n,x)]_{\alpha})+(1-a)\cdot f^{((\alpha))}([(n,y)]_{\alpha})
, so
f^{((\alpha))}
is
concave. Similarly, if
f
is convex then so is

f^{((\alpha))}
. Since superlinearity (resp. sublinearity) is
equivalent to
\mathbb{I}
-homogeneity plus concavity (resp. convexity) by
Remark
4.5
,
f^{((\alpha))}
is
superlinear, resp. sublinear if
f
is.

4. The fact that
\mathrm{tscope}_{\alpha}(\mathfrak{B})
is the free preordered cone
over
\mathfrak{B}
is an immediate consequence of items 2 and 3. Hence

\mathrm{tscope}_{\alpha}
define a functor that is left adjoint to the
forgetful functor from preordered cones to preordered pointed
barycentric algebras. As such, it is determined in a unique way up
to natural isomorphism in the category of preordered cones, hence is
independent of
\alpha
, up to natural isomorphism.
\Box

The map
\eta^{\mathrm{tscope},\alpha}_{\mathfrak{B}}
is almost a preorder embedding, as the following
Lemma
4.15
shows. This rests on
the following clever observation; the first part is due to Neumann

[
37
, Lemma 2]
, the second part is due to Keimel and
Plotkin
[
29
, Lemma 2.8]
, while the remaining is a trivial
consequence.

Fact 4.14

In a barycentric algebra
\mathfrak{B}
, if
x+_{a}z=y+_{a}z
for some

a\in{]0,1[}
, then
x+_{a}z=y+_{a}z
for every

a\in{]0,1[}
. In a preordered barycentric algebra
\mathfrak{B}
, if

x+_{a}z\leq y+_{a}z
for some
a\in{]0,1[}
, then

x+_{a}z\leq y+_{a}z
for every
a\in{]0,1[}
.

It follows that in a pointed barycentric algebra, if

r\cdot x=r\cdot y
for some
r\in{]0,1[}
, then

r\cdot x=r\cdot y
for every
r\in{]0,1[}
, and if

r\cdot x\leq r\cdot y
for some
r\in{]0,1[}
, then

r\cdot x\leq r\cdot y
for every
r\in{]0,1[}
.

Lemma 4.15

Let
\alpha\in{]0,1[}
. For any two points
x
,
y
in a
preordered pointed barycentric algebra
\mathfrak{B}
, if

\eta^{\mathrm{tscope},\alpha}_{\mathfrak{B}}(x)\leq_{\alpha}\eta^{\mathrm{tscope},\alpha}_{\mathfrak{B}}(y)
in
\mathrm{tscope}_{\alpha}(\mathfrak{B})
,
then
r\cdot x\leq r\cdot y
for some
r\in{]0,1[}
, and then
for every
r\in{]0,1[}
.

Proof.

If
\eta^{\mathrm{tscope},\alpha}_{\mathfrak{B}}(x)\leq_{\alpha}\eta^{\mathrm{tscope},\alpha}_{\mathfrak{B}}(y)
, then we can write

\eta^{\mathrm{tscope},\alpha}_{\mathfrak{B}}(x)
as
[(n,x^{\prime})]_{\alpha}
,
\eta^{\mathrm{tscope},\alpha}_{\mathfrak{B}}(y)
as

[(n,y^{\prime})]_{\alpha}
, where
x^{\prime}\leq y^{\prime}
. Since

\eta^{\mathrm{tscope},\alpha}_{\mathfrak{B}}(x)=[(0,x)]_{\alpha}=[(n,x^{\prime})]_{\alpha}
, we have

(0,x)\equiv_{\alpha}(n,x^{\prime})
, so

\alpha^{k}\cdot x=\alpha^{k-n}\cdot x^{\prime}
for some
k\geq n
, by
Lemma
4.9
. Similarly,

\alpha^{k^{\prime}}\cdot y=\alpha^{k^{\prime}-n}\cdot y^{\prime}
for some
k^{\prime}\geq n
.
Multiplying by powers of
\alpha
if necessary (and using the second
equation of Lemma
4.3
), we may assume that

k=k^{\prime}
. Since
x^{\prime}\leq y^{\prime}
, we obtain that

\alpha^{k-n}\cdot x^{\prime}\leq\alpha^{k-n}\cdot x^{\prime}
, hence that

\alpha^{k}\cdot x\leq\alpha^{k}\cdot y
. We can therefore take

r\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\alpha^{k}
—or
\alpha^{k+1}
in order to ensure that

r<1
. Then
r\cdot x\leq r\cdot y
for every

r\in{]0,1[}
, by Fact
4.14
.
\Box

Frustratingly, but crucially,
r
cannot be forced to be equal to
1

in Lemma
4.15
. We will see why
in Example
4.18
. In the meantime, let
us observe that the situation is nicer with pointed semitopological
barycentric algebras.

Lemma 4.16

Let
\mathfrak{B}
be a pointed semitopological barycentric algebra,
considered with its specialization preordering
\leq
. Then:

1.

for all
x,y\in\mathfrak{B}
, if
r\cdot x\leq r\cdot y
for every

r\in{[0,1[}
, then
x\leq y
;

2.

\eta^{\mathrm{tscope},\alpha}_{\mathfrak{B}}
is a preorder embedding, where

\mathrm{tscope}_{\alpha}(\mathfrak{B})
is given the preordering
\leq_{\alpha}
of
Proposition
4.12
.

Proof.

1. In order to show that
x\leq y
, we consider any open
neighborhood
U
of
x
, and we show that
y\in U
. The map

\_\cdot x
is continuous from
\mathbb{R}_{+}
to
\mathfrak{B}
by assumption, so

(\_\cdot x)^{-1}(U)
is a Scott-open subset of
\mathbb{R}_{+}
. By
assumption, it contains
1
, so it is of the form
]t,\infty[
with

0<t<1
or equal to the whole of
\mathbb{R}_{+}
. In any case, it
contains some
r\in{]0,1[}
. Then
r\cdot x\in U
, and since

U
is upwards-closed and
r\cdot x\leq r\cdot y
,
r\cdot y
is
also in
U
. In other words,
(\_\cdot y)^{-1}(U)
is a
Scott-open subset of
\mathbb{R}_{+}
that contains
r
. Since
r<1
and
Scott-open sets are upwards-closed, it also contains
1
, so

y\in U
. Since
U
is arbitrary, it follows that
x\leq y
.

2. By Lemma
4.15
, for any two
points
x,y\in\mathfrak{B}
such that

\eta^{\mathrm{tscope},\alpha}_{\mathfrak{B}}(x)\leq_{\alpha}\eta^{\mathrm{tscope},\alpha}_{\mathfrak{B}}(y)
, then the assumption of
item 1 is satisfied; so
x\leq y
. In other words,
\eta^{\mathrm{tscope},\alpha}_{\mathfrak{B}}

is a preorder embedding.
\Box

Remark 4.17

Let
\alpha\in{]0,1[}
, and
\mathfrak{B}
be a pointed
T_{0}

semitopological barycentric algebra. Then the strange situations
depicted in Remark
4.10
do not happen,
namely, for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
and for all
x,y\in\mathfrak{B}
,

(n,x)\equiv_{\alpha}(n,y)
if and only if
x=y
. Hence the
equivalence classes with respect to
\alpha
are all of the form

\{(n_{0},x_{0}),(n_{0}+1,\alpha\cdot x_{0}),\cdots,(n_{0}+k,\alpha^{k}\cdot x_{0}),\cdots\}
for a unique
n_{0}\in\mathbb{N}
and a unique

x_{0}\in\mathfrak{B}
. It also follows that
\eta^{\mathrm{tscope},\alpha}_{\mathfrak{B}}
is injective.

Proof.

If
(n,x)\equiv_{\alpha}(n,y)
, then by
Lemma
4.9
,

\alpha^{k-n}\cdot x=\alpha^{k-n}\cdot y
for some
k\geq n
.
Then
r\cdot x=r\cdot y
, where
r\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\alpha^{k+1-n}<1
,
so
r\cdot x=r\cdot y
for every
r\in{]0,1[}
, by
Fact
4.14
. By
Lemma
4.16
,
x\leq y
and

y\leq x
, so
x=y
since
\mathfrak{B}
is
T_{0}
.

Given an element
u
of
\mathrm{tscope}_{\alpha}(\mathfrak{B})
—an equivalence
class—let
n_{0}
be the least
n\in\mathbb{N}
such that some pair

(n,x)
is in
u
. We have just seen that there is a unique point

x_{0}\in\mathfrak{B}
such that
(n_{0},x_{0})
is in
u
. Then all the

\equiv_{\alpha}
-equivalent points
(n_{0}+k,\alpha^{k}\cdot x_{0})
are
in the equivalence class. By the first part of the remark, any pair
of the form
(n_{0}+k,y)
must satisfy
y=\alpha^{k}\cdot x_{0}
, and
there is no pair of the form
(n,x)
with
n<n_{0}
by minimality
of
n_{0}
.

In particular, for all points
x,y\in\mathfrak{B}
,
\eta^{\mathrm{tscope},\alpha}_{\mathfrak{B}}(x)
is
the equivalence class

\{(0,x),(1,\alpha\cdot x),\cdots,(n,\alpha^{n}\cdot x),\cdots\}
and
\eta^{\mathrm{tscope},\alpha}_{\mathfrak{B}}(y)
is the equivalence class

\{(0,y),(1,\alpha\cdot y),\cdots,(n,\alpha^{n}\cdot y),\cdots\}
. If they are equal, then in particular their (unique)
elements with first component equal to
0
are equal, namely
x=y
;
so
\eta^{\mathrm{tscope},\alpha}_{\mathfrak{B}}
is injective.
\Box

Example 4.18

Let us consider
\mathfrak{B}\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\mathfrak{B}^{KP}
, with the two points
x
and
y

given in Example
4.11
. We have seen
that
r\cdot x=r\cdot y
for every
r\in{]0,1[}
. In
particular,
\alpha\cdot x=\alpha\cdot y
, so

(0,x)\equiv_{\alpha}(1,\alpha\cdot x)=(1,\alpha\cdot y)\equiv_{\alpha}(0,y)
. In other words,

\eta^{\mathrm{tscope},\alpha}_{\mathfrak{B}}(x)=\eta^{\mathrm{tscope},\alpha}_{\mathfrak{B}}(x)
, showing that
\eta^{\mathrm{tscope},\alpha}_{\mathfrak{B}}
is
not injective. It is not an order-embedding either, because
x\not\leq y
.

In general, we have the following equivalences. The property
mentioned in item 3 below is called (OC2) by Keimel and Plotkin

[
29
]
.

Lemma 4.19

For a preordered pointed barycentric algebra
\mathfrak{B}
, the following are
equivalent:

1.

\eta^{\mathrm{tscope},\alpha}_{\mathfrak{B}}
is a preorder embedding for some
\alpha\in{]0,1[}
;

2.

\eta^{\mathrm{tscope},\alpha}_{\mathfrak{B}}
is a preorder embedding for every

\alpha\in{]0,1[}
;

3.

for all
r\in{]0,1[}
and
x,y\in\mathfrak{B}
,
r\cdot x\leq r\cdot y
implies
x\leq y
;

4.

there is a linear preorder embedding of
\mathfrak{B}
in some
preordered cone.

Proof.

1\mathrel{\Rightarrow}4
is obvious, in light of
Proposition
4.12
and
Theorem
4.13
, item 1.

4\mathrel{\Rightarrow}3
. Let
i\colon\mathfrak{B}\to\mathfrak{C}
be a linear preorder
embedding of
\mathfrak{B}
in some preordered cone
\mathfrak{C}
. Let us also
assume that
r\cdot x\leq r\cdot y
, where
0<r<1
. Then

i(r\cdot x)=r\cdot i(x)
is less than or equal to

i(r\cdot y)=r\cdot i(y)
. Multiplying by
1/r
on both
sides,
i(x)\leq i(y)
. Since
i
is a preorder embedding,

x\leq y
.

3\mathrel{\Rightarrow}2
. Let
\alpha\in{]0,1[}
be arbitrary, and let

x,y\in\mathfrak{B}
be such that
\eta^{\mathrm{tscope},\alpha}_{\mathfrak{B}}(x)\leq_{\alpha}\eta^{\mathrm{tscope},\alpha}_{\mathfrak{B}}(y)
.
By Lemma
4.15
,

r\cdot x\leq r\cdot y
for some
r\in{]0,1[}
. Then item 3
implies
x\leq y
.

Finally,
2\mathrel{\Rightarrow}1
is clear.
\Box

4.3
Semitopological telescopes

The construction
\mathrm{tscope}_{\alpha}(\mathfrak{B})
works in the semitopological
case, too: we simply take a colimit in
\mathbf{Top}
instead of
\mathbf{Set}
.

Definition 4.20

For every pointed semitopological barycentric algebra
\mathfrak{B}
, we give

\mathrm{tscope}_{\alpha}(\mathfrak{B})={(\coprod_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\mathfrak{B})}/{\equiv_{\alpha}}
the quotient topology, where

\coprod_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\mathfrak{B}
has the coproduct topology.

We will need the following characterization of its open subsets, whose
proof we leave as an exercise.

Lemma 4.21

Let
\mathfrak{B}
be a pointed semitopological barycentric algebra and

\alpha\in{]0,1[}
. For short, let

q\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}[\_]_{\alpha}\colon\coprod_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\mathfrak{B}\to\mathrm{tscope}_{\alpha}(\mathfrak{B})
be the quotient map. The open subsets of

\mathrm{tscope}_{\alpha}(\mathfrak{B})
are the sets
q[V_{\infty}]
with

V_{\infty}\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\coprod_{n\in\mathbb{N}}V_{n}=\{(n,x)\mid n\in\mathbb{N},x\in V_{n}\}
, where each
V_{n}
is open in
\mathfrak{B}
and

V_{n}=(\alpha\cdot\_)^{-1}(V_{n+1})
for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
.
Additionally,
V_{\infty}=q^{-1}(q[V_{\infty}])
.

Theorem 4.22

Let
\alpha\in{]0,1[}
. For every pointed semitopological
barycentric algebra
\mathfrak{B}
,

1.

\mathrm{tscope}_{\alpha}(\mathfrak{B})
, with the quotient topology given in
Definition
4.20
, is a semitopological cone;

2.

the map
\eta^{\mathrm{tscope},\alpha}_{\mathfrak{B}}
is linear and continuous;

3.

for every continuous map
f\colon\mathfrak{B}\to\mathfrak{C}
to a
semitopological cone
\mathfrak{C}
that commutes with
\alpha\cdot\_
,
there is a unique continuous map

f^{((\alpha))}\colon\mathrm{tscope}_{\alpha}(\mathfrak{B})\to\mathfrak{C}
that
commutes with
\alpha\cdot\_
and such that

f^{((\alpha))}\circ\eta^{\mathrm{tscope},\alpha}_{\mathfrak{B}}=f
; if
f
is affine
(resp. concave, convex, strict, linear, superlinear, sublinear),
then so is
f^{((\alpha))}
; if
f
is
\mathbb{I}
-homogeneous, then

f^{((\alpha))}
is positively homogeneous.

4.

In particular,
\mathrm{tscope}_{\alpha}(\mathfrak{B})
is the free
semitopological cone over the pointed semitopological barycentric
algebra
\mathfrak{B}
, and this is independent of
\alpha
(up to natural
isomorphisms of semitopological cones).

Proof.

1. We verify that
\mathrm{tscope}_{\alpha}(\mathfrak{B})
is a pointed semitopological
barycentric algebra. In light of Proposition
4.12
,
only separate continuity of the barycentric algebra operations has
to be checked.

Let us fix
a\in[0,1]
and
v\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}[(n,y)]_{\alpha}
of

\mathrm{tscope}_{\alpha}(\mathfrak{B})
; we verify that
\_+_{a}v
is continuous from

\mathrm{tscope}_{\alpha}(\mathfrak{B})
to itself. Let
q
be the quotient map of

\coprod_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\mathfrak{B}
onto
\mathrm{tscope}_{\alpha}(\mathfrak{B})
. By definition
of topological quotients and of topologies on coproducts, it
suffices to verify that
f_{m}\colon x\mapsto[(m,x)]_{\alpha}+_{a}v

is continuous for every
m\in\mathbb{N}
. Let
V
be an open subset of

\mathrm{tscope}_{\alpha}(\mathfrak{B})
. For every point
x\in\mathfrak{B}
,

f_{m}(x)=[(\max(m,n),\alpha^{\max(m,n)-m}\cdot x]_{\alpha}+_{a}[(\max(m,n),\alpha^{\max(m,n)-n}\cdot y]_{\alpha}=[(\max(m,n),(\alpha^{\max(m,n)-m}\cdot x)+_{a}(\alpha^{\max(m,n)-n}\cdot y))]_{\alpha}
, so
x\in f_{m}^{-1}(V)
if and only if

x\in g^{-1}(q^{-1}(V))
, where
g
is the map

x\mapsto(\max(m,n),(\alpha^{\max(m,n)-m}\cdot x)+_{a}(\alpha^{\max(m,n)-n}\cdot y))
, which is continuous from
\mathfrak{B}

to
\coprod_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\mathfrak{B}
, since
+_{a}
is separately continuous
and since
\alpha^{\max(m,n)-m}\cdot
is continuous, by
Lemma
4.3
.

We now fix
u\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}[(n,x)]_{\alpha}
and
v\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}[(n,y)]_{\alpha}

(with the same
n
), and we verify that
a\mapsto u+_{a}v
is
continuous from
[0,1]
to
\mathrm{tscope}_{\alpha}(\mathfrak{B})
. Since that is
the composition of
q
with the map
a\mapsto(n,x+_{a}y)
,
continuity is immediate.

2. The map
\eta^{\mathrm{tscope},\alpha}_{\mathfrak{B}}
is linear by
Theorem
4.13
, item 1. A
subtle point here is that linearity includes
\mathbb{I}
-homogeneity,
which is defined with respect to a least element of
\mathfrak{B}
(viz.,

\bot
) and a least element of
\mathrm{tscope}_{\alpha}(\mathfrak{B})
. Now
