\eta^{\mathrm{tscope},\alpha}_{\mathfrak{B}}(\bot)=[(0,\bot)]_{\alpha}
is a least element of

\mathrm{tscope}_{\alpha}(\mathfrak{B})
with respect to
\leq_{\alpha}
, and this is what we
used in Theorem
4.13
. In the
current setting, we need to check that
[(0,\bot)]_{\alpha}
is a
least element of
\mathrm{tscope}_{\alpha}(\mathfrak{B})
in its specialization
preordering instead. In other words, we need to check that the only
open subset
V
of
\mathrm{tscope}_{\alpha}(\mathfrak{B})
that contains

[(0,\bot)]_{\alpha}
is the whole of
\mathrm{tscope}_{\alpha}(\mathfrak{B})
. Using
the notations of Lemma
4.21
, we must have

V=q[V_{\infty}]
and

V_{\infty}=\coprod_{n\in\mathbb{N}}V_{n}=\{(n,x)\mid n\in\mathbb{N},x\in V_{n}\}
, where
V_{n}=(\alpha\cdot\_)^{-1}(V_{n+1})
for every

n\in\mathbb{N}
. Then
(0,\bot)
is in
V_{\infty}
, so
\bot\in V_{0}
,
and this entails that
V_{0}=\mathfrak{B}
. Since

\bot\in V_{0}=(\alpha\cdot\_)^{-1}(V_{1})
,

\alpha\cdot\bot=\bot
is in
V_{1}
, so
V_{1}=\mathfrak{B}
as well. By
an easy induction on
n\in\mathbb{N}
, we obtain that
\bot\in V_{n}
,
hence
V_{n}=\mathfrak{B}
, for every
n
. It follows that

V=\mathrm{tscope}_{\alpha}(\mathfrak{B})
.

Next,
\eta^{\mathrm{tscope},\alpha}_{\mathfrak{B}}
is the composition of
x\mapsto(0,x)
from

\mathfrak{B}
to
\coprod_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\mathfrak{B}
and of the quotient map onto

\mathrm{tscope}_{\alpha}(\mathfrak{B})
, so it is continuous.

3. Given any continuous map
f\colon\mathfrak{B}\to\mathfrak{C}
that commutes with

\alpha\cdot\_
,
f
is monotonic, so there is a unique map

f^{((\alpha))}\colon\mathrm{tscope}_{\alpha}(\mathfrak{B})\to\mathfrak{C}
that commutes
with
\alpha\cdot\_
and such that

f^{((\alpha))}\circ\eta^{\mathrm{tscope},\alpha}_{\mathfrak{B}}=f
, and it is monotonic by
Theorem
4.13
, item 2.
Additionally, it is affine (resp. concave, convex, strict, linear,
superlinear, sublinear) if
f
is, by the same proposition, items 2
and 3. It remains to show that
f^{((\alpha))}
is continuous.
By definition of topological quotients and topologies on coproducts,
it suffices to show that the map

x\mapsto f^{((\alpha))}([(n,x)]_{\alpha})
is continuous from

\mathfrak{B}
to
\mathfrak{C}
, for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
. For every
x\in\mathfrak{B}
,

f^{((\alpha))}([(n,x)]_{\alpha})=(1/\alpha)^{n}\cdot f(x)
, and
the result then follows from the continuity of
f
and of scalar
multiplication.

4. It follows immediately that
\mathrm{tscope}_{\alpha}(\mathfrak{B})
, with the
quotient topology, is the free semitopological cone over the pointed
semitopological barycentric algebra
\mathfrak{B}
. Since free objects are
unique up to natural isomorphism, the construction is independent of

\alpha
up to natural isomorphism of semitopological cones.
\Box

Remark 4.23

There is no reason to believe that the specialization preordering of

\mathrm{tscope}_{\alpha}(\mathfrak{B})
, with the topology of
Definition
4.20
would be the preordering
\leq_{\alpha}

defined in Proposition
4.12
, in general. We
will see in Lemma
4.29
when the two
preorderings coincide.

Problem 4.24

Let
\mathfrak{B}
be a pointed topological barycentric algebra and

\alpha\in{]0,1[}
. Is
\mathrm{tscope}_{\alpha}(\mathfrak{B})
necessarily a
topological cone?

Definition 4.25
(Strictly embeddable)

A
strict embedding
of a pointed semitopological barycentric
algebra
\mathfrak{B}
in a semitopological cone
\mathfrak{C}
is a linear topological
embedding of
\mathfrak{B}
in
\mathfrak{C}
.
\mathfrak{B}
is
strictly embeddable
if
and only if there is a strict embedding of
\mathfrak{B}
in some
semitopological cone
\mathfrak{C}
.

Lemma 4.26

Let
\mathfrak{B}
be a pointed
T_{0}
semitopological barycentric algebra. If

\mathfrak{B}
is strictly embeddable, then for every
a\in{]0,1]}
, for
every
\mathbb{I}
-homogeneous lower semicontinuous map

h\colon\mathfrak{B}\to\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
, there is an
\mathbb{I}
-homogeneous lower
semicontinuous map
h^{\prime}\colon\mathfrak{B}\to\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
such that

h^{\prime}(a\cdot x)=h(x)
for every
x\in\mathfrak{B}
. If
h
is linear
(resp., superlinear, sublinear), then so is
h^{\prime}
.

Proof.

We cannot just define
h^{\prime}
by
h^{\prime}(x)\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}h(\frac{1}{a}\cdot x)
,
since only scalar multiplication by numbers in
[0,1]
is permitted
in pointed barycentric algebras.

Let us fix
\alpha\in{]0,1[}
. Given
h
and
a
as above, let

h^{\prime}(x)\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}h^{((\alpha))}(\frac{1}{a}\cdot\eta^{\mathrm{tscope},\alpha}_{\mathfrak{B}}(x))
.
Multiplying by
\frac{1}{a}
is permitted, because we perform it in
the cone
\mathrm{tscope}_{\alpha}(\mathfrak{B})
. By
Theorem
4.22
,
h^{\prime}
is an

\mathbb{I}
-homogeneous map from
\mathfrak{B}
to
\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
, which is linear (resp. superlinear, sublinear) if
h
is, owing notably to the fact that

\eta^{\mathrm{tscope},\alpha}_{\mathfrak{B}}
is linear. For every
x\in\mathfrak{B}
,

h^{\prime}(a\cdot x)=h^{((\alpha))}(\frac{1}{a}\cdot\eta^{\mathrm{tscope},\alpha}_{\mathfrak{B}}(a\cdot x))=h^{((\alpha))}(\frac{1}{a}\cdot a\cdot\eta^{\mathrm{tscope},\alpha}_{\mathfrak{B}}(x))
(since
\eta^{\mathrm{tscope},\alpha}_{\mathfrak{B}}
is linear)

=h^{((\alpha))}(\eta^{\mathrm{tscope},\alpha}_{\mathfrak{B}}(x))=h(x)
.

Proposition 4.27

The following are equivalent for a pointed
T_{0}
semitopological
barycentric algebra
\mathfrak{B}
:

1.

\mathfrak{B}
is strictly embeddable;

2.

\eta^{\mathrm{tscope},\alpha}_{\mathfrak{B}}
is a topological embedding for every
\alpha\in{]0,1[}
;

3.

\eta^{\mathrm{tscope},\alpha}_{\mathfrak{B}}
is a topological embedding for some
\alpha\in{]0,1[}
;

4.

the proper open subsets of
\mathfrak{B}
are exactly the sets

h^{-1}(]1,\infty])
where
h
ranges over the
\mathbb{I}
-homogeneous
lower semicontinuous maps from
\mathfrak{B}
to
\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
;

5.

the sets
h^{-1}(]1,\infty])
where
h
ranges over the

\mathbb{I}
-homogeneous lower semicontinuous maps from
\mathfrak{B}
to
\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}

form a subbase of its topology;

6.

the map
\alpha\cdot\_\colon\mathfrak{B}\to\mathfrak{B}
is full, for every

\alpha\in{]0,1[}
;

7.

the map
\alpha\cdot\_\colon\mathfrak{B}\to\mathfrak{B}
is full, for some

\alpha\in{]0,1[}
.

Proof.

1\mathrel{\Rightarrow}2
. Let
i\colon\mathfrak{B}\to\mathfrak{C}
be a strict embedding of
\mathfrak{B}

in a semitopological cone
\mathfrak{C}
. In particular,
i
is linear. We
fix an arbitrary
\alpha\in{]0,1[}
. For every open subset
U

of
\mathfrak{B}
, there is an open subset
V
of
\mathfrak{C}
such that

U=i^{-1}(V)
. By
Theorem
4.22
, item 3,

i^{((\alpha))}\colon\mathrm{tscope}_{\alpha}(\mathfrak{B})\to\mathfrak{C}
is linear,
continuous and
i^{((\alpha))}\circ\eta^{\mathrm{tscope},\alpha}_{\mathfrak{B}}=i
. In
particular, since
i
is injective, so is
\eta^{\mathrm{tscope},\alpha}_{\mathfrak{B}}
. Also,

U=(i^{((\alpha))}\circ\eta^{\mathrm{tscope},\alpha}_{\mathfrak{B}})^{-1}(V)=(\eta^{\mathrm{tscope},\alpha}_{\mathfrak{B}})^{-1}\allowbreak((i^{((\alpha))})^{-1}(V))
,
showing that
U=(\eta^{\mathrm{tscope},\alpha}_{\mathfrak{B}})^{-1}(V^{\prime})
where
V^{\prime}
is the open
set
(i^{((\alpha))})^{-1}(V)
. Hence
\eta^{\mathrm{tscope},\alpha}_{\mathfrak{B}}
is full.
Since
\eta^{\mathrm{tscope},\alpha}_{\mathfrak{B}}
is continuous by
Theorem
4.22
, item 2,

\eta^{\mathrm{tscope},\alpha}_{\mathfrak{B}}
is a topological embedding.

2\mathrel{\Rightarrow}3
is obvious.

3\mathrel{\Rightarrow}4
. We fix
\alpha\in{]0,1[}
so that
\eta^{\mathrm{tscope},\alpha}_{\mathfrak{B}}
is
a topological embedding, thanks to item 3. The open subsets of
\mathfrak{B}

are exactly the sets of the form
U\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}{(\eta^{\mathrm{tscope},\alpha}_{\mathfrak{B}})}^{-1}(V)

where
V
is open in
\mathrm{tscope}_{\alpha}(\mathfrak{B})
. If
\bot\in U
, then

[(0,\bot)]_{\alpha}=\eta^{\mathrm{tscope},\alpha}_{\mathfrak{B}}(\bot)
is in
V
. Conversely, if

[(0,\bot)]_{\alpha}\in V
, and since
[(0,\bot)]_{\alpha}
is the
zero element, hence is least in
\mathrm{tscope}_{\alpha}(\mathfrak{B})
, we must have

V=\mathrm{tscope}_{\alpha}(\mathfrak{B})
, so
U=\mathfrak{B}
, whence
\bot\in U
. Hence

\bot\in U
if and only if
U
is non-proper if and only if

[(0,\bot)]_{\alpha}\in V
if and only if
V
is non-proper. It
follows that the proper open subsets
U
of
\mathfrak{B}
are exactly the
sets
{(\eta^{\mathrm{tscope},\alpha}_{\mathfrak{B}})}^{-1}(V)
where
V
is a proper open subset of

\mathrm{tscope}_{\alpha}(\mathfrak{B})
.

If so, then
V=(M^{V})^{-1}(]1,\infty])
, where
M^{V}
is the upper
Minkowski function of
V
(see Section
2
), and

M^{V}
is positively homogeneous and lower semicontinuous. Since

\eta^{\mathrm{tscope},\alpha}_{\mathfrak{B}}
is itself linear and continuous by
Theorem
4.22
, item 2,

h\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}M^{V}\circ\eta^{\mathrm{tscope},\alpha}_{\mathfrak{B}}
is
\mathbb{I}
-homogeneous and lower
semicontinuous. It follows that
U=h^{-1}(]1,\infty])
.

Conversely, every set of the form
h^{-1}(]1,\infty])
with
h

\mathbb{I}
-homogeneous and lower semicontinuous is proper (since

h(\bot)=0\not\in{]1,\infty]}
) and open.

4\mathrel{\Rightarrow}5
is clear.

5\mathrel{\Rightarrow}2\mathrel{\Rightarrow}1
. We assume that the sets
h^{-1}(]1,\infty])

where
h
ranges over the
\mathbb{I}
-homogeneous lower semicontinuous
maps from
\mathfrak{B}
to
\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
form a subbase of the topology on
\mathfrak{B}
.
Let
\alpha\in{]0,1[}
be arbitrary. Since

h^{((\alpha))}\circ\eta^{\mathrm{tscope},\alpha}_{\mathfrak{B}}=h
, every subbasic open set

h^{-1}(]1,\infty])
is equal to

{(\eta^{\mathrm{tscope},\alpha}_{\mathfrak{B}})}^{-1}((h^{((\alpha))})^{-1}(]1,\infty]))
.
Hence
\eta^{\mathrm{tscope},\alpha}_{\mathfrak{B}}
is full. Since
\eta^{\mathrm{tscope},\alpha}_{\mathfrak{B}}
is continuous and
since
\mathfrak{B}
is
T_{0}
,
\eta^{\mathrm{tscope},\alpha}_{\mathfrak{B}}
is a topological embedding. This
shows item 2, and then item 1, since
\mathrm{tscope}_{\alpha}(\mathfrak{B})
is a
semitopological cone, by
Theorem
4.22
, item 1.

1\mathrel{\Rightarrow}6
. The map
\alpha\cdot\_
is linear and continuous,
and since
\mathfrak{B}
is
T_{0}
, we only have to show that it is full,
namely that every open subset
U
of
\mathfrak{B}
can be written as

(\alpha\cdot\_)^{-1}(V)
for some open subset
V
of
\mathfrak{B}
. If

U=\mathfrak{B}
, then we just take
V\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\mathfrak{B}
. Otherwise, by
Proposition
4.27
,

U=h^{-1}(]1,\infty])
for some
\mathbb{I}
-homogeneous lower
semicontinuous map from
\mathfrak{B}
to
\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
. By
Lemma
4.26
, there is an

\mathbb{I}
-homogeneous lower semicontinuous map
h^{\prime}\colon\mathfrak{B}\to\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}

such that
h^{\prime}(\alpha\cdot x)=h(x)
for every
x\in\mathfrak{B}
. Let

V\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}{h^{\prime}}^{-1}(]1,\infty])
. Then

(\alpha\cdot\_)^{-1}(V)=\{x\in\mathfrak{B}\mid h^{\prime}(\alpha\cdot x)>1\}=h^{-1}(]1,\infty])=U
.

6\mathrel{\Rightarrow}7
is clear.

7\mathrel{\Rightarrow}3
. We consider an arbitrary open subset
U
of
\mathfrak{B}
, and
we wish to find an open subset of
\mathrm{tscope}_{\alpha}(\mathfrak{B})
such that

(\eta^{\mathrm{tscope},\alpha}_{\mathfrak{B}})^{-1}(V)=U
. We let
U_{0}\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}U
, then, by
induction on
n
, we pick an open subset
U_{n+1}
of
\mathfrak{B}
such that

U_{n}=(\alpha\cdot\_)^{-1}(U_{n+1})
, using the fact that

\alpha\cdot\_
is full. Then we define
U_{\infty}
as

\coprod_{n\in\mathbb{N}}U_{n}=\{(n,x)\mid n\in\mathbb{N},x\in U_{n}\}
,
an open subset of
\coprod_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\mathfrak{B}
. By
Lemma
4.21
, the image
q[U_{\infty}]
of

U_{\infty}
under the quotient map
q\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}[\_]_{\alpha}
is open in

\mathrm{tscope}_{\alpha}(\mathfrak{B})
.

Then

(\eta^{\mathrm{tscope},\alpha}_{\mathfrak{B}})^{-1}(q[U_{\infty}])=\{x\in\mathfrak{B}\mid[(0,x)]_{\alpha}\in q[U_{\infty}]\}=\{x\in\mathfrak{B}\mid(0,x)\in q^{-1}(q[U_{\infty}])\}=\{x\in\mathfrak{B}\mid(0,x)\in U_{\infty}\}=U_{0}=U
.
Hence
\eta^{\mathrm{tscope},\alpha}_{\mathfrak{B}}
is full. Since
\mathfrak{B}
is
T_{0}
and
\eta^{\mathrm{tscope},\alpha}_{\mathfrak{B}}

is continuous
(Theorem
4.22
, item 2),

\eta^{\mathrm{tscope},\alpha}_{\mathfrak{B}}
is a topological embedding.
\Box

Problem 4.28

Is every pointed semitopological barycentric algebra strictly
embeddable? Corollary
6.23
will show
that this is the case for locally linear
T_{0}
pointed
semitopological barycentric algebras.

Lemma 4.29

Let
\mathfrak{B}
be a pointed semitopological barycentric algebra and

\alpha\in{]0,1[}
. We write
\preceq
for the specialization
preordering of
\mathrm{tscope}_{\alpha}(\mathfrak{B})
, to distinguish it from the
preordering
\leq_{\alpha}
of Proposition
4.12
.
Then, for all
u,v\in\mathrm{tscope}_{\alpha}(\mathfrak{B})
, if
u\leq_{\alpha}v
then

u\preceq v
, and the converse implication holds if
\mathfrak{B}
is
strictly embeddable and
T_{0}
.

Proof.

Let
u\leq_{\alpha}v
. We can write
u
as
[(n,x)]_{\alpha}
and
v

as
[(n,y)]_{\alpha}
so that
x\leq y
. Every open neighborhood of

[(n,x)]_{\alpha}
in
\mathrm{tscope}_{\alpha}(\mathfrak{B})
is of the form

q[V_{\infty}]
as given in Lemma
4.21
. Using the
same notations as there,
[(n,x)]_{\alpha}=q(n,x)
, so

(n,x)\in q^{-1}(q[V_{\infty}])=V_{\infty}
, and hence

x\in V_{n}
. Since
x\leq y
,
y
is in
V_{n}
, so

(n,y)\in V_{\infty}
and therefore

[(n,y)]_{\alpha}\in q[V_{\infty}]
. It follows that

[(n,x)]_{\alpha}\preceq[(n,y)]_{\alpha}
.

We now assume that
\mathfrak{B}
is strictly embeddable and
T_{0}
. Let

u,v\in\mathrm{tscope}_{\alpha}(\mathfrak{B})
and let us assume that

u\not\leq_{\alpha}v
. Let us write
u
as
[(n,x)]_{\alpha}
and
v
as

[(n,y)]_{\alpha}
for some common
n\in\mathbb{N}
. Whichever
n
we
take, and whichever
x
and
y
, we must have
x\not\leq y
, so
there must be an open neighborhood
U
of
x
in
\mathfrak{B}
that does not
contain
y
. By Proposition
4.27
,

\eta^{\mathrm{tscope},\alpha}_{\mathfrak{B}}
is a topological embedding, so there is an open subset

V_{1}
of
\mathrm{tscope}_{\alpha}(\mathfrak{B})
such that

U={(\eta^{\mathrm{tscope},\alpha}_{\mathfrak{B}})}^{-1}(V_{1})
. Let

V\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}{(\alpha^{n}\cdot\_)}^{-1}(V_{1})
.

We verify that
u\in V
. It suffices to show that

\alpha^{n}\cdot u\in V_{1}
. But

\alpha^{n}\cdot u=\alpha^{n}\cdot[(n,x)]_{\alpha}=[(n,\alpha^{n}\cdot x)]_{\alpha}
(by Proposition
4.12
, since

a\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\alpha^{n}
is in
[0,1]
)

=[(n-1,\alpha^{n-1}\cdot x)]_{\alpha}=\cdots=[(0,x)]_{\alpha}=\eta^{\mathrm{tscope},\alpha}_{\mathfrak{B}}(x)
. Hence we have to show that

\eta^{\mathrm{tscope},\alpha}_{\mathfrak{B}}(x)\in V_{1}
, namely that

x\in(\eta^{\mathrm{tscope},\alpha}_{\mathfrak{B}})^{-1}(V_{1})=U
; and indeed
x\in U
.
Similarly,
\alpha^{n}\cdot v=[(0,y)]_{\alpha}=\eta^{\mathrm{tscope},\alpha}_{\mathfrak{B}}(y)
,
and since
y\not\in U=(\eta^{\mathrm{tscope},\alpha}_{\mathfrak{B}})^{-1}(V_{1})
,

\alpha^{n}\cdot v\not\in V_{1}
, so
v\not\in V
.
\Box

4.4
The free semitopological cone over
{\mathbf{V}}_{\leq 1}X

Theorem 4.30

For every topological space
X
,
{\mathbf{V}}_{b}X
is the free cone over
the pointed barycentric algebra
{\mathbf{V}}_{\leq 1}X
(with
distinguished element the zero valuation), and
{\mathbf{V}}_{b}X
is the
free semitopological cone over the pointed semitopological
barycentric algebra
{\mathbf{V}}_{\leq 1}X
.

As a consequence,
{\mathbf{V}}_{b}X
and
\mathrm{tscope}_{\alpha}({\mathbf{V}}_{\leq 1}X)

are naturally isomorphic semitopological cones for every

\alpha\in{]0,1[}
.

Proof.

Let
f
be a linear map from
{\mathbf{V}}_{\leq 1}X
to a cone
\mathfrak{C}
. We
must show that it has a unique linear extension
\hat{f}
from

{\mathbf{V}}_{b}X
to
\mathfrak{C}
. Let
f_{1}
be the restriction of
f
to

{\mathbf{V}}_{1}X
: this is an affine map from
{\mathbf{V}}_{1}X
to
\mathfrak{C}
, and any
linear extension of
f
to
{\mathbf{V}}_{b}X
must also be one of
f_{1}
.
But such linear extensions are unique, by
Theorem
3.27
.

As for existence, we define
\hat{f}
as the extension
\hat{f}_{1}

obtained from
f_{1}
by using Theorem
3.27
. By this
theorem,
\hat{f}
is linear. Also,
\hat{f}
maps the zero valuation
to
0
and every non-zero element
\nu\in{\mathbf{V}}_{b}X
to

\nu(X)\cdot f(\frac{1}{\nu(X)}\cdot\nu)
; when

\nu\in{\mathbf{V}}_{\leq 1}X
, the latter is equal to
f(\nu)
since

f
is
\mathbb{I}
-homogeneous. Therefore
\hat{f}
extends
f
, not just

f_{1}
.

When
\mathfrak{C}
is a semitopological cone, the various spaces of
continuous valuations are given their weak topologies, and
f
is
continuous, then
f_{1}
is continuous, too, since
{\mathbf{V}}_{1}X
is a
topological subspace of
{\mathbf{V}}_{\leq 1}X
, and then

\hat{f}=\hat{f}_{1}
is continuous by Theorem
3.27
.
\Box

4.5
The free pointed barycentric algebra over a barycentric
algebra

There is a free
pointed
barycentric algebra over any
barycentric algebra, and it is very easily constructed.

Definition 4.31

For every barycentric algebra
\mathfrak{B}
, let
\mathrm{cone}_{\leq 1}(\mathfrak{B})
be
the subset of
\mathrm{cone}(\mathfrak{B})
consisting of
0
and the pairs

(r,x)
with
0<r\leq 1
; we take
0
as distinguished element.
If
\mathfrak{B}
is a preordered barycentric algebra,
\mathrm{cone}_{\leq 1}(\mathfrak{B})

is preordered by the restriction of the ordering
\leq^{\mathrm{cone}}
on

\mathrm{cone}(\mathfrak{B})
(see Definition
3.3
),
which makes
0
least. If
\mathfrak{B}
is a semitopological barycentric
algebra,
\mathrm{cone}_{\leq 1}(\mathfrak{B})
is given the subspace topology
induced by the inclusion in
\mathrm{cone}(\mathfrak{B})
with the cone topology
(see Definition
3.15
); this makes
0
least in the
specialization preordering.

We have already seen the following notion in
Example
3.24
.

Definition 4.32

Let
\mathfrak{B}
be a barycentric algebra. The
level

\ell(u)
of
an element
u\in\mathrm{cone}(\mathfrak{B})
is defined by
\ell(0)\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}0
,

\ell(r,x)\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}r
.

Hence
\mathrm{cone}_{\leq 1}(\mathfrak{B})
is the subset of
\mathrm{cone}(\mathfrak{B})

consisting of elements of level at most
1
. The following is
immediate from the definition of the algebraic operations on

\mathrm{cone}(\mathfrak{B})
(see Section
3.1
).

Fact 4.33

The level map
\ell
is linear:
\ell(u+v)=\ell(u)+\ell(v)
,
\ell(0)=0
,
\ell(a\cdot u)=a\,\ell(u)
.

We extend the notion of semi-concave map so that the codomain is a
pointed barycentric algebra, not necessarily a preordered cone, in the obvious
way.

Definition 4.34

A
semi-concave
map from a semitopological barycentric algebra

\mathfrak{B}
to a preordered pointed barycentric algebra
\mathfrak{A}
is a
function
h\colon\mathfrak{B}\to\mathfrak{A}
such that for all
x,y\in\mathfrak{B}
and

a\in[0,1]
,
h(x+_{a}y)\geq a\cdot h(x)
.

Theorem 4.35

For every barycentric algebra
\mathfrak{B}
(resp., preordered barycentric
algebra),

1.

\mathrm{cone}_{\leq 1}(\mathfrak{B})
is a pointed barycentric algebra
(resp., a preordered pointed barycentric algebra);

2.

the map
\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{B}}
is an injective affine map from
\mathfrak{B}

(resp., an injective affine preorder embedding) into

\mathrm{cone}_{\leq 1}(\mathfrak{B})
;

3.

for every map (resp., monotonic map)
f\colon\mathfrak{B}\to\mathfrak{A}
to
a pointed barycentric algebra
\mathfrak{A}
, there is a unique

\mathbb{I}
-homogeneous map

f^{\leftslice,\leq 1}\colon\mathrm{cone}_{\leq 1}(\mathfrak{B})\to\mathfrak{A}
such
that
f^{\leftslice,\leq 1}\circ\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{B}}=f
; if
f
is affine
(resp. concave, convex) then
f^{\leftslice,\leq 1}
is linear
(resp. superlinear, sublinear).

4.

In particular,
\mathrm{cone}_{\leq 1}(\mathfrak{B})
is the free pointed
barycentric algebra (resp., the free preordered pointed
barycentric algebra) over the barycentric algebra (resp., the
preordered barycentric algebra)
\mathfrak{B}
.

Proof.

1. For every
a\in[0,1]
, for all
u,v\in\mathrm{cone}_{\leq 1}(\mathfrak{B})
, the levels
\ell(u)
and
\ell(v)
are less than or equal
to
1
. Using the linearity of
\ell
,
\ell(u+_{a}v)=a\,\ell(u)+(1-a)\,\ell(v)\leq 1
, so
u+_{a}\in\mathrm{cone}_{\leq 1}(\mathfrak{B})
. Hence
\mathrm{cone}_{\leq 1}(\mathfrak{B})
is convex. By
Lemma
3.8
,
\mathrm{cone}_{\leq 1}(\mathfrak{B})
is a (resp. preordered) barycentric algebra.

When
\mathfrak{B}
is preordered, the distinguished element
0
is least, so

\mathrm{cone}_{\leq 1}(\mathfrak{B})
is a preordered pointed barycentric algebra.

2. For every
x\in\mathfrak{B}
,
\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{B}}(x)=(1,x)
is in

\mathrm{cone}_{\leq 1}(\mathfrak{B})
. The map
\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{B}}
is injective and
affine. If
\mathfrak{B}
is preordered, then
\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{B}}
is also a preorder
embedding by Proposition
3.4
, item 2.

3. Let
f\colon\mathfrak{B}\to\mathfrak{A}
be semi-concave (resp., and monotonic). If

f^{\leftslice,\leq 1}
exists, then it must map
0
to
\bot
, since
every
\mathbb{I}
-homogeneous map is strict (see
Proposition
4.6
). For every
r\in{]0,1]}
, for every

x\in\mathfrak{B}
, we must also have

f^{\leftslice,\leq 1}(r,x)=f^{\leftslice,\leq 1}(r\cdot(1,x))=r\cdot f(x)
since
f^{\leftslice,\leq 1}
is
\mathbb{I}
-homogeneous and

f^{\leftslice,\leq 1}\circ\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{B}}=\mathrm{id}_{\mathfrak{B}}
. This shows
uniqueness.

As for existence, we simply define
f^{\leftslice,\leq 1}
just like

f^{\leftslice}
: it maps
0
to the least element
\bot
of
\mathfrak{A}
and

(r,x)
to
r\cdot f(x)
for all
r\in{]0,1]}
and
x\in\mathfrak{B}
.
Let us check that
f^{\leftslice,\leq 1}
is
\mathbb{I}
-homogeneous. For
every
a\in[0,1]
, either
a=0
and

f^{\leftslice,\leq 1}(a\cdot(r,x))=f^{\leftslice,\leq 1}(0)=\bot
,
or
a>0
and

f^{\leftslice,\leq 1}(a\cdot(r,x))=f^{\leftslice,\leq 1}(ar,x)=ar\cdot f(x)=a\cdot(r\cdot f(x))
(by the second equality of
Lemma
4.3
)

=a\cdot f^{\leftslice,\leq 1}(r,x)
.

If
f
is concave, we verify that
f^{\leftslice,\leq 1}
is concave,
too, hence superlinear. Let
a\in[0,1]
. The inequality

f^{\leftslice,\leq 1}(u+_{a}v)\geq f^{\leftslice,\leq 1}(u)+_{a}f^{\leftslice,\leq 1}(v)
is obvious (and is an equality) if
a=0
or
if
a=1
, so we assume that
0<a<1
. If
u=0
, the inequality
is clear (and is also an equality), too, and similarly if
v=0
.
Hence let us write
u
as
(r,x)
and
v
as
(s,y)
, with

0<r,s\leq 1
and
x,y\in\mathfrak{B}
. Then:

\displaystyle f^{\leftslice,\leq 1}(u+_{a}v)

\displaystyle=f^{\leftslice,\leq 1}(ar+(1-a)s,x+_{\frac{ar}{ar+(1-a)s}}y)

\displaystyle=(ar+(1-a)s)\cdot f(x+_{\frac{ar}{ar+(1-a)s}}y)

\displaystyle\geq(ar+(1-a)s)\cdot(f(x)+_{\frac{ar}{ar+(1-a)s}}f(y))

since
f
is concave. We claim that the latter is equal to

f^{\leftslice,\leq 1}(u)+_{a}f^{\leftslice,\leq 1}(v)
. We have:

\displaystyle f^{\leftslice,\leq 1}(u)+_{a}f^{\leftslice,\leq 1}(v)

\displaystyle=(r\cdot f(x))+_{a}(s\cdot f(y))

We recall the formula
c\cdot z=z+_{c}\bot
defining
\cdot
in

\mathfrak{A}
. In order to reach our goal, we embed
\mathfrak{A}
in a cone

\mathfrak{C}\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\mathrm{cone}(\mathfrak{A})
through
\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{A}}
, using
Theorem
3.22
. We write
cz
for the scalar
multiplication of
c
by
z
in
\mathrm{cone}(\mathfrak{A})
, in order not to
confuse it with
c\cdot z
. Letting

x^{\prime}\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{A}}(f(x))
and

y^{\prime}\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{A}}(f(y))
, it remains to verify that

(x^{\prime}+_{\frac{ar}{ar+(1-a)s}}y^{\prime})+_{ar+(1-a)s}\bot=(x^{\prime}+_{r}\bot)+_{a}(y^{\prime}+_{s}\bot)
:

\displaystyle(x^{\prime}+_{\frac{ar}{ar+(1-a)s}}y^{\prime})+_{ar+(1-a)s}\bot

\displaystyle=(ar+(1-a)s)(\frac{ar}{ar+(1-a)s}x^{\prime}+\frac{(1-a)s}{ar+(1-a)s}y^{\prime})

\displaystyle\qquad+(1-(ar+(1-a)s))\bot

\displaystyle=arx^{\prime}+(1-a)sy^{\prime}+(1-(ar+(1-a)s))\bot

while:

\displaystyle(x^{\prime}+_{r}\bot)+_{a}(y^{\prime}+_{s}\bot)

\displaystyle=a(rx^{\prime}+(1-r)\bot)+(1-a)(sy^{\prime}+(1-s)\bot)

\displaystyle=arx^{\prime}+(1-a)sy^{\prime}+(a(1-r)+(1-a)(1-s))\bot,

and those are indeed equal since

a(1-r)+(1-a)(1-s)=1-(ar+(1-a)s)
, as one easily checks.
The fact that
f^{\leftslice,\leq 1}
is sublinear if
f
is convex, and
linear if
f
is affine is proved similarly, replacing
\geq
by

\leq
, or by
=
.

In case
\mathfrak{B}
is preordered, we show that
f^{\leftslice,\leq 1}
is
monotonic, assuming that
f
is. Let
u\leq v
in

\mathrm{cone}_{\leq 1}(\mathfrak{B})
. If
u=0
, then

f^{\leftslice,\leq 1}(u)=\bot\leq f^{\leftslice,\leq 1}(v)
. Hence we
may assume that
u
is of the form
(r,x)
with
0<r\leq 1
and

x\in\mathfrak{B}
,
v
is of the form
(s,y)
with
0<s\leq 1
and

y\in\mathfrak{B}
,
u+u^{\prime}=(s,y_{1})
for some
u^{\prime}\in\mathrm{cone}(\mathfrak{B})
(not
just
\mathrm{cone}_{\leq 1}(\mathfrak{B})
, although a quick check shows that
necessarily such a
u^{\prime}
must in fact lie in
\mathrm{cone}_{\leq 1}(\mathfrak{B})
)
and
y_{1}\leq y
in
\mathfrak{B}
. Then

f^{\leftslice,\leq 1}(u)=r\cdot f(x)
. If
u^{\prime}=0
, then

(s,y_{1})=(r,x)
, so
x=y_{1}\leq y
and

r\cdot f(x)\leq r\cdot f(y)=s\cdot f(y)
(since
r=s
)

=f^{\leftslice,\leq 1}(v)
. Otherwise,
u^{\prime}
is of the form

(r^{\prime},x^{\prime})
with
r^{\prime}>0
and
x^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{B}
, and

(s,y_{1})=(r+r^{\prime},x+_{\frac{r}{r+r^{\prime}}}x^{\prime})
. Then

f^{\leftslice,\leq 1}(v)=s\cdot f(y)\geq s\cdot f(y_{1})
(since

f
is monotonic)

=(r+r^{\prime})\cdot f(x+_{\frac{r}{r+r^{\prime}}}x^{\prime})\geq(r+r^{\prime})\cdot\frac{r}{r+r^{\prime}}\cdot f(x)
(since
f
is semi-concave)
=r\cdot f(x)

(by the second equation of Lemma
4.3
)

=f^{\leftslice,\leq 1}(u)
.

4. Trivial consequence of item 3.
\Box

We have a similar result with pointed semitopological barycentric
algebras, except that
\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{B}}
is not necessarily a topological
embedding; we will see that
\mathfrak{B}^{-}

(Example
3.24
) is a counterexample in
Example
4.39
.

Theorem 4.36

For every semitopological barycentric algebra
\mathfrak{B}
,

1.

\mathrm{cone}_{\leq 1}(\mathfrak{B})
is a pointed semitopological
barycentric algebra;

2.

its proper open subsets are the upwards-closed subsets of

{]0,1]}_{\sigma}\times\mathfrak{B}
, the latter being given the product
topology, and where upwards-closed is with respect to the
preordering
\leq^{\mathrm{cone}}
on
\mathrm{cone}(\mathfrak{B})
, restricted to

\mathrm{cone}_{\leq 1}(\mathfrak{B})
;

3.

the map
\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{B}}
is an injective affine continuous map
from
\mathfrak{B}
into
\mathrm{cone}_{\leq 1}(\mathfrak{B})
;

4.

for every semi-concave (resp., concave, affine) continuous map

f\colon\mathfrak{B}\to\mathfrak{A}
to a pointed semitopological barycentric
algebra
\mathfrak{A}
, there is a unique
\mathbb{I}
-homogeneous (resp.,
superlinear, linear) continuous map

f^{\leftslice,\leq 1}\colon\mathrm{cone}_{\leq 1}(\mathfrak{B})\to\mathfrak{A}
such
that
f^{\leftslice,\leq 1}\circ\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{B}}=f
.

In particular,
\mathrm{cone}_{\leq 1}(\mathfrak{B})
with the cone topology is
the free pointed semitopological barycentric algebras
\mathfrak{B}
.

5.

The following are equivalent:

(a)

\mathfrak{B}
embeds in a pointed semitopological algebra
\mathfrak{A}

through an affine continuous map;

(b)

\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{B}}\colon\mathfrak{B}\to\mathrm{cone}_{\leq 1}(\mathfrak{B})
is a
topological embedding;

(c)

\mathfrak{B}
is embeddable (in the sense of
Definition
3.14
, namely in a
semitopological cone).

Proof.

1. Just as in the proof of Theorem
4.35
,

\mathrm{cone}_{\leq 1}(\mathfrak{B})
is a convex subset of
\mathrm{cone}(\mathfrak{B})
with a
least element, and we conclude by Lemma
3.8
.

2. Let us agree to say “upwards-closed” for upwards-closed with
respect to the preordering on
\mathrm{cone}(\mathfrak{B})
, or its restriction to

\mathrm{cone}_{\leq 1}(\mathfrak{B})
—not the componentwise preordering on
(\mathbb{R}_{+}\smallsetminus\{0\})\times\mathfrak{B}
.

The proper open subsets of
\mathrm{cone}(\mathfrak{B})
are the upwards-closed
subsets of
(\mathbb{R}_{+}\smallsetminus\{0\})\times\mathfrak{B}
, where the latter is given
the product topology. Given such an open set
U
,

U\cap\mathrm{cone}_{\leq 1}(\mathfrak{B})
is upwards-closed in

\mathrm{cone}_{\leq 1}(\mathfrak{B})
, and is a union of sets of the form

({]t,\infty[}\times U)\cap\mathfrak{B}
where
t>0
and
U
is open in

\mathfrak{B}
. The latter are just sets of the form
{]t,1]}\times U
if

t<1
and empty otherwise, and they are all open in

{]0,1]}_{\sigma}\times\mathfrak{B}
.

Conversely, let
W
be an upwards-closed open subset of

{]0,1]}_{\sigma}\times\mathfrak{B}
, still in the preordering on

\mathrm{cone}_{\leq 1}(\mathfrak{B})
. We write
W
as

\bigcup_{i\in I}({]t_{i},1]}\times U_{i})
where
0<t_{i}<1
and

U_{i}
is open in
\mathfrak{B}
. Let

W^{\prime}\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\bigcup_{i\in I}({]t_{i},\infty[}\times U_{i})\cup({]1,\infty[}\times\mathfrak{B})
.
W^{\prime}
is open in

(\mathbb{R}_{+}\smallsetminus\{0\})_{\sigma}\times\mathfrak{B}
and

W^{\prime}\cap\mathrm{cone}_{\leq 1}(\mathfrak{B})=W
. Let us verify that
W^{\prime}
is
upwards-closed in
\mathrm{cone}(\mathfrak{B})
. Let
u
be any point in
W^{\prime}
and

v
be above
u
in
\mathrm{cone}(\mathfrak{B})
. Since
u\neq 0
, we write
u

as
(r,x)
with
r>0
and
x\in\mathfrak{B}
. Since
u\leq^{\mathrm{cone}}v
,
v
must
be of the form
(s,y)
, and there must be a point

u^{\prime}\in\mathrm{cone}(\mathfrak{B})
and an element
y_{1}\in\mathfrak{B}
such that

u+u^{\prime}=(s,y_{1})
and
y_{1}\leq y
. If
s>1
, then

v\in{]1,\infty[}\times\mathfrak{B}\subseteq W^{\prime}
, so let us assume

s\leq 1
. Then, since
u+u^{\prime}=(r,x)+u^{\prime}=(s,y_{1})
, in
particular
r\leq s\leq 1
. The fact that
u\in W^{\prime}
and the
definition of
W^{\prime}
implies that
u\in{]t_{i},\infty[}\times U_{i}

for some
i\in I
; therefore
u
is in
W
. Since
W
is
upwards-closed in
\mathrm{cone}_{\leq 1}(\mathfrak{B})
,
u\leq^{\mathrm{cone}}v
, and since
s\leq 1
hence
v\in\mathrm{cone}_{\leq 1}(\mathfrak{B})
,
v
is also in
W
.
It is clear that
W\subseteq W^{\prime}
, so
v
is in
W^{\prime}
.

3. For every
x\in\mathfrak{B}
,
\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{B}}(x)=(1,x)
is in

\mathrm{cone}_{\leq 1}(\mathfrak{B})
. The remaining claims are from
Theorem
3.22
, item 2.

4. Let
f\colon\mathfrak{B}\to\mathfrak{A}
be semi-concave and continuous.
Uniqueness is by Theorem
4.35
, item 3, considering

\mathfrak{B}
as a preordered cone with its specialization preordering. We
turn to existence. We have to define
f^{\leftslice,\leq 1}
as in
Theorem
4.35
(whence the same notation): it maps

0
to the least element
\bot
of
\mathfrak{A}
and
(r,x)
to

r\cdot f(x)
for all
r\in{]0,1]}
and
x\in\mathfrak{B}
.

It remains to show that
f^{\leftslice,\leq 1}
is continuous, and this
is done as in the proof of Theorem
3.22
, item 3.
As in item 2, “upwards-closed” will mean upwards-closed in the
preordering on
\mathrm{cone}(\mathfrak{B})
or its restriction to
\mathrm{cone}_{\leq 1}(\mathfrak{B})
.

Let
V
be an open subset of
\mathfrak{A}
. If
\bot\in V
, then

V=\mathfrak{A}
since
\bot
is least in
\mathfrak{A}
, and then

(f^{\leftslice,\leq 1})^{-1}(V)=\mathrm{cone}_{\leq 1}(\mathfrak{B})
is open.
Hence we assume that
\bot\not\in V
. Then

(f^{\leftslice,\leq 1})^{-1}(V)
does not contain
0
, and we show
that
(f^{\leftslice,\leq 1})^{-1}(V)
is open in

{]0,1]}_{\sigma}\times\mathfrak{B}
and upwards-closed in

\mathrm{cone}_{\leq 1}(\mathfrak{B})
, relying on item 2 above.

For every
u\in(f^{\leftslice,\leq 1})^{-1}(V)
, for every

v\in\mathrm{cone}_{\leq 1}(\mathfrak{B})
such that
u\leq^{\mathrm{cone}}v
, we verify that

v\in(f^{\leftslice,\leq 1})^{-1}(V)
. Since
u\neq 0
,
v
is also
different from
0
, and we write
v
as
(s,y)
with
0<s\leq 1

and
y\in\mathfrak{B}
. There is a point
u^{\prime}\in\mathrm{cone}(\mathfrak{B})
and an
element
y_{1}\in\mathfrak{B}
such that
u+u^{\prime}=(s,y_{1})
and
y_{1}\leq y
.
(Yes, we do take the sum
u+u^{\prime}
in
\mathrm{cone}(\mathfrak{B})
, and we expect it
to yield
(s,y_{1})
, which is in
\mathrm{cone}_{\leq 1}(\mathfrak{B})
.) Then

f^{\leftslice,\leq 1}(v)=s\cdot f(y)\geq s\cdot f(y_{1})
(since

f
, being continuous, is monotonic)

=f^{\leftslice,\leq 1}(u+u^{\prime})\geq f^{\leftslice,\leq 1}(u)
(since
f

is monotonic and
u+u^{\prime}\geq u
by Remark
3.5
).
Since
V
is upwards-closed,
f^{\leftslice,\leq 1}(v)\in V
.
Therefore
v\in(f^{\leftslice,\leq 1})^{-1}(V)
.

Next, we check that
(f^{\leftslice,\leq 1})^{-1}(V)
is open in

{]0,1]}_{\sigma}\times\mathfrak{B}
. The restriction of
f^{\leftslice,\leq 1}

to
{]0,1]}_{\sigma}\times\mathfrak{B}
is the function

(r,x)\mapsto r\cdot f(x)
. This is Scott-continuous in
r
and
continuous in
x
, since
f
is continuous. We note that
]0,1]

is a continuous dcpo, whose way-below relation is
<
. By Ershov’s
observation, this restriction of
f^{\leftslice,\leq 1}
is jointly
continuous from
{]0,1]}_{\sigma}\times\mathfrak{B}
to
\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
, so

(f^{\leftslice,\leq 1})^{-1}(V)
is open in

{]0,1]}_{\sigma}\times\mathfrak{B}
with the product topology.

5.
(a)\mathrel{\Rightarrow}(b)
. Let
i\colon\mathfrak{B}\to\mathfrak{A}
be an affine
topological embedding in a pointed semitopological algebra
\mathfrak{A}
.
By item 4,
i^{\leftslice,\leq 1}
is a linear continuous map from

\mathrm{cone}_{\leq 1}(\mathfrak{B})
to
\mathfrak{A}
. For every open subset
U
of

\mathfrak{B}
, there is an open subset
V
of
\mathfrak{A}
such that

U=i^{-1}(V)
, since
i
is a topological embedding. Using the
fact that
i=i^{\leftslice,\leq 1}\circ\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{B}}
,
U
is the
inverse image of the open set
(i^{\leftslice,\leq 1})^{-1}(V)
under

\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{B}}
. Hence
\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{B}}
is full, and therefore a
topological embedding.

(b)\mathrel{\Rightarrow}(c)
. The inclusion map from
\mathrm{cone}_{\leq 1}(\mathfrak{B})

into
\mathrm{cone}(\mathfrak{B})
is an affine topological embedding by
definition. Composing it with

\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{B}}\colon\mathfrak{B}\to\mathrm{cone}_{\leq 1}(\mathfrak{B})
, we obtain a
topological embedding of
\mathfrak{B}
in
\mathrm{cone}(\mathfrak{B})
, which is affine
by item 3.

Finally,
(c)\mathrel{\Rightarrow}(a)
is obvious, since every semitopological
cone is a pointed semitopological algebra, in particular.
\Box

This leads to the following, perhaps surprising, result.

Corollary 4.37

Every pointed semitopological barycentric algebra is embeddable.

Proof.

Let
\mathfrak{B}
be a pointed semitopological barycentric algebra. The
identity map is an affine topological embedding. By the

(a)\mathrel{\Rightarrow}(c)
implication of
Theorem
4.36
, item 5,
\mathfrak{B}
is embeddable.
\Box

Corollary
4.37
does
not
say that
every pointed semitopological barycentric algebra
\mathfrak{B}
embeds through
an affine topological embedding that preserves bottom elements (a
strict affine, or linear, map). And indeed, the embedding obtained in
the proof, namely
\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{B}}
, maps
\bot
to
(1,\bot)
, not to
0
.

Remark 4.38

For every topological barycentric algebra
\mathfrak{B}
,

\mathrm{cone}_{\leq 1}(\mathfrak{B})
is a pointed topological, not just pointed
semitopological barycentric algebra. Indeed,
\mathrm{cone}(\mathfrak{B})
is a
topological cone in this case by Theorem
3.25
,
and
\mathrm{cone}_{\leq 1}(\mathfrak{B})
embeds through the identity map, namely
is a convex subspace of
\mathrm{cone}(\mathfrak{B})
; we conclude by
Lemma
3.8
.

Example 4.39

There is no affine topological embedding of the topological
barycentric algebra
\mathfrak{B}^{-}
described in
Example
3.24
in any pointed semitopological
barycentric algebra. Indeed, by
Theorem
4.36
, item 5,
\mathfrak{B}^{-}
embeds in a
pointed semitopological barycentric algebra if and only if it is
embeddable. But
\mathfrak{B}^{-}
is not embeddable, as we have seen in
Example
3.24
.

4.6
The free pointed barycentric algebra over
{\mathbf{V}}_{1}X

Applying the
\mathrm{cone}_{\leq 1}
construction to
{\mathbf{V}}_{1}X
gives

{\mathbf{V}}_{\leq 1}
, up to isomorphism, thanks to the following result.

Theorem 4.40

For every topological space
X
,
{\mathbf{V}}_{\leq 1}X
, with
distinguished element the zero valuation, is the free pointed
barycentric algebra over the barycentric algebra
{\mathbf{V}}_{1}X
, and

{\mathbf{V}}_{\leq 1}X
is the free pointed semitopological barycentric
algebra over the semitopological barycentric algebra

{\mathbf{V}}_{1}X
.

As a consequence,
{\mathbf{V}}_{\leq 1}X
and

\mathrm{cone}_{\leq 1}({\mathbf{V}}_{1}X)
are naturally isomorphic
semitopological cones.

Proof.

Let
f
be an affine map from
{\mathbf{V}}_{1}X
to a pointed barycentric
algebra
\mathfrak{B}
. We need to show that
f
extends to a unique linear
map
\hat{f}
from
{\mathbf{V}}_{\leq 1}X
to
\mathfrak{B}
, which is continuous if

f
is (assuming
\mathfrak{B}
semitopological, and giving
{\mathbf{V}}_{1}X
and

{\mathbf{V}}_{\leq 1}X
their weak topologies.)

Uniqueness is proved as in Theorem
3.27
: if
\hat{f}

exists, then it must map the zero valuation to
\bot
and every
non-zero element
\nu
of
{\mathbf{V}}_{\leq 1}X
to

\nu(X)\cdot f(\frac{1}{\nu(X)}\cdot\nu)
, using the fact that

f
is
\mathbb{I}
-homogenous.

We therefore define
\hat{f}
as mapping the zero valuation to
\bot

and every non-zero element
\nu
of
{\mathbf{V}}_{\leq 1}X
to

\nu(X)\cdot f(\frac{1}{\nu(X)}\cdot\nu)
. By definition,

\hat{f}
is strict. In order to see that
\hat{f}
is linear, it
suffices to show that it is affine, thanks to
Proposition
4.6
. We observe that

g\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{B}}\circ f
is an affine map from
{\mathbf{V}}_{1}X
to

\mathrm{cone}(\mathfrak{B})
, because
\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{B}}
is affine. By
Theorem
3.27
,
g
extends to a unique linear map

\hat{g}
from
{\mathbf{V}}_{b}X
to
\mathrm{cone}(\mathfrak{B})
, and then we note that

\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{B}}\circ\hat{f}=\hat{g}
. Indeed, by the uniqueness of

\hat{g}
, this follows from the fact that
\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{B}}\circ\hat{f}

coincides with
g
on
{\mathbf{V}}_{1}X
, a consequence of the fact that

\hat{f}
coincides with
f
on
{\mathbf{V}}_{1}X
. Then, for all

\mu,\nu\in{\mathbf{V}}_{\leq 1}X
and for every
a\in[0,1]
,

\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{B}}(\hat{f}(\mu+_{a}\nu))=\hat{g}(\mu+_{a}\nu)=\hat{g}(\mu)+_{a}\hat{g}(\nu)
(since
\hat{g}
is linear, hence affine)

=\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{B}}(\hat{f}(\mu))+_{a}\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{B}}(\hat{f}(\nu))=\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{B}}(\hat{f}(\mu)+_{a}\hat{f}(\nu))
(since
\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{B}}
is
affine). Since
\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{B}}
is injective,

\hat{f}(\mu+_{a}\nu)=\hat{f}(\mu)+_{a}\hat{f}(\nu)
, showing that

\hat{f}
is affine.

When
\mathfrak{B}
is a pointed semitopological barycentric algebra,

{\mathbf{V}}_{1}X
and
{\mathbf{V}}_{b}X
are given their weak topologies, and
f

is continuous,
\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{B}}
is injective, affine and continuous by
Theorem
3.22
, item 2. In particular,
g
is
continuous, so
\hat{g}
is continuous by Theorem
3.27
.
In other words,
\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{B}}\circ f
is continuous. By
Corollary
4.37
,
\mathfrak{B}
is embeddable, and
by the equivalence between (a) and (b) in
Theorem
3.22
, item 4,
\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{B}}
is a
topological embedding. The continuity of
f
then follows from that
of
\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{B}}\circ f
.
\Box

4.7
Barycenters, part 3

In pointed barycentric algebras, there is an extended notion of
barycenters. The following is an analogue of Definition and
Proposition
3.1
. It would be tempting to define

\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\cdot x_{i}
, where
\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}
is now less
than or equal to
1
, not equal to
1
, by saying something like: “it
is the only point
x
that is mapped by
\eta^{\mathrm{tscope},\alpha}_{\mathfrak{B}}
to

\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\cdot\eta^{\mathrm{tscope},\alpha}_{\mathfrak{B}}(x_{i})
”. That would be wrong,
since
\eta^{\mathrm{tscope},\alpha}_{\mathfrak{B}}
is not injective in general. Hence we give an ad
hoc definition, and then we verify a property in the spirit of the
latter sentence, but relying on
\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{B}}
instead of
\eta^{\mathrm{tscope},\alpha}_{\mathfrak{B}}

(item 3).

Def. and Proposition 4.41

For every
n\in\mathbb{N}
, let

\widetilde{\Delta}_{n}\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\{(a_{1},\cdots,a_{n})\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{n}\mid\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\leq 1\}
.

Let
\mathfrak{B}
be a pointed barycentric algebra. For every
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
for all points
x_{1}
, …,
x_{n}
of
\mathfrak{B}
and for every

(a_{1},\cdots,a_{n})\in\widetilde{\Delta}_{n}
, there is a point of

\mathfrak{B}
, which we write as
\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\cdot x_{i}
, and defined as

(\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i})\cdot\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{a_{i}}{\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}}\cdot x_{i}
(where the latter sum is defined in
Proposition
3.1
) when
\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\neq 0
(in
particular
n\geq 1
), and to
\bot
otherwise. We call it the

barycenter
of the points
x_{i}
with weights
a_{i}
. Then:

1.

this construction is invariant under permutations: for every
bijection
\sigma
of
\{1,\cdots,n\}
onto itself,

\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\cdot x_{i}=\sum_{j=1}^{n}a_{\sigma(i)}\cdot x_{\sigma(i)}
;

2.

for every linear function
f
from
\mathfrak{B}
to a pointed
barycentric algebra
\mathfrak{A}
,

f(\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\cdot x_{i})=\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\cdot f(x_{i})
;

3.

\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\cdot x_{i}
is the only point
x
of
\mathfrak{B}

such that

\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{B}}(x)=\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\cdot\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{B}}(x_{i})+(1-\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i})\cdot\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{B}}(\bot)
.

Proof.

Items 1 and 2 are immediate consequences of Definition and
Proposition
3.1
. As for item 3, let

a\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}
, so that
a_{0}=1-a
. We recall that

\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{B}}
is affine and injective. Injectivity entails the
uniqueness claim of item 3. Let

x\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\cdot x_{i}
, as defined in the
proposition. If
a\neq 0
, then:

\displaystyle\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{B}}(x)

\displaystyle=\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{B}}(a\cdot\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{a_{i}}{a}\cdot x_{i})

\displaystyle=\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{B}}(\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{a_{i}}{a}\cdot x_{i}+_{a}\bot)

by definition of
\cdot
on
\mathfrak{B}

\displaystyle=a\cdot\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{B}}(\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{a_{i}}{a}\cdot x_{i})+(1-a)\cdot\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{B}}(\bot)

since
\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{B}}
is affine

\displaystyle=a\cdot\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{a_{i}}{a}\cdot\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{B}}(x_{i})+(1-a)\cdot\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{B}}(\bot)

by Def. and Prop.
3.1

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\cdot\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{B}}(x_{i})+(1-a)\cdot\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{B}}(\bot).

If
a=0
, then
\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{B}}(x)=\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{B}}(\bot)=\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\cdot\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{B}}(x_{i})+(1-a)\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{B}}(\bot)
,
since
a_{i}=0
for every
i\in\{1,\cdots,n\}
.
\Box

Lemma 4.42

Let
\mathfrak{B}
be a preordered pointed barycentric algebra, and
x_{1}
,
…,
x_{n}
be finitely many points of
\mathfrak{B}
. The function

(a_{1},\cdots,a_{n})\mapsto\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\cdot x_{i}
is monotonic
from
\widetilde{\Delta}_{n}
, with the pointwise ordering, to
\mathfrak{B}
.

Proof.

This is clear if
n=0
, so let us assume
n\geq 1
. We claim that
this function is monotonic in

a_{n}\in[0,1-\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}a_{i}]
. By invariance under
permutations (Definition and Proposition
4.41
,
item 1), it will be monotonic in every
a_{i}
. Let

f(a_{n})\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\cdot x_{i}
, understanding that

a_{1}
, …,
a_{n-1}
,
x_{1}
, …,
x_{n}
are fixed. We wish
to show that
f
is monotonic. Since
\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{B}}
is a preorder
embedding in the preordered cone
\mathrm{cone}(\mathfrak{B})

(Proposition
3.4
), it suffices to show
that
\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{B}}\circ f
is monotonic. Now, and using
\cdot
for
scalar multiplication in
\mathfrak{B}
and
.
for scalar multiplication in

\mathrm{cone}(\mathfrak{B})
,

\displaystyle\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{B}}(f(a_{n}))

\displaystyle=\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{B}}\left((\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i})\cdot\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{a_{i}}{\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}}\cdot x_{i}\right)

\displaystyle=\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{B}}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{a_{i}}{\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}}\cdot x_{i}+_{\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}}\bot\right)

by definition of
\cdot

\displaystyle=(\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}).\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{B}}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{a_{i}}{\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}}\cdot x_{i}\right)+(1-\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}).\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{B}}(\bot)

since
\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{B}}
is affine

\displaystyle=(\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}).\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{a_{i}}{\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}}.\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{B}}(x_{i})+(1-\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i})\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{B}}(\bot)

by Def. and Prop.
3.1

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}.\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{B}}(x_{i})+(1-\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i})\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{B}}(\bot)

\displaystyle=y+a_{n}.\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{B}}(x_{n})+(A-a_{n}).\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{B}}(\bot),

where
y\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}a_{i}.\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{B}}(x_{i})
and

A\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}1-\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}a_{i}
are independent of
a_{n}
.
Hence, for all
a,a^{\prime}\in[0,1-\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}a_{i}]
such that

a\leq a^{\prime}
,

\displaystyle\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{B}}(f(a^{\prime}))

\displaystyle=y+(a^{\prime}-a).\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{B}}(x_{n})+a.\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{B}}(x_{n})

\displaystyle\qquad+(A-a^{\prime}).\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{B}}(\bot)

\displaystyle\geq y+(a^{\prime}-a).\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{B}}(\bot)+a.\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{B}}(x_{n})

\displaystyle\qquad+(A-a^{\prime}).\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{B}}(\bot),

since
\bot\leq x_{n}
and
\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{B}}
and
(a^{\prime}-a).\_
are
monotonic; and that is equal to

y+a.\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{B}}(x_{n})+(A-a).\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{B}}(\bot)=\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{B}}(f(a))
.
\Box

We topologize
\widetilde{\Delta}_{n}
with the subspace topology induced
by the inclusion in
(\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+})^{n}
; we remember that
\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
has the
Scott topology of its usual ordering. Since
\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
is a continuous
dcpo,
(\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+})^{n}
is also a continuous dcpo, and the product topology
on
\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}^{n}
coincides with the Scott topology of the componentwise
ordering
[
19
, Proposition 5.1.54]
. It is easy to see
that
\widetilde{\Delta}_{n}
is Scott-closed in
\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}^{n}
. It follows
that the topology on
\widetilde{\Delta}_{n}
is also the Scott topology
of the componentwise ordering (the Scott topology on a Scott-closed
subset of a continuous dcpo coincides with the subspace topology

[
19
, Exercise 5.1.52]
).

Proposition 4.43

Let
\mathfrak{B}
be a pointed semitopological (resp. topological)
barycentric algebra. For every
n\in\mathbb{N}
, the function

(a_{1},\cdots,a_{n},x_{1},\cdots,x_{n})\mapsto\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\cdot x_{i}
is separately (resp. jointly) continuous from

\widetilde{\Delta}_{n}\times\mathfrak{B}\times\cdots\times\mathfrak{B}
to
\mathfrak{B}
.

Let
f
be this function. The semitopological case needs some
clarification: in this case, Proposition
4.43

says that

f(a_{1},\cdots,\allowbreak a_{n},\allowbreak x_{1},\cdots,x_{n})
is
separately continuous in the tuple
(a_{1},\cdots,a_{n})
(jointly in

(a_{1},\allowbreak\cdots,\allowbreak a_{n})\in\widetilde{\Delta}_{n}
,
hence also separately in each
a_{i}
) and in the points
x_{1}
, …,

x_{n}
.

Proof.

This is obvious if
n=0
, so we assume
n\geq 1
in the sequel. By
Corollary
4.37
,
\mathfrak{B}
is embeddable. By
Theorem
3.22
, item 4, we may take
\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{B}}

as an affine topological embedding in the semitopological cone

\mathfrak{C}\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\mathrm{cone}(\mathfrak{B})
. Beware that
\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{B}}
is not linear in
general, equivalently not
\mathbb{I}
-homogeneous: we will keep the notation

\cdot
for scalar multiplication in
\mathfrak{B}
, and we will use
.
for
scalar multiplication in
\mathfrak{C}
, and we will remember that

\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{B}}(a\cdot x)\neq a.\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{B}}(x)
in general. For
all
(a_{1},\cdots,a_{n})\in\widetilde{\Delta}_{n}
and

x_{1},\cdots,x_{n}\in\mathfrak{B}
,

\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{B}}(f(a_{1},\cdots,a_{n},x_{1},\cdots,\allowbreak x_{n}))
is
equal to
\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{B}}(\bot)
if
a_{1}=\cdots=a_{n}=0
. Otherwise,

\displaystyle\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{B}}(f(a_{1},\cdots,a_{n},x_{1},\cdots,x_{n}))

\displaystyle=\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{B}}\left((\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i})\cdot\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{a_{i}}{\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}}\cdot x_{i}\right)

by Def. and Prop.
4.41

\displaystyle=\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{B}}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{a_{i}}{\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}}\cdot x_{i}+_{\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}}\bot\right)

by definition of
\cdot

\displaystyle=(\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}).\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{B}}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{a_{i}}{\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}}\cdot x_{i}\right)+(1-\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}).\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{B}}(\bot)

since
\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{B}}
is affine

\displaystyle=(\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}).\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{a_{i}}{\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}}.\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{B}}(x_{i})+(1-\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}).\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{B}}(\bot)

by Def. and Prop.
3.1

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}.\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{B}}(x_{i})+(1-\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}).\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{B}}(\bot).

The equality of the first and last terms continues to hold when

a_{1}=\cdots=a_{n}=0
. This formula shows that
\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{B}}\circ f
is
separately continuous in
x_{1}
, …,
x_{n}
, and jointly
continuous in
x_{1}
, …,
x_{n}
if
\mathfrak{B}
is topological.

Fixing
a_{1}
, …,
a_{n-1}
,
x_{1}
, …,
x_{n}
,

\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{B}}(f(a_{1},\cdots,a_{n},x_{1},\cdots,x_{n}))
is a function

g(a_{n})
of
a_{n}
, which we can write as

y+a_{n}.\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{B}}(x_{n})+(A-a_{n}).\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{B}}(\bot)
, where

y\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}a_{i}.\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{B}}(x_{i})
and

A\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}1-\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}a_{i}
;
a_{n}
varies in
[0,A]
. If

A=0
,
g
is vacuously continuous. Otherwise,

g(a_{n})=y+A.(\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{B}}(x_{n})+_{a_{n}/A}\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{B}}(\bot))
,
and that is continuous in
a_{n}\in[0,A]
(with its usual metric
topology) since every cone is semitopological as a preordered
barycentric algebra (see Lemma
3.8
).
Additionally,
g
is monotonic, since

f(a_{1},\cdots,a_{n},x_{1},\cdots,x_{n})
is monotonic in

(a_{1},\cdots,a_{n})
, by Lemma
4.42
, and since

\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{B}}
is continuous hence monotonic. It follows that
g
is
continuous from
[0,A]_{\sigma}
to
\mathfrak{C}
: for every open subset
V

of
\mathfrak{C}
,
g^{-1}(V)
is an upwards-closed, open subset of
[0,A]
,
hence a Scott-open subset of
[0,A]
.

We now fix
x_{1}
, …,
x_{n}
only, and we consider the function

h\colon(a_{1},\cdots,a_{n})\mapsto\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{B}}(f(a_{1},\cdots,a_{n},x_{1},\cdots,x_{n}))
from
\widetilde{\Delta}_{n}
to
\mathfrak{C}
. By
invariance under permutations (Definition and
Proposition
4.41
, item 1),
h
is separately
continuous in each
a_{i}
, varying in

[0,1-\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq j\leq n\\
j\neq i\end{subarray}}a_{j}]
. We claim
that it is jointly continuous. It is tempting to appeal to Ershov’s
observation, but the domain of
h
is not a Cartesian product.
Instead, we prove it by hand. Let
V
be an open subset of
\mathfrak{C}
.
Since
V
is upwards-closed, by Lemma
4.42

h^{-1}(V)
is upwards-closed in
\widetilde{\Delta}_{n}
. Let

{(\vec{a}_{i})}_{i\in I}
be a directed family with supremum
\vec{a}

in
\widetilde{\Delta}_{n}
, and let us assume that

\vec{a}\in h^{-1}(V)
. We write
\vec{a}
as
(a_{1},\cdots,a_{n})

and
\vec{a}_{i}
as
(a_{i1},\cdots,a_{in})
. For each

j\in\{0,1,\cdots,n\}
, let

\vec{a}_{i}[j]\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}(a_{i1},\cdots,a_{ij},a_{j+1},\cdots,a_{n})
. Then
{(\vec{a}_{i}[j])}_{i\in I}
is a directed family in

\widetilde{\Delta}_{n}
whose supremum is
\vec{a}
, and we claim that

\vec{a}_{i}[j]\in h^{-1}(V)
for some
i\in I
. This is proved by
induction on
j
. This is clear if
j=0
, since then

\vec{a}_{i}[j]=\vec{a}
for every
i\in I
, and

\vec{a}\in h^{-1}(V)
. In the induction step, we assume that

\vec{a}_{i}[j]\in h^{-1}(V)
, where
0<j<n
. The element

a_{j+1}
is the supremum of the directed family

{(a_{i^{\prime}(j+1)})}_{i^{\prime}\in I}
, and since
h
is continuous in its

(j+1)
st argument, there is an
i^{\prime}\in I
such that

(a_{i1},\cdots,a_{ij},a_{i^{\prime}(j+1)},a_{j+2},\cdots,a_{n})
is in

h^{-1}(V)
. By directedness, there is an index
i^{\prime\prime}\in I
such
that
(a_{i1},\cdots,a_{in})
and
(a_{i^{\prime}1},\cdots,a_{i^{\prime}n})
are
both less than or equal to
(a_{i^{\prime\prime}1},\cdots,a_{i^{\prime\prime}n})
. Since

h^{-1}(V)
is upwards-closed,

(a_{i^{\prime\prime}1},\cdots,a_{i^{\prime\prime}j},a_{i^{\prime\prime}(j+1)},a_{j+2},\cdots,a_{n})\in h^{-1}(V)
, namely
\vec{a}_{i^{\prime\prime}}[j+1]\in h^{-1}(V)
. The
induction is complete. In particular, for
j\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}n
, there is an

i\in I
such that
\vec{a}_{i}[n]\in h^{-1}(V)
. But

\vec{a}_{i}[n]=\vec{a}_{i}
, so
\vec{a}_{i}\in h^{-1}(V)
. This shows
that
h^{-1}(V)
is (Scott-)open in
\widetilde{\Delta}_{n}
, and
therefore
h
is continuous.

When
\mathfrak{B}
is semitopological, we have just shown that

\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{B}}\circ f
is separately continuous in

(a_{1},\cdots,a_{n})
,
x_{1}
, …,
x_{n}
. Since
\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{B}}
is a
topological embedding,
f
is itself separately continuous.

When
\mathfrak{B}
is topological, we have shown more:
\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{B}}\circ f

is separately continuous as a function of the tuple

(a_{1},\cdots,a_{n})\in\widetilde{\Delta}_{n}
and of the tuple

(x_{1},\cdots,x_{n})\in\mathfrak{B}^{n}
(jointly in
(x_{1},\cdots,x_{n})
).
Since
\widetilde{\Delta}_{n}
is a continuous dcpo,

\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{B}}\circ f
is jointly continuous, thanks to Ershov’s
observation. Therefore
f
is jointly continuous, using the fact
that
\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{B}}
is a topological embedding.
\Box

5
Convexity

We have already defined convex and concave sets, but only in cones.
The definition in barycentric algebras is the same.

Definition 5.1
(Convex, concave)

A subset
A
of a barycentric algebra
\mathfrak{B}
is
convex
if and
only if for all
x,y\in A
and for every
a\in[0,1]
,
x+_{a}y

is in
A
. It is
concave
if and only if
\mathfrak{B}\smallsetminus A
is
convex.

The theory of convex subsets of barycentric algebras to come in this
section largely parallels the theory of convex subsets of cones

[
30
, Section 4]
.

Lemma 5.2

All intersections of convex subsets of a barycentric algebra
\mathfrak{B}
are
convex. All directed unions of convex subsets of
\mathfrak{B}
are convex.

Proof.

The first claim is immediate. For the second claim, let us consider
a directed family
{(A_{i})}_{i\in I}
of convex subsets of
\mathfrak{B}
, and

A\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\bigcup_{i\in I}A_{i}
. Let us take any two points

x,y\in A
. Then
x
is in some
A_{i}
and
y
is in some
A_{j}
.
By directedness, there is a
k\in I
such that
A_{k}
contains both

A_{i}
and
A_{j}
. Therefore
x
and
y
are in
A_{k}
. For every

a\in[0,1]
,
x+_{a}y
is in
A_{k}
since
A_{k}
is convex, hence
in
A
.
\Box

5.1
Convex hulls

We recall the notion of barycenters
\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\cdot x_{i}
from
Definition and Proposition
3.1
. We also recall that

\Delta_{n}
is the standard simplex

\{\vec{\alpha}\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{n}\mid\sum_{i=1}^{n}\alpha_{i}=1\}
.

Def. and Lemma 5.3
(Convex hull)

Let
\mathfrak{B}
be a barycentric algebra. For every subset
A
of
\mathfrak{B}
,
there is a smallest convex subset of
\mathfrak{B}
containing
A
. This is
called the
convex hull

\mathop{\mathrm{conv}}A
of
A
, and is exactly the
set of barycenters
\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\cdot x_{i}
where
n\geq 1
,

(a_{1},\cdots,a_{n})\in\Delta_{n}
and each
x_{i}
is in
A
.

Proof.

Let
B
be the indicated set of barycenters. Let us show that
B

is a convex set. We consider two elements of
B
. Without loss of
generality, we can write them as

x\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\cdot x_{i}
and

y\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}b_{i}\cdot x_{i}
with
a_{i},b_{i}\geq 0
,

\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}b_{i}=1
, using the same sequence
of points
x_{i}\in A
,
1\leq i\leq n
, since we are allowed to use
zero as weights
a_{i}
or
b_{i}
. (We implicitly reason in a cone in
which
\mathfrak{B}
embeds through an injective affine map, typically

\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{B}}
.) For every
a\in[0,1]
,
x+_{a}y
is equal to

\sum_{i=1}^{n}(aa_{i}+(1-a)b_{i})\cdot x_{i}
. Since

aa_{i}+(1-a)b_{i}\geq 0
and

\sum_{i=1}^{n}(aa_{i}+(1-a)b_{i})=a\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}+(1-a)\sum_{i=1}^{n}b_{i}=a+(1-a)=1
,
a\cdot x+(1-a)\cdot y
is in

B
.

Let us now show that
B
is the smallest convex set containing
A
.
To that end, we consider any convex set
E
containing
A
, and a
barycenter
x\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\cdot x_{i}
where every
x_{i}

is in
A
,
n\geq 1
and
(a_{1},\cdots,a_{n})\in\Delta_{n}
. We
show that
x
is in
E
by induction on
n\in\mathbb{N}
, using the
inductive formula of Definition and Proposition
3.1
,
item 1. If
a_{n}=1
(a case that includes the base case
n=1
),

x=x_{n}
is in
A
, hence in
E
. Otherwise,

x=(\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}\frac{a_{i}}{1-a_{n}}x_{i})+_{1-a_{n}}x_{n}
. By
induction hypothesis

x^{\prime}\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}\frac{a_{i}}{1-a_{n}}x_{i}
is in
E
;

x_{n}
is in
A
hence in
E
, so
x\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}x^{\prime}+_{1-a_{n}}x_{n}
is in

E
, which is convex.
\Box

Lemma 5.4

For every affine map
f\colon\mathfrak{A}\to\mathfrak{B}
between barycentric
algebras, for every subset
A
of
\mathfrak{A}
,

f[\mathop{\mathrm{conv}}A]=\mathop{\mathrm{conv}}{f[A]}
.

Proof.

Every element of
\mathop{\mathrm{conv}}A
is of the form

\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\cdot x_{i}
with
n\geq 1
,

(a_{1},\cdots,a_{n})\in A
and
x_{1},\cdots,x_{n}\in A
, by
Definition and Lemma
5.3
. By Definition
and Proposition
3.1
, item 4, and since
f
is affine,

f(\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\cdot x_{i})=\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\cdot f(x_{i})
is
therefore in
\mathop{\mathrm{conv}}{f[A]}
. Conversely,
f[\mathop{\mathrm{conv}}A]
is convex
since
\mathop{\mathrm{conv}}A
is convex and
f
is affine, it contains
f[A]
,
hence it contains the smallest convex set that contains
f[A]
,
which is
\mathop{\mathrm{conv}}{f[A]}
by definition.
\Box

Lemma 5.5

Let
\mathfrak{B}
be a barycentric algebra, and
A_{1}
, …,
A_{n}
be
finitely many non-empty convex subsets of
\mathfrak{B}
. The set of all
barycenters
\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\cdot x_{i}
where
x_{i}\in A_{i}
for
each
i
,
n\geq 1
and
(a_{1},\cdots,a_{n})\in\Delta_{n}
, is equal
to
\mathop{\mathrm{conv}}{(A_{1}\cup\cdots\cup A_{n})}
.

Proof.

Let
A
be this set of barycenters. We consider any two barycenters

\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\cdot x_{i}
and
\sum_{i=1}^{n}b_{i}\cdot y_{i}
, where

x_{i},y_{i}\in A_{i}
. As usual, we implicitly reason in a cone in
which
\mathfrak{B}
embeds through an injective affine map, typically

\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{B}}
. For every
a\in{]0,1[}
,

\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\cdot x_{i}+_{a}\sum_{i=1}^{n}b_{i}\cdot y_{i}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}aa_{i}\cdot x_{i}+(1-a)b_{i}\cdot y_{i}
. For each
i
,
let
c_{i}\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}{aa_{i}+(1-a)b_{i}}
. If
c_{i}\neq 0
, we can write

aa_{i}\cdot x_{i}+(1-a)b_{i}\cdot y_{i}
as
c_{i}
times the point

z_{i}\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\frac{aa_{i}}{c_{i}}x_{i}+\frac{(1-a)b_{i}}{c_{i}}y_{i}=x_{i}+_{\frac{aa_{i}}{c_{i}}}y_{i}
, which is in
A_{i}
since
A_{i}
is
convex. If
c_{i}=0
, we define
z_{i}
as
x_{i}
or as
y_{i}
: again

z_{i}
is in
A_{i}
, and since
c_{i}=0
,
aa_{i}=(1-a)b_{i}=0
, so

aa_{i}x_{i}+(1-a)b_{i}y_{i}=0=c_{i}\cdot z_{i}
again. Hence

\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\cdot x_{i}+_{a}\sum_{i=1}^{n}b_{i}\cdot y_{i}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}c_{i}\cdot z_{i}
. Since
c_{i}\geq 0
and

\sum_{i=1}^{n}c_{i}=a\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}+(1-a)\sum_{i=1}^{n}b_{i}=a+(1-a)=1
,
\sum_{i=1}^{n}c_{i}\cdot z_{i}
is in
A
.

This shows that
A
is convex. Since
A
contains each
A_{i}
, it
also contains
\mathop{\mathrm{conv}}{(A_{1}\cup\cdots\cup A_{n})}
. Conversely,
every point of
A
is a barycenter of points from

A_{1}\cup\cdots\cup A_{n}
, hence is in

\mathop{\mathrm{conv}}{(A_{1}\cup\cdots\cup A_{n})}
by Definition and
Lemma
5.3
; whence

A=\mathop{\mathrm{conv}}{(A_{1}\cup\cdots\cup A_{n})}
.
\Box

5.2
Closed convex hulls

Def. and Proposition 5.6
(Closed convex hull)

In a semitopological barycentric algebra
\mathfrak{B}
, the closure of every
convex subset is convex.

For every subset
A
of
\mathfrak{B}
, there is a smallest closed convex
subset containing
A
. This is called the
closed convex hull

\mathop{\overline{\mathop{\mathrm{conv}}}}A
of
A
, and is equal to
cl(\mathop{\mathrm{conv}}A)
.

Proof.

Let
A
be a convex subset of
\mathfrak{B}
, and let us imagine that there
were two points
x,y\in cl(A)
and
a\in[0,1]
such that

x+_{a}y
is not in
cl(A)
. Let
f\colon\mathfrak{B}\times\mathfrak{B}\to\mathfrak{B}
be
the map defined by
f(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}x^{\prime}+_{a}y^{\prime}
. This is a
separately continuous map, and
f(x,y)
is in the (open)
complement
V
of
cl(A)
. Since
f
is continuous in its second
argument,
f(x,\_)^{-1}(V)
is an open neighborhood of
y
, hence
intersects
cl(A)
. It must therefore also intersect
A
, say at

y^{\prime}
. Then
f(x,y^{\prime})
is in
V
. Since
f
is continuous in its
first argument,
f(\_,y^{\prime})^{-1}(V)
is an open neighborhood of

x
, hence intersects
cl(A)
, hence also
A
, say at
x^{\prime}
. We
have obtained that
f(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})
is in
V
. This is impossible,
since
f(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})
is in
A
, owing to the fact that
A
is convex.

It follows that, for every subset
A
of
\mathfrak{B}
,
cl(\mathop{\mathrm{conv}}A)
is not
only closed but also convex. Every closed convex subset
B
of
\mathfrak{B}

containing
A
must contain
\mathop{\mathrm{conv}}A
(since
B
is convex), and
therefore also
cl(\mathop{\mathrm{conv}}A)
(since
B
is closed). This shows
that
cl(\mathop{\mathrm{conv}}A)
is the smallest closed convex set containing

A
.
\Box

Lemma 5.7

For every affine continuous map
f\colon\mathfrak{A}\to\mathfrak{B}
between
semitopological barycentric algebras, for every subset
A
of

\mathfrak{A}
,
f[\mathop{\overline{\mathop{\mathrm{conv}}}}A]\subseteq\mathop{\overline{\mathop{\mathrm{conv}}}}{f[A]}
.

Proof.

Since
f
is continuous,
f[\mathop{\overline{\mathop{\mathrm{conv}}}}A]=f[cl(\mathop{\mathrm{conv}}A)]\subseteq cl(f[\mathop{\mathrm{conv}}A])
. By Lemma
5.4
,
f[\mathop{\mathrm{conv}}A]=\mathop{\mathrm{conv}}{f[A]}
, and then
cl(\mathop{\mathrm{conv}}{f[A]})=\mathop{\overline{\mathop{\mathrm{conv}}}}{f[A]}
.
\Box

5.3
Saturated convex hulls

Def. and Proposition 5.8
(Saturated convex hull)

Let
\mathfrak{B}
be a preordered (e.g., semitopological) barycentric
algebra. For every convex subset
A
of
\mathfrak{C}
,
\upc A
is convex.

For every subset
A
of
\mathfrak{C}
, there is a smallest upwards-closed
convex subset containing
A
, called the
saturated convex
hull
of
A
, and this is
\upc\mathop{\mathrm{conv}}A
.

Proof.

If
A
is convex and
x,y\in\upc A
, then
x\geq x^{\prime}
and

y\geq y^{\prime}
for some
x^{\prime},y^{\prime}\in A
. For every
a\in{]0,1[}
,

x+_{a}y
is above
x^{\prime}+_{a}y^{\prime}
since
+_{a}
is monotonic;

x^{\prime}+_{a}y^{\prime}\in A
since
A
is convex, so
x+_{a}y
is in
\upc A
.

By the first part of the lemma, for every subset
A
of
\mathfrak{C}
,

\upc\mathop{\mathrm{conv}}A
is upwards-closed and convex. We consider any
upwards-closed convex subset
B
of
\mathfrak{C}
containing
A
. Then
B

contains
\mathop{\mathrm{conv}}A
since
B
is convex, hence
\upc\mathop{\mathrm{conv}}A
since

B
is upwards-closed.
\Box

Lemma 5.9

For every affine monotonic map
f\colon\mathfrak{A}\to\mathfrak{B}
between
preordered barycentric algebras (in particular for every affine
continuous map between semitopological barycentric algebras), for
every subset
A
of
\mathfrak{A}
,

f[\upc\mathop{\mathrm{conv}}A]\subseteq\upc\mathop{\mathrm{conv}}{f[A]}
.

Proof.

Since
f
is monotonic,

f[\upc\mathop{\mathrm{conv}}A]\subseteq\upc f[\mathop{\mathrm{conv}}A]
. We conclude since

f[\mathop{\mathrm{conv}}A]=\mathop{\mathrm{conv}}{f[A]}
by Lemma
5.4
.
\Box

Proposition 5.10

Let
\mathfrak{B}
be a topological barycentric algebra, and
K_{1}
, …,

K_{n}
be finitely many convex compact subsets of
\mathfrak{B}
. The convex
hull
\mathop{\mathrm{conv}}(K_{1}\cup\cdots\cup K_{n})
is convex and compact, and
the upwards-closed convex hull

\upc\mathop{\mathrm{conv}}(K_{1}\cup\cdots\cup K_{n})
is convex and compact
saturated.

Proof.

Removing those sets
K_{i}
that are empty if necessary, we may assume
that every
K_{i}
is non-empty. By Lemma
5.5
,

\mathop{\mathrm{conv}}{(K_{1}\cup\cdots\cup K_{n})}
is the image of the compact set

\Delta_{n}\times\prod_{i=1}^{n}K_{i}
(
\Delta_{n}
is compact by
Lemma
3.29
) under the map

(a_{1},\cdots,\allowbreak a_{n},x_{1},\cdots,x_{n})\mapsto\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\cdot x_{i}
of Definition and
Proposition
3.1
, which is continuous by
Proposition
3.28
; hence

\mathop{\mathrm{conv}}{(K_{1}\cup\cdots\cup K_{n})}
is compact. The remaining
assertions are clear.
\Box

Corollary 5.11

For every finite subset
E
of a topological barycentric algebra (in
particular, of a topological cone),
\mathop{\mathrm{conv}}E
is convex and compact,
and
\upc\mathop{\mathrm{conv}}E
is convex and compact saturated.

Proof.

Letting
E\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\{x_{1},\cdots,x_{n}\}
, we apply
Proposition
5.10
to the sets

K_{1}\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\{x_{1}\}
, …,
K_{n}\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\{x_{n}\}
.
\Box

Remark 5.12

The sets
K_{i}
in Proposition
5.10
have to
be compact
and
convex already. The following example shows
that the convex hull of a compact set
K
is
not
compact in
general, even in a topological cone.

Example 5.13

Let
X
be a topological space, and
{(x_{n})}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
be a
sequence converging to some point
x_{\omega}
in
X
. We assume
that:
(a)
for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
, there is an open neighborhood

U_{n}
of
x_{n}
that does not contain any
x_{m}
with
m\neq n
, in
particular not
x_{\omega}
;
(b)
for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
, there is an
open neighborhood
V_{n}
of
x_{\omega}
that does not contain
x_{0}
,
…,
x_{n}
. (For example,
X\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\mathbb{R}
or
X
equal to the
compact Hausdorff space
[-1,1]
with its usual metric topology,

x_{n}\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}1/2^{n}
,
x_{\omega}\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}0
,

U_{n}\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}{]3/4\cdot 1/2^{n},3/2\cdot 1/2^{n}[}
,

V_{n}\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}{]-1,3/4\cdot 1/2^{n}[}
.) Let
\mathfrak{C}
be the topological
cone
{\mathbf{V}}X
, and

K\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\{\delta_{x_{n}}\mid n\in\mathbb{N}\}\cup\{\delta_{x_{\omega}}\}
. Then
K
is compact, but
\mathop{\mathrm{conv}}K
is not.

Proof.

In order to show that
K
is compact, we use Alexander’s subbase
lemma, which says that
K
is compact if and only if for every open
cover of
K
by subbasic open sets has a finite subcover

[
19
, Theorem 4.4.29]
. Here, such an open cover is of
the form
{([W_{i}>r_{i}])}_{i\in I}
, where each
W_{i}
is open in

X
and
r_{i}>0
. Since
\delta_{x_{\omega}}\in K
, there is an
index
i\in I
such that
\delta_{x_{\omega}}\in[W_{i}>r_{i}]
,
namely such that
x_{\omega}\in W_{i}
and
r_{i}<1
. Since

{(x_{n})}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
converges to
x_{\omega}
in
X
, there is an

n_{0}\in\mathbb{N}
such that
x_{n}\in U_{i}
for every
n\geq n_{0}
, so
that every
\delta_{x_{n}}
is in
[W_{i}>r_{i}]
for
n\geq n_{0}
. The
remaining finitely many continuous valuations
\delta_{x_{n}}
,

n<n_{0}
, can be covered by finitely many of the remaining open
sets in the original cover.

By Definition and Lemma
5.3
, the elements
of
\mathop{\mathrm{conv}}K
are the simple probability valuations of the form

\sum_{n\in N}a_{n}\delta_{x_{n}}
with

N\subseteq\mathbb{N}\cup\{\omega\}
finite,
a_{n}>0
and

\sum_{n\in N}a_{n}=1
.

Now let us assume that
\mathop{\mathrm{conv}}K
is compact. We consider the
sequence of elements

\nu_{k}\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\sum_{i=0}^{k}\frac{1}{2^{i+1}}\delta_{x_{n}}+\frac{1}{2^{k+1}}\delta_{x_{\omega}}
,
k\in\mathbb{N}
. Each
\nu_{k}
is in

\mathop{\mathrm{conv}}K
, because

\sum_{i=0}^{k}\frac{1}{2^{i+1}}+\frac{1}{2^{k+1}}=1
. Since

\mathop{\mathrm{conv}}K
is compact, this sequence must have a cluster point

\nu\in\mathop{\mathrm{conv}}K
, namely, every open neighborhood of
\nu
must
contain
\nu_{k}
for infinitely many indices
k\in\mathbb{N}

[
19
, Proposition 4.7.22]
. We have characterized the
elements of
\mathop{\mathrm{conv}}K
above: so
\nu
must be of the form

\sum_{n\in N}a_{n}\delta_{x_{n}}
with

N\subseteq\mathbb{N}\cup\{\omega\}
finite,
a_{n}>0
and

\sum_{n\in N}a_{n}=1
.

If
\omega\in N
, then
a_{\omega}>0
. Let us fix

r\in{]0,a_{\omega}[}
. For every
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
\nu
is in

[V_{n}>r]
. By definition of a cluster point, there are infinitely
many values of
k
such that
\nu_{k}\in[V_{n}>r]
. But, taking

k>n
,

\nu_{k}(V_{n})=\sum_{n+1\leq i\leq k}\frac{1}{2^{i+1}}+\frac{1}{2^{k+1}}
since none of the points
x_{0}
, …,
x_{n}
are in

V_{n}
by
(b)
. Therefore
\nu_{k}(V_{n})=\frac{1}{2^{n+1}}
. This is
impossible since
\nu_{k}(V_{n})>r
for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
.
Therefore
\omega
is not in
N
. In other words,

N\subseteq\mathbb{N}
.

For every
n\in N
, for every
r\in{]0,a_{n}[}
,
\nu
is in

[U_{n}>r]
, so by definition of a cluster point,
\nu_{k}(U_{n})>r

for infinitely many values of
k
. Taking
k>n
,

\nu_{k}(U_{n})=\frac{1}{2^{n+1}}
since
U_{n}
contains
x_{n}
but
not
x_{m}
for any
m\neq n
by
(a)
. This implies that for every

n\in N
, for every
r\in{]0,a_{n}[}
,
\frac{1}{2^{n+1}}>r
, in
other words, that
a_{n}\leq\frac{1}{2^{n+1}}
for every
n\in N
.
Since
\sum_{n\in N}a_{n}=1
, it follows that

\sum_{n\in N}\frac{1}{2^{n+1}}\geq 1
, and that is impossible
since
N
is finite. We have reached a contradiction, and we must
conclude that
\mathop{\mathrm{conv}}K
is not compact.
\Box

6
Local convexity

In semitopological cones, there are at least three notions of local
convexity: local convexity in the sense of Keimel
[
30
, Definition
4.8]
,
weak
local convexity or local convexity
in the sense of Heckmann
[
23
, Section 6.3]
, and local
linearity
[
15
, Definition 3.9]
. The author does not know
whether local convexity and weak local convexity are different
notions on semitopological cones, or whether there are any non-locally
convex semitopological cones at all. An example of a locally convex,
non-locally linear topological cone is given in
[
15
, Example 3.15]
.

There seems to be even more notions of local convexity in
semitopological barycentric algebras. We will attempt to focus on the
closest analogues of the above three notions. On top of that, we will
also consider the natural adaptation of Keimel’s notion of local
convex-compactness
[
30
, Definition 4.8]
to
semitopological barycentric algebras.

6.1
Locally convex semitopological barycentric algebras

Definition 6.1
(Locally convex)

A semitopological cone, and more generally a semitopological
barycentric algebra is
locally convex
if and only if its
topology has a base of convex open subsets.

Equivalently, a semitopological barycentric algebra
\mathfrak{B}
is locally
convex if and only if for every
x\in\mathfrak{B}
, every open neighborhood of

x
contains a convex open neighborhood of
x
.

Yet another equivalent characterization is: a semitopological
barycentric algebra is locally convex if and only if its topology has
a
sub
base of convex open subsets. This is because, given such
a subbase, the finite intersections of subbasic open sets form a base,
and are all convex by Lemma
5.2
.

Example 6.2

For every space
X
,
{\mathbf{V}}X
and
{\mathbf{V}}_{b}X
are locally convex
topological cones and
{\mathbf{V}}_{1}X
and
{\mathbf{V}}_{\leq 1}X
are locally
convex topological barycentric algebras. Local convexity stems from
the fact that the subbasic open set
[h>r]
is convex for every

h\in\mathcal{L}X
and for every
r\in\mathbb{R}_{+}\smallsetminus\{0\}
.
Additionally,
{\mathbf{V}}X
and
{\mathbf{V}}_{b}X
are topological by
Example
2.5
, and
{\mathbf{V}}_{1}X
and
{\mathbf{V}}_{\leq 1}X

are topological, being convex subspaces of
{\mathbf{V}}X
, thanks to
Lemma
3.8
. Similarly,
{\mathbf{V}}_{\mathrm{f}}X

and
{\mathbf{V}}_{\mathrm{p}}X
are locally convex topological cones.

Example 6.3

For every space
X
, let
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{p}}X
be
\mathcal{L}X
with the
topology of pointwise convergence. This topology has subbasic open
sets
\{h\colon X\to\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}\mid h(x)>r\}
where
x\in X
and

r\in\mathbb{R}_{+}\smallsetminus\{0\}
, and they are all convex, so
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{p}}X

is locally convex.

Example 6.4

Every sup-semilattice
X
, seen as a semitopological barycentric
algebra (Example
3.11
), is locally convex.
In fact,
every
Scott-open subset
U
of
X
is convex, since
the barycenters of any two points
x
and
y
in
U
are
x
,
y
,
and
x\vee y
, which are all in
U
, because
U
is upwards-closed.

Example 6.5

The topological barycentric algebra
\mathfrak{B}^{-}
of
Example
3.24
is locally convex. In fact, all
its open subsets are convex.

The following result gives more examples of locally convex barycentric
algebras, and is a direct extension of a similar result on c-cones

[
30
, Lemma 6.12]
. The argument is due to
J. D. Lawson. The same result appears in

[
23
, Proposition 6.3]
. We have introduced s-barycentric
algebras in Example
3.12
.

Proposition 6.6

Every semitopological barycentric algebra whose underlying
topological space is a c-space is a locally convex topological
barycentric algebra. In particular:

1.

every c-cone is a locally convex topological cone;

2.

every pointed continuous s-barycentric algebra
is a locally convex pointed topological barycentric algebra in its
Scott topology.

Proof.

Let
\mathfrak{B}
be a semitopological barycentric algebra and a c-space.
By Proposition
3.13
,
\mathfrak{B}
is topological.

1. Let
x
be a point of
\mathfrak{B}
and
U
be an open neighborhood of

x
in
\mathfrak{B}
. Since
\mathfrak{B}
is a c-space, there is a point
x_{1}\in U

such that
x\in\text{int}({\upc x_{1}})
. Since
x_{1}\in U
and
\mathfrak{B}

is a c-space, there is a point
x_{2}\in U
such that

x_{1}\in\text{int}({\upc x_{2}})
. We continue this way and we obtain
a chain of points
x_{n}
in
U
,
n\geq 1
, with

x_{n}\in\text{int}({\upc{x_{n+1}}})
. Let

V\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\bigcup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\text{int}({\upc{x_{n}}})
.
V
is open,
contains
x
, and is included in
U
.
V
is also equal to

\bigcup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\upc x_{n}
: the inclusion

V\subseteq\bigcup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\upc x_{n}
is clear, while conversely

\bigcup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\upc x_{n}\subseteq\bigcup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\text{int}({\upc{x_{n+1}}})\subseteq V
. Since each of the sets
\upc x_{n}
is
convex,
V
is convex as well by Lemma
5.2
.

2. Every continuous s-barycentric algebra is a topological
barycentric algebra (see Example
3.12
), and a
c-space in its Scott topology.
\Box

We conclude this section with an alternate characterization of local
convexity for cones, and of (pointed) semitopological barycentric
algebras whose free (pointed) semitopological cone is locally convex.

Proposition 6.7

A semitopological cone
\mathfrak{C}
is locally convex if and only if every
positively homogeneous lower semicontinuous map

h\colon\mathfrak{C}\to\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
is a pointwise supremum of superlinear
lower semicontinuous maps.

Proof.

Let
\mathfrak{C}
be locally convex, and
h\colon\mathfrak{C}\to\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
be
positively homogeneous and lower semicontinuous. Let
\mathcal{C}

be the collection of superlinear lower semicontinuous maps from
\mathfrak{C}

to
\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
that are pointwise below
h
. We claim that the
pointwise supremum
\sup\mathcal{C}
is equal to
h
. Otherwise,
there is a point
x\in\mathfrak{C}
such that

\sup_{g\in\mathcal{C}}g(x)<h(x)
. Let
a\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
be such
that
\sup_{g\in\mathcal{C}}g(x)<a<h(x)
. The set

h^{-1}(]a,\infty])
is open in
\mathfrak{C}
, and is proper since it does
not contain
0
; indeed,
h(0)=0
is not in
]a,\infty]
. Since

\mathfrak{C}
is locally convex, there is a convex open neighborhood
U
of

x
included in
h^{-1}(]a,\infty])
. Since the latter is proper,
so is
U
. Therefore its upper Minkowski functional
M^{U}
is a
superlinear lower semicontinuous map from
\mathfrak{C}
to
\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
. Since

U\subseteq h^{-1}(]a,\infty])=((1/a)\cdot h)^{-1}(]1,\infty])
and
(1/a)\cdot h
is positively homogeneous and lower
semicontinuous, the order isomorphism between positively homogeneous
maps and proper open subsets tells us that
M^{U}\leq(1/a)\cdot h
.
Now
g\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}a\cdot M^{U}
is superlinear, lower semicontinuous, and
below
h
, hence in
\mathcal{C}
. But
M^{U}(x)>1
since

x\in U
, so
g(x)>a
. This contradicts the fact that

\sup_{g\in\mathcal{C}}g(x)<a
.

Conversely, let us assume that every positively homogeneous lower
semicontinuous map
h\colon\mathfrak{C}\to\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
is a pointwise supremum
of superlinear lower semicontinuous maps. For every
x\in\mathfrak{C}
, for
every open neighborhood
U
of
x
, we look for a convex open
neighborhood of
x
included in
U
. If
U=\mathfrak{C}
, we simply take

\mathfrak{C}
itself. Otherwise,
U
is proper, hence
M^{U}
is positively
homogeneous and lower semicontinuous. Additionally,
M^{U}(x)>1
.
By assumption,
M^{U}
is a pointwise supremum of superlinear lower
semicontinuous maps, so one of them, call it
g
, is such that
