g(x)>1
; we note that we must have
g\leq M^{U}
. Then

g^{-1}(]1,\infty])
is (proper) convex and open, contains
x
and
is included in
(M^{U})^{-1}(]1,\infty])=U
. Hence
\mathfrak{C}
is
locally convex.
\Box

Proposition 6.8

For every pointed semitopological barycentric algebra
\mathfrak{B}
, for
every
\alpha\in{]0,1[}
, the following are equivalent:

1.

the free semitopological cone
\mathrm{tscope}_{\alpha}(\mathfrak{B})
is locally
convex;

2.

every
\mathbb{I}
-homogeneous lower semicontinuous map

h\colon\mathfrak{B}\to\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
is a pointwise supremum of superlinear
lower semicontinuous maps.

Proof.

1\mathrel{\Rightarrow}2
. Let
h\colon\mathfrak{B}\to\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
be
\mathbb{I}
-homogeneous and
lower semicontinuous. By
Theorem
4.22
, item 3,

h^{((\alpha))}
is positively homogeneous. By
Proposition
6.7
and item 1,

h^{((\alpha))}
is a pointwise supremum of superlinear lower
semicontinuous maps
g_{i}
,
i\in I
. Then

h=h^{((\alpha))}\circ\eta^{\mathrm{tscope},\alpha}_{\mathfrak{B}}=\sup_{i\in I}g_{i}\circ\eta^{\mathrm{tscope},\alpha}_{\mathfrak{B}}
, and each map
g_{i}\circ\eta^{\mathrm{tscope},\alpha}_{\mathfrak{B}}
is superlinear
and lower semicontinuous, since
\eta^{\mathrm{tscope},\alpha}_{\mathfrak{B}}
is itself linear and
continuous, by
Theorem
4.22
, item 2.

2\mathrel{\Rightarrow}1
. Let
g
be a positively homogeneous lower
semicontinuous map from
\mathrm{tscope}_{\alpha}(\mathfrak{B})
to
\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
. Then

g\circ\eta^{\mathrm{tscope},\alpha}_{\mathfrak{B}}
is
\mathbb{I}
-homogeneous and lower semicontinuous.
By item 2, it is a supremum of superlinear lower semicontinuous maps

h_{i}\colon\mathfrak{B}\to\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
,
i\in I
. Hence

g\circ\eta^{\mathrm{tscope},\alpha}_{\mathfrak{B}}=\sup_{i\in I}h_{i}=\sup_{i\in I}(h_{i}^{((\alpha))}\circ\eta^{\mathrm{tscope},\alpha}_{\mathfrak{B}})=(\sup_{i\in I}h_{i}^{((\alpha))})\circ\eta^{\mathrm{tscope},\alpha}_{\mathfrak{B}}
. For every
\mathbb{I}
-homogeneous
lower semicontinuous function
f
,
f^{((\alpha))}
is unique , so

g=\sup_{i\in I}h_{i}^{((\alpha))}
. For each
i\in I
, since

h_{i}
is superlinear and lower semicontinuous, so is

h_{i}^{((\alpha))}
, by
Theorem
4.22
, item 3.
\Box

Proposition 6.9

For every semitopological barycentric algebra
\mathfrak{B}
, the free
semitopological cone
\mathrm{cone}(\mathfrak{B})
is locally convex if and only if
every semi-concave lower semicontinuous map
h\colon\mathfrak{B}\to\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}

is a pointwise supremum of concave lower semicontinuous maps.

Proof.

We use Remark
3.23
. The positively homogeneous
lower semicontinuous maps from
\mathrm{cone}(\mathfrak{B})
to
\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
are
exactly the maps
h^{\leftslice}
where
h
is semi-concave and lower
semicontinuous. By Proposition
6.7
,

\mathrm{cone}(\mathfrak{B})
is locally convex if and only if every such map is a
pointwise supremum of superlinear lower semicontinuous maps
q_{i}

from
\mathrm{cone}(\mathfrak{B})
to
\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
, where
i
ranges over some index
set
I
. If so, then by Remark
3.23
,

q_{i}=g_{i}^{\leftslice}
for some unique concave lower semicontinuous map

g_{i}\colon\mathfrak{B}\to\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
, and

h=h^{\leftslice}\circ\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{B}}=\sup_{i\in I}g_{i}^{\leftslice}\circ\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{B}}=\sup_{i\in}g_{i}
. Conversely, if
h
is a pointwise
supremum of concave lower semicontinuous maps
g_{i}
,
i\in I
, then

h^{\leftslice}=\sup_{i\in I}g_{i}^{\leftslice}
, so
h^{\leftslice}
is a pointwise
supremum of superlinear lower semicontinuous maps; as
h
is
arbitrary,
\mathrm{cone}(\mathfrak{B})
is locally convex.
\Box

6.2
Locally affine semitopological barycentric algebras

A semitopological cone
\mathfrak{C}
is
locally linear
if and only if
its topology has a subbase of open half-spaces
[
15
, Definition
3.9]
. We may restrict to proper open half-spaces, and this
will remain a subbase. Because of the order-isomorphism between
proper open half-spaces and linear lower semicontinuous maps, we
obtain the following, which justifies the name.

Fact 6.10

A semitopological cone
\mathfrak{C}
is locally linear if and only if its
topology has a subbase of open sets of the form

\Lambda^{-1}(]1,\infty])
, where
\Lambda
ranges over the linear
lower semicontinuous maps from
\mathfrak{C}
to
\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
.

This leads us to the following generalization of the notion to
barycentric algebras.

Definition 6.11
(Locally affine, locally linear)

A pointed semitopological barycentric algebra
\mathfrak{B}
is
locally
affine
if and only if its topology has a subbase of open sets of
the form
\Lambda^{-1}(]1,\infty])
, where
\Lambda
ranges over
the affine lower semicontinuous maps from
\mathfrak{B}
to
\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
.

A pointed semitopological barycentric algebra
\mathfrak{B}
is
locally
linear
if and only if its topology has a subbase of open sets of
the form
\Lambda^{-1}(]1,\infty])
, where
\Lambda
ranges over
the linear lower semicontinuous maps from
\mathfrak{B}
to
\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
.

Example 6.12

For every topological space
X
,
{\mathbf{V}}X
,
{\mathbf{V}}_{b}X
,
{\mathbf{V}}_{\mathrm{p}}X
,

{\mathbf{V}}_{\mathrm{f}}X
are locally linear. Indeed, every subbasic open set

[h>r]
is an open half-space.

Example 6.13

Given a pointed semitopological barycentric algebra
\mathfrak{B}
that embeds
in another one
\mathfrak{A}
(e.g., in a semitopological cone) through a
linear topological embedding, if
\mathfrak{A}
is locally linear, then so
is
\mathfrak{B}
. Hence, for every topological space
X
, the
T_{0}
pointed
semitopological barycentric algebras
{\mathbf{V}}_{\leq 1}X
,

{\mathbf{V}}_{\leq 1,\mathrm{p}}X
and
{\mathbf{V}}_{\leq 1,\mathrm{f}}X
, whose inclusion
maps into
{\mathbf{V}}X
,
{\mathbf{V}}_{\mathrm{p}}X
and
{\mathbf{V}}_{\mathrm{f}}X
respectively are
linear topological embeddings, are locally linear.

Example 6.14

Given a semitopological barycentric algebra
\mathfrak{B}
that embeds in
another one
\mathfrak{A}
(e.g., in a semitopological cone) through an
affine topological embedding, if
\mathfrak{A}
is locally affine, then so
is
\mathfrak{B}
. Hence, for every topological space
X
, the
T_{0}

topological barycentric algebras
{\mathbf{V}}_{1}X
,
{\mathbf{V}}_{1,\mathrm{p}}X
and

{\mathbf{V}}_{1,\mathrm{f}}X
, whose inclusion maps into
{\mathbf{V}}X
,
{\mathbf{V}}_{\mathrm{p}}X

and
{\mathbf{V}}_{\mathrm{f}}X
respectively are affine topological embeddings,
are locally affine.

Example 6.15

For every space
X
,
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{p}}X
(see
Example
6.3
) is locally linear, since the
subbasic open set
\{h\colon X\to\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}\mid h(x)>r\}
is an
open half-space.

By definition, every locally affine semitopological barycentric
algebra is linearly separated in the following sense.

Definition 6.16
(Linearly separated)

A semitopological barycentric algebra
\mathfrak{B}
is
linearly
separated
if and only if for all
x,y\in\mathfrak{B}
such that

x\not\leq y
, there is an affine lower semicontinuous map

\Lambda
such that
\Lambda(x)>\Lambda(y)
.

This is called
linearly
separated and not
affinely

separated because the notion was originally intended on cones, where
we can require
\Lambda
to be linear, not affine, as the following
lemma shows. Keimel
[
30
, Section 7]
calls

convex-
T_{0}
the
T_{0}
semitopological cones that are linearly
separated; and indeed, all locally convex semitopological cones, not
just the locally linear ones, are linearly separated: this is stated
for
T_{0}
semitopological cones in

[
30
, Corollary 9.3]
, but is valid even without the

T_{0}
requirement.

Lemma 6.17

1.

A pointed semitopological barycentric algebra, in particular a
semitopological cone,
\mathfrak{B}
is linearly separated if and only if
for all
x,y\in\mathfrak{B}
such that
x\not\leq y
, there is a linear
lower semicontinuous map
\Lambda
such that

\Lambda(x)>\Lambda(y)
.

2.

A semitopological cone
\mathfrak{C}
is linearly separated if and only
if for all
x,y\in\mathfrak{C}
,
x\leq y
if and only if every open
half-space that contains
x
also contains
y
.

Proof.

1. Let
\mathfrak{B}
be a pointed semitopological barycentric algebra. The
if direction is clear, since every linear map is affine.
Conversely, let us assume that
\mathfrak{B}
is linearly separated, and let

x,y\in\mathfrak{B}
be such that
x\not\leq y
. There is a lower
semi-continuous affine map
\Lambda
such that

\Lambda(x)>\Lambda(y)
. It cannot be the case that

\Lambda(\bot)=\infty
, otherwise
\Lambda
would be the constant
function with value
\infty
, since
\Lambda
is lower
semicontinuous hence monotonic, and then

\infty=\Lambda(y)<\Lambda(x)
would be impossible.
Therefore the function
\Lambda^{\prime}
defined by
\Lambda^{\prime}(x)\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\Lambda(x)-\Lambda(\bot)
is well-defined.
\Lambda^{\prime}
is
lower semicontinuous, affine, and strict, hence it is linear by
Proposition
4.6
. Additionally,
\Lambda^{\prime}(x)>\Lambda^{\prime}(y)
(even when
\Lambda(x)=\infty
; note that
\Lambda(y)<\infty
since
\Lambda(y)<\Lambda(x)
).

2. This follows from item 1 and the fact that proper half-spaces

H
are in bijection with linear lower semicontinuous maps. For the
unique non-proper half-space
\mathfrak{C}
, the implication

x\in\mathfrak{C}\mathrel{\Rightarrow}y\in\mathfrak{C}
is vacuously true.
\Box

Example 6.18

For every topological space
X
, and whether
\circledast
is nothing,
“
\leq 1
”, “
1
” or “
b
”,
{\mathbf{V}}_{\circledast}X
is linearly
separated, and similarly with
{\mathbf{V}}_{\circledast,\mathrm{f}}X
and

{\mathbf{V}}_{\circledast,\mathrm{p}}X
. Indeed, if
\mu\not\leq\nu
, then there is
an open subset
U
of
X
such that
\mu(U)>\nu(U)
. We define

\Lambda\colon{\mathbf{V}}_{\circledast}X\to\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
by

\Lambda(\xi)\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\xi(U)
: this is an affine lower
semicontinuous map, and
\Lambda(\mu)>\Lambda(\nu)
.

Example 6.19

Since the restriction of an affine map to any convex subset is
affine, every convex subspace of a linearly separated
semitopological barycentric algebra is itself linearly separated.
This is another way of deriving the fact that the spaces

{\mathbf{V}}_{\circledast}X
,
{\mathbf{V}}_{\circledast,\mathrm{f}}X
and
{\mathbf{V}}_{\circledast,\mathrm{p}}X

are linear separated, from just the knowledge that
{\mathbf{V}}X
is .

Example 6.20

For every topological space
X
,
\mathcal{L}X
is linearly separated,
and that is not only true for the Scott topology, but also for the
coarser compact-open topology and for the even coarser topology of
pointwise convergence. Indeed, if
h\not\leq h^{\prime}
in
\mathcal{L}X
,
then
h(x)>h^{\prime}(x)
for some
x\in X
. The map

\Lambda\colon\mathcal{L}X\to\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
defined by

\Lambda(k)\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}k(x)
for every
k\in\mathcal{L}X
is linear and
lower semicontinuous, whatever the topology we take among the three
listed above on
\mathcal{L}X
, and
\Lambda(h)>\Lambda(h^{\prime})
.

Local linearity and linear separation play related roles through the
following construction, a direct generalization of the topological
duals of cones
[
30
, Example 5.5]
.

Def. and Lemma 6.21

For every semitopological barycentric algebra
\mathfrak{B}
(resp. pointed
semitopological barycentric algebra
\mathfrak{B}
), there is a topological
cone
\mathfrak{B}^{\star}
(resp.
\mathfrak{B}^{*}
) of affine (resp. linear) lower
semicontinuous maps from
\mathfrak{B}
to
\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
, with topology generated
by subbasic open sets

[x>r]\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\{\Lambda\in\mathfrak{C}\mid\Lambda(x)>r\}
where
x

ranges over
\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
and
r
over
\mathbb{R}_{+}\smallsetminus\{0\}
. Then:

1.

\mathfrak{B}^{\star*}
(resp.
\mathfrak{B}^{**}
) are locally linear topological
cones;

2.

the map
\eta^{\star*}_{\mathfrak{B}}\colon\mathfrak{B}\to\mathfrak{B}^{\star*}
(resp.
\eta^{**}_{\mathfrak{B}}\colon\mathfrak{B}\to\mathfrak{B}^{**}
) defined by

\eta^{\star*}_{\mathfrak{B}}(x)(\Lambda)\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\Lambda(x)
for every

\Lambda\in\mathfrak{B}^{\star}
(resp.
\eta^{**}_{\mathfrak{B}}(x)(\Lambda)\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\Lambda(x)
for every

\Lambda\in\mathfrak{B}^{*}
) is affine (resp. linear) and continuous.

Additionally,
\eta^{\star*}_{\mathfrak{B}}
(resp.
\eta^{**}_{\mathfrak{B}}
) is:

3.

a preorder embedding if and only if
\mathfrak{B}
is linearly
separated;

4.

full if and only if
\mathfrak{B}
is locally affine (resp. linear);

5.

an affine topological embedding if and only if
\mathfrak{B}
is locally
affine (resp. locally linear) and
T_{0}
.

Proof.

1. Let
\mathfrak{C}\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\mathfrak{B}^{\star}
(resp.
\mathfrak{B}^{*}
). With pointwise
addition and scalar multiplication,
\mathfrak{C}
is a cone. The inverse
image of
[x>r]
by addition is

\{(\Lambda,\Lambda^{\prime})\in\mathfrak{C}\times\mathfrak{C}\mid\Lambda(x)+\Lambda^{\prime}(x)>r\}=\{(\Lambda,\Lambda^{\prime})\in\mathfrak{C}\times\mathfrak{C}\mid\text{for
some }a,b\in\mathbb{R}_{+}\text{ such that }a+b>r,\Lambda(x)>a,\Lambda^{\prime}(x)>b\}=\bigcup_{a,b\in\mathbb{R}_{+},a+b>r}[x>a]\times[x>b]
, so addition is (jointly) continuous. For every

a\in\mathbb{R}_{+}\smallsetminus\{0\}
, the inverse image of
[x>r]
by

(a\cdot\_)
is
[x>r/a]
, so
(a\cdot\_)
is continuous; it is
also continuous when
a=0
, since it is a constant map in that case.
For every
\Lambda\in\mathfrak{C}
, the inverse image of
[x>r]
by

(\_\cdot\Lambda)
is
]r/\Lambda(x),\infty]
if

0<\Lambda(x)<\infty
, empty if
\Lambda(x)=0
, and

]0,\infty]
if
\Lambda(x)=\infty
.
\mathfrak{C}
is therefore a
topological cone.

2. In the following, we will write
\eta
for
\eta^{\star*}_{\mathfrak{B}}
(resp. for
\eta^{**}_{\mathfrak{B}}
) and
\mathfrak{C}
for
\mathfrak{B}^{\star}
(resp.
\mathfrak{B}^{*}
). The function

\eta
is continuous because

\eta^{-1}([\Lambda>r])=\Lambda^{-1}(]r,\infty])
for all

\Lambda\in\mathfrak{C}
and
r\in\mathbb{R}_{+}\smallsetminus\{0\}
. It is affine: for all

x,y\in\mathfrak{B}
and
a\in[0,1]
,
\eta(x+_{a}y)
maps every

\Lambda\in\mathfrak{C}
to

\Lambda(x+_{a}y)=a\,\Lambda(x)+(1-a)\,\Lambda(y)
(since

\Lambda
is affine)

=a\,\eta(x)(\Lambda)+(1-a)\,\eta(y)(\Lambda)
.
Additionally, when
\mathfrak{B}
is pointed (and
\mathfrak{C}=\mathfrak{B}^{**}
consists of
linear maps),
\eta
is strict, since

\eta(\bot)(\Lambda)=\Lambda(\bot)=0
for every

\Lambda\in\mathfrak{C}
; therefore
\eta=\eta^{**}_{\mathfrak{B}}
is linear, thanks
to Proposition
4.6
.

3. Let
\mathfrak{B}
be linearly separated. Since
\eta
is continuous, it
is monotonic. In order to see that it is a preorder embedding, we
must show that for every two points
x,y\in\mathfrak{B}
such that

x\not\leq y
,
\eta(x)\not\leq\eta(y)
. In the non-pointed
case, since
\mathfrak{B}
is linearly separated, there is a
\Lambda\in\mathfrak{B}^{\star}

such that
\Lambda(x)>\Lambda(y)
. In the pointed case, we can
take
\Lambda\in\mathfrak{B}^{*}
by Lemma
6.17
, item 1. In
any case, there is a
\Lambda\in\mathfrak{C}
such that

\Lambda(x)>\Lambda(y)
, namely such that

\eta(x)(\Lambda)>\eta(x)(\Lambda)
. Therefore

\eta(x)\not\leq\eta(y)
.

Conversely, if
\eta
is a preorder embedding, then for every two
points
x,y\in\mathfrak{B}
such that
x\not\leq y
,

\eta(x)\not\leq\eta(y)
, so there is a
\Lambda\in\mathfrak{B}^{\star}

(in
\mathfrak{B}^{*}
is
\mathfrak{B}
is pointed) such that

\eta(x)(\Lambda)\not\leq\eta(y)(\Lambda)
. Therefore

\Lambda(x)>\Lambda(y)
, so
\mathfrak{B}
is linearly separated.

4. If
\mathfrak{B}
is locally affine (resp. pointed and locally linear),
then
\mathfrak{B}
has a subbase of open sets of the form

\Lambda^{-1}(]1,\infty])
with
\Lambda\in\mathfrak{C}
, and every one of
them is the inverse image of the open set
[\Lambda>1]
under

\eta
, so
\eta
is full.

Conversely, if
\eta
is full, then a subbase of the topology on

\mathfrak{B}
is given by sets of the form
\eta^{-1}(U)
, where
U
ranges
over any given subbase of the topology of
\mathfrak{B}^{\star*}
(
\mathfrak{B}^{**}

if
\mathfrak{B}
is pointed). By taking the standard subbase consisting of
sets
[\Lambda>r]
with
\Lambda\in\mathfrak{B}^{\star}
(resp.
\mathfrak{B}^{*}
)
and
r\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
, we obtain a subbase of the topology on
\mathfrak{B}

consisting of sets of the form
\eta^{-1}([\Lambda>r])=\{x\in\mathfrak{B}\mid\Lambda(\eta(x))>r\}=(\Lambda\circ\eta)^{-1}(r)
. Since
\eta
and
\Lambda
are both affine (resp. linear),
\mathfrak{B}
is locally linear.

5. If
\mathfrak{B}
is
T_{0}
, then
\mathfrak{B}
is locally affine if and only if

\eta
is full by item 4, hence a topological embedding.
Conversely, if
\eta
is a topological embedding, then
\mathfrak{B}
is
locally affine by item 4, and we claim that
\mathfrak{B}
is
T_{0}
. For all

x,y\in\mathfrak{B}
such that
x\leq y
and
y\leq x
, we have

\eta(x)\leq\eta(y)
and
\eta(y)\leq\eta(x)
, namely

\Lambda(x)\leq\Lambda(y)
and
\Lambda(y)\leq\Lambda(x)

for every
\Lambda\in\mathfrak{B}^{\star}
(resp.
\mathfrak{B}^{*}
); so

\eta(x)=\eta(y)
. Since
\eta
is injective,
x=y
.
\Box

Lemma 6.22

Let
\mathfrak{B}
be a semitopological barycentric algebra (resp. a pointed
semitopological barycentric algebra). Then:

1.

\mathfrak{B}
is linearly separated if and only if there is an affine
(resp. linear) continuous map
\eta\colon\mathfrak{B}\to\mathfrak{C}
of
\mathfrak{B}
to
a locally linear semitopological cone
\mathfrak{C}
that is a preorder
embedding with respect to the underlying specialization orderings.

2.

\mathfrak{B}
is locally affine (resp. locally linear) if and only if
there is an affine (resp. linear) full continuous map

\eta\colon\mathfrak{B}\to\mathfrak{C}
of
\mathfrak{B}
into a locally linear
semitopological cone
\mathfrak{C}
.

3.

\mathfrak{B}
is
T_{0}
and locally affine (resp.
T_{0}
and locally
linear) if and only if it embeds in a locally linear
semitopological cone
\mathfrak{C}
through an affine (resp. linear)
topological embedding.

Proof.

1. If
\eta\colon\mathfrak{B}\to\mathfrak{C}
is affine (resp. linear), continuous
and a preorder embedding, and if
\mathfrak{C}
is a locally linear
semitopological cone, then given any two points
x,y\in\mathfrak{B}
such
that
x\not\leq y
, we have
\eta(x)\not\leq\eta(y)
. Since

\mathfrak{C}
is locally linear, there is an affine lower semicontinuous map

\Lambda\colon\mathfrak{C}\to\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
such that

\Lambda(\eta(x))>\Lambda(\eta(y))
(resp. and we can take

\Lambda
to be linear in the pointed case, thanks to
Lemma
6.17
, item 1). Then
\Lambda\circ\eta
is
affine (resp. linear) and maps
x
to a strictly larger value than

y
. It follows that
\mathfrak{B}
is linearly separated. The converse
implication is by Definition and Lemma
6.21
, item 3.

2. If
\eta\colon\mathfrak{B}\to\mathfrak{C}
is an affine (resp. linear) full
continuous map, where
\mathfrak{C}
is a locally linear semitopological cone,
then every open subset of
\mathfrak{B}
is a union of finite intersections of
sets of the form
\eta^{-1}(V)
with
V
open in
\mathfrak{C}
, and every
such set
V
is a union of finite intersections of sets of the form

\Lambda^{-1}(]1,\infty])
with
\Lambda\colon\mathfrak{C}\to\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}

affine (resp. linear, using Lemma
6.17
, item 1) and
lower semicontinuous. The maps
\Lambda\circ\eta
are then affine
(resp. linear) and lower semicontinuous, and since the
corresponding sets
(\Lambda\circ\eta)^{-1}(]1,\infty])
form a
subbase of the topology of
\mathfrak{B}
,
\mathfrak{B}
is locally affine (resp. locally linear). The converse implication is by Definition and
Lemma
6.21
, item 4.

3. Immediate consequence of item 2, since full maps with a
T_{0}

domain are topological embeddings.
\Box

Corollary 6.23

Every locally affine
T_{0}
semitopological barycentric algebra is
embeddable. Every locally linear
T_{0}
pointed semitopological
barycentric algebra is strictly embeddable.

Local linearity does not just imply local convexity, but also
topologicity.

Proposition 6.24

Every locally affine semitopological barycentric algebra
\mathfrak{B}
, in
particular every locally linear pointed semitopological barycentric
algebra, and every locally linear semitopological cone is locally
convex and topological.

Proof.

Local convexity is obvious, since
\Lambda^{-1}(]1,\infty])
is
convex for every affine lower semicontinuous map

\Lambda\colon\mathfrak{B}\to\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
. Let
f
be the function

(x,a,y)\mapsto x+_{a}y
from
\mathfrak{B}\times[0,1]\times\mathfrak{B}
to

\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
. For every affine (resp. linear) lower semicontinuous map

\Lambda\colon\mathfrak{B}\to\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
, let

(x,a,y)\in f^{-1}(\Lambda^{-1}(]1,\infty]))
. Hence

\Lambda(x+_{a}y)>1
, namely

a\,\Lambda(x)+(1-a)\,\Lambda(y)>1
. Since
\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
is a
topological cone (Example
2.2
), there is an open
neighborhood
U
of
\Lambda(x)
in
\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
, an open neighborhood

V
of
\Lambda(y)
in
\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
and an an open neighborhood
I
of

a
in
[0,1]
such that
a^{\prime}\,s+(1-a^{\prime})\,t>1
for all

(s,a^{\prime},t^{\prime})\in U\times I\times V
. In particular, for all

(x^{\prime},a^{\prime},y^{\prime})\in\Lambda^{-1}(U)\times I\times\Lambda^{-1}(V)
,
x^{\prime}+_{a^{\prime}}y^{\prime}\in\Lambda^{-1}(]1,\infty])
, so

(x^{\prime},a^{\prime},y^{\prime})\in f^{-1}(\Lambda^{-1}(]1,\infty]))
.

It remains to see that
\Lambda^{-1}(U)
is open in
\mathfrak{B}
: if
U
is
of the form
]r,\infty]
with
r>0
, then

\Lambda^{-1}(U)=((1/r)\cdot\Lambda)^{-1}(]1,\infty])
, which
is open since
(1/r)\cdot\Lambda
is affine (resp. linear) and
lower semicontinuous; if
U=]0,\infty]
, then

\Lambda^{-1}(U)=\bigcup_{r>0}\Lambda^{-1}(]r,\infty])
, and
if
U=[0,\infty]
then
\Lambda^{-1}(U)=\mathfrak{B}
. Similarly with

\Lambda^{-1}(V)
.
\Box

Remark 6.25

Local linearity is strictly stronger than local convexity. Let us
consider any continuous complete lattice
L
, namely any complete
lattice that is continuous as a dcpo. Being a pointed
sup-semilattice,
L
is a cone and
L_{\sigma}
is a semitopological
cone (see Example
2.4
). It is shown in

[
15
, Remark 3.15]
that letting
L\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}{\mathcal{O}}\mathbb{R}
,
where
\mathbb{R}
has its usual metric topology, is locally convex but
not locally linear.

We will give another example of a non-locally linear cone in
Example
6.39
.

6.3
Dual cones

We have introduced the topological cones
\mathfrak{B}^{\star}
and
\mathfrak{B}^{*}
in
Definition and Lemma
6.21
. We call the topology defined
there the
weak
∗
Scott topology
. This is the coarsest
topology that makes the map

\Lambda\in\mathfrak{B}^{*}\text{ (resp.\ }\in\mathfrak{B}^{\star}\text{) }\mapsto\Lambda(x)
lower semicontinuous for every
x\in\mathfrak{B}
, and extends
the notion of dual cone
\mathfrak{C}^{*}
of a semitopological cone

[
30
, Example 5.5]
.

Remark 6.26

Given a pointed semitopological barycentric algebra
\mathfrak{B}
, the sets

[x>1]
,
x\in\mathfrak{B}
, already form a subbase of the weak
∗
Scott
topology on
\mathfrak{B}^{*}
, since for every
r\in\mathbb{R}_{+}\smallsetminus\{0\}
,

[x>r]
can be expressed as
[(1/r)\cdot x>1]
.

Lemma 6.27

Let
\mathfrak{B}
be a (resp. pointed) semitopological barycentric algebra .
The specialization ordering of
\mathfrak{B}^{\star}
(resp.
\mathfrak{B}^{*}
) with its
weak
∗
Scott topology is the pointwise ordering.

Proof.

Because
\mathfrak{B}^{\star}
(resp.
\mathfrak{B}^{*}
) is a subspace of

{(\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+})}^{\mathfrak{B}}
, with the product topology, whose specialization
ordering is pointwise.
\Box

Example 6.28

For every topological space
X
,
{\mathbf{V}}X
is isomorphic to

(\mathcal{L}X)^{*}
as a topological cone
[
33
, Satz 8.1]
. In
one direction, every
\nu\in{\mathbf{V}}X
is mapped to the linear lower
semicontinuous functional
h\in\mathcal{L}X\mapsto\int h\,d\nu
.
In the other direction, every linear lower semicontinuous functional

\Lambda
is mapped to
U\in{\mathcal{O}}X\mapsto\Lambda(\chi_{U})
,
where
\chi_{U}
is the characteristic function of
U
.

Several properties of
{\mathbf{V}}X
generalize to dual cones.

Proposition 6.29

For every (resp. pointed) semitopological barycentric algebra
\mathfrak{B}
,

\mathfrak{B}^{\star}
(resp.
\mathfrak{B}^{*}
) is a locally linear topological cone.
In particular, for every semitopological cone
\mathfrak{C}
,
\mathfrak{C}^{*}
is a
locally linear topological cone.

Proof.

\mathfrak{B}^{\star}
(resp.
\mathfrak{B}^{*}
) is a topological cone by Definition and
Lemma
6.21
. The sets
[x>r]
with
x\in\mathfrak{B}
and

r\in\mathbb{R}_{+}\smallsetminus\{0\}
are open half-spaces and form a subbase of
the topology, so
\mathfrak{B}^{*}
(resp.
\mathfrak{B}^{*}
) is locally linear.
\Box

Another property is sobriety (see
[
19
, Chapter 8]
). A
closed set
C
is
irreducible
if and only if it is non-empty,
and for any two closed subsets
C_{1}
and
C_{2}
such that

C\subseteq C_{1}\cup C_{2}
,
C
is included in
C_{1}
or in
C_{2}
. For
every point
x\in X
,
\dc x
is irreducible closed. A space in
which those are the only irreducible closed subsets is called

quasi-sober
. A
sober space
is a
T_{0}
quasi-sober
space. All
T_{2}
spaces, all continuous dcpos are sober. Every
retract of a sober space is sober, every equalizer

[f=g]\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\{x\in X\mid f(x)=g(x)\}
of two continuous maps

f,g\colon X\to Y
where
X
is sober and
Y
is
T_{0}
is itself
sober.

Now
{\mathbf{V}}X
is sober
[
23
, Proposition 5.1]
, and this
generalizes as follows. The proof is the same as the one Heckmann
gives for
{\mathbf{V}}X
.

Theorem 6.30

For every (resp. pointed) semitopological barycentric algebra
\mathfrak{B}
,

\mathfrak{B}^{\star}
(resp.
\mathfrak{B}^{*}
) is sober. In particular,
\mathfrak{C}^{*}
is
sober for every semitopological cone
\mathfrak{C}
.

Proof.

\mathfrak{B}^{\star}
is a subspace of
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{p}}\mathfrak{B}
(see
Example
6.3
), which is sober, see

[
52
, Lemma 5.8]
for example. This is the subspace
of elements
h
satisfying the equations

h(x+_{a}y)=a\,h(x)+(1-a)\,h(y)
for all
x,y\in\mathfrak{B}
and

a\in[0,1]
, hence it occurs as the equalizer
[f=g]
of the
continuous maps
f,g\colon\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{p}}\mathfrak{B}\to\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}^{I}
, where

I\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\mathfrak{B}\times[0,1]\times\mathfrak{B}
,

f(h)\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}{(h(x+_{a}y))}_{(x,a,y)\in I}
,

g(h)\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}{(a\,h(x)+(1-a)\,h(y))}_{(x,a,y)\in I}
for
every
h\in\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{p}}X
.

We reason similarly with
\mathfrak{B}^{*}
when
\mathfrak{B}
is pointed, using the
additional equation
\Lambda(\bot)=0
. We recall that a linear
map is the same thing as a strict affine map, by
Proposition
4.6
.
\Box

Dually to Example
6.28
, we must mention the

Schröder-Simpson theorem
, announced in

[
44
,
45
]
. The first published proof is due to
Keimel
[
31
]
, and an elementary proof can be found in

[
20
]
. Precisely, Theorem 2.3 (resp. Corollary 2.4) of

[
20
]
states that every linear lower semicontinuous map

\Lambda\colon{\mathbf{V}}_{b}X\to\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
(resp.
\Lambda\colon{\mathbf{V}}X\to\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
) is of the form

\nu\mapsto\int h\,d\nu
for a unique lower semicontinuous map

h\colon X\to\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
; explicitly,
h(x)\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\Lambda(\delta_{x})

for every
x\in X
. This yields a bijection between
({\mathbf{V}}_{b}X)^{*}

(resp.
({\mathbf{V}}X)^{*}
) and
\mathcal{L}X
, which is an isomorphism of
cones. Its inverse maps every
h\in\mathcal{L}X
to

\nu\mapsto\int h\,d\nu
.

This is also a homeomorphism, provided that we equip

\Lambda X
with the weakScott topology, defined as follows.

Definition 6.31
(weakScott topology)

The
weakScott topology
on a semitopological barycentric
algebra
\mathfrak{B}
is the coarsest topology that makes every

\Lambda\in\mathfrak{B}^{\star}
lower semicontinuous. A subbase is given by
the sets
\Lambda^{-1}(]r,\infty])
with
\Lambda\in\mathfrak{B}^{\star}

and
r\in\mathbb{R}_{+}\smallsetminus\{0\}
. We write
\mathfrak{B}_{\mathit{wS}}
for
\mathfrak{B}
with the
weakScott topology.

Remark 6.32

If
\mathfrak{B}
is a pointed semitopological barycentric algebra (for
example, if
\mathfrak{B}
is a semitopological cone
\mathfrak{C}
), then the weakScott
topology is also the coarsest topology that makes every

\Lambda\in\mathfrak{B}^{*}
(namely, where
\Lambda
is linear, not just
affine) lower semicontinuous. A subbase is given by the sets

\Lambda^{-1}(]1,\infty])
with
\Lambda\in\mathfrak{B}^{*}
; equivalently,
when
\mathfrak{B}
is a cone
\mathfrak{C}
, by the open half-spaces of
\mathfrak{C}
. Indeed,
every set
\Lambda^{-1}(]r,\infty])
, where
\Lambda
is affine
and lower semicontinuous and
r>0
, can be rewritten as

{\Lambda_{r}}^{-1}(]1,\infty])
where

\Lambda_{r}(x)\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\frac{1}{r-\Lambda(\bot)}\cdot(\Lambda(x)-\Lambda(\bot))
for every
x\in\mathfrak{B}
if
\Lambda(\bot)<r
; if

\Lambda(\bot)=r
,

\Lambda^{-1}(]r,\infty])=\bigcup_{s>r}\Lambda^{-1}(]s,\infty])=\bigcup_{s>r}\Lambda_{s}^{-1}(]1,\infty])
, and if

\Lambda(\bot)>r
, then
\Lambda^{-1}(]r,\infty])
is the whole
space
\mathfrak{B}
. We note that
\Lambda_{r}
is linear and lower
semicontinuous; linearity is because
\Lambda_{r}
is affine and
strict, and relying on Proposition
4.6
.

Explicitly, the weakScott topology on
({\mathbf{V}}_{b}X)^{*}
, or on
({\mathbf{V}}X)^{*}
, equated with
\mathcal{L}X
, is given by subsets of the form
[\nu>r]\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\{h\in\mathcal{L}X\mid\int h\,d\nu>r\}
, with
\nu\in{\mathbf{V}}_{b}X
(resp.
\nu\in{\mathbf{V}}X
) and
r\in\mathbb{R}_{+}\smallsetminus\{0\}
. We
sum the above mentioned theorem by Schröder and Simpson as follows.

Theorem 6.33

For every topological space
X
,
({\mathbf{V}}_{b}X)^{*}
(resp.
({\mathbf{V}}X)^{*}
) and
(\mathcal{L}X)_{\mathit{wS}}
are isomorphic as topological
cones through the maps

h\in\mathcal{L}X\mapsto(\nu\mapsto\int h\,d\nu)
and

\psi\in({\mathbf{V}}_{b}X)^{*}\text{( resp.}({\mathbf{V}}X)^{*}\text{)}\mapsto(x\in X\mapsto\psi(\delta_{x}))
.

Schröder and Simpson also show that, for every topological space

X
, every linear lower semicontinuous map from
{\mathbf{V}}_{\leq 1}X
to

\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
is of the form
\nu\mapsto\int h\,d\nu
for a unique
lower semicontinuous map
h

[
20
, Corollary 2.5]
, leading
to the following.

Theorem 6.34

For every topological space
X
,
({\mathbf{V}}_{\leq 1}X)^{*}
and

(\mathcal{L}X)_{\mathit{wS}}
are isomorphic as topological cones through the
maps
h\in\mathcal{L}X\mapsto(\nu\mapsto\int h\,d\nu)
and

\psi\in({\mathbf{V}}_{\leq 1}X)^{*}\mapsto(x\in X\mapsto\psi(\delta_{x}))
.

This can also be deduced from the previous theorem and
Theorem
4.30
; we leave this as an exercise (replay the
proof below, replacing Theorem
3.27
by
Theorem
4.30
). The third and final theorem that
Schröder and Simpson show, and which is not mentioned in

[
20
]
, is the following.

Proposition 6.35

For every topological space
X
, every affine lower semicontinuous
map
\psi
from
{\mathbf{V}}_{1}X
to
\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
is of the form

\nu\mapsto\int h\,d\nu
for a unique lower semicontinuous map

h\colon X\to\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
.

Proof.

Uniqueness is trivial: we must have
h(x)=\psi(\delta_{x})
for
every
x\in X
. As for existence, since
{\mathbf{V}}_{b}X
is the free
semitopological cone over the semitopological barycentric algebra

{\mathbf{V}}_{1}X
by Theorem
3.27
,
\psi
has a (unique)
linear, lower semicontinuous extension

\hat{\psi}\colon{\mathbf{V}}_{b}X\to\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
. By
Theorem
6.36
, there is an
h\in\mathcal{L}X
such that
for every
\nu\in{\mathbf{V}}_{b}X
,
\hat{\psi}(\nu)=\int h\,d\nu
.
This holds in particular for every
\nu\in{\mathbf{V}}_{1}X
, for which

\hat{\psi}(\nu)=\psi(\nu)
.
\Box

We obtain the following immediately.

Theorem 6.36

For every topological space
X
,
({\mathbf{V}}_{1}X)^{\star}
and

(\mathcal{L}X)_{\mathit{wS}}
are isomorphic as topological cones through the
maps
h\in\mathcal{L}X\mapsto(\nu\mapsto\int h\,d\nu)
and

\psi\in({\mathbf{V}}_{1}X)^{*}\mapsto(x\in X\mapsto\psi(\delta_{x}))
.

Remark 6.37

The following facts hold about the weakScott topology.

(i)

The weakScott topology is always coarser than the original
topology on
\mathfrak{B}
, and sometimes strictly coarser, even on
semitopological cones (we will illustrate this in
Example
6.39
below).

(ii)

The affine (resp. linear when
\mathfrak{B}
is pointed) lower
semicontinuous maps from
\mathfrak{B}
or from
\mathfrak{B}_{\mathit{wS}}
to
\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
are
the same: notably, for every affine lower semicontinuous map

\Lambda\colon\mathfrak{B}\to\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
,
\Lambda
is also lower
semicontinuous from
\mathfrak{B}_{\mathit{wS}}
to
\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
, because for every

r\in\mathbb{R}_{+}\smallsetminus\{0\}
,
\Lambda^{-1}(]r,\infty])
is open in

\mathfrak{B}_{\mathit{wS}}
by definition.

(iii)

Hence
{(\mathfrak{B}_{\mathit{wS}})}^{\star}=\mathfrak{B}^{\star}
, and

{(\mathfrak{B}_{\mathit{wS}})}^{*}=\mathfrak{B}^{*}
if
\mathfrak{B}
is pointed. In particular,

{(\mathfrak{B}_{\mathit{wS}})}^{**}=\mathfrak{B}^{**}
.

(iv)

\mathfrak{B}_{\mathit{wS}}
is always locally linear, because its topology has
a base of open sets of the form
\Lambda^{-1}(]1,\infty])
with

\Lambda\in\mathfrak{B}^{\star}={(\mathfrak{B}_{\mathit{wS}})}^{\star}
.

(v)

In particular, the weakScott topology coincides with the
original topology on
\mathfrak{B}
if and only if
\mathfrak{B}
is locally linear.

(vi)

On cones
\mathfrak{C}
of the form
\mathcal{L}X
, the weakScott topology
is the coarsest one such that

h\mapsto\int_{x\in X}h(x)\,d\nu
is lower semicontinuous
from
\mathcal{L}X
to
\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
, for every
\nu\in{\mathbf{V}}X
. This
follows from the isomorphism between
{\mathbf{V}}X\cong(\mathcal{L}X)^{*}

of Example
6.28
, and Remark
6.37
.

Lemma 6.38

For every space
X
, the weakScott topology on
\mathcal{L}X
is finer
than the topology of pointwise convergence on
\mathcal{L}X
, and is
coarser than the original, Scott topology on
\mathcal{L}X
. These three
topologies coincide if
X
is a c-space, in particular if
X
is a
continuous dcpo in its Scott topology.

Proof.

For the first part, the subbasic open subset
[x>r]=\{h\in\mathcal{L}X\mid h(x)>r\}
of the topology of pointwise convergence
is equal to the subbasic open subset
[\delta_{x}>r]
of the
weakScott topology.

For the second part, it suffices to show that
[\nu>r]
is
Scott-open for every
\nu\in{\mathbf{V}}X
and every

r\in\mathbb{R}_{+}\smallsetminus\{0\}
, and this is easy.

Finally, if
X
is a c-space, and since
\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
is a bc-domain
(even a complete lattice), then the Scott topology coincides with
the topology of pointwise convergence by
[
21
, Proposition
11.2]
. Since the weakScott topology lies
inbetween, it is equal to both of them.
\Box

Example 6.39

The Scott topology on
\mathcal{L}\mathbb{Q}
is strictly finer than the
weakScott topology. In particular,
\mathcal{L}\mathbb{Q}
is not locally
linear.

Proof.

We proceed in a series of steps.
(A)
For every space
X
, the
topology of pointwise convergence is coarser than the weakScott
topology. Indeed, the subbasic open subset

[x>r]=\{h\in\mathcal{L}X\mid h(x)>r\}
of the topology of
pointwise convergence is equal to the subbasic open subset

[\delta_{x}>r]
of the weakScott topology.

Let a
discrete valuation
be a possibly infinite sum
\sum_{i\in I}a_{i}\delta_{x_{i}}
, where
a_{i}\in\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
(not just
\mathbb{R}_{+}

as with simple valuations).

We claim:
(B)
given any topological space
X
on which every
continuous valuation is discrete,
(\mathcal{L}X)_{\mathit{wS}}=\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{p}}X
,
namely the weakScott topology on
\mathcal{L}X
coincides with the
topology of pointwise convergence. In light of
(A)
, it suffices
to show that
[\nu>r]
is open in the topology of pointwise
convergence, where
\nu
is any continuous valuation and

r\in\mathbb{R}_{+}\smallsetminus\{0\}
. By assumption,
\nu
is of the form

\sum_{i\in I}a_{i}\delta_{x_{i}}
. Without loss of generality, we
can also assume
a_{i}\neq 0
for every
i\in I
. Let

h\in[\nu>r]
, hence
\sum_{i\in I}a_{i}h(x_{i})>r
, or
equivalently
r\ll\sum_{i\in I}a_{i}h(x_{i})
. There is a finite
subset
J
of
I
such that
r<\sum_{i\in J}a_{i}h(x_{i})
.
Because multiplication and addition are continuous on
\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
,
there are numbers
r_{i}
(
i\in J
) such that
r_{i}\ll a_{i}h(x_{i})

for every
i\in J
and
r\ll\sum_{i\in J}r_{i}
. Then

h\in\bigcap_{i\in J}[(r_{i}/a_{i})\ll x_{i}]
(where
[s\ll x]

denotes
[x>s]
if
s>0
,
\bigcup_{t>0}[x>t]
if
s=0
,
hence is open in the topology of pointwise convergence), and for
every
k\in\bigcap_{i\in J}[(r_{i}/a_{i})\ll x_{i}]
,

\sum_{i\in I}a_{i}k(x_{i})\geq\sum_{i\in J}a_{i}k(x_{i})\geq\sum_{i\in J}r_{i}>r
; therefore
k\in[\nu>r]
.

(B)
applies to
X=\mathbb{Q}
. Indeed, let
\nu
be any continuous
valuation on
\mathbb{Q}
. For every
q\in\mathbb{Q}
, let

a_{q}\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\inf_{\epsilon>0}\nu(]q-\epsilon,q+\epsilon[)
. We
verify that
\nu=\sum_{q\in\mathbb{Q}}a_{q}\delta_{q}
, namely that

\nu(U)=\sum_{q\in U\cap\mathbb{Q}}a_{q}
for every open subset

U
of
\mathbb{R}
.

•

Let us check that
\nu(U)\leq\sum_{q\in U\cap\mathbb{Q}}a_{q}
.
If
a_{q}=\infty
for some
q\in U\cap\mathbb{Q}
, this is
obvious. Otherwise, we enumerate the elements of
\mathbb{Q}
as

{(q_{n})}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
. For every
\epsilon>0
, for every

n\in\mathbb{N}
such that
q_{n}\in U
, one can find
\epsilon_{n}>0

such that

a_{q}\geq\nu(]q_{n}-\epsilon_{n},q_{n}+\epsilon_{n}[)-\epsilon/2^{n+1}
. Then
U
is included in

\bigcup_{n\in\mathbb{N},q_{n}\in U}]q_{n}-\epsilon_{n},q_{n}+\epsilon_{n}[
, so

\nu(U)\leq\sum_{n\in\mathbb{N},q_{n}\in U}\nu(]q_{n}-\epsilon_{n},q_{n}+\epsilon_{n}[)

\leq\sum_{n\in\mathbb{N},q_{n}\in U}a_{q_{n}}+\sum_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\epsilon/2^{n+1}=\sum_{q\in U\cap\mathbb{Q}}a_{q}+\epsilon
.
Taking infima over
\epsilon>0
,

\nu(U)\leq\sum_{q\in U\cap\mathbb{Q}}a_{q}
.

•

Conversely, let us check that

\nu(U)\geq\sum_{q\in U\cap\mathbb{Q}}a_{q}
. If
a_{q}=\infty

for some
q\in U\cap\mathbb{Q}
, then, picking
\epsilon>0
such
that
{]q-\epsilon,q+\epsilon[}\subseteq U
, we have

\nu(]q-\epsilon,q+\epsilon[)=\infty
, hence

\nu(U)=\infty
, so that the inequality is obvious. Otherwise,
we show that
\sum_{q\in A}a_{q}\leq\nu(U)
for every finite
subset
A
of
U\cap\mathbb{Q}
. The result will follow by taking
suprema over
A
. We enumerate the finitely many elements of
A

as
q_{1}
, …,
q_{n}
. Let

\epsilon\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\min_{1\leq i<j\leq n}|q_{i}-q_{j}|
. This is
strictly positive since
A
is finite. For each
i
,

1\leq i\leq n
, let
\epsilon_{i}>0
be such that

{]q_{i}-\epsilon_{i},q_{i}+\epsilon_{i}[}\subseteq U
, and such that

\epsilon_{i}<\epsilon/2
. Then all the intervals

]q_{i}-\epsilon_{i},q_{i}+\epsilon_{i}[
are pairwise disjoint, and
therefore

\sum_{q\in A}a_{q}\leq\sum_{i=1}^{n}\nu(]q_{i}-\epsilon_{i},q_{i}+\epsilon_{i}[)=\nu(\bigcup_{i=1}^{n}]q_{i}-\epsilon_{i},q_{i}+\epsilon_{i}[)\leq\nu(U)
.

Next, we claim:
(C)
given any infinite compact subset
Q
of a

T_{1}
topological space
X
,

[Q>1]\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\{h\in\mathcal{L}\mid\forall x\in Q,h(x)>1\}
is
not open in the topology of pointwise convergence. Indeed, let us
imagine that
[Q>1]
contains a basic open set

A\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\{h\in\mathcal{L}X\mid h(x_{1})>r_{1},\cdots,h(x_{n})>r_{n}\}
in the topology of pointwise convergence, where the elements

x_{i}
are pairwise distinct and
r_{1},\cdots,r_{n}>0
. We pick any

x\in Q\smallsetminus\{x_{1},\cdots,x_{n}\}
. This is possible because
Q

is infinite. Since
X
is
T_{1}
, there are open neighborhoods
U_{i}

of
x_{i}
that do not contain
x
, for each
i
,
1\leq i\leq n
. We
pick
s_{1}>r_{1}
, …,
s_{n}>r_{n}
, and we let

h\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\sup_{i=1}^{n}s_{i}\chi_{U_{i}}
. Then
h
is lower
semicontinuous, and
h(x_{i})\geq s_{i}>r_{i}
for every
i
, so
h

is in
A
. However,
h(x)=0
, by construction. It follows that

h
is not in
[Q>1]
.

The sets

[Q>r]\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\{h\in\mathcal{L}\mid\forall x\in Q,h(x)>r\}

with
Q
compact and
r\in\mathbb{R}_{+}\smallsetminus\{0\}
form a subbase of the

compact-open topology
on
\mathcal{L}X
. It is also finer than
the topology of pointwise convergence, because the subbasic open
subsets
[x>r]
of the latter can be written as
[\upc x>r]
.
The compact-open topology is coarser than the Scott
topology. The core of the argument consists in showing
that for every directed family
{(h_{i})}_{i\in I}
with (pointwise)
supremum
h
in
\mathcal{L}X
, if
h\in[Q>r]
then some
h_{i}
is in

[Q>r]
. But
h\in[Q>r]
means that
Q\subseteq h^{-1}(]r,\infty])=\bigcup_{i\in I}h_{i}^{-1}(]r,\infty])
, and since
Q

is compact, there is an
i\in I
such that
Q\subseteq h_{i}^{-1}(]r,\infty])
, that is,
h_{i}\in[Q>r]
.

We conclude. The space
\mathbb{Q}
is Hausdorff hence
T_{1}
. Let

Q\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\{1/2^{n}\mid n\in\mathbb{N}\}\cup\{0\}
. It is easy to see
that
Q
is compact and infinite in
\mathbb{Q}
. By
(C)
, the
compact-open topology on
\mathcal{L}\mathbb{Q}
is strictly finer than the
topology of pointwise convergence. We have just argued that it is
coarser than the Scott topology, so the Scott topology is also
strictly finer than the topology of pointwise convergence. The
latter coincides with the weakScott topology by
(B)
, which applies
to
X=\mathbb{Q}
, as we have seen. We have proved that the weakScott
and Scott topologies differ on
\mathcal{L}\mathbb{Q}
. Hence
\mathcal{L}\mathbb{Q}

is not locally linear, thanks to Remark
6.37
,
item
(v)
.
\Box

6.4
Weak local convexity and barycenters, part 4

The following notion of local convexity was introduced and studied by
Heckmann
[
23
, Section 6.3]
, on cones. The name he used
for that notion was “locally convex”, but that should not be
confused with local convexity, as defined in
Section
6.1
.

Definition 6.40
(Weakly locally convex)

A semitopological barycentric algebra
\mathfrak{B}
is
weakly locally
convex
if and only if every point
x\in\mathfrak{B}
has a neighborhood
base of convex sets, namely if and only if every open neighborhood

U
of
x
contains a convex (not necessarily open) neighborhood of

x
.

Remark 6.41

Every locally convex semitopological barycentric algebra is weakly
locally convex. In the realm of topological real vector spaces,
there would be no difference between the two notions. We will see
that there is no difference either on consistent barycentric
algebras and cones, which are the subject of
Section
7
(see
Corollary
7.10
). See also
Corollary
9.11
for another case where
local convexity is implied by apparently weaker properties.

Problem 6.42

Is every weakly locally convex semitopological barycentric algebra
in fact locally convex? If so, local convexity and weak local
convexity would be the same notion. The same question applies to
semitopological cones.

Problem 6.43

Is every semitopological barycentric algebra weakly locally convex?
If so, the notion would be redundant. There are several examples of
non-locally convex real vector spaces. The most well-known are the
space
\ell^{p}(\mathbb{N})
of sequences of natural numbers with finite

\ell^{p}
norm, with the topology induced by that norm, when

0<p<1

[
54
, page 768]
, or the space of
Lebesgue measurable maps from
[0,1]
to
\mathbb{R}
up to measure
0

under the norm
||f||\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\int_{0}^{1}|f(t)|/(1+|f(t)|)\,dt

[
38
]
. None of these examples seem to adapt to
the case of cones or of barycentric algebras.

The following is due to Heckmann in the case of cones

[
23
, Theorems 6.7, 6.8]
. The map
\beta
constructed
below is the
barycenter map
, and
\beta(\nu)
is the

barycenter
of
\nu
.

Theorem 6.44
(Barycenters, part 4)

Let
\mathfrak{C}
be an ordered cone (resp. an ordered pointed barycentric
algebra
\mathfrak{B}
, resp. an ordered barycentric algebra
\mathfrak{B}
).

There is a unique linear (resp. linear, resp. affine) map
\beta

from
{\mathbf{V}}_{\mathrm{f}}\mathfrak{C}
(resp.
{\mathbf{V}}_{\leq 1,\mathrm{f}}\mathfrak{B}
, resp.
{\mathbf{V}}_{1,\mathrm{f}}\mathfrak{B}
) to
\mathfrak{C}
(resp., to
\mathfrak{B}
) such that

\beta(\delta_{x})=x
for every
x\in\mathfrak{C}
(resp., in
\mathfrak{B}
), and it
is monotonic. Explicitly,

\beta(\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\delta_{x_{i}})=\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\cdot x_{i}

for every simple (resp. simple subnormalized, resp. simple
normalized) valuation
\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\delta_{x_{i}}
.

If
\mathfrak{C}
(resp.
\mathfrak{B}
) is a weakly locally convex
T_{0}

semitopological cone (resp., a weakly locally convex
T_{0}
pointed
semitopological barycentric algebra, resp. a weakly locally convex

T_{0}
semitopological barycentric algebra), then
\beta
is
continuous.

The notation
\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\cdot x_{i}
is clear if
\mathfrak{C}
is a cone;
it is justified by Definition and Proposition
3.1
in the
case of barycentric algebras and by Definition and
Proposition
4.41
in the case of pointed barycentric
algebras.

Proof.

If
\beta
exists and
\mathfrak{C}
is a cone, then linearity implies that

\beta(\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\delta_{x_{i}})=\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\cdot x_{i}

for every simple valuation
\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\delta_{x_{i}}
, showing
uniqueness. When
\mathfrak{C}
is an barycentric algebra and

\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}=1
, we claim that we must have a similar
formula, by induction on
n\geq 1
, and using Definition and
Proposition
3.1
: if
a_{n}=1
, then
a_{i}=0
for every

i\neq n
, so
\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\delta_{x_{i}}=\delta_{x_{n}}
, whose
image under
\beta
must be equal to

x_{n}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\cdot x_{i}
; otherwise,

\beta(\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\delta_{x_{i}})=\beta((1-a_{n})\cdot\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{a_{i}}{1-a_{n}}\delta_{x_{i}}+a_{n}\delta_{x_{n}})=\beta(\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{a_{i}}{1-a_{n}}\delta_{x_{i}})+_{1-a_{n}}\beta(\delta_{x_{n}})=(\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}\frac{a_{i}}{1-a_{n}}\cdot x_{i})+_{1-a_{n}}x_{n}
(by induction hypothesis and since

\beta(\delta_{x_{n}})=x_{n}
)
=\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\cdot x_{i}
. When

\mathfrak{C}
is a pointed barycentric algebra, we use Definition and
Proposition
4.41
, then

\beta(\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\delta_{x_{i}})
must be equal to

(\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i})\cdot\beta(\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{a_{i}}{\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}}\delta_{x_{i}})=(\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i})\cdot(\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{a_{i}}{\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}}\cdot x_{i})=\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\cdot x_{i}
if
\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\neq 0
, since

\beta
is linear; if
\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}=0
,

\beta(\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\delta_{x_{i}})=\beta(0)=\beta(0\cdot\delta_{\bot})=0\cdot\bot=\bot
.

In order to show that
\beta
exists, we simply define

\beta(\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\cdot x_{i})
as

\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\cdot x_{i}
; if
\mathfrak{C}
is a barycentric algebra and

\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}=1
, we rely on Definition and
Proposition
3.1
to make sense of it, and if
\mathfrak{C}
is a
pointed barycentric algebra and
\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\leq 1
, we rely
on Definition and Proposition
4.41
. We additionally
need to show that if

\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\delta_{x_{i}}=\sum_{j=1}^{m}b_{j}\delta_{y_{j}}
,
then
\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\cdot x_{i}=\sum_{j=1}^{m}b_{j}\cdot y_{j}
. We
will show that if

\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\delta_{x_{i}}\leq\sum_{j=1}^{m}b_{j}\delta_{y_{j}}
,
then
\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\cdot x_{i}\leq\sum_{j=1}^{m}b_{j}\cdot y_{j}
,
which will show this (since

\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\delta_{x_{i}}=\sum_{j=1}^{m}b_{j}\delta_{y_{j}}
will
imply that

\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\cdot x_{i}\leq\sum_{j=1}^{m}b_{j}\cdot y_{j}
and

\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\cdot x_{i}\geq\sum_{j=1}^{m}b_{j}\cdot y_{j}
, and
since
\mathfrak{C}
is ordered, namely since
\leq
is antisymmetric), and
also that
\beta
is monotonic.

We use Jones’ splitting lemma
[
26
, Theorem 4.10]
, see
also
[
13
, Proposition IV-9.18]
. We have already
used it in the proof of Proposition
3.26
. By
this splitting lemma, there is a transport matrix

{(t_{ij})}_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq i\leq n\\
1\leq j\leq m\end{subarray}}
of
non-negative real numbers such that:
(a)
the only non-zero numbers

t_{ij}
are such that
x_{i}\leq y_{j}
,
(b)

\sum_{j=1}^{m}t_{ij}=a_{i}
for every
i\in\{1,\cdots,n\}
, and

(c)

\sum_{i=1}^{n}t_{ij}\leq b_{j}
for every

j\in\{1,\cdots,m\}
. In case
\mathfrak{C}
is a (pointed) ordered
barycentric algebra
\mathfrak{B}
, we use the injective affine preorder
embedding
\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{B}}
of
\mathfrak{C}=\mathfrak{B}
into
\mathrm{cone}(\mathfrak{B})
of
Proposition
3.4
, and we obtain:

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\cdot\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{B}}(x_{i})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq i\leq n\\
1\leq j\leq m\end{subarray}}t_{ij}\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{B}}(x_{i})

by
(b)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq i\leq n\\
1\leq j\leq m\\
x_{i}\leq y_{j}\end{subarray}}t_{ij}\cdot\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{B}}(x_{i})

by
(a)

\displaystyle\leq^{\mathrm{cone}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq i\leq n\\
1\leq j\leq m\\
x_{i}\leq y_{j}\end{subarray}}t_{ij}\cdot\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{B}}(y_{j})

since
\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{B}}
is monotonic

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq i\leq n\\
1\leq j\leq m\end{subarray}}t_{ij}\cdot\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{B}}(y_{j})

by
(a)

\displaystyle\leq^{\mathrm{cone}}\sum_{j=1}^{m}b_{j}\cdot\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{B}}(y_{j})

by
(c)
,

and since the cone operations on
\mathrm{cone}(\mathfrak{B})
are monotonic.
Since
\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{B}}
is an affine preorder embedding, we conclude that

\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\cdot x_{i}\leq\sum_{j=1}^{m}b_{j}\cdot y_{j}
. In
case
\mathfrak{C}
is an ordered cone, the proof is the same, simply removing
all mention of
\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{B}}
.

We have shown that
\beta
is well-defined and monotonic. When
\mathfrak{C}

is a cone, it is clearly linear. When
\mathfrak{C}
is a barycentric
algebra, we show that
\beta
is affine as follows. Let

a\in[0,1]
and
\mu\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\delta_{x_{i}}

\nu\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}b_{i}\delta_{x_{i}}
be in

{\mathbf{V}}_{1,\mathrm{f}}\mathfrak{B}
, where
\mu
and
\nu
are built using the same
list of points
x_{i}
, without loss of generality, and
n\geq 1
.
Using
\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{B}}
as above,

\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{B}}(\beta(\mu+_{a}\nu))=\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{B}}(\sum_{i=1}^{n}(aa_{i}+(1-a)b_{i})\delta_{x_{i}})=\sum_{i=1}^{n}(aa_{i}+(1-a)b_{i})\cdot\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{B}}(x_{i})
, while

\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{B}}(\beta(\mu)+_{a}\beta(\nu))=a\cdot\beta(\mu)+(1-a)\cdot\beta(\nu)
(since
\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{B}}
is affine)

=a\cdot\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\cdot\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{B}}(x_{i})+(1-a)\cdot\sum_{i=1}^{n}b_{i}\cdot\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{B}}(x_{i})
, and that is the same
value. Since
\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{B}}
is injective,

\beta(\mu+_{a}\nu)=\beta(\mu)+_{a}\beta(\nu)
. When
\mathfrak{C}
is
an ordered pointed barycentric algebra
\mathfrak{B}
, additionally, the least
element of
{\mathbf{V}}_{\leq 1,\mathrm{f}}\mathfrak{B}
is the zero valuation
0
, and
then
\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{B}}(\beta(0))
is equal to
\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{B}}(\bot)
by
Definition and Proposition
4.41
, item 3; since

\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{B}}
is injective,
\beta(0)=\bot
, showing that
\beta

is strict, hence linear, by Proposition
4.6
.

We now claim that
\beta
is continuous, assuming additionally that

\mathfrak{C}
is weakly locally convex,
T_{0}
and topological (as a cone,
resp. a pointed barycentric algebra, resp. a barycentric algebra).
Let
\nu\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\sum_{i\in I}a_{i}\delta_{x_{i}}
, where
I
is
finite,
a_{i}>0
and
x_{i}\in\mathfrak{C}
for each
i\in I
, and let
V

be an open neighborhood of
\beta(\nu)
. Our aim is to find an
open neighborhood
\mathcal{U}
of
\mu
included in

\beta^{-1}(V)
.

Let
m
be the cardinality of
I
. If
\mathfrak{C}
is a topological
cone—not just a semitopological cone—, the map that sends

(\vec{c},\vec{z})\in{(\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+})}^{m}\times\mathfrak{C}^{m}
to

\sum_{i\in I}c_{i}\cdot z_{i}
is jointly continuous, so there are
open neighborhoods
]r_{i},\infty]
of
a_{i}
in
\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
(
r_{i}>0
)
and
V_{i}
of
x_{i}
in
\mathfrak{C}
, for each
i\in I
, such that for all

c_{i}>r_{i}
and
z_{i}\in V_{i}
,
i\in I
,

\sum_{i\in I}c_{i}\cdot z_{i}
is in
V
. If
\mathfrak{C}
is a topological
barycentric algebra, the similar map

(\vec{c},\vec{z})\mapsto\sum_{i\in I}c_{i}\cdot z_{i}
is jointly
continuous from
\Delta_{m}\times\mathfrak{C}^{m}
to
\mathfrak{C}
by
Proposition
3.28
. Additionally,
Lemma
3.29
tells us that
\Delta_{m}
has the
subspace topology induced by the inclusion in
{(\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+})}^{m}
, so
there are open neighborhoods
]r_{i},\infty]
of
a_{i}
in
\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}

(
r_{i}>0
) and
V_{i}
of
x_{i}
in
\mathfrak{C}
, for each
i\in I
, such
that for all
\vec{c}\in\Delta_{m}
with
c_{i}>r_{i}
for every

i\in I
, and for all
z_{i}\in V_{i}
with
i\in I
,

\sum_{i\in I}c_{i}\cdot z_{i}
is in
V
, as in the cone case. If

\mathfrak{C}
is a pointed topological barycentric algebra, then we use
Proposition
4.43
:

(\vec{c},\vec{z})\mapsto\sum_{i\in I}c_{i}\cdot z_{i}
is jointly
continuous from
\widetilde{\Delta}_{m}\times\mathfrak{C}^{m}
to
\mathfrak{C}
, and
therefore there are open neighborhoods
]r_{i},\infty]
of
a_{i}
in

\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
(
r_{i}>0
) and
V_{i}
of
x_{i}
in
\mathfrak{C}
, for each

i\in I
, such that for all
\vec{c}\in\widetilde{\Delta}_{m}
with

c_{i}>r_{i}
for every
i\in I
, and for all
z_{i}\in V_{i}
with

i\in I
,
\sum_{i\in I}c_{i}\cdot z_{i}
is in
V
, as in the cone
and topological barycentric algebra cases.

By weak local convexity, we can pick a further convex neighborhood

C_{i}
of
z_{i}
included in
V_{i}
for each
i\in I
. We also pick
real numbers
c_{i}
such that
r_{i}<c_{i}<a_{i}
for every
i\in I
.

For every non-empty subset
A
of
I
, let

V_{A}\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\bigcup_{i\in A}\text{int}({C_{i}})
and

c_{A}\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\sum_{i\in A}c_{i}
. We define
\mathcal{U}
as

\bigcap_{A\subseteq I,A\neq\emptyset}[V_{A}>c_{A}]
, seen as an
open subset of
{\mathbf{V}}_{\mathrm{f}}\mathfrak{C}
, or
{\mathbf{V}}_{1,\mathrm{f}}\mathfrak{C}
, or
{\mathbf{V}}_{\leq 1,\mathrm{f}}\mathfrak{C}
, depending on the case.

For every non-empty subset
A
of
I
,
V_{A}
contains every
z_{i}

with
i\in A
, so
\nu(V_{A})\geq\sum_{i\in A}a_{i}>c_{A}
, and
this shows that
\nu
is in
\mathcal{U}
.

We must now show that
\mathcal{U}
is included in
\beta^{-1}(V)
.
Let
\mu\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\sum_{j\in J}b_{j}\delta_{y_{j}}
, where
J
is a
finite set,
b_{j}\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
and
y_{j}\in\mathfrak{C}
for every
j\in J
, and
let us assume that
\mu
is in
\mathcal{U}
. By definition,

\mu(V_{A})>c_{A}
for every non-empty subset
A
of
I
, that is:

(*)

\sum_{j\in J,y_{j}\in V_{A}}b_{j}>\sum_{i\in A}c_{i}
. We
will now use the splitting lemma in a non-standard way—but in the
same way that Heckmann used it
[
23
, Theorem 6.7]
.

Let us consider the disjoint sum
I+J
, with the ordering

\sqsubseteq
defined by: for all
i\in I
and
j\in J
,

i\sqsubseteq j
if and only if
y_{j}\in\text{int}({C_{i}})
; no

j\in J
is below any
i\in I
, all elements of
I
are pairwise
incomparable, and all elements of
J
are pairwise incomparable.
For every non-empty
A\subseteq I
, let
A^{+}
be the set of indices

j\in J
such that
i\sqsubseteq j
for some
i\in A
. Then

A^{+}=\{j\in J\mid\exists i\in A,y_{j}\in\text{int}({C_{i}})\}=\{j\in J\mid y_{j}\in V_{A}\}
. Property
(*)
, proved above, states
that
\sum_{i\in A}c_{i}<\sum_{j\in A^{+}}b_{j}
, for every
non-empty subset
A
of
I
. By Jones’ splitting lemma, there is a
transport matrix
{(t_{ij})}_{i\in I,j\in J}
whose only non-zero
entries are such that
i\sqsubseteq j
, such that

\sum_{j\in J}t_{ij}=c_{i}
for every
i\in I
, and

\sum_{i\in I}t_{ij}\leq b_{j}
for every
j\in J
.

Using the latter inequality,

\mu=\sum_{j\in J}b_{j}\delta_{y_{j}}\geq\sum_{i\in I,j\in J}t_{ij}\delta_{y_{j}}=\sum_{i\in I}\sum_{j\in J}t_{ij}\delta_{y_{j}}=\sum_{i\in I}\sum_{j\in J,y_{j}\in\text{int}({C_{i}})}t_{ij}\delta_{y_{j}}
—the latter equality rests on the
fact that only the entries with
i\sqsubseteq j
, namely

y_{j}\in\text{int}({C_{i}})
, are non-zero. We will show that

\beta(\mu)\in V
, distinguishing the cases where
\mathfrak{C}
is a cone
or a (pointed) barycentric algebra.

If
\mathfrak{C}
is a cone, since
\beta
is monotonic,

\beta(\mu)\geq\sum_{i\in I}\sum_{j\in J,y_{j}\in\text{int}({C_{i}})}t_{ij}\cdot y_{j}
. For each
i\in I
,

z_{i}\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\sum_{j\in J,y_{j}\in\text{int}({C_{i}})}\frac{t_{ij}}{c_{i}}\cdot y_{j}
is a linear combination of points
y_{j}
that are
all in the convex set
C_{i}
, with coefficients that sum up to

\sum_{j\in J,i\sqsubseteq j}\frac{t_{ij}}{c_{i}}=\sum_{j\in J}\frac{t_{ij}}{c_{i}}=1
(since
t_{ij}=0
when

i\not\sqsubseteq j
). Therefore
z_{i}
is in
C_{i}
for every

i\in I
. Moreover,

\beta(\mu)\geq\sum_{i\in I}c_{i}\cdot z_{i}
. Since

z_{i}\in C_{i}\subseteq V_{i}
and
c_{i}>r_{i}
for each
i\in I
, by
definition of
V_{i}
and
r_{i}
we have

\sum_{i\in I}c_{i}\cdot z_{i}\in V
. Since
V
is upwards-closed,

\beta(\mu)
is in
V
.

If
\mathfrak{C}
is a pointed barycentric algebra
\mathfrak{B}
,

\sum_{j\in J}b_{j}\leq 1
and
\sum_{i\in I}c_{i}\leq 1
, then we
embed
\mathfrak{B}
in
\mathrm{cone}(\mathfrak{B})
through the injective affine preorder
embedding
\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{B}}
(see
Proposition
3.4
). From

\mu\geq\sum_{i\in I,j\in J,y_{j}\in\text{int}({C_{i}})}t_{ij}\delta_{y_{j}}
and since
\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{B}}\circ\beta
, we obtain that

\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{B}}(\beta(\mu))\geq\sum_{i\in I,j\in J,y_{j}\in\text{int}({C_{i}})}t_{ij}\cdot\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{B}}(y_{j})
. (The computation
of
\beta(\mu)
is valid in this case, in other words, we apply

\beta
to a subnormalized simple valuation, since

\sum_{i\in I,j\in J,y_{j}\in\text{int}({C_{i}})}t_{ij}\leq\sum_{j\in J}b_{j}\leq 1
.) We define
z_{i}
as above, so that

z_{i}\in C_{i}\subseteq V_{i}
, and then

\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{B}}(\beta(\mu))\geq\sum_{i\in I}c_{i}\cdot\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{B}}(z_{i})=\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{B}}(\sum_{i\in I}c_{i}\cdot z_{i})
. Since

\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{B}}
is a preorder embedding,

\beta(\mu)\geq\sum_{i\in I}c_{i}\cdot z_{i}
. Since each
c_{i}

is in
V_{i}
and
c_{i}>r_{i}
,
\sum_{i\in I}c_{i}\cdot z_{i}
is in

V
, and then
\beta(\mu)\in V
since
V
is upwards-closed, as in
the cone case.

If
\mathfrak{C}
is a (not necessarily pointed) barycentric algebra
\mathfrak{B}
and

\sum_{j\in J}b_{j}=\sum_{i\in I}c_{i}=1
, the argument is the
same. We only have to pay attention to the validity of the
computation of
\beta(\mu)
, which relies on the fact that

\sum_{i\in I,j\in J,y_{j}\in\text{int}({C_{i}})}t_{ij}=\sum_{i\in I,j\in J,i\sqsubseteq j}t_{ij}=\sum_{i\in I,j\in J}t_{ij}
(since
t_{ij}=0
when
i\not\sqsubseteq j
)

=\sum_{i\in I}c_{i}=1
.
\Box

6.5
Linear and affine retractions

A
retraction

r\colon X\to Y
is a continuous map between
topological spaces such that there is continuous map

s\colon Y\to X
(the
section
) with
r(s(y))=y
for every

y\in Y
.
Y
is then a
retract
of
X
. It is easy to see
that a retract of a
T_{0}
space is
T_{0}
. Retracts preserve many
other properties, and we will state those we need along the way.

The notion of linear retractions below is due to Heckmann

[
23
, Proposition 6.6]
, and affine retractions are the
natural extension to barycentric algebras.

Definition 6.45
(Linear retraction, affine retraction)

An
affine retraction
is any retraction
r\colon\mathfrak{B}\to\mathfrak{A}

between semitopological barycentric algebras that is also affine.
Then
\mathfrak{A}
is a
affine retract
of
\mathfrak{B}
.

A
strict affine retraction
, also known as a
linear
retraction
, is an affine retraction between pointed
semitopological algebras that is strict. If
r\colon\mathfrak{B}\to\mathfrak{A}

is a linear retraction, then
\mathfrak{A}
is a
linear retract
of

\mathfrak{B}
.

We remember that strict affine maps and linear maps between pointed
barycentric algebras are the same thing, by
Proposition
4.6
. Hence a linear retraction
r
is
simply one that is linear as a function.

Remark 6.46

In Definition
6.45
, beware that we do not require the
associated section to be affine, strict or linear in any way.

Remark 6.47

Given a pointed
T_{0}
semitopological barycentric algebra
\mathfrak{A}
,
every affine retraction
r\colon\mathfrak{B}\to\mathfrak{A}
from a pointed
semitopological barycentric algebra is strict, hence linear.
Indeed, letting
s
be the associated section, for every

y\in\mathfrak{A}
, we have
\bot\leq s(y)
, hence

r(\bot)\leq r(s(y))=y
, since
r
, being continuous, is
monotonic. Hence
r(\bot)
is least in
\mathfrak{A}
, and is the unique
least element in
\mathfrak{A}
because
\mathfrak{A}
is
T_{0}
.

Proposition 6.48

The following hold.

1.

Every affine retract of a weakly locally convex
semitopological barycentric algebra is weakly locally convex; in
particular, every linear retract of a weakly locally convex
pointed semitopological barycentric algebra (resp. of a weakly
locally convex semitopological cone) is weakly locally convex.

2.

Every affine retract of a topological barycentric algebra is
topological; in particular, every linear retract of a pointed
topological barycentric algebra (resp. of a topological cone) is
topological.

3.

Every weakly locally convex
T_{0}
topological barycentric
algebra
\mathfrak{B}
is an affine retract of the
T_{0}
locally affine
topological barycentric algebra
{\mathbf{V}}_{1,\mathrm{f}}\mathfrak{B}
, through the
retraction
\beta\colon{\mathbf{V}}_{1,\mathrm{f}}\mathfrak{B}\to\mathfrak{B}
and the section

\eta_{\mathfrak{B}}\colon x\mapsto\delta_{x}
.

If
\mathfrak{B}
is a weakly locally convex pointed
T_{0}
topological
barycentric algebra, then
\mathfrak{B}
is a linear retract of the
T_{0}

locally linear pointed topological barycentric algebra

{\mathbf{V}}_{\leq 1,\mathrm{f}}\mathfrak{B}
through

\beta\colon{\mathbf{V}}_{\leq 1,\mathrm{f}}\mathfrak{B}\to\mathfrak{B}
and
\eta_{\mathfrak{B}}
.

If
\mathfrak{B}
is a weakly locally convex
T_{0}
topological cone
\mathfrak{C}
,
then
\mathfrak{C}
is a linear retract of the
T_{0}
locally linear
topological cone
{\mathbf{V}}_{\mathrm{f}}\mathfrak{C}
through

\beta\colon{\mathbf{V}}_{\mathrm{f}}\mathfrak{C}\to\mathfrak{C}
and
\eta_{\mathfrak{C}}
.

As consequences,

4.

the weakly locally convex
T_{0}
topological barycentric
algebras are exactly the semitopological barycentric algebras
\mathfrak{B}

that occur as affine retracts of locally linear
T_{0}
topological
barycentric algebras;

5.

the weakly locally convex
T_{0}
pointed topological
barycentric algebras are exactly the semitopological barycentric
algebras
\mathfrak{B}
that occur as linear retracts of locally linear

T_{0}
pointed topological barycentric algebras;

6.

the weakly locally convex pointed
T_{0}
topological cones are
exactly the semitopological cones
\mathfrak{C}
that occur as linear
retracts of locally convex
T_{0}
topological, or even of locally
linear
T_{0}
topological cones.

Proof.

Let
r\colon\mathfrak{B}\to\mathfrak{A}
be an affine retraction, with associated
section
s
, from a semitopological barycentric algebra
\mathfrak{B}
onto a
semitopological barycentric algebra
\mathfrak{A}
.

1. Let
\mathfrak{B}
be weakly locally convex,
y\in\mathfrak{A}
and
V
be an
open neighborhood of
y
in
\mathfrak{A}
. Then
r^{-1}(V)
is an open
neighborhood of
s(y)
. Since
\mathfrak{B}
is weakly locally convex, there
is a convex subset
C
of
r^{-1}(V)
whose interior contains

s(y)
. Then
s^{-1}(\text{int}({C}))
is an open neighborhood of

y
. The image
r[C]
is convex because
r
is affine, and is
included in
V
. Moreover,
s^{-1}(\text{int}({C}))
is included in

r[C]
: for every
z\in s^{-1}(\text{int}({C}))
,
z=r(s(z))
is
the image of
s(z)\in\text{int}({C})\subseteq C
under
r
.
Therefore
\mathfrak{D}
is weakly locally convex.

2. We now assume that
\mathfrak{B}
is topological. Let

f\colon\mathfrak{A}\times[0,1]\times\mathfrak{A}\to\mathfrak{A}
map
(x,a,y)

to
x+_{a}y
in
\mathfrak{A}
, and let
g
be the similarly defined
function on
\mathfrak{B}
. We know that
g
is continuous, and we wish to
show that
f
is, too. For all
x,y\in\mathfrak{A}
and
a\in[0,1]
,

f(x,a,y)=x+_{a}y=r(s(x))+_{a}r(s(y))=r(s(x)+_{a}s(y))=r(g(s(x),a,s(y)))
since
r
is affine. Hence

f=r\circ g\circ(s\times\mathrm{id}\times s)
, which is
therefore continuous.

In the case of cones, we recall that a semitopological cone is
topological as a cone if and only if it is topological as a
barycentric algebra, by Lemma
3.9
.

Before we proceed with item 3, we recall that every retract of a

T_{0}
space is
T_{0}
. Hence every semitopological barycentric
algebra (resp. pointed, resp. every semitopological cone) that
occurs as an affine retract of a locally convex (hence weakly
locally convex)
T_{0}
topological barycentric algebra (resp. pointed, resp. semitopological cone) is itself weakly locally
convex,
T_{0}
and topological.

3. Let
\mathfrak{B}
be a weakly locally convex
T_{0}
topological
barycentric algebra. Using Theorem
6.44
,

\beta\colon{\mathbf{V}}_{1,\mathrm{f}}\mathfrak{B}\to\mathfrak{B}
is a retraction with section
the map
\eta_{\mathfrak{B}}\colon x\mapsto\delta_{x}
, and
\beta
is
affine. The map
\eta_{\mathfrak{B}}
is continuous since the inverse image
of
[U>r]
is
U
if
r<1
, empty otherwise. We reason
similarly in the case where
\mathfrak{B}
is pointed, or a cone, in which
case
\beta
is linear.

Moreover,
{\mathbf{V}}_{\mathrm{f}}\mathfrak{B}
is
T_{0}
, so its subspaces

{\mathbf{V}}_{1,\mathrm{f}}\mathfrak{B}
and
{\mathbf{V}}_{\leq 1,\mathrm{f}}\mathfrak{B}
are, too.

{\mathbf{V}}_{\mathrm{f}}\mathfrak{B}
is topological (Example
2.5
) and
locally linear (Example
6.12
).
{\mathbf{V}}_{1}X
and

{\mathbf{V}}_{\leq 1}X
are topological, being convex subspaces of

{\mathbf{V}}X
, thanks to Lemma
3.8
.

{\mathbf{V}}_{\leq 1}X
is locally linear (see
Example
6.13
) and
{\mathbf{V}}_{1}X
is locally affine
(see Example
6.14
).

Items 4, 5, 6 follow immediately.
\Box

6.6
The free weakly locally convex (pointed) topological barycentric algebras

Heckmann shows that
{\mathbf{V}}_{\mathrm{f}}X
is the free weakly locally convex

T_{0}
topological cone
[
23
, Theorem 6.7]
. We retrieve
this here, as well as natural extensions of this result to (pointed)
barycentric algebras.

Theorem 6.49

For every
T_{0}
topological cone
\mathfrak{C}
,
{\mathbf{V}}_{\mathrm{f}}\mathfrak{C}
is the free
weakly locally convex
T_{0}
topological cone over
\mathfrak{C}
.

For every pointed
T_{0}
topological barycentric algebra
\mathfrak{B}
,

{\mathbf{V}}_{\leq 1,\mathrm{f}}\mathfrak{B}
is the free weakly locally convex pointed

T_{0}
topological barycentric algebra over
\mathfrak{B}
.

For every
T_{0}
topological barycentric algebra
\mathfrak{B}
,

{\mathbf{V}}_{1,\mathrm{f}}\mathfrak{B}
is the free weakly locally convex
T_{0}

topological barycentric algebra over
\mathfrak{B}
.

Namely,
{\mathbf{V}}_{\mathrm{f}}\mathfrak{C}
is a weakly locally convex (even locally
linear)
T_{0}
topological cone, and for every weakly locally convex

T_{0}
topological cone
\mathfrak{D}
and every linear continuous map

f\colon\mathfrak{C}\to\mathfrak{D}
, there is a unique linear continuous map

\hat{f}\colon{\mathbf{V}}_{\mathrm{f}}\mathfrak{C}\to\mathfrak{D}
such that

\hat{f}(\delta_{x})=f(x)
for every
x\in\mathfrak{C}
, namely

\hat{f}\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}f\circ\beta
. Similarly with pointed
T_{0}

topological barycentric algebras and linear continuous maps, or with

T_{0}
topological barycentric algebras and affine continuous maps
instead of
T_{0}
topological cones and linear continuous maps.

Proof.

That
{\mathbf{V}}_{\mathrm{f}}\mathfrak{C}
is a locally linear
T_{0}
topological cone is
part of Proposition
6.48
. Given
f
and
\mathfrak{D}

as above, if
\hat{f}
exists then by linearity it must map every
simple valuation
\nu\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\sum_{x\in A}a_{x}\delta_{x}
to

\sum_{x\in A}a_{x}f(x)
, so
\hat{f}
is unique. Conversely,

\hat{f}\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}f\circ\beta
fits, where
\beta
is given by
Theorem
6.44
. The argument is the same
with (pointed)
T_{0}
topological barycentric algebras and linear (or
affine) continuous maps.
\Box

6.7
Sober spaces, and barycenters, part 5

Heckmann also shows that
{\mathbf{V}}_{\mathrm{p}}X
is the free weakly locally
convex
sober
topological cone over
X

[
23
, Theorem 6.8]
. We show a similar result for
(pointed) barycentric algebras, with a similar technique.

Every space
X
has a
sobrification
, and that is a sober space

X^{s}
, together with a continuous map
\eta\colon X\to X^{s}
, such
that for every continuous map
f\colon X\to Y
where
Y
is sober,
there is a unique continuous map
\widehat{f}\colon X^{s}\to Y
such
that
\widehat{f}\circ\eta=f
(see
[
19
, Chapter 8]
).
Sobrifications are unique up to isomorphism, and the
canonical
sobrification

{\mathcal{S}}X
is built as the space of all irreducible
closed subsets of
X
with the
hull-kernel topology
, whose open
subsets are the sets of the form

\diamond U\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\{C\in{\mathcal{S}}X\mid C\cap U\neq\emptyset\}
,

U\in{\mathcal{O}}X
. The map
\eta
is defined as

\eta^{{\mathcal{S}}}\colon X\to{\mathcal{S}}X
, where
\eta^{{\mathcal{S}}}(x)\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\dc x
for
every
x\in X
. The specialization ordering of
{\mathcal{S}}X
is
inclusion. The map
U\mapsto\diamond U
defines an order
isomorphism between
{\mathcal{O}}X
and
{\mathcal{O}}{{\mathcal{S}}X}
.

Heckmann shows that
{\mathbf{V}}_{\mathrm{p}}X
(not
{\mathbf{V}}X
in general) is a
sobrification of
{\mathbf{V}}_{\mathrm{f}}X
, with inclusion map for
\eta

[
23
, Theorem 5.5]
. We establish a similar statement for

{\mathbf{V}}_{\leq 1,\mathrm{p}}X
, and for
{\mathbf{V}}_{1,\mathrm{p}}X
when
X
is pointed.
The latter requires a simple trick, originally due to Abbas Edalat

[
8
, Section 3]
.

Lemma 6.50

For every topological space
X
,
{\mathbf{V}}_{1}X
is homeomorphic to

{\mathbf{V}}_{\leq 1}(X\smallsetminus\dc\bot)
. In one direction, the
homeomorphism maps every
\nu\in{\mathbf{V}}_{1}X
to

\nu^{\prime}\in{\mathbf{V}}_{\leq 1}(X\smallsetminus\dc\bot)
, defined by

\nu^{\prime}(U)\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\nu(U)
for every

U\in{\mathcal{O}}(X\smallsetminus\dc\bot)
; in the other direction, every

\nu^{\prime}\in{\mathbf{V}}_{\leq 1}(X\smallsetminus\dc\bot)
is mapped to the

\nu\in{\mathbf{V}}_{1}X
defined by
\nu(U)\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\nu^{\prime}(U)
if

\bot\not\in U
,
\nu(U)\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}1
otherwise.

The homeomorphism restricts to one between
{\mathbf{V}}_{1,\mathrm{f}}X
and

{\mathbf{V}}_{\leq 1,\mathrm{f}}(X\smallsetminus\dc\bot)
.

Proof.

Let
g
be the map
\nu\mapsto\nu^{\prime}
. For every

U\in{\mathcal{O}}(X\smallsetminus\dc\bot)
,
U
is also open in
X
, because

\dc\bot
is closed in
X
, so
X\smallsetminus\dc X
is open in
X
.
Hence the definition of
\nu^{\prime}=g(\nu)
makes sense. It is also
clear that
\nu^{\prime}
is a subprobability valuation. For every open
subset
U
of
X\smallsetminus\dc\bot
, for every
r\in\mathbb{R}_{+}\smallsetminus\{0\}
,

g^{-1}([U>r])=[U>r]
, so
g
is continuous.

Let
f
be the map
\nu^{\prime}\to\nu
. For every subprobability
valuation
\nu^{\prime}
on
X
,
\nu\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}f(\nu^{\prime})
is strict
(
\nu(\emptyset)=0
); it is modular: for all open subsets
U
and

V
of
X
,
\nu(U)+\nu(V)=\nu(U\cup V)+\nu(U\cap V)
if

\bot\not\in U,V
, while if
\bot\in U
(hence
U=X
),

\nu(U)+\nu(V)=\nu(U\cup V)+\nu(U\cap V)
(because

U=X=U\cup V
and
U\cap V=V
), and symmetrically if

\bot\in V
. Additionally,
\nu
is Scott-continuous, because any
directed family of open sets
{(U_{i})}_{i\in I}
has a union that
contains
\bot
if and only if some
U_{i}
contain
\bot
. The map

f
is continuous, because
f^{-1}([U>r])=[U>r]
if

\bot\not\in U
, otherwise
U=X
and then
[U>r]
is the whole of

{\mathbf{V}}_{1}X
or the empty set depending on whether
r<1
or not, and
its inverse image under
f
is open, too. It is clear that
f
and

g
are inverse of each other.

Finally,
g
maps every simple probability valuation

\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\delta_{x_{i}}
on
X
to

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i=1\\
x_{i}\not\leq\bot\end{subarray}}^{n}a_{i}\delta_{x_{i}}
, and

g
maps every simple subprobability valuation

\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\delta_{x_{i}}
on
X\smallsetminus\dc\bot
to

\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\delta_{x_{i}}+(1-\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i})\delta_{\bot}
.

\Box

Theorem 6.51

Let
X
be a topological space, and
\circledast
be nothing or
“
\leq 1
”. Then
{\mathbf{V}}_{\circledast,\mathrm{p}}X
is a sobrification of

{\mathbf{V}}_{\circledast,\mathrm{f}}X
. When
\circledast
is “1”,
{\mathbf{V}}_{1,\mathrm{f}}X
is
a sobrification of
{\mathbf{V}}_{1,\mathrm{f}X}
if
X
is pointed, namely if

X
has a least element in its specialization preordering.

Proof.

The case where
\circledast
is nothing was dealt with by Heckmann. When

\circledast
is “
\leq 1
”,
{\mathbf{V}}_{\leq 1,\mathrm{p}}X
is a closed subspace
of
{\mathbf{V}}_{\mathrm{p}}X
, since it is equal to the complement of
[X>1]
,
which is open. Every closed subspace of a sober space is sober. In
fact, the sober subspaces of a sober space are exactly those that
are closed in the
Skula topology
, which is the coarsest
topology containing both the open and the closed subsets of
X

[
27
, Corollary 3.5]
.

We will now use the fact that given any space
Y
included in a
sober space
Z
, the
Skula closure

cl_{s}(Y)
of
Y
in
Z
,
namely its closure in the Skula topology, is a sobrification of
Y

[
27
, Corollary 3.6]
.

We take
Y\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}{\mathbf{V}}_{\leq 1,\mathrm{f}}X
,
Z\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}{\mathbf{V}}_{\mathrm{p}}X
;
Z

is sober
[
23
, Proposition 5.1]
. We have seen that

{\mathbf{V}}_{\leq 1,\mathrm{p}}X
is closed, hence Skula-closed in
Z
. Since
it contains
Y
,
cl_{s}(Y)\subseteq{\mathbf{V}}_{\leq 1,\mathrm{p}}X
. We show
the reverse implication by showing that
{\mathbf{V}}_{\leq 1,\mathrm{p}}X
is
included in every Skula-closed subset of
Z
that contains
Y
;
equivalently, by showing that every Skula-open subset of
Z
that
intersects
{\mathbf{V}}_{\leq 1,\mathrm{p}}X
intersects
Y
. Given any
Skula-open subset
\mathcal{O}
of
Z
that intersects

{\mathbf{V}}_{\leq 1,\mathrm{p}}X
, we pick
\nu
in the intersection, and then

\nu
is in
\mathcal{U}\cap\mathcal{C}\subseteq\mathcal{O}
for
some open subset
\mathcal{U}
of
Z
and some closed subset

\mathcal{C}
of
Z
, by definition of the Skula topology. By
definition of the weak topology (on
Z={\mathbf{V}}_{\mathrm{p}}X
),
\nu
is in
some intersection of subbasic open sets

\bigcap_{i=1}^{n}[U_{i}>r_{i}]
included in
\mathcal{U}
. Lemma 5.4
of
[
23
]
states that for every bounded point-continuous
valuation
\nu
on
X
, if
\nu
is in some intersection of subbasic
open sets
\bigcap_{i=1}^{n}[U_{i}>r_{i}]
, then there is a simple
valuation
\mu\leq\nu
in the same intersection. In our case,

\mu
is then in
\mathcal{U}
, and is also in
\mathcal{C}
since

\mu\leq\nu
and every closed set is downwards-closed. Since

\mu\leq\nu
,
\mu
is a simple valuation and

\nu\in{\mathbf{V}}_{\leq 1,\mathrm{p}}X
,
\mu
is in
{\mathbf{V}}_{\leq 1,\mathrm{f}}X
.
Hence
\mathcal{O}
intersects
{\mathbf{V}}_{\leq 1,\mathrm{f}}X
at
\mu
.

When
\circledast
is “
1
” and
X
is pointed, we use
Lemma
6.50
:
{\mathbf{V}}_{1}X
is homeomorphic to

{\mathbf{V}}_{\leq 1}(X\smallsetminus\dc\bot)
, by a homeomorphism that
restricts to one between
{\mathbf{V}}_{1,\mathrm{f}}X
and

{\mathbf{V}}_{\leq 1,\mathrm{f}}(X\smallsetminus\dc\bot)
. The result then follows
from the first part of the theorem, applied to the space

X\smallsetminus\dc\bot
.
\Box

The map
\beta
below extends the map
\beta
of
Theorem
6.44
. According, we also call it
the
barycenter map
.

Theorem 6.52
(Barycenters, part 5)

Let
\mathfrak{C}
be a weakly locally convex, sober topological cone. There
is a unique linear continuous map
\beta\colon{\mathbf{V}}_{\mathrm{p}}\mathfrak{C}\to\mathfrak{C}

such that
\beta(\delta_{x})=x
for every
x\in\mathfrak{C}
.

Given a weakly locally convex sober pointed topological barycentric
algebra
\mathfrak{B}
, there is a unique linear continuous map
\beta
from

{\mathbf{V}}_{\leq 1,\mathrm{p}}\mathfrak{B}
to
\mathfrak{B}
such that
\beta(\delta_{x})=x
for
every
x\in\mathfrak{B}
, and a unique affine continuous map
\beta
from

{\mathbf{V}}_{1,\mathrm{p}}\mathfrak{B}
to
\mathfrak{B}
such that
\beta(\delta_{x})=x
for
every
x\in\mathfrak{B}
.

One should beware that the existence and uniqueness of
\beta
in the
last case, from
{\mathbf{V}}_{1,\mathrm{p}}\mathfrak{B}
to
\mathfrak{B}
, still requires
\mathfrak{B}
to be
pointed.

Proof.

We deal with the first part of the Theorem first. This is essentially
Theorem 6.8 of
[
23
]
.

Let us consider the continuous map

\beta\colon{\mathbf{V}}_{\mathrm{f}}\mathfrak{C}\to\mathfrak{C}
of
Theorem
6.44
. Since
{\mathbf{V}}_{\mathrm{p}}\mathfrak{C}
is the
sobrification of
{\mathbf{V}}_{\mathrm{f}}\mathfrak{C}
,
\beta
extends to unique
continuous map from
{\mathbf{V}}_{\mathrm{p}}\mathfrak{C}
to
\mathfrak{C}
, which we temporarily
write as
\hat{\beta}
.

For every
a\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
, the maps
\nu\mapsto a\hat{\beta}(\nu)
and

\nu\mapsto\hat{\beta}(a\cdot\nu)
are continuous from

{\mathbf{V}}_{\mathrm{p}}\mathfrak{C}
to
\mathfrak{C}
, and coincide on simple valuations:

a\hat{\beta}(\sum_{x\in A}a_{x}\delta_{x})=a\sum_{x\in A}a_{x}\cdot x=\sum_{x\in A}aa_{x}\cdot x=\hat{\beta}(\sum_{x\in A}aa_{x}\delta_{x})
. The continuous extension of a continuous map from

{\mathbf{V}}_{\mathrm{f}}\mathfrak{C}
to its sobrification
{\mathbf{V}}_{\mathrm{p}}\mathfrak{C}
is unique, so

a\hat{\beta}(\nu)=\hat{\beta}(a\cdot\nu)
for all
a\in\mathbb{R}_{+}

and
\nu\in{\mathbf{V}}_{\mathrm{p}}\mathfrak{C}
.

Next, we show that
\hat{\beta}
preserves sums. Given a simple
valuation
\mu\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\sum_{x\in A}a_{x}\delta_{x}
, the maps

\nu\mapsto\beta(\mu)+\hat{\beta}(\nu)
and

\nu\mapsto\hat{\beta}(\mu+\nu)
are continuous from

{\mathbf{V}}_{\mathrm{p}}\mathfrak{C}
to
\mathfrak{C}
, and coincide on simple valuations. By the
same argument on sobrifications as above, they define the same map.
Explicitly,

\hat{\beta}(\mu+\nu)=\hat{\beta}(\mu)+\hat{\beta}(\nu)
for every
simple valuation
\mu
and every point-continuous valuation
\nu
.
It follows that, given any fixed point-continuous valuation
\nu
,
the maps
\mu\mapsto\hat{\beta}(\mu)+\hat{\beta}(\nu)
and
