\mu\mapsto\hat{\beta}(\mu+\nu)
are continuous from
{\mathbf{V}}_{\mathrm{p}}\mathfrak{C}

to
\mathfrak{C}
, and coincide on simple valuations. Again, they coincide by
the universal property of sobrifications, hence

\hat{\beta}(\mu+\nu)=\hat{\beta}(\mu)+\hat{\beta}(\nu)
for all

\mu,\nu\in{\mathbf{V}}_{\mathrm{p}}\mathfrak{C}
.

This much shows the existence of a linear continuous map

\hat{\beta}\colon{\mathbf{V}}_{\mathrm{p}}\mathfrak{C}\to\mathfrak{C}
such that

\hat{\beta}(\delta_{x})=x
for every
x\in\mathfrak{C}
. Uniqueness is
clear: any such
\hat{\beta}
must restrict to a linear continuous map

\beta\colon{\mathbf{V}}_{\mathrm{f}}\mathfrak{C}\to\mathfrak{C}
such that
\beta(\delta_{x})=x

for every
x\in\mathfrak{C}
; such a
\beta
is unique by
Theorem
6.44
, and then
\hat{\beta}
is its
unique continuous extension to its sobrification
{\mathbf{V}}_{\mathrm{p}}\mathfrak{C}
.

Second, we deal with the case of a weakly locally convex pointed
sober topological barycentric algebra
\mathfrak{B}
. We consider the linear
continuous map
\beta\colon{\mathbf{V}}_{\leq 1,\mathrm{f}}\mathfrak{B}\to\mathfrak{B}
of
Theorem
6.44
, which we extend to a unique
continuous map
\hat{\beta}\colon{\mathbf{V}}_{\leq 1,\mathrm{p}}\mathfrak{B}\to\mathfrak{B}
,
using Theorem
6.51
. Since the least element of

{\mathbf{V}}_{\leq 1,\mathrm{p}}\mathfrak{B}
is the zero valuation
0
, which is in

{\mathbf{V}}_{\leq 1,\mathrm{f}}\mathfrak{B}
,
\hat{\beta}(0)=\beta(0)=\bot
, using
the fact that
\beta
is linear hence strict. Hence
\hat{\beta}
is
itself strict.

We claim that
\hat{\beta}
is affine, which will show that it is
linear, thanks to Proposition
4.6
. The argument
is the same as for sums. For every
a\in[0,1]
, for every

\mu\in{\mathbf{V}}_{\leq 1,\mathrm{f}}\mathfrak{B}
, the functions

\nu\mapsto\beta(\mu)+_{a}\hat{\beta}(\nu)
and

\nu\mapsto\hat{\beta}(\mu+_{a}\nu)
are continuous from

{\mathbf{V}}_{\leq 1,\mathrm{p}}\mathfrak{B}
to
\mathfrak{B}
, and coincide on

{\mathbf{V}}_{\leq 1,\mathrm{f}}\mathfrak{B}
, since
\beta
is affine. Hence they
coincide on
{\mathbf{V}}_{\leq 1,\mathrm{p}}\mathfrak{B}
, by the universal property of
sobrifications. Since
\hat{\beta}(\mu)=\beta(\mu)
, this shows
that
\hat{\beta}(\mu+_{a}\nu)=\hat{\beta}(\mu)+_{a}\hat{\beta}(\nu)

for all
\mu\in{\mathbf{V}}_{\leq 1,\mathrm{f}}\mathfrak{B}
and

\nu\in{\mathbf{V}}_{\leq 1,\mathrm{p}}\mathfrak{B}
. It follows that, for every

\nu\in{\mathbf{V}}_{\leq 1,\mathrm{p}}\mathfrak{B}
, the maps

\mu\mapsto\hat{\beta}(\mu+_{a}\nu)
and

\mu\mapsto\hat{\beta}(\mu)+_{a}\hat{\beta}(\nu)
, which are
continuous from
{\mathbf{V}}_{\leq 1,\mathrm{p}}\mathfrak{B}
to
\mathfrak{B}
, coincide on

{\mathbf{V}}_{\leq 1,\mathrm{f}}\mathfrak{B}
. Hence they coincide on

{\mathbf{V}}_{\leq 1,\mathrm{p}}\mathfrak{B}
, by the universal property of
sobrifications.

We have just shown the existence of a linear continuous map

\hat{\beta}\colon{\mathbf{V}}_{\leq 1,\mathrm{p}}\mathfrak{B}\to\mathfrak{B}
such that

\hat{\beta}(\delta_{x})=x
for every
x\in\mathfrak{B}
. Uniqueness is
proved as in the case of cones.

Third, we still consider a weakly locally convex sober pointed
topological barycentric algebra
\mathfrak{B}
, but we focus on

{\mathbf{V}}_{1,\mathrm{p}}
rather than
{\mathbf{V}}_{\leq 1,\mathrm{p}}
. We have just
built a linear continuous map

\beta\colon{\mathbf{V}}_{\leq 1,\mathrm{p}}\mathfrak{B}\to\mathfrak{B}
. By restriction to

{\mathbf{V}}_{1,\mathrm{p}}\mathfrak{B}
, we obtain an affine continuous map
\beta_{1}

from
{\mathbf{V}}_{1,\mathrm{p}}\mathfrak{B}
to
\mathfrak{B}
such that
\beta_{1}(\delta_{x})=x

for every
x\in\mathfrak{B}
. Uniqueness is, once again, proved as in the
case of cones: given any affine continuous map
\hat{\beta}
from

{\mathbf{V}}_{1,\mathrm{p}}\mathfrak{B}
to
\mathfrak{B}
such that
\hat{\beta}(\delta_{x})=x
for
every
x\in\mathfrak{B}
, its restriction to
{\mathbf{V}}_{1,\mathrm{f}}\mathfrak{B}
is uniquely
determined by Theorem
6.44
, and then

\hat{\beta}
is its unique continuous extension to its sobrification

{\mathbf{V}}_{1,\mathrm{p}}\mathfrak{B}
. The fact that
{\mathbf{V}}_{1,\mathrm{p}}\mathfrak{B}
is a
sobrification of
{\mathbf{V}}_{1,\mathrm{f}}\mathfrak{B}
is by
Theorem
6.51
, which applies because
\mathfrak{B}
is
pointed.
\Box

Remark 6.53

Theorem
6.52
does not have a
statement about the existence of
\beta\colon{\mathbf{V}}_{1,\mathrm{p}}\mathfrak{B}\to\mathfrak{B}
for non-pointed weakly locally convex sober topological
barycentric algebras
\mathfrak{B}
. This is because using
Theorem
6.51
as we did in the proof requires
\mathfrak{B}
to
be pointed anyway.

Corollary 6.54

The weakly locally convex sober topological cones are exactly the
semitopological cones
\mathfrak{C}
that occur as linear retracts of locally
convex (even locally linear) sober topological cones; explicitly,
one such linear retraction is
\beta\colon{\mathbf{V}}_{\mathrm{p}}\mathfrak{C}\to\mathfrak{C}
.

The weakly locally convex sober pointed topological barycentric
algebras are exactly the pointed semitopological barycentric
algebras
\mathfrak{B}
that occur as linear retracts of locally linear sober
pointed topological barycentric algebras; one such linear retraction
is
\beta\colon{\mathbf{V}}_{\leq 1,\mathrm{p}}\mathfrak{B}\to\mathfrak{B}
.

Proof.

Every linear retract of a weakly locally convex sober topological
cone is weakly locally convex and topological by
Proposition
6.48
and sober, because sobriety
is preserved by retracts. Conversely, if
\mathfrak{C}
is a weakly locally
convex sober topological cone, then the map
\beta
of
Theorem
6.52
is a linear
retraction, with section
\eta_{\mathfrak{C}}\colon\mathfrak{C}\to{\mathbf{V}}_{\mathrm{p}}\mathfrak{C}
.
Moreover,
{\mathbf{V}}_{\mathrm{p}}\mathfrak{C}
is topological
(Example
2.5
), locally linear
(Example
6.12
), and sober, being a sobrification
(Theorem
6.51
).

The argument is the same for pointed barycentric algebras
\mathfrak{B}

instead of cones.
{\mathbf{V}}_{\leq 1,\mathrm{p}}\mathfrak{B}
is topological since it
is a subspace of the topological cone
{\mathbf{V}}_{\mathrm{p}}\mathfrak{B}
, locally linear
(Example
6.13
) and sober
(Theorem
6.51
).
\Box

The following is also due to Heckmann
[
23
]
, in the case
of cones.

Theorem 6.55

For every sober topological cone
\mathfrak{C}
,
{\mathbf{V}}_{\mathrm{p}}\mathfrak{C}
is the free
weakly locally convex sober topological cone over
\mathfrak{C}
.

For every sober pointed topological barycentric algebra
\mathfrak{B}
,

{\mathbf{V}}_{\leq 1,\mathrm{p}}\mathfrak{B}
is the free weakly locally convex sober
pointed topological barycentric algebras over
\mathfrak{B}
.

Namely,
{\mathbf{V}}_{\mathrm{p}}\mathfrak{C}
is a weakly locally convex, even locally
linear, sober topological cone, and for every weakly locally convex
sober topological cone
\mathfrak{D}
and every linear continuous map

f\colon\mathfrak{C}\to\mathfrak{D}
, there is a unique linear continuous map

\hat{f}\colon{\mathbf{V}}_{\mathrm{p}}\mathfrak{C}\to\mathfrak{D}
such that

\hat{f}(\delta_{x})=f(x)
for every
x\in\mathfrak{C}
, namely

\hat{f}\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}f\circ\beta
. Similarly with
\mathfrak{B}
instead of
\mathfrak{C}
,
pointed topological barycentric algebras instead of topological
cones, and
{\mathbf{V}}_{\leq 1,\mathrm{p}}
instead of
{\mathbf{V}}_{\mathrm{p}}
.

Proof.

That
{\mathbf{V}}_{\mathrm{p}}\mathfrak{C}
is a locally convex
T_{0}
topological cone is
part of Corollary
6.54
. Given
f
and

\mathfrak{D}
as above, if
\hat{f}
exists then by linearity it must map
every simple valuation
\nu\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\sum_{x\in A}a_{x}\delta_{x}
to

\sum_{x\in A}a_{x}\cdot f(x)=f(\beta(\nu))
, so
\hat{f}
is
determined uniquely on
{\mathbf{V}}_{\mathrm{f}}\mathfrak{C}
. Since
\mathfrak{D}
is sober, by the
uniqueness part in the universal property of sobrifications and
since
{\mathbf{V}}_{\mathrm{p}}\mathfrak{C}
is a sobrification of
{\mathbf{V}}_{\mathrm{f}}\mathfrak{C}

(Theorem
6.51
),
\hat{f}
is determined uniquely on
the whole of
{\mathbf{V}}_{\mathrm{p}}\mathfrak{C}
. Conversely,

\hat{f}\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}f\circ\beta
fits, where
\beta
is given by
Theorem
6.52
.
The argument is similar with pointed topological barycentric
algebras instead of topological cones and adding “
\leq 1
”
indices to
{\mathbf{V}}
everywhere.
\Box

7
Consistent barycentric algebras

There are a few theorems on semitopological cones
\mathfrak{C}
that require
addition to be
almost open
, in the sense that
\upc(U+V)
is
open for all open subsets
U
and
V
of
\mathfrak{C}

[
30
, Definition 4.6]
. There are two natural
extensions of the notion to semitopological barycentric algebras.

Definition 7.1
(Consistent barycentric algebra)

A semitopological barycentric algebra
\mathfrak{B}
is
consistent
if
and only if for all open subsets
U
and
V
of
\mathfrak{B}
, for every open
subset
I
of
[0,1]
,
\upc(U+_{I}V)
is open in
\mathfrak{B}
, where

U+_{I}V\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\{x+_{a}y\mid x\in U,y\in V,a\in I\}
.

It is
strongly consistent
if and only if for all open subsets

U
and
V
of
\mathfrak{B}
, for every
a\in{[0,1]}
,
\upc(U+_{a}V)
is
open in
\mathfrak{B}
, where

U+_{a}V\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\{x+_{a}y\mid x\in U,y\in V\}
.

Fact 7.2

A semitopological barycentric algebra
\mathfrak{B}
is strongly consistent if
and only if for all open subsets
U
and
V
of
\mathfrak{B}
, for every

a\in{]0,1[}
,
\upc(U+_{a}V)
is open in
\mathfrak{B}
. Indeed, for
a\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}0
,
\upc(U+_{a}V)=\upc V=V
and for
a\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}1
,
\upc(U+_{a}V)=\upc U=U
, and both are open by assumption.

Fact 7.3

Every strongly consistent semitopological barycentric algebra is
consistent.

Indeed, for all open subsets
U
and
V
of a strongly consistent
barycentric algebra
\mathfrak{B}
, for every open subset
I
of
[0,1]
,

\upc(U+_{I}V)=\bigcup_{a\in I}\upc(U+_{a}V)
, which is a union
of open sets if
\mathfrak{B}
is strongly consistent.

Example 7.4

Every sup-semilattice
L
(see
Example
3.11
) is strongly consistent,
hence consistent by Fact
7.3
.
Indeed, let us rely on Fact
7.2
, and let us
fix
a\in{]0,1[}
. For all Scott-open subsets
U
and
V
,

U+_{a}V=\{x\vee y\mid x\in U,y\in V\}=U\cap V
: for all

x\in U
and
y\in V
,
x\vee y
is both in
U
and in
V
, since
both are upwards-closed, and conversely, every element

z\in U\cap V
can be written as
z\vee z
, where
z\in U
and

z\in V
. Hence
\upc(U+_{a}V)=U\cap V
is open.

Lemma 7.5

A semitopological barycentric algebra
\mathfrak{B}
is consistent if and only
if
U+_{I}V
is open for all open subsets
U
and
V
of
\mathfrak{B}
and
every non-empty open interval
I
included in
]0,1[
.

Proof.

The only if direction is clear. In the if direction, let
U
and

V
be open in
\mathfrak{B}
and
I
be an open subset of
[0,1]
. Then
I

is a union of a family
{(I_{k})}_{k\in K}
of non-empty intervals of
the form
]a,b[
with
0\leq a<b\leq 1
, or
[0,b[
or
]a,1]
.
We rewrite the latter two as the union of
]0,b[
with
\{0\}
, and
as the union of
]a,1[
with
\{1\}
, if necessary; so we may
assume that every
I_{k}
is a non-empty interval included in

]0,1[
, or
\{0\}
or
\{1\}
. By assumption, if
I_{k}
is of the
first kind,
\upc(U+_{I_{k}}V)
is open in
\mathfrak{B}
, while

\upc(U+_{\{0\}}V)=\upc V=V
and

\upc(U+_{\{1\}}V)=\upc U=U
. Therefore

\upc(U+_{I}V)=\bigcup_{k\in K}\upc(U+_{I_{k}}V)
is open in

\mathfrak{B}
.
\Box

Proposition 7.6

The following are equivalent for a semitopological cone
\mathfrak{C}
:

1.

addition is almost open on
\mathfrak{C}
;

2.

\mathfrak{C}
is strongly consistent, seen as a semitopological
barycentric algebra;

3.

\mathfrak{C}
is consistent, seen as a semitopological barycentric
algebra.

Proof.

1\mathrel{\Rightarrow}2
. If addition is almost open on
\mathfrak{C}
, then let
U
and

V
be two open subsets of
\mathfrak{C}
and
a\in{]0,1[}
. We have

U+_{a}V=(a\cdot U)+((1-a)\cdot V)=(\frac{1}{a}\cdot\_)^{-1}(U)+(\frac{1}{1-a}\cdot\_)^{-1}(V)
. Since

(\frac{1}{a}\cdot\_)^{-1}(U)
and

(\frac{1}{1-a}\cdot\_)^{-1}(V)
are open in
\mathfrak{C}
,

\upc(U+_{a}V)
is open. Hence
\mathfrak{C}
is strongly consistent as a
semitopological barycentric algebra, thanks to
Fact
7.2
.

2\mathrel{\Rightarrow}3
is by Fact
7.3
.

3\mathrel{\Rightarrow}1
. Let us assume that
\mathfrak{C}
is consistent as a
semitopological barycentric algebra. Let
U
and
V
be two open
subsets of
\mathfrak{C}
. For every
z\in\upc(U+V)
, there are points

x\in U
and
y\in V
such that
x+y\leq z
. Since
\_\cdot x

is continuous from
\mathbb{R}_{+}
to
\mathfrak{C}
, there is a number
b\in{]0,1[}

such that
b\cdot x\in U
, and similarly there is a number

c\in{]0,1[}
such that
c\cdot y\in V
. Let

U^{\prime}\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}(b\cdot\_)^{-1}(U)
and

V^{\prime}\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}(c\cdot\_)^{-1}(V)
. Since
b\cdot x\in U
,
x
is
in
U^{\prime}
, and similarly
y\in V^{\prime}
. Let
\epsilon>0
be such that

1-2\epsilon>\max(b,c)
. For all
x^{\prime}\in U^{\prime}
,
y^{\prime}\in U^{\prime}
and

a\in{]\frac{1}{2}-\epsilon,\frac{1}{2}+\epsilon[}
,

2a>1-2\epsilon>b
and
2(1-a)>1-2\epsilon>c
, so

2a\cdot x^{\prime}\geq b\cdot x^{\prime}
is in
U
,

2(1-a)\cdot y^{\prime}\geq c\cdot y^{\prime}
is in
V
, and therefore

2\cdot(x^{\prime}+_{a}y^{\prime})=2a\cdot x^{\prime}+2(1-a)\cdot y^{\prime}
is in

U+V
. We have just shown that

2\cdot(U^{\prime}+_{]\frac{1}{2}-\epsilon,\frac{1}{2}+\epsilon[}V^{\prime})

is included in
U+V
. Since
2\cdot\_
is monotonic, it follows
that
W\subseteq\upc(U+V)
, where

W\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}2\cdot\upc(U^{\prime}+_{]\frac{1}{2}-\epsilon,\frac{1}{2}+\epsilon[}V^{\prime})
. By assumption,

\upc(U^{\prime}+_{]\frac{1}{2}-\epsilon,\frac{1}{2}+\epsilon[}V^{\prime})
is
open in
\mathfrak{C}
. Using the equality

2\cdot A=(\frac{1}{2}\cdot\_)^{-1}(A)
,
2\cdot A
is open for
every open set
A
; so
W
is open in
\mathfrak{C}
. Since
x\in U^{\prime}
and

y\in V^{\prime}
,
2\cdot(x+_{\frac{1}{2}}y)
is in
W
, equivalently

x+y\in W
, and since
x+y\leq z
,
z
is in
W
. We have
therefore found an open neighborhood
W
of
z
included in

\upc(U+V)
, for every
z\in\upc(U+V)
, and this shows that

\upc(U+V)
is open in
\mathfrak{C}
.
\Box

There is another case where consistency and strong consistency
coincide.

Lemma 7.7

For every topological barycentric algebra
\mathfrak{B}
,
\mathfrak{B}
is strongly
consistent if and only if
\mathfrak{B}
is consistent.

Proof.

Let
U
and
V
be two open subsets of
\mathfrak{B}
and
a\in{]0,1[}
.
In order to show that
\upc(U+_{a}V)
is open, we consider any
point
z\in\upc(U+_{a}V)
, and we will find an open neighborhood

W
of
z
contained in
\upc(U+_{a}V)
. Since

z\in\upc(U+_{a}V)
, there are two points
x\in U
and
y\in V

such that
x+_{a}y\leq z
. The function

f\colon(x^{\prime},a^{\prime},y^{\prime})\mapsto x^{\prime}+_{a^{\prime}}y^{\prime}
is continuous since

\mathfrak{B}
is topological, and
f(x,1,y)=x\in U
, so there is a
basic open set
U^{\prime}\times I\times V^{\prime}
(with
U^{\prime}
,
I
,
V^{\prime}
open in

\mathfrak{B}
,
[0,1]
and
\mathfrak{B}
respectively) containing
(x,1,y)
and
mapped by
f
to points of
U
. Replacing
U^{\prime}
by
U\cap U^{\prime}
and

V^{\prime}
by
V\cap V^{\prime}
if necessary, we may assume that

U^{\prime}\subseteq U
and
V^{\prime}\subseteq V
. We may also assume that

I={]1-\epsilon,1]}
for some
\epsilon\in{]0,1[}
. For all

x^{\prime}\in U^{\prime}
,
r\in I
and
y^{\prime}\in V^{\prime}
,
x^{\prime}+_{r}y^{\prime}\in U
, and since

y^{\prime}\in V^{\prime}\subseteq V
,
(x^{\prime}+_{r}y^{\prime})+_{a}y^{\prime}\in\upc(U+_{a}V)
.
Now
(x^{\prime}+_{r}y^{\prime})+_{a}y^{\prime}=x^{\prime}+_{ra}y^{\prime}
(a fact that is easier to
see by embedding
\mathfrak{B}
in a cone first), and
ra
ranges over

{](1-\epsilon)a,a]}
when
r
ranges over
I
. Hence

U^{\prime}+_{](1-\epsilon)a,a]}V^{\prime}\subseteq\upc(U+_{a}V)
.

Symmetrically, we can find an open neighborhood
U^{\prime\prime}\subseteq U
of

x
, an open neighborhood
I^{\prime}\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}{[0,\epsilon^{\prime}[}
of
0
and an
open neighborhood
V^{\prime\prime}\subseteq V
of
y
such that

x^{\prime}+_{r}y^{\prime}\in V
for all
x^{\prime}\in U^{\prime\prime}
,
r\in I^{\prime}
and
y^{\prime}\in V^{\prime\prime}
.
We now use the equality

x^{\prime}+_{a}(x^{\prime}+_{r}y^{\prime})=x^{\prime}+_{1-(1-a)(1-r)}y^{\prime}
, and we realize that

1-(1-a)(1-r)=a+(1-a)r
ranges over
[a,a+(1-a)\epsilon^{\prime}[

when
r
ranges over
I^{\prime}
. Hence

U^{\prime\prime}+_{[a,a+(1-a)\epsilon^{\prime}[}V^{\prime\prime}\subseteq\upc(U+_{a}V)
.
Together with

U^{\prime}+_{](1-\epsilon)a,a]}V^{\prime}\subseteq\upc(U+_{a}V)
, this allows
us to infer that

(U^{\prime}\cap U^{\prime\prime})+_{](1-\epsilon)a,a+(1-a)\epsilon^{\prime}[}(V^{\prime}\cap V^{\prime\prime})

is included in
\upc(U+_{a}V)
. Let

W\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\upc((U^{\prime}\cap U^{\prime\prime})+_{](1-\epsilon)a,a+(1-a)\epsilon[}(V^{\prime}\cap V^{\prime\prime}))
; this is open since
\mathfrak{B}
is consistent, and

W\subseteq\upc(U+_{a}V)
. Since
x\in U^{\prime}\cap U^{\prime\prime}
,

a\in{](1-\epsilon)a,a+(1-a)\epsilon[}
and
y\in V^{\prime}\cap V^{\prime\prime}
,

z
is in
W
, and this completes the proof.
\Box

Problem 7.8

Is every consistent semitopological barycentric algebra strongly
consistent? A counterexample, if it exists, cannot be topological
(because of Lemma
7.7
), and cannot be a cone
(by Proposition
7.6
).

7.1
Convex open sets in consistent semitopological barycentric
algebras

Proposition 7.9

Let
\mathfrak{B}
be a semitopological barycentric algebra.

1.

If
\mathfrak{B}
is consistent, then the interior of every convex
saturated subset is convex.

2.

If
\mathfrak{B}
is consistent, then for every
n\geq 1
, for every
open subset
V
of
\Delta_{n}
, for all open subsets
U_{1}
, …,

U_{n}
of
\mathfrak{B}
, the set

\upc\{\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\cdot x_{i}\mid(a_{1},\cdots,a_{n})\in V,x_{1}\in U_{1},\cdots,x_{n}\in U_{n}\}
is open in
\mathfrak{B}
.

3.

If
\mathfrak{B}
is consistent and weakly locally convex, then for
every open subset
U
of
\mathfrak{B}
,
\upc\mathop{\mathrm{conv}}U
is open and convex,
and is the smallest convex open set containing
U
.

Proof.

1. Let
A
be a convex saturated subset of
\mathfrak{C}
. Let

x,y\in\text{int}({A})
,
a\in[0,1]
and
I
be any open
neighborhood of
a
in
[0,1]
with its standard topology. Since

\mathfrak{B}
is consistent,
U\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\upc(\text{int}({A})+_{I}\text{int}({A}))
is
open, and
x+_{a}y\in U
. Additionally, for every
z\in U
, there
are two points
x^{\prime},y^{\prime}\in\text{int}({A})
(hence in
A
) and a number

a^{\prime}\in I
such that
x^{\prime}+_{a^{\prime}}y^{\prime}\leq z
. Since
A
is convex and
saturated,
z\in A
. This shows that
U\subseteq A
, hence

U\subseteq\text{int}({A})
, and therefore
x+_{a}y\in\text{int}({A})
.

2. Let

\mathrm{Bary}(V;U_{1},\cdots,U_{n})\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\upc\{\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\cdot x_{i}\mid(a_{1},\cdots,a_{n})\in V,x_{1}\in U_{1},\cdots,x_{n}\in U_{n}\}
, for each
n\geq 1
. We show that

\upc\mathrm{Bary}(V;U_{1},\cdots,U_{n})
is open by induction on

n\geq 1
, using the formula of Definition and
Proposition
3.1
, item 1, for the sum

\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\cdot x_{i}
. When
n=1
, either
V
is empty and

\upc\mathrm{Bary}(V;U_{1})=\emptyset
, or
V=\{(1)\}
and

\upc\mathrm{Bary}(V;U_{1})=\upc U_{1}=U_{1}
, which is open. In
the induction case, let
V^{\prime}\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}V\smallsetminus\{(0,\cdots,0,1)\}
.
This is also open in
\Delta_{n}
, and

\upc\mathrm{Bary}(V;U_{1},\cdots,U_{n})
is equal to

\upc\mathrm{Bary}(V^{\prime};U_{1},\cdots,U_{n})
if

(0,\cdots,0,1)\not\in V
, to

\upc\mathrm{Bary}(V^{\prime};U_{1},\cdots,U_{n})\cup U_{n}
otherwise.
Hence it suffices to show that

\upc\mathrm{Bary}(V^{\prime};U_{1},\cdots,U_{n})
is open in
\mathfrak{B}
. Let
Z

be the subspace of
\Delta_{n-1}\times{]0,1]}
with the product
of the usual metric topologies. Let

f\colon\Delta_{n}\smallsetminus\{(0,\cdots,0,1)\}\to Z
map every

(a_{1},\cdots,a_{n})
to

((\frac{a_{1}}{1-a_{n}},\cdots,\frac{a_{n-1}}{1-a_{n}}),1-a_{n})
,
and

g\colon Z\to\Delta_{n}\smallsetminus\{(0,\cdots,0,\allowbreak 1)\}

map every
((b_{1},\cdots,b_{n-1}),b_{n})
to

(b_{n}b_{1},\allowbreak\cdots,\allowbreak b_{n}b_{n-1},\allowbreak 1-b_{n})
. The functions
f
and
g
are inverse of each other, and
continuous. In particular,
f[V^{\prime}]=g^{-1}(V^{\prime})
is open in
Z
,
so we can write it as a union
\bigcup_{j\in J}V_{j}\times I_{j}

where each
V_{j}
is open in
\Delta_{n-1}
and
I_{j}
is open in

]0,1]
. Then:

\displaystyle\upc\mathrm{Bary}(V^{\prime};U_{1},\cdots,U_{n})

\displaystyle=\upc\biggl\{\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}\frac{a_{i}}{1-a_{n}}\cdot x_{i}\right)+_{1-a_{n}}x_{n}

\displaystyle\qquad\mid(a_{1},\cdots,a_{n})\in V^{\prime},x_{1}\in U_{1},\cdots,x_{n}\in U_{n}\biggr\}

\displaystyle=\upc\biggl\{\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}b_{i}\cdot x_{i}\right)+_{b_{n}}x_{n}

\displaystyle\qquad\mid((b_{1},\cdots,b_{n-1}),b_{n})\in f[V^{\prime}],

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad x_{1}\in U_{1},\cdots,x_{n}\in U_{n}\biggr\}

\displaystyle=\bigcup_{j\in J}\upc\biggl\{\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}b_{i}\cdot x_{i}\right)+_{b_{n}}x_{n}

\displaystyle\qquad\mid(b_{1},\cdots,b_{n-1})\in V_{j},b_{n}\in I_{j},

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad x_{1}\in U_{1},\cdots,x_{n}\in U_{n}\biggr\}

\displaystyle=\bigcup_{j\in J}\upc(\mathrm{Bary}(V_{j};U_{1},\cdots,U_{n-1})+_{I_{j}}U_{n}).

For every
j\in J
,

\upc(\mathrm{Bary}(V_{j};U_{1},\cdots,U_{n-1})+_{I_{j}}U_{n})\subseteq\upc(\upc\mathrm{Bary}(V_{j};U_{1},\allowbreak\cdots,\allowbreak U_{n-1})+_{I_{j}}U_{n})
since

\mathrm{Bary}(V_{j};U_{1},\cdots,U_{n-1})\subseteq\upc\mathrm{Bary}(V_{j};U_{1},\cdots,U_{n-1})
. Conversely, every
element
z
of

\upc(\upc\mathrm{Bary}(V_{j};U_{1},\cdots,U_{n-1})+_{I_{j}}U_{n})

is larger than or equal to
x+_{a}y
for some

x\in\upc\mathrm{Bary}(V_{j};U_{1},\cdots,U_{n-1})
, some

a\in I_{j}
and some
y\in U_{n}
. Then
x\geq x^{\prime}
for some

x^{\prime}\in\mathrm{Bary}(V_{j};U_{1},\cdots,U_{n-1})
, so

z\geq x^{\prime}+_{a}y
, since
+_{a}
is continuous, hence monotonic. It
follows that

\upc(\upc\mathrm{Bary}(V_{j};U_{1},\cdots,U_{n-1})+_{I_{j}}U_{n})

is included in, and therefore equal to, the set

\upc(\mathrm{Bary}(V_{j};U_{1},\cdots,U_{n-1})+_{I_{j}}U_{n})
. We
have shown that

\upc\mathrm{Bary}(V^{\prime};U_{1},\cdots,\allowbreak U_{n})=\bigcup_{j\in J}\upc(\upc\mathrm{Bary}(V_{j};U_{1},\cdots,U_{n-1})+_{I_{j}}U_{n})
. By induction hypothesis,

\upc\mathrm{Bary}(V_{j};U_{1},\cdots,U_{n-1})
is open. Since

\mathfrak{B}
is consistent,

\upc(\upc\mathrm{Bary}(V_{j};U_{1},\cdots,\allowbreak U_{n-1})+_{I_{j}}U_{n})
is open for every
j\in J
, so

\upc\mathrm{Bary}(V^{\prime};\allowbreak U_{1},\allowbreak\cdots,\allowbreak U_{n})
is open in
\mathfrak{B}
.

3.
\upc\mathop{\mathrm{conv}}U
is convex by Definition and
Proposition
5.8
. In order to show that it is
open, we take any
x\in\upc\mathop{\mathrm{conv}}U
, and we find an open
neighborhood of
x
included in
\upc\mathop{\mathrm{conv}}U
. By Definition and
Lemma
5.3
,

x\geq\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\cdot x_{i}
, for some points
x_{i}
is in

U
, some coefficients
a_{i}\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
, and where

\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}=1
. Let
V
be some open neighborhood of

(a_{1},\cdots,a_{n})
in
\Delta_{n}
. Using weak local convexity, for
each
i
, we can find a convex subset
C_{i}
of
U
whose interior

U_{i}\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\text{int}({C_{i}})
contains
x_{i}
.

Let
W\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\upc\mathrm{Bary}(V;U_{1},\cdots,U_{n})
, as defined
and studied in item 2. Notably,
W
is open in
\mathfrak{B}
. By
construction,
x\in W
.
W
is the upward closure of a collection
of barycenters
\sum_{i=1}^{n}a^{\prime}_{i}\cdot x^{\prime}_{i}
where

(a^{\prime}_{1},\cdots,a^{\prime}_{n})\in V\subseteq\Delta_{n}
and

x^{\prime}_{1}\in U_{1}\subseteq C_{1}
, …,
x^{\prime}_{n}\in U_{n}\subseteq C_{n}
.
Therefore
W
is included in

\upc\{\sum_{i=1}^{n}a^{\prime}_{i}\cdot x^{\prime}_{i}\mid(a^{\prime}_{1},\cdots,a^{\prime}_{n})\in\Delta_{n},x^{\prime}_{1}\in C_{1},\cdots,x^{\prime}_{n}\in C_{n}\}
, which is equal to

\upc\mathop{\mathrm{conv}}{(C_{1}\cup\cdots\cup C_{n})}
by
Lemma
5.5
. By definition of saturated convex
hulls (Definition and Proposition
5.8
), and since

C_{1}\cup\cdots\cup C_{n}\subseteq U\subseteq\upc\mathop{\mathrm{conv}}U
,

\upc\mathop{\mathrm{conv}}{(C_{1}\cup\cdots\cup C_{n})}
is included in

\upc\mathop{\mathrm{conv}}U
, hence
W\subseteq\upc\mathop{\mathrm{conv}}U
. This shows that

W
is an open neighborhood of
x
included in
\upc\mathop{\mathrm{conv}}U
, and
since
x
is arbitrary in
\upc\mathop{\mathrm{conv}}U
,
\upc\mathop{\mathrm{conv}}U
is open in

\mathfrak{B}
.

In order to show that
\upc\mathop{\mathrm{conv}}U
is the smallest convex open set
containing
U
, we consider any convex open set
V
containing

U
.
V
contains
\mathop{\mathrm{conv}}U
by definition, and therefore also

\upc\mathop{\mathrm{conv}}U
, since
V
is upwards-closed.
\Box

Corollary 7.10

Every consistent, weakly locally convex semitopological barycentric
algebra is locally convex.

Proof.

Let
\mathfrak{B}
be a consistent, weakly locally convex semitopological
barycentric algebra. Let also
x\in\mathfrak{B}
, and
U
be an open
neighborhood of
x
in
\mathfrak{B}
. Since
\mathfrak{B}
is weakly locally convex,
there is a convex subset
C
of
\mathfrak{B}
such that

x\in\text{int}({C})\subseteq C\subseteq U
. By
Proposition
7.9
, item 1,
\text{int}({C})
is convex.
\Box

Henceforth we will therefore say “consistent locally convex” instead
of “consistent weakly locally convex”.

7.2
Lower semicontinuous envelopes

Finally, we will use the notion of lower semicontinuous envelope of a
monotonic map
f\colon X\to\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
. This is the largest lower
semicontinuous map
\check{f}
less than or equal to
f
, and is
obtained as the pointwise supremum of all lower semicontinuous maps
less than or equal to
f
. This section parallels

[
30
, Lemma 5.7]
for barycentric algebras instead of
cones.

Lemma 7.11

For every semitopological barycentric algebra
\mathfrak{B}
, for every map

f\colon\mathfrak{B}\to\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
, if
f
is convex, then so is
\check{f}
.

Proof.

Let
f\colon\mathfrak{B}\to\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
be convex. We fix
a\in{]0,1[}
and

x\in\mathfrak{B}
, and we show that

\check{f}(x+_{a}y)\leq af(x)+(1-a)\check{f}(y)
for every

y\in\mathfrak{B}
. The claim is clear if
f(x)=\infty
, since in that
case
af(x)+(1-a)\check{f}(y)=\infty
. Hence, let us assume
that
f(x)<\infty
. We define
g\colon\mathfrak{B}\to\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
by

g(y)\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\frac{1}{1-a}\max(\check{f}(x+_{a}y)-af(x),0)

for every
y\in\mathfrak{B}
. This defines a lower semicontinuous function

g
. For every
y\in\mathfrak{B}
,

g(y)\leq\frac{1}{1-a}\max(f(x+_{a}y)-af(x),0)\leq\frac{1}{1-a}\max((1-a)f(y),0)=f(y)
since
f
is convex. Since

\check{f}
is the largest lower semicontinuous function below
f
,

g\leq\check{f}
. This entails that

\check{f}(x+_{a}y)-af(x)\leq(1-a)\check{f}(y)
, namely

\check{f}(x+_{a}y)\leq af(x)+(1-a)\check{f}(y)
, for every

y\in\mathfrak{B}
.

Knowing that, we fix
a\in{]0,1[}
and
y\in\mathfrak{B}
, and we show
that
\check{f}(x+_{a}y)\leq a\check{f}(x)+(1-a)\check{f}(y)

for every
x\in\mathfrak{B}
. The argument is similar. This is obvious if

\check{f}(y)=\infty
. Otherwise, we let

g(x)\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\frac{1}{a}\max(\check{f}(x+_{a}y)-(1-a)\check{f}(y),0)
, defining a lower semicontinuous function

g\colon\mathfrak{B}\to\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
such that
g\leq f
by the result of the
previous paragraph; hence
g\leq\check{f}
, so that

\check{f}(x+_{a}y)\leq a\check{f}(x)+(1-a)\check{f}(y)
for
every
x\in\mathfrak{B}
. This inequality remains true for
a\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}0
or
for
a\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}1
, trivially, so
\check{f}
is convex.
\Box

Lemma 7.12

For every concave monotonic map
f
from a consistent
semitopological barycentric algebra
\mathfrak{B}
to
\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
,
\check{f}
is
concave, too.

Proof.

We deal with the first part first. Let us assume that
\check{f}
is
not concave. There are two points
x,y\in\mathfrak{B}
and a real number

a\in[0,1]
such that

\check{f}(x+_{a}y)<a\check{f}(x)+(1-a)\check{f}(y)
.
Additionally,
a
must be different from
0
and
1
, since

\check{f}(x+_{0}y)=\check{f}(y)=0\check{f}(x)+1\check{f}(y)
, and similarly when
a=1
.

Let
r\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
be such that

\check{f}(x+_{a}y)<r<a\check{f}(x)+(1-a)\check{f}(y)
. We
recall that:
(*)
on
\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
,
s\ll t
if and only if
s=0
or

s<t
. In particular,
r\ll a\check{f}(x)+(1-a)\check{f}(y)
.
Since scalar multiplication and addition are Scott-continuous on

\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
, and
a
is the supremum of the strictly positive numbers
strictly below
a
, and similarly for
1-a
, there are two numbers

\beta,\gamma>0
such that
\beta<a
,
\gamma<1-a
, and

r\ll\beta\check{f}(x)+\gamma\check{f}(y)
. Then

a\in{]\beta,1-\gamma[}\subseteq{]0,1[}
. By There are

b,c\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
such that
r\leq b+c
,
b\ll\beta\check{f}(x)
,
and
c\ll\gamma\check{f}(y)
. Using
(*)
,

\frac{b}{\beta}\ll\check{f}(x)
and

\frac{c}{\gamma}\ll\check{f}(y)
. Hence
x
is in the open set

U\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}{\check{f}}^{-1}(\hbox to0.0pt{$\uparrow$\hss}\raise 2.15277pt\hbox{$\uparrow$}\frac{b}{\beta})
and
y
is in
the open set
V\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}{\check{f}}^{-1}(\hbox to0.0pt{$\uparrow$\hss}\raise 2.15277pt\hbox{$\uparrow$}\frac{c}{\gamma})
.
Since
\mathfrak{B}
is consistent,

W\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\upc(U+_{]\beta,1-\gamma[}V)
is open in
\mathfrak{B}
.

Let
g\colon\mathfrak{B}\to\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
be defined by

g(z)\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\max(\check{f}(z),(b+c)\chi_{W}(z))
for every

z\in\mathfrak{B}
. This is lower semicontinuous, and above
\check{f}
. We
claim that
g\leq f
. Since
\check{f}\leq f
, it suffices to show
that
(b+c)\chi_{W}\leq f
, namely that for every
z\in W
,

b+c\leq f(z)
. Given that
z\in W
, we find
x^{\prime}\in U
,

y^{\prime}\in V
and
a^{\prime}\in{]\beta,1-\gamma[}
such that

x^{\prime}+_{a^{\prime}}y^{\prime}\leq z
. Then

\frac{b}{\beta}\ll\check{f}(x^{\prime})\leq f(x^{\prime})
and

\frac{c}{\gamma}\ll\check{f}(y^{\prime})\leq f(y^{\prime})
, in particular

b\leq\beta f(x^{\prime})
and
c\leq\gamma f(y^{\prime})
, so

b+c\leq\beta f(x^{\prime})+\gamma f(y^{\prime})\leq a^{\prime}f(x^{\prime})+(1-a^{\prime})f(y^{\prime})
, where the second inequality comes from

a^{\prime}\in{]\beta,1-\gamma[}
. Using the fact that
f
is concave
and monotonic,

a^{\prime}f(x^{\prime})+(1-a^{\prime})f(y^{\prime})\leq f(x^{\prime}+_{a^{\prime}}y^{\prime})\leq f(z)
, so

b+c\leq f(z)
, as desired.

Hence
g\leq f
is lower semicontinuous, and
\check{f}\leq g
. By
the maximality of
\check{f}
,
g=\check{f}
. Therefore

\check{f}(x+_{a}y)=g(x+_{a}y)\geq(b+c)\chi_{W}(x+_{a}y)=b+c\geq r>\check{f}(x+_{a}y)
, which is impossible.
\Box

Lemma 7.13

For every c-space
X
, for every monotonic map

f\colon X\to\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
, for every
x\in X
,
\check{f}(x)
is the
directed supremum of the values
f(y)
where
y
ranges over the
points of
X
such that
x\in\text{int}({\upc y})
.

Given any subspace
L
of
\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}^{X}
consisting of monotonic maps,
The map
\mathfrak{r}\colon f\mapsto\check{f}
is a retraction of

L
onto its image
L\cap\mathcal{L}X
, with the subspace topology;
the section is the inclusion map.

Proof.

The first part is sometimes referred to as
Scott’s formula

when
X
is a continuous dcpo. For every
x\in X
, the set of
points
y
such that
x\in\text{int}({\upc y})
is directed, since

X
is a c-space. Indeed, given any finite collection of such
points
y_{1}
, …,
y_{n}
,
\bigcap_{i=1}^{n}\text{int}({\upc y_{i}})

is an open neighborhood of
x
, which therefore contains a point
y

such that
x\in\text{int}({\upc y})
. Let
g(x)
be the directed
supremum
\sup_{y\in X,x\in\text{int}({\upc y})}f(y)
, for
every
x\in X
.

The function
g
is lower semicontinuous, since

g^{-1}(]t,\infty])=\bigcup_{y\in X,f(y)>r}\text{int}({\upc y})
. Since
f
is monotonic and
x\in\text{int}({\upc y})

implies
y\leq x
,
f\leq g
. Hence
g\leq\check{f}
. Let us
imagine that the inequality is strict. For some
x\in X
,

g(x)<\check{f}(x)
, and therefore there is an
r\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
such
that
g(x)<r<\check{f}(x)
. Then
x
is in the open set

U\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}{(\check{f})}^{-1}(]r,\infty])
, and since
X
is a
c-space, there is a point
y\in U
such that

x\in\text{int}({\upc y})
. In particular,
f(y)>r
, and the
definition of
g
entails that
g(x)>r
, which is impossible.
Therefore
g=\check{f}
.

For the second part, we let
\mathfrak{s}
be the inclusion map of

L\cap\mathcal{L}X
into
L
. For every
f\in L\cap\mathcal{L}X
,

\mathfrak{r}(\mathfrak{s}(f))
is the largest lower semicontinuous
map less than or equal to
f
, and that must be
f
since
f
is
already lower semicontinuous. The inclusion map
\mathfrak{s}
is
clearly continuous.

The sets of the form
[x>r]_{L}\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\{f\in L\mid f(x)>r\}

with
x\in X
and
r\in\mathbb{R}_{+}\smallsetminus\{0\}
form a subbase of the
topology on
L
. In order to show that
\mathfrak{r}
is continuous,
it is enough to show that

\mathfrak{r}^{-1}([x>r]_{L}\cap\mathcal{L}X)
is open in
L
. Its
elements are the (monotonic) functions
f
such that

\sup_{y\in X,x\in\text{int}({\upc y})}f(y)>r
, namely such
that
f\in[y>r]
for some
y\in X
such that

x\in\text{int}({\upc y})
. In other words,

\mathfrak{r}^{-1}([x>r]_{L}\cap\mathcal{L}X)=\bigcup_{y\in X,x\in\text{int}({\upc y})}[y>r]_{L}
, so
\mathfrak{r}
is
continuous.
\Box

7.3
The Plotkin-Banach-Alaoglu theorem

The Banach-Alaoglu theorem states that the closed unit ball in a
topological dual of a topological vector space is compact. Plotkin
gives a variant of this result on continuous d-cones whose addition
preserves the way-below relation
[
40
, Theorem 2]
; we
will see how this related to consistency in
Section
7.4
. We show a similar
generalization to duals of consistent semitopological barycentric
algebras. The proof is basically the same.

In a non-Hausdorff setting, compactness has to be replaced by stable
compactness (see
[
19
, Chapter 9]
). A
stably
compact space
is a sober, locally compact, coherent, and compact
topological space. We explain each adjective.

•

A space is
locally compact
if and only if every point has
a base of compact neighborhoods, or equivalently of compact
saturated neighborhoods. One should beware that, in a non-Hausdorff
setting, this property is not equivalent with the more usual
definition (every point has a compact neighborhood), which is
strictly weaker. Every locally compact space is core-compact

[
19
, Theorem 5.2.9]
, and the two notions are
equivalent for a sober space
[
19
, Theorem 8.3.10]
.

•

A space is
coherent
if and only if the intersection of
any two compact saturated subsets is compact (and saturated).

\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
and
[0,1]_{\sigma}
are stably compact spaces. In general,
every
compact pospace

(X,\preceq)
, namely every compact
space
X
with a partial ordering
\preceq
whose graph is closed in

X\times X
, gives rise to a stably compact space
X^{\uparrow}
, whose
elements are those of
X
, and whose open subsets are the open subsets
of
X
that are upwards-closed with respect to
\preceq
. The
specialization ordering of
X^{\uparrow}
is
\preceq
, and this
construction exhausts all possible stably compact spaces: given any
stably compact space
X
,
(X^{\text{{patch}}},\leq)
is a compact pospace and

(X^{\text{{patch}}})^{\uparrow}=X
, where
\leq
is the specialization ordering
of
X
and
X^{\text{{patch}}}
is
X
with the
patch topology
, the
coarser topology that contains the open subsets of
X
and the
complements of compact saturated subsets of
X
. The patch topology
makes sense for any space, not just stably compact spaces, but the
properties just mentioned only hold for stably compact spaces.

Every product of stably compact spaces is stably compact, and the
product of the patch topologies is the patch topology of the product;
and retracts preserve sobriety, local compactness, coherence, and
compactness, hence also stable compactness. A subset that is closed
in
X^{\text{{patch}}}
is called
patch-closed
(in
X
), and similarly
with patch-compact. A
patch-continuous
map
f\colon X\to Y

between stably compact spaces is a continuous map from
X^{\text{{patch}}}
to

Y^{\text{{patch}}}
. Every compact pospace is compact Hausdorff.

We will use the following gimmick
[
18
, Definition 5.4]
.
Let
T
be a set, and
A
be a topological space. A

patch-continuous inequality
on
T,A
is any formula
E
of the
form:

f(\_(t_{1}),\ldots,\_(t_{m}))\mathrel{\dot{\leq}}g(\_(t^{\prime}_{1}),\ldots,\_(t^{\prime}_{n}))

where
f
and
g
are patch-continuous maps from
A^{n}
to
A
, and

t_{1}
, …,
t_{m}
,
t^{\prime}_{1}
, …,
t^{\prime}_{n}
are
m+n
fixed elements
of
T
.
E

holds
at
\alpha\colon T\to A
iff

f(\alpha(t_{1}),\ldots,\alpha(t_{m}))\leq g(\alpha(t^{\prime}_{1}),\ldots,\alpha(t^{\prime}_{n}))
, where
\leq
is the specialization quasi-ordering of

A
. A
patch-continuous system

\Sigma
on
T,A
is a
(possibly infinite) set
\Sigma
of patch-continuous inequalities on

T,A
.
\Sigma

holds
at
\alpha\colon T\to A
iff every
element of
\Sigma
holds at
\alpha
. We also allow equations, since

a\dot{=}b
is equivalent to
a\mathrel{\dot{\leq}}b
and
b\mathrel{\dot{\leq}}a
. In this
setting, the subset
[\Sigma]
of
A^{T}
of all maps

\alpha\colon T\to A
such that
\Sigma
holds at
\alpha
is
patch-closed in
A^{T}

[
18
, Proposition 5.5]
.

Here is the variant of the Plotkin-Banach-Alaoglu theorem on
barycentric algebras.

Theorem 7.14

For every consistent semitopological barycentric algebra
\mathfrak{B}
whose
underlying topological space is a c-space,
\mathfrak{B}^{\star}
is stably
compact. If
\mathfrak{B}
is also pointed (for example, a consistent
semitopological cone), then
\mathfrak{B}^{*}
is stably compact.

Proof.

We consider the space
\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}^{\mathfrak{B}}
with the product topology. Since

\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
is stably compact,
\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}^{\mathfrak{B}}
is stably compact, too. The
subset
L
of all affine (resp. linear) monotonic maps from
\mathfrak{B}
to

\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
is the collection defined by the following inequalities:

\_(x)\leq\_(y)
for all pairs
x\leq y
in
\mathfrak{B}
,

\_(x+_{a}y)=a\,\_(x)+(1-a)\,\_(y)
for all
x,y\in\mathfrak{B}

and
a\in[0,1]
(resp. and
\_(\bot)=0
, since a map is
linear if and only if it is strict and affine, by
Proposition
4.6
). We note that
+
is
patch-continuous on
\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
, equivalently it is continuous with
respect to the usual Hausdorff topology on
\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
, and
multiplication by
a\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
is, too. This is therefore a
patch-continuous system, from which it follows that
L
is
patch-closed, hence stably compact when given the subspace topology
from
\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}^{\mathfrak{B}}
.

For every
f\in L
,
\check{f}
is convex by
Lemma
7.11
, and concave by
Lemma
7.12
, which applies since
\mathfrak{B}
is
consistent. Therefore
\check{f}
is in
\mathfrak{B}^{\star}
(resp.
\mathfrak{B}^{*}
).
Since
\mathfrak{B}
is a c-space, the map

\mathfrak{r}\colon f\mapsto\check{f}
of
Lemma
7.13
is a retraction of
\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}^{\mathfrak{B}}
onto

L\cap\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{p}}\mathfrak{B}
, namely onto
\mathfrak{B}^{\star}
(resp.
\mathfrak{B}^{*}
). As
a retract of a stably compact space,
\mathfrak{B}^{\star}
(resp.
\mathfrak{B}^{*}
) is
then stably compact.
\Box

Given any topological space
X
, the complements of compact saturated
sets form subbasic open sets for a topology, the
cocompact

topology. If
X
is stably compact, the cocompact topology consists
exactly of the complements of compact saturated sets. The set of
points of
X
with the cocompact topology is the
de Groot dual

X^{\text{{d}}}
of
X
, and
X^{{\text{{d}}}{\text{{d}}}}=X
when
X
is stably compact. The
compact saturated subsets of
X
are the closed subsets of
X^{\text{{d}}}
,
and also the upwards-closed patch-closed subsets of
X
. (Once again,
see
[
19
, Chapter 9]
.) We characterize the cocompact
topology on
\mathfrak{B}^{\star}
and on
\mathfrak{B}^{*}
. Once again, the proof strategy
is due to Plotkin
[
40
]
.

Definition 7.15
(Lower-open topology)

Given a (resp. pointed) semitopological barycentric algebra
\mathfrak{B}
,
an open subset
U
of
\mathfrak{B}
, and
r\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
, the set
[U\geq r]
is
defined as the collection of elements
\Lambda
of
\mathfrak{B}^{\star}

(resp.
\mathfrak{B}^{*}
) such that
U\subseteq\Lambda^{-1}([r,\infty])
.

The
lower-open
topology on
\mathfrak{B}^{\star}
(resp.
\mathfrak{B}^{*}
)
is generated by the complements
[U<r]
of the sets
[U\geq r]

where
U\in{\mathcal{O}}\mathfrak{B}
and
r\in\mathbb{R}_{+}\smallsetminus\{0\}
.

Alternatively,
[U\geq r]
is the set of all
\Lambda\in\mathfrak{B}^{\star}

(resp.
\mathfrak{B}^{*}
) such that
\Lambda(x)\geq r
for every
x\in U
.

Given a topological space
X
with specialization preordering
\leq
,
a
dual topology

{\mathcal{O}}^{\prime}
on
X
is a topology whose
specialization preordering is the opposite preordering
\geq
. A

separating dual topology
is a dual topology
{\mathcal{O}}^{\prime}
on

X
such that, for all points
x,y\in X
such that
x\not\leq y
,
there is an open neighborhood
U\in{\mathcal{O}}X
of
x
and an element

U^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{O}}^{\prime}
containing
y
such that
U\cap U^{\prime}=\emptyset
.

Given a topological space
X
, the cocompact topology on
X
is always
dual, because saturated subsets are upwards-closed, hence their
complements are downwards-closed. If
X
is locally compact, then the
cocompact topology is also separating. Indeed, if
x\not\leq y
,
then
x
is in the open set
X\smallsetminus\dc y
. Since
X
is locally
compact, there is a compact saturated subset
Q
of
X
and an open
subset
U
of
Q
such that

x\in U\subseteq Q\subseteq X\smallsetminus\dc y
. Hence
x
is in
U
,

y
is in
X\smallsetminus Q
, and
U\cap(X\smallsetminus Q)=\emptyset
. The
cocompact topology is also coarser than the patch topology, by
definition.

Conversely, if
X
is stably compact, then the only separating dual
topology coarser than the patch topology on
X
is the cocompact
topology
[
40
, Lemma 1]
.

Proposition 7.16

Let
\mathfrak{B}
be a consistent (resp. pointed) semitopological
barycentric algebra whose underlying space is a c-space. For every

U\in{\mathcal{O}}\mathfrak{B}
and every
r\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
,
[U\geq r]
is compact
saturated in
\mathfrak{B}^{\star}
(resp.
\mathfrak{B}^{*}
).

The cocompact topology coincides with the lower-open topology on

\mathfrak{B}^{\star}
(resp.
\mathfrak{B}^{*}
). A subbase is given by the sets

[\text{int}({\upc x})<r]
with
x\in\mathfrak{B}
and

r\in\mathbb{R}_{+}\smallsetminus\{0\}
.

Proof.

Let
U
be open in
\mathfrak{B}
and
r\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
. As in the proof of
Theorem
7.14
, the subset
L
of all affine (resp. linear) monotonic maps from
\mathfrak{B}
to
\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
is patch-compact, and

\mathfrak{r}\colon\Lambda\mapsto\check{\Lambda}
defines a
retraction of
L
onto
\mathfrak{B}^{\star}
(resp.
\mathfrak{B}^{*}
). The set
A
of
all maps
\Lambda\in L
such that
\Lambda(x)\geq r
for every

x\in U
is defined by patch-continuous inequalities, so
A
is
patch-closed in
L
. Since
A
is upwards-closed in
L
, it is
compact saturated. It follows that its image
B
under

\Lambda\mapsto\check{\Lambda}
is compact in
\mathfrak{B}^{*}
.

We claim that
B=[U\geq r]
. For every
\Lambda\in A
, for every

x\in U
,

\check{\Lambda}(x)=\sup_{y\in\mathfrak{B},x\in\text{int}({\upc y})}\Lambda(y)
by Lemma
7.13
. Since
U
is
Scott-open, some
y\in\mathfrak{B}
such that
x\in\text{int}({\upc x})
is
in
U
, and then
\Lambda(y)\geq r
, whence

\check{\Lambda}(x)\geq r
; so
\check{\Lambda}
is in

B
. Conversely, every map
\Lambda
in
[U\geq r]
is in
A
, and
equal to
\check{\Lambda}
, hence in
B
.

Since
B
is compact and
B=[U\geq r]
,
[U\geq r]
is compact.
It is clear that
[U\geq r]
is upwards-closed in
\mathfrak{B}^{\star}

(resp.
\mathfrak{B}^{*}
), hence it is compact saturated.

Let
{\mathcal{O}}^{\prime}
be the topology generated by subbasic open sets

[\text{int}({\upc x})<r]
with
x\in\mathfrak{B}
and

r\in\mathbb{R}_{+}\smallsetminus\{0\}
.

We claim that
{\mathcal{O}}^{\prime}
is a dual topology on
\mathfrak{B}^{\star}
(resp.
\mathfrak{B}^{*}
). It suffices to show that, for all

\Lambda,\Lambda^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{B}^{\star}
(resp.
\mathfrak{B}^{*}
),

\Lambda\geq\Lambda^{\prime}
if and only if for every
x\in\mathfrak{B}
and
every
r\in\mathbb{R}_{+}\smallsetminus\{0\}
,

\Lambda\in[\text{int}({\upc x})<r]
implies

\Lambda^{\prime}\in[\text{int}({\upc x})<r]
. If

\Lambda\in[\text{int}({\upc x})<r]
and
\Lambda\geq\Lambda^{\prime}
,
clearly
\Lambda^{\prime}
is in
[\text{int}({\upc x})<r]
. Conversely, if
for every
x\in\mathfrak{B}
and every
r\in\mathbb{R}_{+}\smallsetminus\{0\}
,

\Lambda\in[\text{int}({\upc x})<r]
implies

\Lambda^{\prime}\in[\text{int}({\upc x})<r]
, then for every
y\in\mathfrak{B}
,
we claim that
\Lambda(y)\leq\Lambda^{\prime}(y)
. Otherwise, there
would be an
r\in\mathbb{R}_{+}\smallsetminus\{0\}
such that

\Lambda(y)>r>\Lambda^{\prime}(y)
. Since

y\in\Lambda^{-1}(]r,\infty])
and
\mathfrak{B}
is a c-space, there is
an
x\in\Lambda^{-1}(]r,\infty])
such that

y\in\text{int}({\upc x})
. In particular,
x\leq y
, so

\Lambda(y)\geq\Lambda(x)>r
, so

\Lambda\in[\text{int}({\upc x})<r]
, and therefore

\Lambda^{\prime}\in[\text{int}({\upc x})<r]
. But the latter implies

\Lambda^{\prime}(y)<r
, which is impossible. Hence

\Lambda\geq\Lambda^{\prime}
.

We claim that
{\mathcal{O}}^{\prime}
is separating. Let

\Lambda,\Lambda^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{B}^{\star}
(resp.
\mathfrak{B}^{*}
) be such that

\Lambda\not\leq\Lambda^{\prime}
. There is a point
x\in\mathfrak{B}
such that

\Lambda(x)>\Lambda^{\prime}(x)
. Let
r\in\mathbb{R}_{+}\smallsetminus\{0\}
be such
that
\Lambda(x)>r>\Lambda^{\prime}(x)
, and
y\in B
be such that

x\in\text{int}({\upc y})
and
\Lambda(y)>r
. Then
\Lambda
is
in
[y>r]\in{\mathcal{O}}\mathfrak{B}
;
\Lambda^{\prime}
is in

[\text{int}({\upc y})<r]\in{\mathcal{O}}^{\prime}
, otherwise
\Lambda^{\prime}
would be
in
[\text{int}({\upc y})\geq r]
, so
\text{int}({\upc y})
would be
included in
{\Lambda^{\prime}}^{-1}([r,\infty])
, which is impossible
since
x
belongs to the former and not to the latter. Finally,

[y>r]
and
[\text{int}({\upc y})<r]
are disjoint. Indeed,
otherwise there would be an affine (resp. linear) continuous map

h
such that
h(y)>r
and
h(z)<r
for every

z\in\text{int}({\upc y})
, in particular
h(x)<r
. Since
continuous maps are monotonic and
y\leq x
, this is impossible.

Since
{\mathcal{O}}^{\prime}
is dual and separating,
{\mathcal{O}}^{\prime}
coincides with the
cocompact topology. It is coarser than the lower-open topology by
definition, and since every set
[U\geq r]
with
U\in{\mathcal{O}}\mathfrak{B}

and
r\in\mathbb{R}_{+}\smallsetminus\{0\}
is compact saturated, the lower-open
topology is coarser than the cocompact topology. Hence all these
topologies coincide.
\Box

7.4
Consistency and c-spaces

Extending the notion described earlier, a map
h\colon X\to Y
is

almost open
if and only if
\upc h[U]
is open for every open
subset
U
of
X
. A monotonic map
h\colon X\to Y

preserves
the way-below relation
if and only if for all
x,x^{\prime}\in X
such
that
x\ll x^{\prime}
,
h(x)\ll h(x^{\prime})
.

Lemma 7.17

For every c-space
X
, for every topological space
Y
, a
monotonic map
h\colon X\to Y
is almost open if and only if for
all
x,x^{\prime}\in X
such that
x^{\prime}\in\text{int}({\upc x})
,

h(x^{\prime})\in\text{int}({\upc h(x)})
.
For every continuous poset
X
and every poset
Y
, considered
with their Scott topologies, the monotonic map
h
is quasi-open
if and only if it preserves the way-below relation.

Proof.

Let us assume that
h
is quasi-open. If

x^{\prime}\in U\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\text{int}({\upc x})
, then
h(x^{\prime})
is in the
(Scott-)open set
\upc h[U]
. In turn,
\upc h[U]
is included in

\upc h(x)
, hence in its interior.

Conversely, let us assume that
x^{\prime}\in\text{int}({\upc x})
implies

h(x^{\prime})\in\text{int}({\upc h(x)})
. For every open subset
U
of

X
, let
y
be any point of
\upc h[U]
. There is a point

x^{\prime}\in U
such that
h(x^{\prime})\leq y
. Since
X
is a c-space, there
is a point
x\in U
such that
x^{\prime}\in\text{int}({\upc x})
. By
assumption,
h(x^{\prime})\in\text{int}({\upc h(x)})
, so
y
is in the
open set
\text{int}({\upc h(x)})
, which is included in

\upc h[U]
. This shows that
\upc h[U]
is an open neighborhood
of each of its points, hence is itself open.

The second claim is a direct consequence of the first one, and the
fact that in a continuous poset,
y\in\text{int}({\upc x})
if and
only if
x\ll y
.
\Box

Lemma 7.18

Let
\mathfrak{B}
be a semitopological barycentric algebra and a c-space.

\mathfrak{B}
is strongly consistent if and only if for all
x,x^{\prime},y^{\prime},y^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{B}
such that
x^{\prime}\in\text{int}({\upc x})
and
y^{\prime}\in\text{int}({\upc y})
, for every
a\in{]0,1[}
,
x^{\prime}+y^{\prime}
is in
\text{int}({\upc(x+_{a}y)})
.

Let
\mathfrak{C}
be a c-cone.
\mathfrak{C}
is consistent if and only if for all

x,x^{\prime},y,y^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{C}
, if
x^{\prime}\in\text{int}({\upc x})
and

y^{\prime}\in\text{int}({\upc y})
, then

x^{\prime}+y^{\prime}\in\text{int}({\upc(x+y)})
.

Proof.

This is Lemma
7.17
, applied to the operator
+_{a}
in the first case, to
+
in the
second case, and realizing that in a product of two c-spaces,

\text{int}({\upc(x,y)})=\text{int}({\upc x})\times\text{int}({\upc y})
.
\Box

The following is due to
[
51
, Proposition 2.2]
in the
case of continuous d-cones, namely continuous s-cones (see
Example
3.12
) that are directed-complete.

Lemma 7.19

An continuous s-barycentric algebra
\mathfrak{B}
is strongly consistent in
its Scott topology if and only if
+_{a}
preserves the way-below
relation on
\mathfrak{B}
for every
a\in{]0,1[}
, namely: for all

x,x^{\prime},y,y^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{B}
, if
x\ll x^{\prime}
and
y\ll y^{\prime}
then

x+_{a}y\ll x^{\prime}+_{a}y^{\prime}
.

A continuous s-cone
\mathfrak{C}
is consistent in its Scott topology if and
only if addition preserves the way-below relation on
\mathfrak{C}
, namely:
for all
x,x^{\prime},y,y^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{C}
,
x\ll x^{\prime}
and
y\ll y^{\prime}
imply

x+y\ll x^{\prime}+y^{\prime}
.

Proof.

Since every continuous poset is a c-space in its Scott topology, a
continuous s-cone is a c-cone, and a continuous s-barycentric
algebra is a c-space in its Scott topology. We can therefore apply
Lemma
7.18
, and we conclude since

\hbox to0.0pt{$\uparrow$\hss}\raise 2.15277pt\hbox{$\uparrow$}z=\text{int}({\upc z})
for every point
z
.
\Box

Remark 7.20

The situation is simpler with scalar multiplication: on any s-cone

\mathfrak{C}
, for every
a\in\mathbb{R}_{+}\smallsetminus\{0\}
, for all
x,y\in\mathfrak{C}
,
x\ll y
if and only if
a\cdot x\ll a\cdot y
. This is an easy
exercise, since the map
x\mapsto a\cdot x
is an order isomorphism.

Remark 7.21

It is sometimes desirable to consider a dual property: addition

reflects

\ll
(instead of preserving it) if and only if for
all
z\ll x+y
, there are points
x^{\prime}\ll x
and
y^{\prime}\ll y
such
that
z\ll x^{\prime}+y^{\prime}
. That property holds on every continuous s-cone,
consistent or not. Indeed, since
+
is jointly continuous,

+^{-1}(\hbox to0.0pt{$\uparrow$\hss}\raise 2.15277pt\hbox{$\uparrow$}z)
is an open neighborhood of
(x,y)
, hence
contains a product of basic open sets

\text{int}({\upc x})^{\prime}\times\hbox to0.0pt{$\uparrow$\hss}\raise 2.15277pt\hbox{$\uparrow$}y^{\prime}
such that
x\in\text{int}({\upc x})^{\prime}
and

y\in\hbox to0.0pt{$\uparrow$\hss}\raise 2.15277pt\hbox{$\uparrow$}y^{\prime}
. Similarly,
+_{a}
reflects
\ll
for every

a\in{]0,1[}
on every ordered barycentric algebra that is also a
continuous poset.

7.5
Strongly consistent spaces of valuations

We will see below that all our familiar barycentric algebras of
continuous valuations are strongly consistent. We need the following
second decomposition lemma. Its proof is somewhat similar to the
first one (Proposition
3.26
).

Proposition 7.22

Let
X
be a set with a lattice of subsets
\mathcal{L}
, and
\mu
,

\nu
,
\varpi
be three bounded valuations on
(X,\mathcal{L})
. Let

L\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\{U_{1},\cdots,U_{n}\}
be a finite lattice of subsets of

X
included in
\mathcal{L}
, and let us assume that

\mu(U_{i})+\nu(U_{i})\leq\varpi(U_{i})
for every
i
,

1\leq i\leq n
. Then there are two bounded valuations
\mu^{\prime}
and

\nu^{\prime}
on
(X,\mathcal{L})
such that:

1.

\mu^{\prime}+\nu^{\prime}\leq\varpi
;

2.

\mu(U_{i})\leq\mu^{\prime}(U_{i})
and
\nu(U_{i})\leq\nu^{\prime}(U_{i})

for every
i
,
1\leq i\leq n
.

Additionally,
\mu^{\prime}
and
\nu^{\prime}
are finite linear combinations of
constrictions
\varpi_{|C}
of
\varpi
to pairwise disjoint
crescents
C
.
In particular, if
\mathcal{L}
is a topology and
\varpi
is continuous,
then
\mu^{\prime}
and
\nu^{\prime}
are continuous.

Proof.

We extend
\mu
,
\nu
, and
\varpi
uniquely to bounded valuations
on
\mathcal{A}(\mathcal{L})
, using the Smiley-Horn-Tarski theorem. As
in the proof of Proposition
3.26
, let

M\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\mathbb{P}(\{1,\cdots,n\})
, with the Alexandroff topology of
inclusion, let

C_{I}\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\bigcap_{i\in I}U_{i}\smallsetminus\bigcup_{i\not\in I}U_{i}
,
and
f\colon X\to M
be defined by

f(x)\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\{i\in\{1,\cdots,n\}\mid x\in U_{i}\}
. The
crescents
C_{I}
are pairwise disjoint, and, as in the proof of
Proposition
3.26
,
f^{-1}(\upc I)
is in
L

for every
I\in M
.

We claim that
f[\mu]+f[\nu]\leq f[\varpi]
. For every open
subset
V
of
M
,
V
is a finite union of sets
\upc I
,

I\in M
, and
f^{-1}(\upc I)
is in
L
; so
f^{-1}(V)
, which
is a finite union of elements of
L
, is also in
L
, hence equal to

U_{i}
for some
i
. Then

f[\mu](V)+f[\nu](V)=\mu(f^{-1}(V))+\nu(f^{-1}(V))=\mu(U_{i})+\nu(U_{i})\leq\varpi(U_{i})=\varpi(f^{-1}(V))=f[\varpi](V)
.

Since
M
is finite,
f[\mu]
,
f[\nu]
, and
f[\varpi]
are
simple valuations. Explicitly,

f[\mu]=\sum_{I\in M}a_{I}\delta_{I}
where

a_{I}\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\mu(C_{I})
,
f[\nu]=\sum_{I\in M}b_{I}\delta_{I}

where
b_{I}\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\nu(C_{I})
, and

f[\varpi]=\sum_{I\in M}a_{I}\delta_{I}
where

c_{I}\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\varpi(C_{I})
,

By Jones’ splitting lemma (see
[
26
, Theorem 4.10]
or
also
[
13
, Proposition IV-9.18]
), there is a
transport matrix
{(t_{IJ})}_{I\in M,J\in M}
of non-negative
real numbers such that:

(i)

for all
I,J\in M
, if
t_{IJ}\neq 0

then
I\subseteq J
;

(ii)

\sum_{J\in M}t_{IJ}=a_{I}+b_{I}
for
every
I\in M
, and

(iii)

\sum_{I\in M}t_{IJ}\leq c_{J}
for every

J\in M
.

We define two new transport matrices
{(u_{IJ})}_{I\in M,J\in M}

and
{(v_{IJ})}_{I\in M,J\in M}
by:

u_{IJ}\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\frac{a_{I}}{a_{I}+b_{I}}t_{IJ}
and

v_{IJ}\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\frac{b_{I}}{a_{I}+b_{I}}t_{IJ}
if
a_{I}+b_{I}\neq 0
,
otherwise
u_{IJ}\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}v_{IJ}\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}0
. We note that:

4.

if
t_{IJ}=0
then
u_{IJ}=0
and
v_{IJ}=0
;

5.

if
u_{IJ}\neq 0
then
I\subseteq J

(if
I\not\subseteq J
, then
t_{IJ}=0
by
(i)
, so

u_{IJ}=0
by
4
);

6.

if
v_{IJ}\neq 0
then
I\subseteq J

(similar);

7.

u_{IJ}+v_{IJ}=t_{IJ}
(even when

a_{I}+b_{I}=0
, in which case, by
(ii)
,
t_{IJ}=0

for every
J\in M
);

8.

for every
I\in M
,

\sum_{J\in M}u_{IJ}\geq a_{I}
(if
a_{I}+b_{I}\neq 0
, then

\sum_{J\in M}u_{IJ}=\frac{a_{I}}{a_{I}+b_{I}}(a_{I}+b_{I})

by
(ii)
, which is equal to
a_{I}
; otherwise,

a_{I}=0
);

9.

for every
I\in M
,

\sum_{J\in M}v_{IJ}\geq b_{I}
(similar).

For every
J\in M
, we let

a^{\prime}_{J}\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\frac{\sum_{I\in M}u_{IJ}}{c_{J}}
if
c_{J}\neq 0
,

a^{\prime}_{J}\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}0
otherwise; similarly,

b^{\prime}_{J}\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\frac{\sum_{I\in M}v_{IJ}}{c_{J}}
if
c_{J}\neq 0
,

b^{\prime}_{J}\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}0
otherwise. We note that:

10.

a^{\prime}_{J}+b^{\prime}_{J}\leq 1
(since

a^{\prime}_{J}+b^{\prime}_{J}=\frac{\sum_{I\in M}t_{IJ}}{c_{J}}\leq 1

by
7
and
(iii)
, when
c_{J}\neq 0
);

11.

a^{\prime}_{J}c_{J}=\sum_{I\in M}u_{IJ}
(even
if
c_{J}=0
, since in that case
t_{IJ}=0
by
(iii)
,
so
u_{IJ}=0
by definition); and

12.

b^{\prime}_{J}c_{J}=\sum_{I\in M}v_{IJ}
(similar).

Finally, we let
\mu^{\prime}\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\sum_{J\in M}a^{\prime}_{J}\varpi_{|C_{J}}
and

\nu^{\prime}\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\sum_{J\in M}b^{\prime}_{J}\varpi_{|C_{J}}
. If
\mathcal{L}
is a
topology and
\varphi
is continuous, we note that
\mu^{\prime}
and
\nu^{\prime}

are continuous.

Let us verify that
\mu^{\prime}+\nu^{\prime}\leq\varpi
. We have

\mu^{\prime}+\nu^{\prime}=\sum_{J\in M}(a^{\prime}_{J}+b^{\prime}_{J})\varpi_{|C_{J}}\leq\sum_{J\in M}\varpi_{|C_{J}}=\varpi
, using
10
.

We now check that
\mu(U_{i})\leq\mu^{\prime}(U_{i})
for every
i\in I
.
We note that
\varpi_{|C_{J}}(U_{i})=\varpi(C_{J}\cap U_{i})
, which is
equal to
\varpi(C_{J})=c_{J}
if
i\in J
, and to
0
otherwise.
Therefore
\mu^{\prime}(U_{i})=\sum_{J\in M,i\in J}a^{\prime}_{J}c_{J}
. This is
equal to
\sum_{I,J\in M,i\in J}u_{IJ}
by
11
,
hence to the sum of the terms
u_{IJ}
over the pairs
I,J\in M

such that
i\in J
and
I\subseteq J
, since those such that

I\not\subseteq J
are equal to
0
, by
5
. Hence
that sum is larger than or equal to the sum of the terms
u_{IJ}

over the pairs
I,J\in M
such that
i
is in
I
(instead of
J
)
and
I\subseteq J
; indeed,
i\in I\subseteq J
implies

i\in J
. Using
5
again, that sum is equal to

\sum_{I,J\in M,i\in I}u_{IJ}=\sum_{I\in M,i\in I}(\sum_{J\in M}u_{IJ})
, which is larger than or equal to

\sum_{I\in M,i\in I}a_{I}
by
8
, and the
latter is equal to

\sum_{I\in M,i\in I}\mu(C_{I})=\mu(U_{i})
.

The verification that
\nu(U_{i})\leq\nu^{\prime}(U_{i})
for every

i\in I
is similar.
\Box

Theorem 7.23

Let
X
be a topological space, and
\mathfrak{B}
be a convex subspace of

{\mathbf{V}}X
, seen as a topological barycentric algebra. Let
\mathfrak{B}_{b}
be
the collection of bounded continuous valuations in
\mathfrak{B}
. Under the
following assumptions:

(i)

every element of
\mathfrak{B}
is the supremum in
{\mathbf{V}}X
of a
directed family of elements of
\mathfrak{B}_{b}
,

(ii)

for every
\mu\in\mathfrak{B}_{b}
, for every
c\in{]0,1[}
,

c\cdot\mu\in\mathfrak{B}
,

(iii)

for all
\mu,\varpi\in\mathfrak{B}_{b}
, for every
r>1
, every
finite linear combination
\mu^{\prime}
of constrictions of

r\cdot\varpi
to pairwise disjoint crescents such that

\mu(X)=\mu^{\prime}(X)
is in
\mathfrak{B}
,

\mathfrak{B}
is a strongly consistent topological barycentric algebra.

In particular,

1.

{\mathbf{V}}X
,
{\mathbf{V}}_{b}X
,
{\mathbf{V}}_{\mathrm{p}}X
and
{\mathbf{V}}_{\mathrm{f}}X
are
consistent topological cones;

2.

{\mathbf{V}}_{\leq 1}X
,
{\mathbf{V}}_{\leq 1,\mathrm{p}}X
and

{\mathbf{V}}_{\leq 1,\mathrm{f}}X
are strongly consistent topological
barycentric algebras;

3.

if
X
is pointed, then
{\mathbf{V}}_{1}X
,
{\mathbf{V}}_{1,\mathrm{p}}X
and

{\mathbf{V}}_{1,\mathrm{f}}X
are strongly consistent topological
barycentric algebras.

Proof.

We first deal with the second part of the proposition, assuming the
first part. By Example
3.10
,
{\mathbf{V}}X
is a
topological, not just semitopological cone, and therefore it is a
topological barycentric algebras by Lemma
3.8
.
Assumption (ii) is clear for all ten spaces considered in items 1, 2
and 3.

1. When
\mathfrak{B}={\mathbf{V}}X
, assumption (i) is one half of Heckmann’s Theorem 4.2 in

[
23
]
. As for assumption (iii), constrictions of
bounded continuous valuations are continuous valuations, and

{\mathbf{V}}X
is closed under finite linear combinations.

When
\mathfrak{B}={\mathbf{V}}_{\mathrm{p}}X
, assumption (i) is the other half of
Heckmann’s Theorem 4.2 in
[
23
]
. Constrictions of
bounded point-continuous valuations are point-continuous by

[
23
, Section 3.3]
, and bounded point-continuous
valuations are closed under finite linear combinations

[
23
, Section 3.2]
, proving assumption (iii).

When
\mathfrak{C}={\mathbf{V}}_{\mathrm{f}}X
, assumption (i) is vacuously true since
every simple valuation is bounded. Given any simple valuation

\nu\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\delta_{x_{i}}
, it is easy to see that
its constriction to a crescent
C
is equal to

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i=1\\
x_{i}\in C\end{subarray}}^{n}a_{i}\delta_{x_{i}}
, and is
therefore simple. Any finite linear combination of simple
valuations is in
{\mathbf{V}}_{\mathrm{f}}X
, and this establishes
assumption (iii).

When
\mathfrak{C}={\mathbf{V}}_{b}X
, assumption (i) is vacuously true, and the
other two assumptions are trivial.

2. Here assumption (i) is vacuous, since the three given spaces
consist of bounded valuations already. We deal with
assumption (iii). Whether
\mathfrak{B}
is
{\mathbf{V}}_{\leq 1}X
,

{\mathbf{V}}_{\leq 1,\mathrm{p}}X
or
{\mathbf{V}}_{\leq 1,\mathrm{f}}
, for all

\mu,\varpi\in\mathfrak{B}_{b}
, for every
r>1
, let
\mu^{\prime}
be a finite
linear combination of constrictions of
r\cdot\varpi
to pairwise
disjoint crescents such that
\mu(X)=\mu^{\prime}(X)
. By item 1,
\mu^{\prime}

is in
{\mathbf{V}}X
, or
{\mathbf{V}}_{\mathrm{p}}X
, or in
{\mathbf{V}}_{\mathrm{f}}X
, depending
on
\mathfrak{B}
. Since
\mu\in\mathfrak{B}_{b}
,
\mu
is subnormalized, hence so is

\mu^{\prime}
, and therefore
\mu^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{B}
.

3. This follows from item 2 and Edalat’s trick
(Lemma
6.50
).

Let us prove the first part of the proposition.

We consider any two open subsets of
\mathfrak{B}
. By definition, they are
of the form
\mathcal{U}\cap\mathfrak{B}
and
\mathcal{V}\cap\mathfrak{B}
, where

\mathcal{U}
and
\mathcal{V}
are two open subsets of
{\mathbf{V}}X
.
Let also
a\in{]0,1[}
: we will show that the upward closure of

(\mathcal{U}\cap\mathfrak{B})+_{a}(\mathcal{V}\cap\mathfrak{B})
in
\mathfrak{B}
is open, and
this will suffice to show that
\mathfrak{B}
is strongly consistent, thanks
to Lemma
7.2
. Let
\xi
be any element of
the upward closure of

(\mathcal{U}\cap\mathfrak{B})+_{a}(\mathcal{V}\cap\mathfrak{B})
in
\mathfrak{B}
; that is,
there are continuous valuations
\mu\in\mathcal{U}\cap\mathfrak{B}
and

\nu\in\mathcal{V}\cap\mathfrak{B}
such that
\mu+_{a}\nu\leq\xi
. Our
task is to find an open set
\mathcal{W}\cap\mathfrak{B}
, with
\mathcal{W}

open in
{\mathbf{V}}X
, which contains
\xi
and is included in the
upward closure of
(\mathcal{U}\cap\mathfrak{B})+_{a}(\mathcal{V}\cap\mathfrak{B})
in

\mathfrak{B}
.

Every open subset of
{\mathbf{V}}X
is Scott-open (with respect to the
stochastic ordering). This follows from the fact that every
subbasic open set
[U>r]
is Scott-open. Hence
\mathcal{U}
is
Scott-open, and since
\mu
is a supremum of a directed family of
elements of
\mathfrak{B}_{b}
by assumption
(i)
, one of them is in

\mathcal{U}
, hence in
\mathcal{U}\cap\mathfrak{B}_{b}
. We reason similarly
with
\nu
and
\mathcal{V}
, to the effect that we can assume
without loss of generality that
\mu
and
\nu
are bounded
valuations.

By definition of the weak topology,
\mu
is in some finite
intersection of subbasic open sets
[r_{i}\ll U_{i}]
,
1\leq i\leq n
,
with
r_{i}\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
and
U_{i}\in{\mathcal{O}}X
, and that intersection is
included in
\mathcal{U}
; we obtain a similar intersection of
subbasic open sets
[s_{j}\ll V_{j}]
for
\nu
. By adding subbasic
open sets of the form
[0\ll V_{j}]
or
[0\ll U_{i}]
to each
intersection if necessary, we may assume that the family of open
sets
U_{i}
and the family of open sets
V_{j}
are the same. Hence we
will assume that
\nu
is in a finite intersection

\bigcap_{i=1}^{n}[s_{i}\ll U_{i}]
included in
\mathcal{V}
.

We may also assume that the family
{(U_{i})}_{1\leq i\leq n}
is a
lattice
\mathcal{L}
of open subsets of
X
: it suffices to add all sets
of the form
[0\ll U]
, where
U
ranges over the (finite) unions
of (finite) intersections of sets of the form
U_{i}
,

1\leq i\leq n
, if necessary.

We make a final adjustment to the shape of our subbasic open sets.
By construction,
r_{i}\ll\mu(U_{i})
and
s_{i}\ll\nu(U_{i})
for
every
i
,
1\leq i\leq n
. Since

\mu(U_{i})=\sup_{r\in{]0,1[}}r\,\mu(U_{i})
(and this is a
directed supremum) and similarly with
\nu
, there is a

c\in{]0,1[}
such that
r_{i}\ll a_{i}\ll\mu(U_{i})
and

s_{i}\ll b_{i}\ll\nu(U_{i})
for every
i
,
1\leq i\leq n
, where

a_{i}\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}c\,\mu(U_{i})
and
b_{i}\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}c\,\nu(U_{i})
. Now we
have

\mu\in\bigcap_{i=1}^{n}[a_{i}\ll U_{i}]\subseteq\bigcap_{i=1}^{n}[r_{i}\ll U_{i}]\subseteq\mathcal{U}
,

\nu\in\bigcap_{i=1}^{n}[b_{i}\ll U_{i}]\subseteq\bigcap_{i=1}^{n}[s_{i}\ll U_{i}]\subseteq\mathcal{V}
,
\mu
and
\nu
are bounded, and

\mu+_{a}\nu\leq\xi
.

Let
\mathcal{W}\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\bigcap_{i=1}^{n}[c_{i}\ll U_{i}]
, where

c_{i}\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}aa_{i}+(1-a)b_{i}=c\,(a\,\mu(U_{i})+(1-a)\,\nu(U_{i}))
.
For each
i\in\{1,\cdots,n\}
,
a_{i}\ll\mu(U_{i})
hence
a_{i}=0

or
a_{i}<\mu(U_{i})
, and similarly
b_{i}=0
or
b_{i}<\nu(U_{i})
;
then
c_{i}=0
(if
a_{i}=b_{i}=0
), or
c_{i}\neq 0
, so that
a_{i}
or

b_{i}
is non-zero, and since
a\neq 0,1
,

c_{i}<a\,\mu(U_{i})+(1-a)\,\nu(U_{i})\leq\xi(U_{i})
. Therefore

\mathcal{W}
is an open neighborhood of
\xi
in
{\mathbf{V}}X
.
Hence
\mathcal{W}\cap\mathfrak{B}
is an open neighborhood of
\xi
in
\mathfrak{B}
.
We claim that
\mathcal{W}\cap\mathfrak{B}
is included in the upward closure
in
\mathfrak{B}
of
(\mathcal{U}\cap\mathfrak{B})+_{a}(\mathcal{V}\cap\mathfrak{B})
.

In order to see this, let
\xi^{\prime}
be any element of

\mathcal{W}\cap\mathfrak{B}
. By assumption
(i)
, as above, there is a

\varpi\in\mathcal{W}\cap\mathfrak{B}_{b}
such that
\varpi\leq\xi^{\prime}
.
Since
\varpi
is in
\mathcal{W}
, in particular

ca\,\mu(U_{i})+c(1-a)\,\nu(U_{i})\leq\varpi(U_{i})
for every

i
,
1\leq i\leq n
. By the second decomposition lemma
(Proposition
7.22
), we can find two bounded
continuous valuations
\mu^{\prime}
and
\nu^{\prime}
such that

\mu^{\prime}+\nu^{\prime}\leq\varpi
,
ca\,\mu(U_{i})\leq\mu^{\prime}(U_{i})
and

c(1-a)\,\nu(U_{i})\leq\nu^{\prime}(U_{i})
for every
i
,
1\leq i\leq n
.
Additionally
\mu^{\prime}
and
\nu^{\prime}
are finite linear combinations of
constrictions of
\varpi
to pairwise disjoint crescents.

We claim that we can require that
ca\,\mu(X)=\mu^{\prime}(X)
and

c(1-a)\nu(X)=\nu^{\prime}(X)
. By the (first) decomposition lemma
(Proposition
3.26
), we can find two bounded
continuous valuations
\mu^{\prime}_{1}
and
\mu^{\prime}_{2}
on
X
such that: 1.
ca\,\mu=\mu^{\prime}_{1}+\mu^{\prime}_{2}
; 2.
ca\,\mu(U_{i})\leq\mu^{\prime}_{1}(U_{i})

for every
i
,
1\leq i\leq n
; and 3.
ca\,\mu(X)=\mu^{\prime}_{1}(X)
. Additionally,
\mu^{\prime}_{1}
is a finite
linear combination of constrictions of
\mu^{\prime}
to pairwise distinct
crescents, and since
\mu^{\prime}
is itself a finite linear combination of

\varpi
to pairwise distinct crescents, so is
\mu^{\prime}_{1}
. We can
also find two bounded continuous valuations
\nu^{\prime}_{1}
and
\nu^{\prime}_{2}
on

X
such that: 1’.
c(1-a)\,\nu=\nu^{\prime}_{1}+\nu^{\prime}_{2}
; 2’.
c(1-a)\,\nu(U_{i})\leq\nu^{\prime}_{1}(U_{i})
for every
i
,

1\leq i\leq n
; and 3’.
c(1-a)\,\nu(X)=\nu^{\prime}_{1}(X)
. As for

\mu^{\prime}_{1}
,
\nu^{\prime}_{1}
is a finite linear combination of constrictions
of
\varpi
to pairwise distinct crescents. Then replacing
\mu^{\prime}

by
\mu^{\prime}_{1}
and
\nu^{\prime}
by
\nu^{\prime}_{1}
, which are smaller valuations,
the property
\mu^{\prime}+\nu^{\prime}\leq\varpi
will still hold, while we
will retrieve
ca\,\mu(U_{i})\leq\mu^{\prime}(U_{i})
and

c(1-a)\,\nu(U_{i})\leq\nu^{\prime}(U_{i})
thanks to items 2 and 2’, and
additionally items 3 and 3’ will ensure that

ca\,\mu(X)=\mu^{\prime}(X)
and
c(1-a)\,\nu(X)=\nu^{\prime}(X)
, as
announced.

This serves to show that
\frac{1}{a}\cdot\mu^{\prime}
and

\frac{1}{1-a}\cdot\nu^{\prime}
are in
\mathfrak{B}
. Indeed, by assumption (ii)

c\,\mu
and
c\,\nu
are in
\mathfrak{B}
, hence in
\mathfrak{B}_{b}
since
\mu
and

\nu
are bounded. The valuation
\frac{1}{a}\cdot\mu^{\prime}
is a finite
linear combination of constrictions of
r\cdot\varpi
(with

r\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\frac{1}{a}
) to pairwise disjoint crescents and

\frac{1}{a}\cdot\mu^{\prime}(X)=c\,\mu(X)
, so
\mu^{\prime}
is in
\mathfrak{B}
by
assumption (iii). And similarly,
\nu^{\prime}
is in
\mathfrak{B}
.

Since
r_{i}\ll a_{i}=c\,\mu(U_{i})
, we deduce from

ca\,\mu(U_{i})\leq\mu^{\prime}(U_{i})
that

r_{i}\ll\frac{1}{a}\cdot\mu^{\prime}(U_{i})
for every index
i
; so

\frac{1}{a}\cdot\mu^{\prime}
is in
\bigcap_{i=1}^{n}[r_{i}\ll U_{i}]
, hence
in
\mathcal{U}
, hence in
\mathcal{U}\cap\mathfrak{B}
. Similarly,

\frac{1}{1-a}\cdot\nu^{\prime}
is in
\mathcal{V}\cap\mathfrak{B}
. Since

\mu^{\prime}+\nu^{\prime}\leq\varpi\leq\xi^{\prime}
and

\mu^{\prime}+\nu^{\prime}=(\frac{1}{a}\cdot\mu^{\prime})+_{a}(\frac{1}{1-a}\cdot\nu^{\prime})
,
\xi^{\prime}
is in the upward closure of

(\mathcal{U}\cap\mathfrak{B})+(\mathcal{V}\cap\mathfrak{B})
.
Since
\xi^{\prime}
was chosen arbitrarily in
\mathcal{W}\cap\mathfrak{B}
, this
completes our proof.
\Box

8
Separation theorems

8.1
Sandwich theorems

A fundamental theorem in the theory of (semipre)ordered cones is

Roth’s sandwich theorem

[
42
]
: given a
semipreordered cone
\mathfrak{C}
, a sublinear map
p\colon\mathfrak{C}\to\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
, a
superlinear map
q\colon\mathfrak{C}\to\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
, under the assumption that
for all
x,y\in\mathfrak{C}
such that
x\leq y
,
q(x)\leq p(y)
, there
is a linear monotonic map
\Lambda\colon\mathfrak{C}\to\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
such that

q\leq\Lambda\leq p
. The assumption holds if
q\leq p
and
q

or
p
is monotonic. Furthermore, we can require
\Lambda
to be a
minimal sublinear monotonic map between
q
and
p
. We immediately
deduce the following.

Proposition 8.1
(Sandwich theorem, preordered)

Let
\mathfrak{B}
be a preordered barycentric algebra,

p\colon\mathfrak{B}\to\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
be convex,
q\colon\mathfrak{B}\to\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
be
concave, such that for all
x,y\in\mathfrak{B}
such that
x\leq y
,

q(x)\leq p(y)
; for example if
q\leq p
, and
p
or
q
is
monotonic. There is an affine monotonic map

\Lambda\colon\mathfrak{B}\to\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
such that
q\leq\Lambda\leq p
.

If
\mathfrak{B}
is a preordered pointed barycentric algebra and
p
is
strict (for example, sublinear), then
\Lambda
is linear.

Proof.

We embed
\mathfrak{B}
in the preordered cone
\mathrm{cone}(\mathfrak{B})
through the
injective affine preorder embedding
\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{B}}
, see
Proposition
3.4
. We will not need that

\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{B}}
is injective or a preorder embedding, just that it is
affine and monotonic. By Lemma
3.2
,
q^{\leftslice}

is superlinear and
p^{\leftslice}
is sublinear.

We claim that for all
u,v\in\mathrm{cone}(\mathfrak{B})
such that
u\leq^{\mathrm{cone}}v
,

q^{\leftslice}(u)\leq p^{\leftslice}(v)
. If
u=v
, this is clear.
Otherwise,
v
can be written as
(s,y)
and there are

u^{\prime}\in\mathrm{cone}(\mathfrak{B})
and
y_{1}\in\mathfrak{B}
such that
u+u^{\prime}=(s,y_{1})

and
y_{1}\leq y
. Then

q^{\leftslice}(u+u^{\prime})\geq q^{\leftslice}(u)+q^{\leftslice}(v)
(since
q^{\leftslice}

is superlinear)
\geq q^{\leftslice}(u)
. Since
u+u^{\prime}=(s,y_{1})
,

q^{\leftslice}(u)\leq q^{\leftslice}(u+u^{\prime})=sq(y_{1})
, which is less than or
equal to
sp(y)
by the assumption on
p
and
q
. But

sp(y)=p^{\leftslice}(v)
.

By Roth’s sandwich theorem, there is a linear monotonic map

\Lambda_{1}\colon\mathrm{cone}(\mathfrak{B})\to\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
and

q^{\leftslice}\leq\Lambda_{1}\leq p^{\leftslice}
. Now

q=q^{\leftslice}\circ\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{B}}\leq\Lambda_{1}\circ\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{B}}\leq p^{\leftslice}\circ\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{B}}=p
. Therefore, letting

\Lambda\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\Lambda_{1}\circ\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{B}}
,

q\leq\Lambda\leq p
. Additionally, since
\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{B}}
is affine
and monotonic, so is
\Lambda
.

In the case of preordered pointed barycentric algebras, it would be
tempting to use the monotonic linear map
\eta^{\mathrm{tscope},\alpha}_{\mathfrak{B}}
of
\mathfrak{B}
to

\mathrm{tscope}_{\alpha}(\mathfrak{B})
instead, but this would require additional
assumptions on
q
and
p
, and is useless: instead, we use the
first part of the corollary, and we find a monotonic affine map

\Lambda
such that
q\leq\Lambda\leq p
; since
p
is strict,

p(\bot)=0
, so
\Lambda(\bot)=0
, and therefore
\Lambda
is
strict, too. But strict affine maps and linear maps are the same
thing by Proposition
4.6
.
\Box

The topological analogue is
Keimel’s sandwich theorem

[
30
, Theorem 8.2]
: given a semitopological cone

\mathfrak{C}
, a sublinear map
p\colon\mathfrak{C}\to\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
and a lower
semicontinuous superlinear map
q\colon\mathfrak{C}\to\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
such that

q\leq p
, there is a lower semicontinuous linear map
\Lambda
such
that
q\leq\Lambda\leq p
. Keimel deduces it from Roth’s sandwich
theorem. A remarkable fact about Keimel’s sandwich theorem is that we
do not need to assume any continuity or even any monotonicity property
of
p
. Only
q
has to be lower semicontinuous.

Theorem 8.2
(Sandwich theorem, semitopological)

Let
\mathfrak{B}
be a semitopological barycentric algebra,

p\colon\mathfrak{B}\to\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
be convex,
q\colon\mathfrak{B}\to\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
be
concave and lower semicontinuous, and let us assume that
q\leq p
.
There is an affine lower semicontinuous map

\Lambda\colon\mathfrak{B}\to\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
such that
q\leq\Lambda\leq p
.

If
\mathfrak{B}
is pointed and
p
is strict (e.g., sublinear), then

\Lambda
is linear.

Proof.

We use Theorem
3.22
and we obtain that

\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{B}}
is an injective, affine continuous map from
\mathfrak{B}
to the
semitopological cone
\mathrm{cone}(\mathfrak{B})
. Since
q
is concave,

q^{\leftslice}
is superlinear and lower semicontinuous from

\mathrm{cone}(\mathfrak{B})
to
\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
. We cannot use
Theorem
3.22
on
p^{\leftslice}
, since it says nothing
about convex maps, but
p^{\leftslice}
is sublinear by
Lemma
3.2
. Now
q^{\leftslice}\leq p^{\leftslice}
:

q^{\leftslice}(0)=0=p^{\leftslice}(0)
, and for all points
(r,x)
with

r>0
and
x\in\mathfrak{B}
,

q^{\leftslice}(r,x)=r\,q(x)\leq r\,p(x)=p^{\leftslice}(r,x)
.
Then, by using Keimel’s sandwich theorem (and remembering that

p^{\leftslice}
does not have to be lower semicontinuous, or even
monotonic), there is a linear lower semicontinuous map

\Lambda_{1}\colon\mathrm{cone}(\mathfrak{B})\to\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
such that

q^{\leftslice}\leq\Lambda_{1}\leq p^{\leftslice}
. Then

\Lambda\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\Lambda_{1}\circ\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{B}}
is affine and lower
semicontinuous, and
q\leq\Lambda\leq p
.

Finally, if
\mathfrak{B}
is pointed and
p
is strict, then
\Lambda
is
strict, hence linear by Proposition
4.6
.
\Box

The following is proved by Keimel
[
30
, Corollary 9.3, Corollary
9.4]
under the additional assumption that
\mathfrak{C}
is

T_{0}
. That assumption is unneeded. We include the proof for
completeness. One may wonder why we do not state this at the level of
barycentric algebras, and this is because it would be wrong, see
Remark
8.4
.

Proposition 8.3

Let
\mathfrak{C}
be a locally convex semitopological cone. Then:

1.

\mathfrak{C}
is linearly separated;

2.

for every non-empty closed convex subset
C
of
\mathfrak{C}
, every
point
x
outside
C
is contained in some open half-space that
does not intersect
C
.

Proof.

We prove item 2. Item 1 follows: given
x,y\in\mathfrak{C}
such that
x\not\leq y
, we apply item 2 with
C\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\dc y
.

Using the fact that
\mathfrak{C}
is locally convex, there is a convex open
subset
U
of
\mathfrak{C}
that contains
x
and is included in

\mathfrak{C}\smallsetminus C
, namely is disjoint from
C
. Let
q
be the upper
Minkowski functional
M^{U}
; since
U
is proper, convex and open,

q
is superlinear and lower semicontinuous. Let

p\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}M^{\mathfrak{C}\smallsetminus\dc y}
. Since
C
is convex,

\mathfrak{C}\smallsetminus\dc y
is concave; it is open and proper, so
p
is
sublinear (and lower semicontinuous). Additionally,
q\leq p
,
because
U\subseteq\mathfrak{C}\smallsetminus C
. By Keimel’s sandwich theorem,
there is a linear lower semicontinuous map
\Lambda
such that

q\leq\Lambda\leq p
. Then

q^{-1}(]1,\infty])=U\subseteq\Lambda^{-1}(]1,\infty])\subseteq p^{-1}(]1,\infty])=\mathfrak{C}\smallsetminus C
, so

H\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\Lambda^{-1}(]1,\infty])
is an open half-space that
contains
U
(hence
x
) but is disjoint from
C
.
\Box

Remark 8.4

A locally convex semitopological barycentric algebra may fail to be
linearly separated: by Example
6.5
, the
semitopological barycentric algebra
\mathfrak{B}^{-}
of
Example
3.24
is locally convex; it is not
linearly separated because its only semi-concave lower
semicontinuous maps, hence also its only linear lower semicontinuous
maps, are constant.

Cones also enjoy the following
strict separation theorem

[
30
, Theorem 10.5]
: given a locally convex
semitopological cone
\mathfrak{C}
, a convex compact subset
Q
of
\mathfrak{C}
, and a
non-empty closed convex
C
disjoint from
Q
, there is a linear lower
semicontinuous map
\Lambda\colon\mathfrak{C}\to\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
and a number
a>1

such that
\Lambda
takes values in
[a,\infty]
on
Q
and values in

[0,1]
on
C
.

8.2
Convex compact saturated sets

The strict separation theorem has the following consequence.

Lemma 8.5

In a locally convex semitopological cone
\mathfrak{C}
, every convex compact
saturated subset
Q
is the intersection of the open half-spaces
that contain it.

Proof.

That intersection, call it
A
, contains the intersection of all the
