open neighborhoods of
Q
, which coincides with
Q
since
Q
is
saturated. Conversely, we show that
A
is included in
Q
by
showing that every point
x\in\mathfrak{C}\smallsetminus Q
is outside
A
. For
each such
x
,
C\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\dc x
is a non-empty closed convex subset
of
\mathfrak{C}
that is disjoint from
Q
, since
Q
is upwards-closed. By
the strict separation theorem, there is an open half-space
H

containing
Q
and not containing
x
. Hence
x
is not in
A
.
\Box

We apply all this to barycentric algebras. Item 2 below was proved
for cones as
[
30
, Corollary 10.4]
.

Proposition 8.6

In a linearly separated semitopological barycentric algebra
\mathfrak{B}
,
every convex compact saturated subset
Q
of
\mathfrak{B}
is equal to:

1.

the intersection of its open neighborhoods of the form

\Lambda^{-1}(]1,\infty])
with
\Lambda\colon\mathfrak{B}\to\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}

affine (even linear if
\mathfrak{B}
is pointed) and lower semicontinuous;

2.

the filtered intersection of its convex open neighborhoods.

Proof.

1. It suffices to show that for every point
x\in\mathfrak{B}
that is not
in
Q
, there is a convex open neighborhood of
Q
, of the required
form
\Lambda^{-1}(]1,\infty])
, that does not contain
x
.

By Lemma
6.22
, item 1, there is an affine
(even linear when
\mathfrak{B}
is pointed) continuous map

\eta\colon\mathfrak{B}\to\mathfrak{C}
of
\mathfrak{B}
to a locally linear semitopological
cone
\mathfrak{C}
that is also a preorder embedding. In particular,
\mathfrak{C}
is
locally convex.

Since
\eta
is continuous,
\upc\eta[Q]
is compact saturated,
and
\upc\eta[Q]
is convex because
\eta[Q]
is convex, owing
to the fact that
\eta
is affine, and by Definition and
Proposition
5.8
for upward closures.

We claim that
\eta(x)\not\in\upc\eta[Q]
. Otherwise there would be
a point
y\in Q
such that
\eta(y)\leq\eta(x)
. Since
\eta

is a preorder embedding, we would have
y\leq x
, and since
Q
is
upwards-closed,
x
would be in
Q
, which is impossible.

Hence we can apply Lemma
8.5
: there is a
(necessarily proper) open half-space
H
of
\mathfrak{C}
that contains

\upc\eta[Q]
and does not contain
\eta(x)
. Then

U\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\eta^{-1}(H)
is open and convex. We can write
H
as

(M^{H})^{-1}(]1,\infty])
, where
M^{H}
is the upper Minkowski
functional of
H
, and then
U=\Lambda^{-1}(]1,\infty])
, where

\Lambda\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}M^{H}\circ\eta
is affine (even linear when
\mathfrak{B}
is
pointed) and lower semicontinuous. Every point of
Q
is in
U

since
\eta[Q]\subseteq H
, and
x\not\in U
since otherwise

\eta(x)
would be in
H
.

2. By item 1,
Q
is also the intersection of the larger family of
its convex open neighborhoods. Since
\mathfrak{B}
is convex and open, and
since the intersection of any two convex open sets is convex and
open, we conclude.
\Box

9
Local convex-compactness

The notion of a
locally convex-compact
cone was introduced by
Keimel
[
30
, Definition 4.9]
. It easily generalizes
to semitopological barycentric algebras, as follows.

9.1
Locally convex-compact semitopological barycentric
algebras

Definition 9.1

A
locally convex-compact barycentric algebra
is a
semitopological barycentric algebra in which every point has a
neighborhood base consisting of convex compact sets.

It is clear that local convex-compactness implies both
weak

local convexity, and local compactness.

Remark 9.2

A semitopological barycentric algebra is locally convex-compact if
and only if every point has a neighborhood base of convex compact

saturated
sets. Indeed, if
Q
is convex and compact, then

\upc Q
is convex, compact saturated, and contains
Q
, while still
being included in any open neighborhood of
Q
.

We already know that every c-cone, every semitopological barycentric
algebra whose underlying topological space is a c-space, is locally
convex and topological (Proposition
6.6
).
Additionally, we have the following.

Proposition 9.3

Every semitopological barycentric algebra whose underlying
topological space is a c-space is locally convex-compact.

Proof.

Let
\mathfrak{B}
be such a semitopological barycentric algebra. For every
point
x\in\mathfrak{B}
, for every open neighborhood
U
of
x
, there is a
point
y\in U
such that
x\in\text{int}({\upc y})
. We conclude
since
\upc y
is both convex and compact.
\Box

Another source of such barycentric algebras and cones is the
following.

Theorem 9.4

For every core-compact space
X
,
{\mathbf{V}}X
is a locally linear,
locally convex-compact, sober topological cone;
{\mathbf{V}}_{\leq 1}X
is
a locally linear, locally convex-compact, sober pointed topological
barycentric algebra; if
X
is compact in addition, then
{\mathbf{V}}_{1}X

is a locally affine, locally convex-compact, sober topological
barycentric algebra.

Proof.

Theorem 4.1 of
[
22
]
states that
{\mathbf{V}}X
and

{\mathbf{V}}_{\leq 1}X
are locally compact if
X
is core-compact, and
that
{\mathbf{V}}_{1}X
is locally compact if
X
is core-compact and
compact. The proof reveals that a base of compact saturated
neighborhoods of each element
\nu
of the relevant space

{\mathbf{V}}_{\circledast}X
of valuations is given by sets
s_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}(F)
, where:

\displaystyle s_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}(F)

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\Big\{\nu^{\prime}\in{\mathbf{V}}_{\circledast}X\mid\forall h\in\mathcal{L}X,\int h\,d\nu^{\prime}\geq F(h)\Big\},

and
F
ranges over a certain set of superlinear previsions
(subnormalized if
\circledast
is “
\leq 1
”, normalized if
\circledast
is
“
1
”). We will not describe what superlinear previsions are: we
only need to observe that
s_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}(F)
is convex. Hence

{\mathbf{V}}_{\circledast}X
is locally convex-compact. It is sober by Lemma 4.3
of
[
22
]
; the argument is due to Regina Tix

[
52
, Satz 5.4]
, following ideas by Heckmann (see

[
23
, Section 2.3]
). It is locally linear (locally
affine when
\circledast
is “
1
”) by Examples
6.12
,

6.13
and
6.14
. It is topological
by Examples
2.5
and
3.10
.

\Box

Proposition 9.5

Every affine retract of a locally convex-compact semitopological
barycentric algebra is locally convex-compact.

In particular, every linear retract of a locally convex-compact
semitopological cone is locally convex-compact.

Proof.

The proof is as for Proposition
6.48
, item 1.
Let
r\colon\mathfrak{B}\to\mathfrak{A}
be an affine retraction between
semitopological barycentric algebras, with associated section
s
,
where
\mathfrak{B}
is locally convex-compact. Let
y\in\mathfrak{A}
and
V
be
an open neighborhood of
y
in
\mathfrak{A}
. Then
r^{-1}(V)
is an open
neighborhood of
s(y)
, and therefore contains a convex compact
neighborhood
Q
of
s(y)
. Then
s^{-1}(\text{int}({Q}))
is an open
neighborhood of
y
, which is included in
r[Q]
, and
r[Q]
is
convex because
Q
is and
r
is affine, and is compact because
Q

is compact and
r
is continuous. Finally,
r[Q]\subseteq V
,
since
Q\subseteq r^{-1}(V)
.
\Box

9.2
Convenient barycentric algebras

Definition 9.6
(Convenient)

A
convenient barycentric algebra
is a linearly separated,
locally convex-compact, sober topological barycentric algebra.
Similarly with pointed barycentric algebras and cones.

Example 9.7

Since every locally affine semitopological barycentric algebra is
linearly separated, Theorem
9.4
implies that

{\mathbf{V}}X
,
{\mathbf{V}}_{\leq 1}X
and
{\mathbf{V}}_{1}X
are convenient for every
core-compact space
X
.

Example 9.8

Let us call
d-barycentric algebra
any s-barycentric algebra
that is a dcpo in its ordering. (A
d-cone
is an ordered cone
that is a d-barycentric algebra
[
30
, Definition
6.1]
.) The continuous d-barycentric algebras,
with their Scott topology, are the same thing as the semitopological
barycentric algebras whose underlying space is a sober c-space;
indeed, a sober c-space is the same thing as a continuous dcpo with
its Scott topology
[
19
, Proposition 8.3.36]
.

Then every continuous d-barycentric algebra
\mathfrak{B}
that is linearly
separated is a convenient barycentric algebra, and every continuous
d-cone is a convenient cone.

Proof.

By Proposition
6.6
,
\mathfrak{B}
is locally convex
and topological. Hence, by
Proposition
9.3
,
\mathfrak{B}
is locally convex,
locally convex-compact. It is sober, and topological (by Ershov’s
observation) in its Scott topology; only linear separation may be
missing. When
\mathfrak{B}
is a cone, nothing is missing: as we will see in
Proposition
8.3
, every locally convex
semitopological cone is linearly separated.

\Box

Lemma 9.9

Let
\mathfrak{B}
be a locally convex-compact topological barycentric
algebra. For every convex compact saturated subset
Q
of
\mathfrak{B}
and
every convex upwards-closed neighborhood
C
of
Q
, there is a
convex compact saturated subset
Q^{\prime}
of
C
whose interior contains

Q
.

Proof.

Let
Q
and
C
be as given. For each
x\in Q
, there is a convex
compact neighborhood
Q_{x}
of
x
included in
\text{int}({C})
, and we
can take it saturated by Remark
9.2
. Since
Q
is
compact, there is a finite subset
E
of
U
such that

Q\subseteq\bigcup_{x\in E}\text{int}({Q_{x}})
. Let

Q^{\prime}\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\upc\mathop{\mathrm{conv}}{(\bigcup_{x\in E}Q_{x})}
. By
Proposition
5.10
, which we can apply since

\mathfrak{B}
is topological,
Q^{\prime}
is convex and compact saturated. In order
to see that
Q^{\prime}\subseteq C
, we observe that
Q^{\prime}
is the saturated
convex hull of
\bigcup_{x\in E}Q_{x}
, by Definition and
Proposition
5.8
. Since
C
is upwards-closed and
convex, the inclusion then follows from the fact that

Q_{x}\subseteq C
for every
x\in E
. Finally,
Q^{\prime}
contains the
open set
\bigcup_{x\in E}\text{int}({Q_{x}})
, which contains
Q
.
\Box

The following is similar to
[
30
, Proposition 10.6]
,
which states the same for locally convex, locally convex-compact
topological cones, and part of the argument is due to Achim Jung.
Note that, instead of local convexity, we only require linear
separation, as part of the definition of “convenient”.

Proposition 9.10

Let
\mathfrak{B}
be a convenient barycentric algebra. Then every convex
compact saturated subset
Q
of
\mathfrak{B}
has:

1.

a base of convex compact saturated neighborhoods;

2.

and a base of convex open neighborhoods.

In other words, for every open neighborhood
U
of
Q
, there is a
convex compact saturated set
Q^{\prime}
such that

Q\subseteq\text{int}({Q^{\prime}})\subseteq Q^{\prime}\subseteq U
, and there is a
convex open set
V
such that
Q\subseteq V\subseteq U
.

Proof.

For every convex upwards-closed neighborhood
C
of
Q
, there is a
convex compact saturated neighborhood
Q^{\prime}
of
Q
included in
C
,
by Lemma
9.9
. Taking
C\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\mathfrak{B}
, this
entails that the family
\mathcal{F}
of all convex compact saturated
neighborhoods of
Q
is non-empty. Additionally, for any two
elements
Q_{1}
,
Q_{2}
of
\mathcal{F}
,
C\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}Q_{1}\cap Q_{2}
is a
convex upwards-closed neighborhood of
Q
, so there is an element

Q^{\prime}
of
\mathcal{F}
included in
C
, showing that
\mathcal{F}
is a
filtered family. Since
\mathfrak{B}
is sober hence well-filtered, the
intersection
\bigcap\mathcal{F}
is compact saturated and contains

Q
.

By Proposition
8.6
, item 2, which we can apply
since
\mathfrak{B}
is linearly separated,
Q
is the intersection of its
convex open neighborhoods. Each one contains an element of

\mathcal{F}
, by Lemma
9.9
. It follows
that
Q=\bigcap\mathcal{F}
.

1. Let
U
be any open neighborhood of
Q
. From

\bigcap\mathcal{F}\subseteq U
and the fact that
\mathfrak{B}
is
well-filtered, there is an element
Q^{\prime}\in\mathcal{F}
such that

Q^{\prime}\subseteq U
.

2. Let
U
be, once again, an open neighborhood of
Q
. By item 1,
there is a convex compact saturated neighborhood
Q_{0}
of
Q
such
that
Q_{0}\subseteq U
. By item 1 again, there is a convex compact
saturated neighborhood
Q_{1}
of
Q_{0}
such that
Q_{1}\subseteq U
.
Proceeding in this way, we obtain an ascending sequence

Q\subseteq\text{int}({Q_{0}})\subseteq Q_{0}\subseteq\text{int}({Q_{1}})\subseteq Q_{1}\subseteq\cdots\subseteq\text{int}({Q_{n}})\subseteq Q_{n}\subseteq\cdots
, where each
Q_{n}
is convex, compact
saturated, and included in
U
. Let

V\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\bigcup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}Q_{n}
. By
Lemma
5.2
,
V
is convex.
V
is also equal to

\bigcup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\text{int}({Q_{n}})
, hence is open. Finally, it is
clear that
V
contains
Q
and is included in
U
.
\Box

Corollary 9.11

Every convenient barycentric algebra is locally convex.

Proof.

For each point
x
, we apply
Proposition
9.10
, item 2, to the convex
compact saturated
\upc x
.
\Box

9.3
The Smyth poweralgebra

On top of any topological cone
\mathfrak{C}
, one can build its
Smyth
powercone

{\mathcal{Q}}^{cvx}\mathfrak{C}

[
30
, Section 11]
.
The construction extends to topological barycentric algebras.

Definition 9.12

For every topological barycentric algebra
\mathfrak{B}
, its
Smyth
poweralgebra

{\mathcal{Q}}^{\mathrm{cvx}}\mathfrak{B}
over
\mathfrak{B}
is the collection of all
non-empty, convex, compact saturated subsets of
\mathfrak{B}
. For all

Q_{1},Q_{2}\in{\mathcal{Q}}^{\mathrm{cvx}}\mathfrak{B}
, for every
a\in[0,1]
, we define

Q_{1}+^{\sharp}_{a}Q_{2}
as

\upc\{x_{1}+_{a}x_{2}\mid x_{1}\in Q_{1},x_{2}\in Q_{2}\}
.

We require
\mathfrak{B}
to be topological in
Definition
9.12
: this is needed for
+^{\sharp}_{a}

to be well-defined, as the proof of the following lemma shows.

Lemma 9.13

For every topological barycentric algebra
\mathfrak{B}
,
{\mathcal{Q}}^{\mathrm{cvx}}\mathfrak{B}
with
the operations
+^{\sharp}_{a}
,
a\in[0,1]
, is a barycentric
algebra.

Proof.

We first verify that
+^{\sharp}_{a}
is well-defined for every

a\in[0,1]
. For all
Q_{1},Q_{2}\in{\mathcal{Q}}^{\mathrm{cvx}}\mathfrak{B}
,

\upc\{x_{1}+_{a}x_{2}\mid x_{1}\in Q_{1},x_{2}\in Q_{2}\}
is compact
saturated because it is the upward closure of the image of the
compact set
Q_{1}\times Q_{2}
by the (jointly) continuous map

(x_{1},x_{2})\mapsto x_{1}+_{a}x_{2}
. In order to see that it is
convex, it suffices to show that

A\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\{x_{1}+_{a}x_{2}\mid x_{1}\in Q_{1},x_{2}\in Q_{2}\}
is convex,
since this property is preserved by upward closures, as we have seen
in Definition and Proposition
5.8
. For every

b\in[0,1]
, for every two elements
x\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}x_{1}+_{a}x_{2}
and

y\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}y_{1}+_{a}y_{2}
of
A
(with
x_{1},y_{1}\in Q_{1}
and

x_{2},y_{2}\in Q_{2}
),
x+_{b}y=(x_{1}+_{b}y_{1})+_{a}(x_{2}+_{b}y_{2})
, as
one can check easily by embedding
\mathfrak{B}
in a cone and checking the
equation there (this is the entropic law (
2
)).
Since
Q_{1}
is convex,
x_{1}+_{b}y_{1}
is in
Q_{1}
and since
Q_{2}
is
convex,
x_{2}+_{b}y_{2}
is in
Q_{2}
, so
x+_{b}y\in A
. Hence

\upc A
is convex. We have seen that it is compact saturated, and
it is clearly non-empty. Hence it is a well-defined element

Q_{1}+^{\sharp}_{a}Q_{2}
of
{\mathcal{Q}}^{\mathrm{cvx}}\mathfrak{B}
.

We now verify the laws of barycentric algebras.
When
a=1
,
Q_{1}+^{\sharp}_{a}Q_{2}=\upc\{x_{1}\mid x_{1}\in Q_{1},x_{2}\in Q_{2}\}=\upc Q_{1}=Q_{1}
.

When
Q_{1}=Q_{2}
, every point of
Q_{1}+^{\sharp}_{a}Q_{1}
is of the form

x_{1}+_{a}x_{2}
with
x_{1},x_{2}\in Q_{1}
; since
Q_{1}
is convex, every
such point is in
Q_{1}
, so
Q_{1}+^{\sharp}_{a}Q_{1}\subseteq Q_{1}
.
Conversely, for every
x\in Q_{1}
, we can write
x
as
x+_{a}x
,
showing that
Q_{1}\subseteq Q_{1}+^{\sharp}Q_{1}
. Hence
Q_{1}+^{\sharp}Q_{1}=Q_{1}
.

It is clear that
Q_{1}+^{\sharp}_{a}Q_{2}=Q_{2}+^{\sharp}_{1-a}Q_{1}
for
all
Q_{1},Q_{2}\in{\mathcal{Q}}^{\mathrm{cvx}}\mathfrak{B}
.

Finally, we must show that

(Q_{1}+^{\sharp}_{a}Q_{2})+^{\sharp}_{b}Q_{3}=Q_{1}+^{\sharp}_{ab}(Q_{2}+^{\sharp}_{\frac{(1-a)b}{1-ab}}Q_{3})
, for all

Q_{1},Q_{2},Q_{3}\in{\mathcal{Q}}^{\mathrm{cvx}}\mathfrak{B}
and
a,b\in{[0,1[}
. We will
only show that the left-hand side is included in the right-hand
side, as the reverse inclusion is proved similarly. For every

z\in(Q_{1}+^{\sharp}_{a}Q_{2})+^{\sharp}_{b}Q_{3}
, there are points

y\in Q_{1}+^{\sharp}_{a}Q_{2}
and
x_{3}\in Q_{3}
such that

y+_{b}x_{3}\leq z
; then, there are points
x_{1}\in Q_{1}
and

x_{2}\in Q_{2}
such that
x_{1}+_{a}x_{2}\leq y
. Since continuous maps
are monotonic,
(x_{1}+_{a}x_{2})+_{b}x_{3}\leq z
. But

(x_{1}+_{a}x_{2})+_{b}x_{3}=x_{1}+_{ab}(x_{2}+_{\frac{(1-a)b}{1-ab}}x_{3})
, and

x_{2}+_{\frac{(1-a)b}{1-ab}}x_{3}\in Q_{2}+^{\sharp}_{\frac{(1-a)b}{1-ab}}Q_{3}
, so

z\in Q_{1}+^{\sharp}_{ab}(Q_{2}+^{\sharp}_{\frac{(1-a)b}{1-ab}}Q_{3})
.
\Box

The weighted sums used in the following lemma have to be understood as
in Definition and Proposition
3.1
.

Lemma 9.14

Let
\mathfrak{B}
be a topological barycentric algebra.

1.

for all points
x_{1},\cdots,x_{m},y_{1},\cdots,y_{n}\in\mathfrak{B}

(
m,n\geq 1
), for every
a\in[0,1]
,

{\upc\mathop{\mathrm{conv}}}{\{x_{1},\cdots,x_{m}\}}+^{\sharp}_{a}\allowbreak{\upc\mathop{\mathrm{conv}}}{\{y_{1},\cdots,y_{n}\}}=\upc\mathop{\mathrm{conv}}\{x_{i}+_{a}y_{j}\mid 1\leq i\leq m,\allowbreak 1\leq j\leq n\}
;

2.

for all finite non-empty subsets
E_{1}
, …,
E_{n}
of
\mathfrak{B}

and for every
n
-tuple
(a_{1},\cdots,a_{n})
in the standard
simplex
\Delta_{n}
(with
n\geq 1
),

\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\cdot{\upc\mathop{\mathrm{conv}}{E_{i}}}=\upc\mathop{\mathrm{conv}}{\{\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\cdot x_{i}\mid x_{1}\in E_{1},\cdots,x_{n}\in E_{n}\}}
;

3.

for all finite non-empty subsets
E_{1}
, …,
E_{n}
of
\mathfrak{B}
,
the convex closure of

\{\upc\mathop{\mathrm{conv}}{E_{1}},\cdots,\upc\mathop{\mathrm{conv}}{E_{n}}\}
in
{\mathcal{Q}}^{\mathrm{cvx}}\mathfrak{B}

consists of all the sets of the form

\upc\mathop{\mathrm{conv}}{\{\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\cdot x_{i}\mid x_{1}\in E_{1},\cdots,x_{n}\in E_{n}\}}
, where

(a_{1},\cdots,a_{n})\in\Delta_{n}
.

Proof.

1. Every point
z
in the left-hand side is larger than or equal to
some point of the form
x+_{a}y
where
x
is larger than or equal
to some convex combination
\sum_{i=1}^{m}a_{i}\cdot x_{i}
and
y
is
larger than or equal to some convex combination

\sum_{j=1}^{n}b_{j}\cdot y_{j}
, with
(a_{1},\cdots,a_{m})\in\Delta_{m}

and
(b_{1},\cdots,b_{n})\in\Delta_{n}
, by Definition and
Lemma
5.3
. In order to reason on those
sums, we use Definition and Proposition
3.1
, and
therefore we reason in the cone
\mathrm{cone}(\mathfrak{B})
, using the injective
affine map
\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{B}}
.

Using the fact that
\sum_{j=1}^{n}b_{j}=1
, we have

\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{B}}(\sum_{i=1}^{m}a_{i}\cdot x_{i})=\sum_{i=1}^{m}a_{i}\cdot\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{B}}(x_{i})=\sum_{i=1}^{m}a_{i}(\sum_{j=1}^{n}b_{j})\cdot\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{B}}(x_{i})=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq i\leq m\\
1\leq j\leq n\end{subarray}}a_{i}b_{j}\cdot\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{B}}(x_{i})
, and similarly

\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{B}}(\sum_{j=1}^{n}b_{j}\cdot y_{j})=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq i\leq m\\
1\leq j\leq n\end{subarray}}a_{i}b_{j}\cdot\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{B}}(y_{j})
, so

\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{B}}(\sum_{i=1}^{m}a_{i}\cdot x_{i}+_{a}\sum_{j=1}^{n}b_{j}\cdot y_{j})=a\cdot\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq i\leq m\\
1\leq j\leq n\end{subarray}}a_{i}b_{j}\cdot\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{B}}(x_{i})+(1-a)\cdot\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq i\leq m\\
1\leq j\leq n\end{subarray}}a_{i}b_{j}\cdot\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{B}}(y_{j})=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq i\leq m\\
1\leq j\leq n\end{subarray}}a_{i}b_{j}\cdot(a\cdot\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{B}}(x_{i})+(1-a)\cdot\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{B}}(y_{j}))=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq i\leq m\\
1\leq j\leq n\end{subarray}}a_{i}b_{j}\cdot\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{B}}(x_{i}+_{a}y_{j})
(since
\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{B}}
is affine)

=\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{B}}(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq i\leq m\\
1\leq j\leq n\end{subarray}}a_{i}b_{j}\cdot(x_{i}+_{a}y_{j}))
. Since
\eta^{\mathrm{cone}}_{\mathfrak{B}}
is injective,

\sum_{i=1}^{m}a_{i}\cdot x_{i}+_{a}\sum_{j=1}^{n}b_{j}\cdot y_{j}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq i\leq m\\
1\leq j\leq n\end{subarray}}a_{i}b_{j}\cdot(x_{i}+_{a}y_{j})
. Now the left-hand side is less than or equal to

x+_{a}y
(since
+_{a}
is monotonic), which is less than or equal to

z
, so

z\geq\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq i\leq m\\
1\leq j\leq n\end{subarray}}a_{i}b_{j}\cdot(x_{i}+_{a}y_{j})
. Hence

z\in\upc\mathop{\mathrm{conv}}{\{x_{i}+_{a}y_{j}\mid 1\leq i\leq m,1\leq j\leq n\}}
.

Conversely, for every

z\in\upc\mathop{\mathrm{conv}}{\{x_{i}+_{a}y_{j}\mid 1\leq i\leq m,1\leq j\leq n\}}
, there is a tuple of non-negative real numbers
c_{ij}

whose sum equals
1
and such that

z\geq\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq i\leq m\\
1\leq j\leq n\end{subarray}}c_{ij}\cdot(x_{i}+_{a}y_{j})
. Hence
z\geq x+_{a}y
where

x\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq i\leq m\\
1\leq j\leq n\end{subarray}}c_{ij}\cdot x_{i}
and

y\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq i\leq m\\
1\leq j\leq n\end{subarray}}c_{ij}\cdot y_{j}
, showing that

z\in\upc\mathop{\mathrm{conv}}{\{x_{1},\cdots,x_{m}\}}+^{\sharp}_{a}\allowbreak\upc\mathop{\mathrm{conv}}{\{y_{1},\cdots,y_{n}\}}
.

2. By induction on
n
, using Definition and
Proposition
3.1
, item 1. If
a_{n}=1
, then

\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\cdot\upc\mathop{\mathrm{conv}}E_{i}=\upc\mathop{\mathrm{conv}}E_{n}
, while

\upc\mathop{\mathrm{conv}}\{\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\cdot x_{i}\mid x_{1}\in E_{1},\allowbreak\cdots,\allowbreak x_{n}\in E_{n}\}=\upc\mathop{\mathrm{conv}}\{x_{n}\mid x_{1}\in E_{1},\cdots,x_{n}\in E_{n}\}=\upc\mathop{\mathrm{conv}}\{x_{n}\mid x_{n}\in E_{n}\}
(since
E_{1}
, …,
E_{n-1}
are non-empty)

=\upc\mathop{\mathrm{conv}}E_{n}
.

If
a_{n}\neq 1
, then:

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\cdot\upc\mathop{\mathrm{conv}}E_{i}

\displaystyle=\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}\frac{a_{i}}{1-a_{n}}\cdot\upc\mathop{\mathrm{conv}}E_{i}\right)+_{1-a_{n}}\upc\mathop{\mathrm{conv}}E_{n}

\displaystyle=\upc\mathop{\mathrm{conv}}\left\{\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}\frac{a_{i}}{1-a_{n}}\cdot x_{i}\mid x_{1}\in E_{1},\cdots,x_{n-1}\in E_{n-1}\right\}

\displaystyle\quad+_{1-a_{n}}\upc\mathop{\mathrm{conv}}E_{n}\qquad\qquad\text{by induction hypothesis}

\displaystyle=\upc\mathop{\mathrm{conv}}\left\{\Big(\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}\frac{a_{i}}{1-a_{n}}\cdot x_{i}\Big)+_{1-a_{n}}x_{n}\right.

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\mid x_{1}\in E_{1},\cdots,x_{n}\in E_{n}\Big\}\qquad\text{by item~1}

\displaystyle=\upc\mathop{\mathrm{conv}}{\left\{\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\cdot x_{i}\mid x_{1}\in E_{1},\cdots,x_{n}\in E_{n}\right\}}

3. By Definition and Lemma
5.3
, the
desired convex closure is the collection of convex compact saturated
sets of the form
\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\cdot\upc\mathop{\mathrm{conv}}{E_{i}}
, and we
conclude by item 2.
\Box

There is a classical
Smyth hyperspace
construction
{\mathcal{Q}}X
,
consisting of all non-empty compact saturated subsets of a subspace

X
. One of the standard topologies we give it is the
upper
Vietoris
topology, with a base of open sets

\Box U\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\{Q\in{\mathcal{Q}}X\mid Q\subseteq U\}
,

U\in{\mathcal{O}}X
. We write
{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}X
for
{\mathcal{Q}}X
with the Vietoris
topology. Its specialization ordering is
reverse
inclusion.

Definition 9.15
(
{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}^{\mathrm{cvx}}\mathfrak{B}
)

For every topological barycentric algebra
\mathfrak{B}
, let
{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}^{\mathrm{cvx}}\mathfrak{B}
be

{\mathcal{Q}}^{\mathrm{cvx}}\mathfrak{B}
with its
upper Vietoris topology
, generated by
basic open sets

\Box^{\mathrm{cvx}}U\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\{Q\in{\mathcal{Q}}^{\mathrm{cvx}}\mathfrak{B}\mid Q\subseteq U\}
where

U
ranges over the open subsets of
\mathfrak{B}
.

Hence
{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}^{\mathrm{cvx}}\mathfrak{B}
is a subspace of
{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}\mathfrak{B}
.

There is a continuous map
\eta^{\mathcal{Q}}_{X}\colon X\to{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}X
, defined
by
\eta^{\mathcal{Q}}_{X}(x)\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\upc x
on every space
X
. It is
continuous, and in fact full because

(\eta^{\mathcal{Q}}_{X})^{-1}(\Box U)=U
.

Lemma 9.16

For every topological barycentric algebra
\mathfrak{B}
, the map

\eta^{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathfrak{B}}
, defined as sending every
x\in\mathfrak{B}
to
\upc x
,
is affine, continuous and full. It is a topological embedding if

\mathfrak{B}
is
T_{0}
.

Proof.

First,
\upc x
is indeed convex, non-empty, and compact saturated.
Then, for every open subset
U
of
\mathfrak{B}
,

{(\eta^{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathfrak{B}})}^{-1}(\Box^{\mathrm{cvx}}U)=\{x\in\mathfrak{B}\mid\upc x\subseteq U\}=U
, showing that
\eta^{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathfrak{B}}
is continuous and
full; when
\mathfrak{B}
is
T_{0}
,
\eta^{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathfrak{B}}
is therefore a
topological embedding. The fact that
\eta^{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathfrak{B}}
is affine is
the special case
m=1
,
n=1
of
Lemma
9.14
, item 1.
\Box

Lemma 9.17

Given a topological barycentric algebra
\mathfrak{B}
,

1.

the sets
\Box^{\mathrm{cvx}}U
with
U
open in
\mathfrak{B}
form a base of
the topology on
{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}^{\mathrm{cvx}}\mathfrak{B}
;

2.

the specialization ordering on
{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}^{\mathrm{cvx}}\mathfrak{B}
is reverse inclusion

\supseteq
, and in particular
{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}^{\mathrm{cvx}}\mathfrak{B}
is
T_{0}
;

3.

if
\mathfrak{B}
is compact and non-empty, then
{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}^{\mathrm{cvx}}\mathfrak{B}
is pointed,
and
\mathfrak{B}
is its least element (its zero);

4.

if
\mathfrak{B}
is pointed, then
{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}^{\mathrm{cvx}}\mathfrak{B}
is pointed and

\eta^{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathfrak{B}}
is linear; if
\mathfrak{B}
is pointed and
T_{0}
, then

\eta^{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathfrak{B}}
is a linear topological embedding of
\mathfrak{B}
in

{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}^{\mathrm{cvx}}\mathfrak{B}
.

Proof.

1. For all
U,V\in{\mathcal{O}}\mathfrak{B}
,
\Box^{\mathrm{cvx}}U\cap\Box^{\mathrm{cvx}}V
,
while
\Box^{\mathrm{cvx}}\emptyset=\emptyset
. Hence every finite
intersection of subbasic open sets of the form
\Box^{\mathrm{cvx}}U
is
again of this form.

2. For all
Q_{1},Q_{2}\in{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}^{\mathrm{cvx}}\mathfrak{B}
, if
Q_{1}\supseteq Q_{2}
then
for every basic open neighborhood
\Box^{\mathrm{cvx}}U
of
Q_{1}
,
Q_{1}
is
included in
U
, hence also
Q_{2}
, so
Q_{2}\in\Box^{\mathrm{cvx}}U
.
Conversely, if
Q_{1}
is below
Q_{2}
in the specialization
preordering of
{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}^{\mathrm{cvx}}\mathfrak{B}
, every open neighborhood of the form

\Box^{\mathrm{cvx}}U
of
Q_{1}
contains
Q_{2}
; in other words,
for every open subset
U
of
\mathfrak{B}
, if
Q_{1}
is included in
U
, then

Q_{2}\subseteq U
. Since
Q_{1}
is saturated, it is equal to the
intersection of its open neighborhoods
U
, hence
Q_{2}\subseteq Q_{1}
.

3.
\mathfrak{B}
is convex and upwards-closed. If
\mathfrak{B}
is compact and
non-empty, then it is an element of
{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}^{\mathrm{cvx}}\mathfrak{B}
. By item 2, it is the
least element of
{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}^{\mathrm{cvx}}\mathfrak{B}
.

4. If
\mathfrak{B}
is pointed, then it is compact and non-empty, so

{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}^{\mathrm{cvx}}\mathfrak{B}
is pointed by item 3. Then

\eta^{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathfrak{B}}(\bot)=\dc\bot=\mathfrak{B}
, so
\eta^{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathfrak{B}}
is
strict. Since it is affine by Lemma
9.16
, it is
linear by Proposition
4.6
. Finally, when
\mathfrak{B}
is
also
T_{0}
,
\eta^{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathfrak{B}}
is a topological embedding by
Lemma
9.16
.
\Box

We will see that
{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}^{\mathrm{cvx}}\mathfrak{B}
is topological, not just semitopological.
For this, we need
Wallace’s theorem

[
32
, Chapter II,
Theorem 12]
: given
n
topological spaces
X_{1}
,
…,
X_{n}
, and compact subsets
K_{i}
of
X_{i}
for each

i\in\{1,\cdots,n\}
, every open neighborhood
W
of

\prod_{i=1}^{n}K_{i}
contains a product
\prod_{i=1}^{n}U_{i}
where each

U_{i}
is an open neighborhood of
K_{i}
in
X_{i}
.

Proposition 9.18

For every topological barycentric algebra
\mathfrak{B}
,
{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}^{\mathrm{cvx}}\mathfrak{B}
is a
topological barycentric algebra.

Proof.

We wish to show that the map

(Q_{1},a,Q_{2})\mapsto Q_{1}+^{\sharp}_{a}Q_{2}
is continuous from

{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}^{\mathrm{cvx}}\mathfrak{B}\times[0,1]\times{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}^{\mathrm{cvx}}\mathfrak{B}
to
{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}^{\mathrm{cvx}}\mathfrak{B}
. It suffices to
show that the inverse image of any set
\Box^{\mathrm{cvx}}W
, with
W
open
in
\mathfrak{B}
, under this function is open in

{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}^{\mathrm{cvx}}\mathfrak{B}\times[0,1]\times{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}^{\mathrm{cvx}}\mathfrak{B}
. Let us consider any element

(Q_{1},a,Q_{2})
of that inverse image. In other words,

Q_{1}+^{\sharp}_{a}Q_{2}\in\Box^{\mathrm{cvx}}W
. Since
\mathfrak{B}
is topological,
the set

O\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\{(x^{\prime},a^{\prime},y^{\prime})\in\mathfrak{B}\times[0,1]\times\mathfrak{B}\mid x^{\prime}+_{a^{\prime}}y^{\prime}\in W\}
is open, and
Q_{1}+^{\sharp}_{a}Q_{2}\in\Box^{\mathrm{cvx}}W

can be rephrased as
Q_{1}\times\{a\}\times Q_{2}\subseteq O
. By
Wallace’s theorem, there are open neighborhoods
U_{1}
of
Q_{1}
,
I

of
a
and
U_{2}
of
Q_{2}
such that

U_{1}\times I\times U_{2}\subseteq O
. Hence

\Box^{\mathrm{cvx}}{U_{1}}\times I\times\Box^{\mathrm{cvx}}{U_{2}}
is an open
neighborhood of
(Q_{1},a,Q_{2})
in

{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}^{\mathrm{cvx}}\mathfrak{B}\times[0,1]\times{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}^{\mathrm{cvx}}\mathfrak{B}
. It remains to see that it
is included in
\Box^{\mathrm{cvx}}W
. Every triple
(Q^{\prime}_{1},a^{\prime},Q^{\prime}_{2})
in

\Box^{\mathrm{cvx}}{U_{1}}\times I\times\Box^{\mathrm{cvx}}{U_{2}}
is such that

Q^{\prime}_{1}\subseteq U_{1}
,
a^{\prime}\in I
and
Q^{\prime}_{2}\subseteq U_{2}
, so for
all
x^{\prime}_{1}\in Q^{\prime}_{1}
,
a^{\prime}\in I
and
x^{\prime}_{2}\in Q^{\prime}_{2}
,

(x^{\prime}_{1},a^{\prime},x^{\prime}_{2})\in U_{1}\times I\times U_{2}\subseteq O
, namely

x^{\prime}_{1}+_{a^{\prime}}x^{\prime}_{2}\in W
. This entails that

\{x^{\prime}_{1}+_{a^{\prime}}x^{\prime}_{2}\mid x^{\prime}_{1}\in Q^{\prime}_{1},x^{\prime}_{2}\in Q^{\prime}_{2}\}
is included
in
W
, and since
W
is upwards-closed, that

Q^{\prime}_{1}+^{\sharp}_{a^{\prime}}Q^{\prime}_{2}
is included in
W
, equivalently is an
element of
\Box^{\mathrm{cvx}}W
.
\Box

For a topological cone
\mathfrak{C}
,
{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}^{\mathrm{cvx}}\mathfrak{C}
is a topological cone

[
30
, Theorem 11]
.

We turn to linear separation.

Lemma 9.19

Let
\mathfrak{B}
be a topological barycentric algebra. For every affine
lower semicontinuous map
\Lambda\colon\mathfrak{B}\to\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
, the
function
\min\Lambda\colon{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}^{\mathrm{cvx}}\mathfrak{B}\to\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
defined by

\min\Lambda(Q)\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\min_{x\in Q}\Lambda(x)
is affine and
lower semicontinuous. If
\mathfrak{B}
is pointed and
\Lambda
is linear,
then
\min\Lambda
is linear.

Proof.

The min is attained because
\Lambda
is lower semicontinuous and

Q
is compact and non-empty. Hence for every
r\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
,

\min_{x\in Q}\Lambda(x)>r
if and only if for every
x\in Q
,

\Lambda(x)>r
. It follows that

{(\min\Lambda)}^{-1}(]r,\infty])=\Box^{\mathrm{cvx}}{\Lambda^{-1}(]r,\infty])}
; so
\min\Lambda
is continuous.

For all
Q_{1},Q_{2}\in{\mathcal{Q}}^{\mathrm{cvx}}\mathfrak{B}
and for every
a\in[0,1]
,

\min\Lambda(Q_{1}+^{\sharp}_{a}Q_{2})=\min\{\Lambda(x_{1}+_{a}x_{2})\mid x_{1}\in Q_{1},x_{2}\in Q_{2}\}
(since taking the minimum of a
monotonic map over the upward-closure of a non-empty set is the same
as taking the minimum on that set)

=\min\{a\,\Lambda(x_{1})+(1-a)\,\Lambda(x_{2})\mid x_{1}\in Q_{1},x_{2}\in Q_{2}\}
(since
\Lambda
is affine)

=a\,\min\Lambda(Q_{1})+(1-a)\,\min\Lambda(Q_{2})
. Hence

\min\Lambda
is affine.

If
\mathfrak{B}
is pointed then
\mathfrak{B}
itself is the least element of

{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}^{\mathrm{cvx}}\mathfrak{B}
. Then
\min\Lambda(\mathfrak{B})=\Lambda(\bot)
, which is equal
to
0
if
\Lambda
is linear, hence strict. Therefore

\min\Lambda
is strict and affine, hence linear by
Proposition
4.6
.
\Box

Proposition 9.20

For every topological barycentric algebra
\mathfrak{B}
, if
\mathfrak{B}
is linearly
separated then so is
{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}^{\mathrm{cvx}}\mathfrak{B}
.

Proof.

Let
Q_{1},Q_{2}\in{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}^{\mathrm{cvx}}\mathfrak{B}
be such that
Q_{1}\not\supseteq Q_{2}
.
Therefore, there is a point
x\in Q_{2}
that is not in
Q_{1}
.
By Proposition
8.6
, item 1,
Q_{1}
is the
intersection of its open neighborhoods of the form

\Lambda^{-1}(]1,\infty])
with
\Lambda\colon\mathfrak{B}\to\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}

affine and lower semicontinuous. Hence one of them does not contain

x
. With this
\Lambda
, the function
\min\Lambda
is affine and
lower semicontinuous by Lemma
9.19
. Since

Q_{1}\subseteq\Lambda^{-1}(]1,\infty])
, it follows that

\min\Lambda(Q_{1})>1
. Since

x\not\in\Lambda^{-1}(]1,\infty])
and since
x\in Q_{2}
,

\min\Lambda(Q_{2})\leq\Lambda(x)\leq 1
. Hence

\min\Lambda(Q_{1})>\min\Lambda(Q_{2})
.
\Box

In what follows, we will need the following technical lemma. A

monotone convergence space
is a
T_{0}
space that is a dcpo in
its specialization ordering, and in which every open set is Scott-open

[
19
, Definition 8.2.33]
. Every sober space is a
monotone convergence space.

Lemma 9.21

Given any locally finitary compact monotone convergence space
X
,
the topology of
X
is the Scott topology of its specialization
ordering
\leq
, and
X
is sober.

Proof.

We define
\leq^{\sharp}
on the collection of non-empty finite
subsets of
X
by
A\leq^{\sharp}B
if and only if

\upc A\supseteq\upc B
.
Rudin’s Lemma

[
19
, Proposition 5.2.25]
states that in a poset
P
,
for every directed family
{(A_{i})}_{i\in I}
of non-empty finite
subsets of
P
with respect to
\leq^{\sharp}
, there is a directed
subset
D
of
\bigcup_{i\in I}A_{i}
such that
D
intersects every

A_{i}
. As a consequence, if
P
is a dcpo, then for any such
directed family, for every Scott-open subset
U
of
P
that
contains
\bigcap_{i\in I}\upc A_{i}\subseteq U
,
U
contains some

A_{i}
already
[
19
, Proposition 5.8.28]
.

Every open subset is Scott-open, by definition. Conversely, let
U

be a Scott-open subset of
X
. We show that
U
is an open
neighborhood (in the original topology) of any of its points
x
.
Let
D
be the collection of all the non-empty finite sets
A
such
that
x\in\text{int}({\upc A})
. By definition of locally finitary
compactness:
(*)
for every open neighborhood
V
(in the original
topology) of
x
, there is an
A\in D
such that
A\subseteq V
.
(Note that
A
cannot be empty, since
x\in\upc A
.) Taking

V\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}X
in
(*)
shows that
D
is non-empty. Given

A,B\in D
, taking

V\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\text{int}({\upc A})\cap\text{int}({\upc B})
in
(*)
shows
that there is a
C\in D
such that
C\subseteq\text{int}({\upc A})

and
C\subseteq\text{int}({\upc B})
, in particular

A,B\leq^{\sharp}C
. Hence
D
is directed in
\mathbb{P}_{\mathrm{fin}}^{*}(X)
with
respect to
\leq^{\sharp}
.

We claim that
\bigcap_{A\in D}\upc A\subseteq\upc x
. It
suffices to note that every open neighborhood
V
of
x
contains

\bigcap_{A\in D}\upc A
:
(*)
even tells us that
V
contains

\upc A
for some
A\in D
. Since
U
is upwards-closed, it
follows that
\bigcap_{A\in D}\upc A\subseteq U
. Now
U
is
Scott-closed, and
X
is a dcpo in its specialization ordering, so,
using the consequence of Rudin’s Lemma stated at the beginning of
this proof, some
A\in D
is included in
U
. This says that

x\in\text{int}({\upc A})\subseteq\upc A\subseteq U
, so
U
is
an open neighborhood of
x
in the original topology.

We now know that the topology of
X
is the Scott topology. By

[
19
, Exercise 5.2.31]
, the dcpos that are locally
finitary compact are exactly the quasi-continuous dcpos, and every
quasi-continuous dcpo is sober in its Scott topology
[
19
, Exercise
8.2.15]
.
\Box

Proposition 9.22

For every convenient barycentric algebra
\mathfrak{B}
,
{\mathcal{Q}}^{\mathrm{cvx}}\mathfrak{B}
, with
the Scott topology of reversed inclusion
\supseteq
, is a linearly
separated continuous d-barycentric algebra; in particular, it is
convenient. Directed suprema are filtered intersections. Moreover,

{\mathcal{Q}}^{\mathrm{cvx}}\mathfrak{B}={\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}^{\mathrm{cvx}}\mathfrak{B}
, namely the topology of
{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}^{\mathrm{cvx}}\mathfrak{B}
coincides
with the Scott topology.

Proof.

Given any directed family
{(Q_{i})}_{i\in I}
in
{\mathcal{Q}}^{\mathrm{cvx}}\mathfrak{B}
,
namely any filtered family of non-empty convex compact saturated
sets, its intersection
Q\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\bigcap_{i\in I}Q_{i}
is compact
saturated and non-empty, because
\mathfrak{B}
is sober hence well-filtered

[
19
, Proposition 8.3.5]
. A
well-filtered

space is one in which if a filtered intersection

\bigcap_{i\in I}Q_{i}
of compact saturated subsets
Q_{i}
is
included in an open set
U
, then some
Q_{i}
is already included in

U
. In a well-filtered space, every such filtered intersection is
compact saturated
[
19
, Proposition 8.3.6]
. If
Q

were empty, then it would be covered by the empty family, hence some

Q_{i}
would, too, by well-filteredness; but that is impossible.
Finally, any intersection of convex sets is convex, so
Q
is in

{\mathcal{Q}}^{\mathrm{cvx}}\mathfrak{B}
. It is then clear that
Q
is the least upper bound of

{(Q_{i})}_{i\in I}
. Since least upper bound always exists,

{\mathcal{Q}}^{\mathrm{cvx}}\mathfrak{B}
is a dcpo, and directed suprema are filtered
intersections.

Every basic open subset
\Box^{\mathrm{cvx}}U
of
{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}^{\mathrm{cvx}}\mathfrak{B}
is then
Scott-open, since
\bigcap_{i\in Q_{i}}\subseteq U
implies

Q_{i}\subseteq U
for some
i\in I
, by well-filteredness. This
shows that
{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}^{\mathrm{cvx}}\mathfrak{B}
is a monotone convergence space.

Next, we claim that
{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}^{\mathrm{cvx}}\mathfrak{B}
is a c-space. Let us consider an
element
Q\in{\mathcal{Q}}^{\mathrm{cvx}}\mathfrak{B}
, and an open neighborhood
\mathcal{U}
of

Q
. The sets
\Box^{\mathrm{cvx}}U
,
U\in{\mathcal{O}}\mathfrak{B}
, form a base by
Lemma
9.17
, item 1. Hence,
\mathcal{U}
contains a
basic open subset
\Box^{\mathrm{cvx}}U
that contains
Q
; namely,

Q\subseteq U
. By Proposition
9.10
,
Q

has a base of convex compact saturated neighborhoods, so one of
them, call it
Q^{\prime}
, is included in
U
. Since

Q\in\Box^{\mathrm{cvx}}{(\text{int}({Q^{\prime}}))}
, and since

\Box^{\mathrm{cvx}}{(\text{int}({Q^{\prime}}))}
is included in the upward closure

\upc_{{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}^{\mathrm{cvx}}\mathfrak{B}}Q^{\prime}\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\{Q^{\prime\prime}\in{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}^{\mathrm{cvx}}\mathfrak{B}\mid Q^{\prime\prime}\subseteq Q^{\prime}\}
of
Q^{\prime}
in
{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}^{\mathrm{cvx}}\mathfrak{B}
,
Q
is in the interior of

\upc_{{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}^{\mathrm{cvx}}\mathfrak{B}}Q^{\prime}
. Moreover,

Q^{\prime}\in\Box^{\mathrm{cvx}}U\subseteq\mathcal{U}
. Hence
{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}^{\mathrm{cvx}}\mathfrak{B}
is a
c-space.

Every c-space that is a monotone convergence space is sober, and its
topology coincides with the Scott topology, by
Lemma
9.21
; hence
{\mathcal{Q}}^{\mathrm{cvx}}\mathfrak{B}={\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}^{\mathrm{cvx}}\mathfrak{B}
. Since
\mathfrak{B}

is linearly separated, so is
{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}^{\mathrm{cvx}}\mathfrak{B}
by
Proposition
9.20
, hence so is
{\mathcal{Q}}^{\mathrm{cvx}}\mathfrak{B}
, with
its Scott topology. Finally, a continuous d-barycentric algebra
that is linearly separated is convenient, by
Example
9.8
.
\Box

10
Barycenters, part 6

10.1
Barycenters, after Choquet

There is a general notion of barycenter of a continuous valuation,
imitating a definition due to Gustave Choquet
[
5
, Chapter 6,
26.2]
, and due to Ben Cohen, Martín Escardó
and Klaus Keimel
[
6
]
and to the author and Xiaodong
Jia
[
17
]
on semitopological cones
\mathfrak{C}
: a
barycenter

of
\nu\in{\mathbf{V}}\mathfrak{C}
is any point
x_{0}\in C
such that, for every
lower semicontinuous linear map
\Lambda\colon\mathfrak{C}\to\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
,

\Lambda(x_{0})=\int\Lambda\,d\nu
.

We adapt the definition to barycentric algebras and pointed
barycentric algebras.

Definition 10.1
(Barycenter)

Let
\mathfrak{B}
be a semitopological barycentric algebra, and

\nu\in{\mathbf{V}}_{1}\mathfrak{B}
. A
barycenter
of
\nu
is any point

x_{0}
of
\mathfrak{B}
such that for every lower semicontinuous affine map

\Lambda\colon\mathfrak{B}\to\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
,

\Lambda(x_{0})=\int\Lambda\,d\nu
.

If
\mathfrak{B}
is pointed, and
\nu\in{\mathbf{V}}_{\leq 1}\mathfrak{B}
, a

barycenter
of
\nu
is any point
x_{0}
of
\mathfrak{B}
such that for
every lower semicontinuous linear map

\Lambda\colon\mathfrak{B}\to\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
,

\Lambda(x_{0})=\int\Lambda\,d\nu
.

This makes quite a few competing definitions of barycenters. We argue
that any two of them coincide on the intersections of their domains of
definition. We start with the previous definition on cones

[
6
,
17
]
and
Definition
10.1
.

•

A semitopological cone
\mathfrak{C}
is also a pointed semitopological
barycentric algebra, hence the definition of

[
6
,
17
]
and the second part of
Definition
10.1
run a risk of being in
conflict. They are not because linear means the same on
\mathfrak{C}
,
whether we consider it as a cone or as a pointed barycentric
algebra, see Remark
4.5
.

•

A pointed barycentric algebra
\mathfrak{B}
is also a barycentric
algebra, so the two definitions of
Definition
10.1
appear to be in
conflict, at least for probability valuations
\nu\in{\mathbf{V}}_{1}\mathfrak{B}
.
If
x_{0}
is a barycenter in the sense of the first part of
Definition
10.1
, then it is one in the
sense of the second part, because every linear map is affine. If

x_{0}
is a barycenter in the sense of the second part, then let

\Lambda\colon\mathfrak{B}\to\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
be any lower semicontinuous affine
map. If
\Lambda(\bot)=\infty
, then
\Lambda
is the constant
map with value
\infty
by monotonicity, hence

\Lambda(x_{0})=\infty
, while

\int_{x\in\mathfrak{B}}\Lambda(x)\,d\nu=\infty.\nu(\mathfrak{B})=\infty
.
If
\Lambda(\bot)<\infty
, then the function
\Lambda^{\prime}
defined
by
\Lambda^{\prime}(x)\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\Lambda x)-\Lambda(\bot)
is affine and
strict, hence linear by Proposition
4.6
.
Therefore
\Lambda^{\prime}(x_{0})=\int_{x\in\mathfrak{B}}\Lambda^{\prime}(x)\,d\nu
, so

\Lambda(x_{0})=\Lambda^{\prime}(x_{0})+\Lambda(\bot)=\int_{x\in\mathfrak{B}}\Lambda^{\prime}(x)\,d\nu+\int_{x\in\mathfrak{B}}\Lambda(\bot)\,d\nu=\int_{x\in\mathfrak{B}}\Lambda(x)\,d\nu
.

We also have to compare the Choquet-like definitions of barycenters of
Definition
10.1
with our previous notions
of barycenters. We will return to this shortly. We first make a
trivial but important observation.

Lemma 10.2

On a linearly separated
T_{0}
semitopological barycentric algebra,
every probability valuation has at most one barycenter. Similarly
for subprobability valuations on linearly separated
T_{0}
pointed
semitopological barycentric algebras, for continuous valuations on
linearly separated
T_{0}
semitopological cones.

Proof.

Let
x_{0}
and
x_{1}
be two distinct barycenters of the same
probability valuation
\nu
(resp. subprobability valuation, resp. continuous valuation). The
T_{0}
property implies that

x_{0}\not\leq x_{1}
or
x_{1}\not\leq x_{0}
. By symmetry, let us
assume
x_{0}\not\leq x_{1}
. By linear separation, there is an affine
(resp. linear, by Lemma
6.17
, item 1) lower
semicontinuous map
\Lambda
with values in
\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
such that

\Lambda(x_{0})>\Lambda(x_{1})
. But by definition of barycenters,

\Lambda(x_{0})
are
\Lambda(x_{1})
are both equal to the integral
of
\Lambda
with respect to
\nu
, leading to a contradiction.

\Box

Things can go pretty badly without linear separation, as the following
example shows.

Example 10.3

Every point of the locally convex
T_{0}
topological barycentric
algebra
\mathfrak{B}^{-}
(see Example
3.24
and
Example
6.5
) is a barycenter of every
probability valuation on
\mathfrak{B}^{-}
. Indeed, by
Example
3.24
, item 2, every affine lower
semicontinuous map
\Lambda
, which must be semi-concave by
Fact
3.18
, is constant, say with value
c
. For every
point
x_{0}\in\mathfrak{B}
,
\Lambda(x_{0})=c
, and for every probability
valuation
\nu
on
\mathfrak{B}
,

\int_{x\in\mathfrak{B}}\Lambda(x)\,d\nu=\int_{x\in\mathfrak{B}}c\,d\nu=c\nu(\mathfrak{B})=c
.

We recall the barycenter maps
\beta
of
Theorem
6.44
, which maps simple (possibly
subnormalized or normalized) valuations

\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\delta_{x_{i}}
to their barycenters

\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\cdot x_{i}
, as defined in Definition and
Proposition
3.1
or Definition and
Proposition
4.41
in the case of (possibly pointed)
barycentric algebras. We also recall the map, also named
\beta
,
which sends (possibly subnormalized or normalized) point-continuous
valuations to what we claimed to be an extension of the notion of
barycenter in Theorem
6.52
.

Lemma 10.4

1.

For every simple normalized valuation
\nu
on a
semitopological barycentric algebra
\mathfrak{B}
,
\beta(\nu)
as defined
in Theorem
6.44
is a barycenter of

\nu
.

2.

For every simple subnormalized valuation
\nu
on a pointed
semitopological barycentric algebra
\mathfrak{B}
,
\beta(\nu)
as defined
in Theorem
6.44
is a barycenter of

\nu
.

3.

For every simple valuation
\nu
on a semitopological cone

\mathfrak{C}
,
\beta(\nu)
as defined in
Theorem
6.44
is a barycenter of
\nu
.

4.

For every weakly locally convex sober pointed topological
barycentric algebra
\mathfrak{B}
, for every

\nu\in{\mathbf{V}}_{\leq 1,\mathrm{p}}\mathfrak{B}
,
\beta(\nu)
as defined in
Theorem
6.52
is a barycenter of

\nu
.

5.

For every weakly locally convex, sober topological cone
\mathfrak{C}
,
for every
\nu\in{\mathbf{V}}_{\mathrm{p}}\mathfrak{C}
,
\beta(\nu)
as defined in
Theorem
6.52
is a
barycenter of
\nu
.

In all cases, this will be the unique barycenter under an additional
assumption of linear separability and
T_{0}
-ness, by
Lemma
10.2
.

Proof.

1,2,3
. Let
\nu\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\delta_{x_{i}}
be a
simple valuation, which we assume normalized in case 1,
subnormalized in case 2, and arbitrary in case 3. Let
\Lambda
be
an affine (in case 1) or linear (in cases 2 and 3) lower
semicontinuous map with values in
\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
. Then

\int_{x\in\mathfrak{C}}\Lambda(x)\,d\nu=\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\Lambda(x_{i})
since integration is linear, and that is equal to

\Lambda(\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\cdot x_{i})
, by Definition and
Proposition
3.1
, item 4 in case 1, by Definition and
Proposition
4.41
, item 2 in case 2, and by
definition of linearity in the case of cones (case 3). Therefore

\int_{x\in\mathfrak{C}}\Lambda(x)\,d\nu=\Lambda(\beta(\nu))
, so

\beta(\nu)
is a barycenter of
\nu
.

4,5
. Let
\Lambda
be a lower semicontinuous linear map from

\mathfrak{B}
(in case 4) or
\mathfrak{C}
(in case 5) to
\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
. By the first part
of the lemma, the functions that map every
\nu
in

{\mathbf{V}}_{\leq 1}\mathfrak{B}
(resp.
{\mathbf{V}}\mathfrak{C}
) to

\int_{x\in\mathfrak{B}\text{ (resp.\ $\mathfrak{C}$)}}\Lambda(x)\,d\nu
and

\Lambda\circ\beta
coincide on simple valuations. They are both
lower semicontinuous, by definition of the weak topology for the
first one, and because
\beta
is continuous
(Theorem
6.52
) for the second one.
By the universal property of sobrifications, and recalling that

{\mathbf{V}}_{\circledast,\mathrm{p}}X
is a sobrification of
{\mathbf{V}}_{\circledast,\mathrm{f}}X

for every space
X
and where
\circledast
is nothing or “
\leq 1
”
(Theorem
6.51
), they must coincide.

In items 4 and 5 of Lemma
10.4
, sobriety is
required: we will see in Example
10.10
an example of
point-continuous valuation on a weakly locally convex (in fact, even a
locally linear) cone that has no barycenter.

We recall the map
\eta^{**}_{\mathfrak{B}}\colon\mathfrak{B}\to\mathfrak{B}^{**}
, defined by

\eta^{**}_{\mathfrak{B}}(x)(\Lambda)\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\Lambda(x)
. We have the following
restatement of Definition
10.1
.

Fact 10.5

For every semitopological cone
\mathfrak{C}
(resp. pointed semitopological
algebra
\mathfrak{B}
, resp. semitopological algebra
\mathfrak{B}
), for every

\nu\in{\mathbf{V}}\mathfrak{C}
(resp.
{\mathbf{V}}_{\leq 1}\mathfrak{B}
, resp.
{\mathbf{V}}_{1}\mathfrak{B}
),
let

\beta^{*}(\nu)(\Lambda)\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\int_{x\in\mathfrak{B}}\Lambda(x)\,d\nu

for every
\Lambda\in\mathfrak{C}^{*}
(resp.
\mathfrak{B}^{*}
, resp.
\mathfrak{B}^{\star}
).

For every
\nu\in{\mathbf{V}}\mathfrak{C}
(resp.
{\mathbf{V}}_{\leq 1}\mathfrak{B}
, resp.
{\mathbf{V}}_{1}\mathfrak{B}
), a point
x_{0}
is a barycenter of
\nu
if and only if

\eta^{**}_{\mathfrak{B}}(x_{0})=\beta^{*}(\nu)
.

Lemma 10.6

Let
\mathfrak{C}
be a semitopological cone (resp. a pointed semitopological
algebra
\mathfrak{B}
, resp. a semitopological algebra
\mathfrak{B}
), and

\nu\in{\mathbf{V}}\mathfrak{C}
(resp.
{\mathbf{V}}_{\leq 1}\mathfrak{B}
, resp.
{\mathbf{V}}_{1}\mathfrak{B}
).
If
\nu
has a barycenter, then
\beta^{*}(\nu)
is lower
semicontinuous from
\mathfrak{C}^{*}
(resp.
\mathfrak{B}^{*}
, resp.
\mathfrak{B}^{\star}
) to

\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
.

Proof.

We deal with cones. The argument is the same for (pointed)
barycentric algebras. For every point
x_{0}
of
\mathfrak{C}
,

\eta^{**}_{\mathfrak{C}}(x_{0})
is an element of
\mathfrak{C}^{*}
, hence is in particular a
lower semicontinuous map. If
x_{0}
is a barycenter of
\nu
, then

\eta^{**}_{\mathfrak{C}}(x_{0})=\beta^{*}(\nu)
by Fact
10.5
, so

\beta^{*}(\nu)
must be lower semicontinuous.
\Box

Hence, if
\beta^{*}(\nu)
is not lower semicontinuous, then
\nu

cannot have any barycenter.

Conversely,
\beta^{*}(\nu)
is always a linear map from
\mathfrak{C}^{*}
(resp.
\mathfrak{B}^{*}
, resp.
\mathfrak{B}^{\star}
) to
\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
, hence if
\beta^{*}(\nu)
is
lower semicontinuous, then it is an element of
\mathfrak{C}^{**}
(resp.
\mathfrak{B}^{**}
, resp.
\mathfrak{B}^{\star*}
). Then, by Fact
10.5
,

\nu
has a barycenter if and only if that element is in the image of

\eta^{**}_{\mathfrak{C}}
(resp.
\eta^{**}_{\mathfrak{B}}
, resp.
\eta^{**}_{\mathfrak{B}}
).

Another easy observation is that, since
Definition
10.1
only mentions

affine
(or linear) lower semicontinuous maps
\Lambda
, the
notion of barycenter only depends on the weakScott topology on
\mathfrak{B}
.
Indeed,
\mathfrak{B}
and
\mathfrak{B}_{\mathit{wS}}
have exactly the same linear lower
semicontinuous maps to
\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
, by Remark
6.37
,
item (ii).

Fact 10.7

Let
\mathfrak{B}
be a semitopological barycentric algebra (resp. a pointed
semitopological barycentric algebra, resp. a semitopological cone).
For every probability (resp. subprobability, resp. continuous)
valuation
\nu
on
\mathfrak{B}
, the barycenters of
\nu
on
\mathfrak{B}
are
exactly the barycenters of the restriction of
\nu
to

{\mathcal{O}}(\mathfrak{B}_{\mathit{wS}})
on
\mathfrak{B}_{\mathit{wS}}
.
\Box

Given a continuous map
f\colon X\to Y
, the
image valuation

f[\nu]
of
\nu\in{\mathbf{V}}X
is the continuous valuation

V\in{\mathcal{O}}Y\mapsto\nu(f^{-1}(V))
. For every
h\in\mathcal{L}Y
,
we have the
change of variable
formula

\int h\,df[\nu]=\int(h\circ f)\,d\nu
, see for example

[
15
, Lemma 2.10
(vi)
]
; this is easy to prove using the
Choquet formula for the integral.

Lemma 10.8

Let
f\colon\mathfrak{C}\to\mathfrak{D}
be a linear continuous map between two
semitopological cones. For every
\nu\in{\mathbf{V}}\mathfrak{C}
with barycenter

x_{0}
in
\mathfrak{C}
,
f(x_{0})
is a barycenter of
f[\nu]
in
\mathfrak{D}
.

Similarly if
f\colon\mathfrak{B}\to\mathfrak{A}
is a linear continuous map
between pointed semitopological barycentric algebras and

\nu\in{\mathbf{V}}_{\leq 1}\mathfrak{B}
, or if
f\colon\mathfrak{B}\to\mathfrak{A}
is an
affine continuous map between not necessarily pointed
semitopological barycentric algebras and
\nu\in{\mathbf{V}}_{1}\mathfrak{B}
.

Proof.

For every linear lower semicontinuous map

\Lambda\colon\mathfrak{D}\to\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
,
\Lambda\circ f
is linear and
lower semicontinuous. Since
x_{0}
is a barycenter of
\nu
,

\Lambda(f(x_{0}))=\int_{x\in\mathfrak{C}}\Lambda(f(x))\,d\nu
, and
that is equal to
\int_{y\in\mathfrak{D}}\Lambda(y)\,df[\nu]
by the
change of variable formula. The argument is the same on (possibly
pointed) semitopological barycentric algebras, only taking
\Lambda

to be affine for not necessarily pointed barycentric algebras.
\Box

Corollary 10.9

Let
r\colon\mathfrak{C}\to\mathfrak{D}
be a linear retraction between
semitopological cones (resp. a linear retraction between pointed
semitopological barycentric algebras, resp. an affine retraction
between semitopological barycentric algebras), with associated section
s
.

1.

For every continuous (resp. subprobability, resp. probability) valuation
\nu
on
\mathfrak{D}
, if
x_{0}
is a
barycenter of
s[\nu]
in
\mathfrak{C}
, then
r(x_{0})
is a barycenter of

\nu
in
\mathfrak{D}
.

2.

Letting
\circledast
be nothing, resp. “
\leq 1
”, resp. “
1
”, for every function
\beta\colon{\mathbf{V}}_{\circledast}\mathfrak{C}\to\mathfrak{C}

that maps every element of
{\mathbf{V}}_{\circledast}\mathfrak{C}
to one of its
barycenters,

r\circ\beta\circ{\mathbf{V}}s\colon{\mathbf{V}}_{\circledast}\mathfrak{D}\to\mathfrak{D}
maps
every element of
{\mathbf{V}}_{\circledast}\mathfrak{D}
to one of its barycenters.

Proof.

1. By Lemma
10.8
,
r(x_{0})
is a barycenter of
r[s[\nu]]=\nu
.

2. For every
\nu\in{\mathbf{V}}_{\circledast}\mathfrak{D}
,
\beta({\mathbf{V}}s(\nu))=\beta(s[\nu])
is a barycenter of
s[\nu]
by assumption.
By item 1,
r(\beta({\mathbf{V}}s(\nu)))
is a barycenter of
\nu
.
\Box

Example 10.10

Let
X
be a continuous dcpo in its Scott topology, and let us
consider the subcone
\mathfrak{C}
of
\mathcal{L}X
consisting of the step
functions from a topological space
X
to
\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
, namely those
that take only finitely many values. Then, discrete valuations

\sum_{i\in I}a_{i}\delta_{g_{i}}
where each
g_{i}
is a step
function and
\sum_{i\in I}a_{i}g_{i}
takes infinitely many values
have no barycenter in
\mathfrak{C}
. In particular, there are topological,
locally linear cones on which barycenters may fail to exist.

Proof.

Let
\nu\in{\mathbf{V}}\mathfrak{C}
. If
f_{0}
is a barycenter of
\nu
, then by
Lemma
10.8
,
\eta^{**}_{\mathfrak{C}}(f_{0})
is a barycenter of

\eta^{**}_{\mathfrak{C}}[\nu]
. Now we can equate
\mathfrak{C}^{*}
with
{\mathbf{V}}X
, by
Example
6.28
, and then
\mathfrak{C}^{**}
with
\mathcal{L}X
, with the
weakScott topology, by Theorem
6.36
. By
Lemma
6.38
, the weakScott topology coincides with
the Scott topology. Since a continuous dcpo is locally compact,
hence core-compact, in the Scott topology, we can apply
Proposition 4.15 of
[
15
]
, which says that the function

x\mapsto\int_{f\in\mathcal{L}X}f(x)\,d\nu
is the unique
barycenter of
\nu
on
\mathcal{L}X
. Up to the isomorphism between

\mathfrak{C}
and
\mathfrak{C}^{**}
,
\eta^{**}_{\mathfrak{C}}(f_{0})
must therefore be equal to the
map
x\in X\mapsto\int_{f\in\mathcal{L}X}f(x)\,d\nu
. That
isomorphism is simply
\eta^{**}_{\mathfrak{C}}
, hence
f_{0}
must be that map.

When
\nu=\sum_{i\in I}a_{i}\delta_{g_{i}}
,
f_{0}
must then be the
map
x\in X\mapsto\sum_{i\in I}a_{i}g_{i}(x)
. If
f_{0}
takes
infinitely many values, then
f_{0}
cannot be in
\mathfrak{C}
, so
\nu
has
no barycenter.

Finally,
\mathfrak{C}
is locally linear, because, by
Lemma
6.38
, the topology of
\mathfrak{C}
is also the
topology of pointwise convergence, which is always locally linear
and topological.
\Box

Example 10.11

Let
X
be a topological space,
\circledast
be nothing, “
\leq 1
” or
“
1
”, and
\mathfrak{C}\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}{\mathbf{V}}_{\circledast,\mathrm{p}}X
.
\mathfrak{C}
is a cone if

\circledast
is nothing, otherwise a (possibly pointed) barycentric
algebra.
\mathfrak{C}
is locally affine (or locally linear), linearly
separated, topological and sober. Despite this, continuous
valuations in general have no barycenter on
\mathfrak{C}
. Indeed, given any
any continuous, non-point-continuous valuation
\mu
on
X

(subnormalized if
\circledast
is “
\leq 1
”, normalized if
\circledast
is
“
1
), that
i[\mu]\in{\mathbf{V}}_{\circledast}\mathfrak{C}
has no barycenter, where

i\colon X\mapsto\mathfrak{C}
is the map
x\mapsto\delta_{x}
; an example
of such a
\mu
is given in
[
16
]
.

Proof.

We first show that for every
\varpi\in{\mathbf{V}}_{\circledast}\mathfrak{C}
, if
\varpi

has a barycenter
\nu_{0}
on
\mathfrak{C}
, then for every open subset
U
of

X
,
\nu_{0}(U)=\int_{\nu\in\mathfrak{C}}\nu(U)\,d\varpi
. By
definition, we must have

\Lambda(\nu_{0})=\int_{\nu\in\mathfrak{C}}\Lambda(\nu)\,d\varpi
for
every continuous linear (affine if
\circledast
is “
1
”) map
\Lambda

from
\mathfrak{C}
to
{(\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+})}_{\sigma}
. For each given open subset
U
of

X
, let us define
\Lambda\colon{\mathbf{V}}_{\circledast,\mathrm{p}}X\to\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}

by
\Lambda(\nu)\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\nu(U)
. This is linear if
\circledast
is
nothing or “
\leq 1
”, and affine if
\circledast
is “
1
”. This is
continuous since
\Lambda^{-1}(]r,\infty])=[U>r]
. The result
follows.

The map
i
is continuous, since
i^{-1}([U>r])
is equal to
U

if
0\leq r<1
, and to
\emptyset
otherwise. We have just argued
that if
i[\mu]
has a barycenter
\nu_{0}
on
\mathfrak{C}
, then for every
open subset
U
of
X
,

\nu_{0}(U)=\int_{\nu\in\mathfrak{C}}\nu(U)\,di[\mu]=\int_{x\in X}i(x)(U)\,d\mu
, by the change of variable formula. Now

i(x)(U)=\delta_{x}(U)=\chi_{U}(x)
, so
\nu_{0}(U)=\mu(U)
.
Since
U
is arbitrary,
\nu_{0}=\mu
. However a barycenter on
\mathfrak{C}

must be in
\mathfrak{C}
, so
\nu_{0}
must be in
\mathfrak{C}={\mathbf{V}}_{\circledast,\mathrm{p}}X
,
but
\mu
is not point-continuous, and this is impossible.

We conclude by recalling that
{\mathbf{V}}_{\mathrm{p}}X
is sober by

[
23
, Proposition 5.1]
.
{\mathbf{V}}_{\leq 1,\mathrm{p}}X
and

{\mathbf{V}}_{1,\mathrm{p}}X
are sober, too, because they are Skula-closed in

{\mathbf{V}}_{\mathrm{p}}X
. When
\circledast
is nothing,
{\mathbf{V}}_{\circledast,\mathrm{p}}X
is a
topological cone; this was mentioned in
Example
2.5
. When
\circledast
is “
\leq 1
” or
“
1
”, it is a topological barycentric algebra by
Example
3.10
. It was also mentioned that

{\mathbf{V}}_{\circledast,\mathrm{p}}X
is locally convex in
Example
6.2
, in fact locally linear (when

\circledast
is nothing, see Example
6.12
, or when
\circledast

is “
\leq 1
”, see Example
6.13
) or locally
affine (when
\circledast
is “
1
”, see Example
6.14
).
It is linearly separated, as mentioned in
Example
6.18
.
\Box

10.2
Barycenters and convex sets

We will see that barycenters of probability valuations supported on
various kinds of convex sets
A
are in
A
. We first need the
following easy remarks. A continuous valuation
\nu
on a space
X

is
supported
on a subset
A
of
X
if and only if for all

U,V\in{\mathcal{O}}X
such that
U\cap A=V\cap A
,

\nu(U)=\nu(V)
.

Fact 10.12

Let
f\colon X\to Y
be a continuous map, and
A
be a subset of

X
. For every continuous valuation
\nu
on
X
, if
\nu
is
supported on
A
then
f[\nu]
is supported on
f[A]
, on

cl(f[A])
, and on
\upc f[A]
.

Indeed, if
U\cap f[A]=V\cap f[A]
, then

f^{-1}(U)\cap A=f^{-1}(V)\cap A
, so

f[\nu](U)=\nu(f^{-1}(U))=\nu(f^{-1}(V))=f[\nu](V)
.
Then, any continuous valuation supported on a set is also supported on
any larger set, such as
cl(f[A])
or
\upc f[A]
.

Fact 10.13

Let
\mathfrak{B}
be a (resp. pointed) semitopological barycentric algebra,

\mathfrak{C}
be a semitopological cone, and
f\colon\mathfrak{B}\to\mathfrak{C}
be a
continuous map. For every
\nu\in{\mathbf{V}}_{1}\mathfrak{B}
(resp.
\nu\in{\mathbf{V}}_{\leq 1}\mathfrak{B}
), for every point
x\in\mathfrak{B}
, if
x
is a
barycenter of
\nu
then
f(x)
is a barycenter of
f[\nu]
.

Indeed, for every linear lower semicontinuous map we have

\Lambda\colon\mathfrak{C}\to\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
,

\int_{y\in\mathfrak{C}}\Lambda(y)\,df[\nu]=\int_{x\in\mathfrak{B}}\Lambda(f(x))\,d\nu
by the change of variable formula, and the latter is
equal to
\Lambda(f(x_{0}))
since
\Lambda\circ f
is affine (resp. linear) and lower semicontinuous, and by definition of barycenters.

Fact 10.14

Let
\nu
be a continuous valuation on a space
X
, supported on a
subset
A
. For any two maps
f,g\in\mathcal{L}X
such that
f(x)\leq g(x)
for every
x\in A
,
\int f\,d\nu\leq\int g\,d\nu
.

Indeed, we first observe that for all
U,V\in{\mathcal{O}}X
such that

U\cap A\subseteq V\cap A
,
\nu(U)\leq\nu(V)
: if

U\cap A\subseteq V\cap A
, then

U\cap A=(U\cap A)\cap(V\cap A)=(U\cap V)\cap A
, so

\nu(U)=\nu(U\cap V)\leq\nu(V)
. Using the Choquet formula,

\int f\,d\nu=\int_{0}^{\infty}\nu(f^{-1}(]t,\infty]))\,dt
.
Since
f\leq g
on
A
,

A\cap f^{-1}(]t,\infty])\subseteq A\cap g^{-1}(]t,\infty])

for every
t\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
. Therefore

\int f\,d\nu\leq\int_{0}^{\infty}\nu(g^{-1}(]t,\infty]))\,dt=\int g\,d\nu
.

Definition 10.15
(Lc-embeddable)

An
lc-embeddable barycentric algebra
is a semitopological
barycentric algebra that embeds through an affine topological
embedding into a locally convex semitopological cone.

A
pointed
lc-embeddable barycentric algebra is an lc-embeddable
barycentric algebra that is also pointed. We do not require the
embedding to be linear in this case, just affine.

Remark 10.16

By Definition and Lemma
6.21
, every locally affine
T_{0}

semitopological barycentric algebra is lc-embeddable.

Lemma 10.17

1.

Given any non-empty closed convex subset
C
of a locally
convex semitopological cone
\mathfrak{C}
, the barycenters of all
subprobability valuations supported on
C
are in
C
.

2.

The same result holds, more generally, if we replace
\mathfrak{C}
by a
pointed lc-embeddable barycentric algebra.

3.

Given any closed convex subset
C
of an lc-embeddable
barycentric algebra (not necessarily pointed)
\mathfrak{B}
, the
barycenters of all probability valuations supported on
C
are in

C
.

Proof.

1. Let
\nu
be a subprobability valuation supported on
C
, and

x_{0}
be a barycenter of
\nu
. We assume that
x_{0}\not\in C
, and
we aim for a contradiction. By
Proposition
8.3
, item 2, there is an open
half-space
H
containing
x_{0}
and disjoint from
C
. The upper
Minkowski functional
\Lambda\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}M^{H}
is linear and lower
semicontinuous. By definition of barycenters,

\int_{x\in\mathfrak{C}}\Lambda(x)\,d\nu=\Lambda(x_{0})
. However,

\Lambda(x)\leq 1
for every
x\in C
, and
\nu
is supported on

C
. By Fact
10.14
,

\int_{x\in\mathfrak{C}}\Lambda(x)\,d\nu\leq\int_{x\in\mathfrak{C}}1\,d\nu\leq 1
(the last inequality being because
\nu
is a subprobability
valuation), and that contradicts
\Lambda(x_{0})>1
.

2. Let
C
be a non-empty closed convex subset of a pointed
semitopological barycentric algebra
\mathfrak{B}
with an affine topological
embedding
i
into a locally convex semitopological cone
\mathfrak{C}

instead. Then
i[C]
is convex, and
cl(i[C])
is closed and
convex by Definition and Proposition
5.6
. As
above, we let
x_{0}
be a barycenter of a subprobability valuation

\nu
supported on
C
, and we assume that
x_{0}\not\in C
. (We
need
\mathfrak{B}
to be pointed so that this barycenter makes sense.)

We note that
i[\nu]
is supported on
cl(i[C])
by
Fact
10.12
. Also,
i(x_{0})
is a barycenter of

i[\nu]
, by Fact
10.13
.

By item 1,
i(x_{0})
must be in
cl(i[C])
. However, since
i
is
a topological embedding, there is an open subset
V
of
\mathfrak{C}
such
that
\mathfrak{B}\smallsetminus C=i^{-1}(V)
. Hence
x_{0}\in i^{-1}(V)
since

x_{0}\not\in C
, in other words
i(x_{0})\in V
. Since

i(x_{0})\in cl(i[C])
,
V
intersects
cl(i[C])
, and therefore
also
i[C]
; hence
i^{-1}(V)
intersects
C
, which is impossible
since
i^{-1}(V)=\mathfrak{B}\smallsetminus C
.

3. The argument is the same as in item 2 when
C
is non-empty.
When
C
is empty, the statement is vacuously true: if
\nu
is
supported on the empty set, then
\nu
must be the zero valuation,
and cannot be a probability valuation.
\Box

In non-Hausdorff spaces, compact saturated subsets may fail to be
closed, hence we need a separate result for compact saturated convex
subsets.

Lemma 10.18

1.

Given a compact saturated convex subset
Q
of a locally
convex semitopological cone
\mathfrak{C}
, the barycenters of all
continuous valuations
\nu
with
\nu(X)\geq 1
and supported on

Q
are in
Q
.

2.

Given a compact saturated convex subset
Q
of an
lc-embeddable barycentric algebra
\mathfrak{B}
, the barycenters of all
probability valuations supported on
Q
are in
Q
.

Proof.

1. Let
\nu
be a continuous valuation supported on
Q
such that

\nu(\mathfrak{C})\geq 1
, and
x_{0}
be some barycenter of
\nu
. Let us
assume that
x_{0}
is not in
Q
. By the strict separation theorem

[
30
, Theorem 10.5]
with
C\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\dc x_{0}
, there
is a lower semicontinuous linear map
\Lambda\colon\mathfrak{C}\to\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}

and a constant
a>1
such that
\Lambda(x)\leq 1
for every

x\in C
and
\Lambda(x)\geq a
for every
x\in Q
. By
definition of barycenters,

\int_{x\in\mathfrak{C}}\Lambda(x)\,d\nu=\Lambda(x_{0})
. However,

\Lambda(x)\geq a
for every
x\in Q
, and
\nu
is supported on

Q
. By Fact
10.14

\int_{x\in\mathfrak{C}}\Lambda(x)\,d\nu\geq\int_{x\in\mathfrak{C}}a\,d\nu\geq a
(since
\nu(\mathfrak{C})\geq 1
), and that contradicts

\Lambda(x_{0})\leq 1
.

2. If
Q
is a compact saturated convex subset of a semitopological
barycentric algebra
\mathfrak{B}
with an affine topological embedding
i

into a locally convex semitopological cone
\mathfrak{C}
instead, then

i[Q]
is convex, and
\upc i[Q]
is compact saturated and
convex.

Let
x_{0}
be a barycenter of a probability valuation
\nu
supported
on
Q
. Using Fact
10.12
,
i[\nu]
is supported on

\upc i[Q]
. Also,
i(x_{0})
is a barycenter of
i[\nu]
, by
Fact
10.13
. By item 1,
i(x_{0})
must be in

\upc i[Q]
. Hence there is a point
x\in Q
such that

i(x)\leq i(x_{0})
, whence
x\leq x_{0}
since every topological
embedding is a preorder embedding, and therefore
x_{0}\in Q
since

Q
is upwards-closed.
\Box

10.3
A general barycenter existence theorem

We will imitate the main theorem of
[
17
]
, which shows the
existence of barycenters of continuous valuations on convenient cones,
and we will generalize it to convenient (possibly pointed) barycentric
algebras.

Let
\mathop{\mathrm{Min}}Q
denote the set of minimal elements of a subset
Q
of a
topological space. For every compact saturated subset
Q
of a
topological space
X
,
Q=\upc\mathop{\mathrm{Min}}Q
; see, for example, the
comment after Proposition 3.2 in
[
17
]
. The following lemma
is a variant of
[
17
, Lemma 3.3]
.

Lemma 10.19

Let
\mathfrak{B}
be a linearly separated semitopological barycentric algebra
and
Q
be a non-empty compact saturated subset of
\mathfrak{B}
. If the map

\varphi\colon\Lambda\mapsto\min_{x\in Q}\Lambda(x)
is
additive from
\mathfrak{B}^{\star}
to
\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
, then
Q=\upc x
for some

x\in\mathfrak{B}
.

Proof.

The fact that we have not assumed
\mathfrak{B}
to be
T_{0}
will incur a bit
of overhead. Let us write
x\equiv y
if and only if
x\leq y

and
y\leq x
. We have
Q=\upc\mathop{\mathrm{Min}}Q
.
\mathop{\mathrm{Min}}Q
is a union of
equivalence classes with respect to
\equiv
; using the Axiom of
Choice, we pick one element from each, and we collect all those
elements into one set which we call
A
. (If
\mathfrak{B}
is
T_{0}
, then

A
is simply
\mathop{\mathrm{Min}}Q
.) Then
A
is a collection of pairwise
incomparable points, and
Q=\upc A
.

Let us assume that
Q
cannot be written as
\upc x
for any

x\in\mathfrak{B}
, or equivalently, that
A
is not reduced to just one
point.
A
is non-empty, since
Q
is non-empty, so for every

x\in A
,
A
contains a distinct, hence incomparable, point

\overline{x}
.

Since
\mathfrak{B}
is linearly separated, there is an affine lower
semicontinuous map
\Lambda_{x}\colon\mathfrak{B}\to\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
such that

\Lambda_{x}(x)>1
and
\Lambda_{x}(\overline{x})\leq 1
, for each

x\in A
. The family
{(\Lambda_{x}^{-1}(]1,\infty]))}_{x\in A}

is an open cover of
A
, hence of
Q=\upc A
. Since
Q
is
compact, there is a finite subset
E
of
A
such that

Q\subseteq\bigcup_{x\in E}\Lambda_{x}^{-1}(]1,\infty])
. We
note that
E
is non-empty, since
Q
is non-empty.

Let
\Lambda\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\sum_{x\in E}\Lambda_{x}
. This is also an
affine lower semicontinuous map from
\mathfrak{B}
to
\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
. Since

\varphi
is additive by assumption, and the sum defining
\Lambda

is non-empty,

\varphi(\Lambda)=\sum_{x\in E}\varphi(\Lambda_{x})
.

Relying on the compactness of
Q
,

\varphi(\Lambda)=\min_{z\in Q}\Lambda(z)
is equal to

\Lambda(x_{0})
for some
x_{0}\in Q
. Since

Q\subseteq\bigcup_{x\in E}\Lambda_{x}^{-1}(]1,\infty])
, there
is an
x\in E
such that
x_{0}\in\Lambda_{x}^{-1}(]1,\infty])
,
namely such that
\Lambda_{x}(x_{0})>1
. Since
\overline{x}
is not
in
\Lambda_{x}^{-1}(]1,\infty])
,
\Lambda_{x}(\overline{x})\leq 1
,
and therefore

\varphi(\Lambda_{x})=\min_{z\in Q}\Lambda_{x}(z)\leq 1<\Lambda_{x}(x_{0})
. For all the other points
y
of
E
,

\varphi(\Lambda_{y})=\min_{z\in Q}\Lambda_{y}(z)\leq\Lambda_{y}(x_{0})
, so, summing up, we obtain that

\varphi(\Lambda)=\varphi(\Lambda_{x})+\sum_{y\in E\smallsetminus\{x\}}\varphi(\Lambda_{y})<\Lambda_{x}(x_{0})+\sum_{y\in E\smallsetminus\{x\}}\Lambda_{y}(x_{0})=\Lambda(x_{0})=\varphi(\Lambda)
, a
contradiction.
\Box

Theorem 10.20
(Barycenter existence theorem)

Let
\circledast
be nothing, “
\leq 1
” or “
1
”. Let
\mathfrak{B}
be a
convenient barycentric algebra
\mathfrak{B}
, and let us assume that
\mathfrak{B}
is
a cone if
\circledast
is nothing, resp. is pointed if
\circledast
is
“
\leq 1
”, resp. is compact and non-empty if
\circledast
is “
1
”.

Every continuous valuation
\nu
(resp. every subprobability
valuation if
\circledast
is “
\leq 1
”, resp. every probability
valuation if
\circledast
is “
1
”) on
\mathfrak{B}
has a unique barycenter

\beta(\nu)
, and the barycenter map
\beta
is continuous.

Proof.

{\mathcal{Q}}^{\mathrm{cvx}}\mathfrak{B}
is a linearly separated continuous d-barycentric
algebra by Proposition
9.22
, and coincides with

{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}^{\mathrm{cvx}}\mathfrak{B}
. When
\circledast
is “
1
”, we have assumed
\mathfrak{B}
compact
and non-empty, and
\mathfrak{B}
is also compact and non-empty since it is
pointed in the other cases; so
{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}^{\mathrm{cvx}}\mathfrak{B}
is pointed in all cases, by
Lemma
9.17
, item 3. Additionally, if
\circledast
is
nothing, then
\mathfrak{B}
is a topological cone, since every continuous
d-cone is topological by Proposition
6.6
,
and therefore
{\mathcal{Q}}^{\mathrm{cvx}}\mathfrak{B}={\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}^{\mathrm{cvx}}\mathfrak{B}
is a topological cone

[
30
, Theorem 11]
.

By Theorem
6.52
, there is a unique
linear (affine if
\circledast
is “
1
”) lower semicontinuous map

\alpha\colon{\mathbf{V}}_{\circledast,\mathrm{p}}{({\mathcal{Q}}^{\mathrm{cvx}}\mathfrak{B})}\to{\mathcal{Q}}^{\mathrm{cvx}}\mathfrak{B}
such
that
\alpha(\delta_{Q})=Q
for every
Q\in{\mathcal{Q}}^{\mathrm{cvx}}\mathfrak{B}
.
Theorem 4.1 of
[
23
]
states that continuous valuations
and point-continuous valuations coincide on any locally finitary
compact space; this applies to any c-space, hence to any continuous
dcpo with its Scott topology in particular, and therefore to

{\mathcal{Q}}^{\mathrm{cvx}}\mathfrak{B}
. Hence
\alpha
is really a continuous linear (resp. affine if
\circledast
is “
1
”) map from
{\mathbf{V}}_{\circledast}{({\mathcal{Q}}^{\mathrm{cvx}}\mathfrak{B})}

to
{\mathcal{Q}}^{\mathrm{cvx}}\mathfrak{B}
. Additionally,
\alpha
maps every continuous
valuation to a barycenter by Lemma
10.4
, item 4.

Let us consider any element
\nu
of
{\mathbf{V}}_{\circledast}\mathfrak{B}
. Since

\eta^{\mathcal{Q}}
is continuous (Lemma
9.16
), we can
form the image continuous valuation
\eta^{\mathcal{Q}}[\nu]
on

{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}^{\mathrm{cvx}}\mathfrak{B}
. We let
Q\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\alpha(\eta^{\mathcal{Q}}[\nu])
.

For every affine (if
\circledast
is “
1
”, linear otherwise) lower
semicontinuous map
\Lambda\colon\mathfrak{B}\to\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
, by
Lemma
9.19
,
\min\Lambda
is an affine (if

\circledast
is “
1
”, linear otherwise) lower semicontinuous map from

{\mathcal{Q}}^{\mathrm{cvx}}\mathfrak{B}
to
\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
. Since
\alpha
maps every element of

{\mathbf{V}}_{\circledast}{({\mathcal{Q}}^{\mathrm{cvx}}\mathfrak{B})}
to its barycenter, and since the
function
\min\Lambda
of Lemma
9.19
is
affine (if
\circledast
is “
1
”, linear otherwise) and lower
semicontinuous, we must have

\min\Lambda(Q)=\int_{Q^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{Q}}^{\mathrm{cvx}}\mathfrak{B}}\min\Lambda(Q^{\prime})\,d\eta^{\mathcal{Q}}[\nu]
. By the change of variable formula, the
latter integral is equal to

\int_{x\in\mathfrak{B}}\min\Lambda(\upc x)\,d\nu=\int_{x\in\mathfrak{B}}\Lambda(x)\,d\nu
. Also,

\min\Lambda(Q)=\min_{x\in Q}\Lambda(x)=\varphi(\Lambda)
,
taking the notation
\varphi
from Lemma
10.19
. In other
words,
\varphi
is the function

\Lambda\mapsto\int_{x\in\mathfrak{B}}\Lambda(x)\,d\nu
, and that is an
additive function since integration with respect to continuous
valuations is additive in its integrated function. By
Lemma
10.19
(which applies since
\mathfrak{B}
is linearly
separated),
Q=\upc x_{\nu}
for some
x_{\nu}\in\mathfrak{B}
.

For every affine (if
\circledast
is “
1
”, linear otherwise) lower
semicontinuous map
\Lambda\colon\mathfrak{B}\to\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
, the equation

\min\Lambda(Q)=\int_{x\in\mathfrak{B}}\Lambda(x)\,d\nu
then
simplifies to

\Lambda(x_{\nu})=\int_{x\in\mathfrak{B}}\Lambda(x)\,d\nu
, showing that

x_{\nu}
is a barycenter of
\nu
. It is unique by
Lemma
10.2
.

Let us define
\beta
by
\beta(\nu)\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}x_{\nu}
, for every

\nu\in{\mathbf{V}}_{\circledast}\mathfrak{B}
. We recall that
\upc x_{\nu}
is what
we have written as
Q
, which is defined as

\alpha(\eta^{\mathcal{Q}}[\nu])
. We claim that
\beta
is continuous.
For every open subset
U
of
\mathfrak{B}
, for every

\nu\in{\mathbf{V}}_{\circledast}\mathfrak{B}
,
\beta(\nu)\in U
if and only if

\alpha(\eta^{\mathcal{Q}}[\nu])\in\Box^{\mathrm{cvx}}U
. In other words,

\beta^{-1}(U)=(\alpha\circ{\mathbf{V}}_{\circledast}(\eta^{\mathcal{Q}}))^{-1}(\Box^{\mathrm{cvx}}U)
, which is open since
\alpha
and

\eta^{\mathcal{Q}}
are continuous.
\Box
